LAYER 6, THE CELLULAR LAYER: THE OTHER CELLS

The Non-Neuronal Cells of the Human Nervous and Endocrine System

A Complete, Comprehensive, and Thorough Manuscript

Companion volume to the five-volume neuronal catalogue (302 neuron types) in the Certified_Neuroscientist corpus of D. R. Dison, Founder, Artificial Intelligence University (AIU).

Date of compilation: August 9, 2026.


INTRODUCTION: THE OTHER HALF OF THE BRAIN

The five-volume neuronal catalogue told the story of the 86 billion cells that think.

This volume tells the story of the roughly 85 billion cells that make thinking physically possible - and of a small elite among them that, we now know, think along.

The adult human brain contains close to one non-neuronal cell for every neuron, though the ratio varies enormously by region: the cerebral cortex holds about 3.8 non-neuronal cells per neuron, while the tightly packed cerebellum holds about 0.2.

Beyond the skull, the count grows further: every peripheral nerve, every ganglion (a cluster of neuron cell bodies outside the central nervous system), every sense organ, and every endocrine gland (a gland that releases hormones, chemical messengers, into the bloodstream) is staffed overwhelmingly by cells that are not neurons.

This manuscript catalogues all of them: eighty-five cell types and families, each given the same full chapter treatment - the same twenty aspects, in the same order - as the neurons received in the parent catalogue.

Where an aspect genuinely cannot apply to a non-neuronal cell, the chapter says so plainly and says why, because an honest blank is more informative than a forced answer.

Two tags divide the roster.

• (COG) marks a cell type that participates in COGNITION: it actively shapes computation, synaptic plasticity (the strengthening and weakening of connections, which is the physical basis of learning), memory, attention, or the moment-to-moment processing of information.

• (NON-COG) marks a cell type that serves a substrate role: structure, insulation, nourishment, defence, plumbing, barrier duty, hormone manufacture, or developmental scaffolding.

Only cells tagged (COG) receive the two computational sections, ENGLISH READABLE CODE and CODE SAMPLE, because only they perform a computation worth simulating in its own right.

A generation ago every chapter in this volume would have been tagged (NON-COG), because glia (from the Greek word for glue, the non-neuronal cells of nervous tissue) were thought to be passive packing material.

The modern evidence has overturned that verdict for eight cell types, and the chapters below present the overturning honestly, with numbers.

Seven hybrid secretory cells already hold full chapters in the neuronal catalogue, because they are genuine borderline cases - simultaneously neuron-like and gland-like.

They appear here as condensed CROSS-REFERENCE chapters, so that this volume is complete on its own while their full treatment lives in the neuron volumes.

The plan of the volume follows the outline that preceded it.

• Part One: the glia of the central nervous system - astrocytes, the oligodendrocyte lineage, microglia, and the ependymal family.

• Part Two: the glia of the peripheral nervous system.

• Part Three: the supporting cells of the sense organs.

• Part Four: the developmental and stem cell populations.

• Part Five: the vascular, barrier, and meningeal cells.

• Part Six: the immune cells of the nervous system borders.

• Part Seven: the endocrine cells of the major glands.

• Part Eight: the diffuse neuroendocrine system.

• Part Nine: the hybrid and cross-reference roster.

• Part Ten: the cognitive scoreboard, and what the whole roster teaches.


PART ONE. THE GLIA OF THE CENTRAL NERVOUS SYSTEM


SECTION 1. THE ASTROCYTES - THE STAR CELLS

The neuronal catalogue told the story of the cells that fire, but every one of those cells lives inside a second, silent society that this volume now brings into the light.

The astrocyte, named from the Greek for star cell, is the most abundant and most versatile glial cell of the central nervous system (CNS), the brain and spinal cord.

A single human astrocyte of the cortex may touch on the order of a million synapses, the junctions where one neuron signals another, making it the third partner of the tripartite synapse alongside the sending and receiving terminals.

Astrocytes clear neurotransmitter from the synaptic cleft, buffer potassium ions, feed neurons lactate, steer local blood flow, hold up the blood-brain barrier, and release gliotransmitters, glial signalling molecules such as glutamate, adenosine triphosphate (ATP) and D-serine, that tune synaptic strength.

They do not spike, yet they compute in slow calcium, integrating neuronal activity over seconds and answering with chemistry rather than voltage.

Because silencing astrocytes demonstrably impairs learning and memory, several members of this family carry the (COG) tag, and two of the six cells in this section exist only in humans and their closest relatives.

Read these six chapters as a tour from the grey matter to the white, then up through the human-specific layers of the cortex, and finally into the cerebellum, where the family reaches its most geometrically disciplined forms.


CHAPTER 1. PROTOPLASMIC ASTROCYTE (Grey-Matter Domain Astrocyte) (The Million-Synapse Gardener Cell) (COG)

Somewhere in your cortex right now, a single cell is listening in on roughly a million conversations at once, and no neuron can match that audience.

It does not interrupt with spikes; it answers in slow tides of calcium and puffs of chemistry that decide which conversations grow louder and which fade.

Every synapse in the grey matter is assigned to exactly one of these cells, the way every house is assigned to exactly one postal district.

Million-Synapse names its audience: one human protoplasmic astrocyte contacts on the order of 270,000 to two million synapses.

Gardener names its work: it feeds, prunes, waters with blood flow, and clears the chemical weeds of glutamate and potassium from its plot.

Cell, rather than Neuron, marks it as glia, the non-firing partner class of the nervous system.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The protoplasmic astrocyte is the bushy astrocyte of the grey matter, the neuron-body-rich tissue, and it is the workhorse of the family, tiling the cortex and other grey regions in non-overlapping territories called domains.

Within its domain it wraps synapses in fine leaflets, clears the excitatory transmitter glutamate through the excitatory amino acid transporters EAAT1 and EAAT2, siphons excess potassium ions away from busy neurons, shuttles lactate fuel from blood to neuron, ends its larger processes in endfeet on blood vessels to command local blood flow, and releases gliotransmitters that strengthen or weaken the synapses it enwraps.

Because blocking its calcium signalling or its D-serine release measurably impairs long-term potentiation and learning, it is tagged (COG).

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

A small round cell body sprouts a handful of primary branches that ramify into thousands of progressively finer processes, ending in leaflets so thin they escape light microscopy, giving the whole cell the look of a dense spongy bush.

Human cells extend roughly tenfold more primary processes positive for glial fibrillary acidic protein (GFAP), the astrocyte's signature skeleton protein, than rodent cells, and their fine processes are correspondingly more elaborate.

Each cell keeps to its own territory, overlapping with its neighbours only at the outermost few percent of its volume.

SIZE:

The human cell's domain is about 143 micrometres across, a micrometre being a thousandth of a millimetre, against about 56 micrometres in the mouse, a 2.6-fold difference in diameter and roughly 16.5-fold in volume.

The cell body itself is a modest 10 to 20 micrometres, and the synapse count per domain is estimated at 270,000 to 2 million in human cortex against 20,000 to 120,000 in rodent, moderate confidence because the human figure is scaled from rodent synapse density rather than counted directly.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Layer-specific molecular subtypes are emerging from single-cell gene reading, with upper-layer and deep-layer cortical astrocytes differing in gene expression, and hippocampal, striatal and cortical protoplasmic astrocytes forming regionally distinct populations.

Treat the protoplasmic astrocyte as one morphological class hiding a growing molecular family.

WHERE IT LIVES:

All grey matter of the central nervous system (CNS): cortical layers 2 through 6, hippocampus, striatum, thalamus, brainstem nuclei and spinal grey matter.

Its endfeet tile an explicit interface, the outer wall of every capillary and arteriole in its territory, making it the middleman between blood and neuron.

NUMBER:

The human brain holds roughly 85 billion non-neuronal cells alongside its 86 billion neurons, of which glia in the cerebral cortex outnumber neurons about 3.8 to 1.

Astrocytes are commonly estimated at 20 to 40 percent of glia, giving a rough figure of 10 to 20 billion astrocytes in the brain, the majority protoplasmic, low to moderate confidence because direct whole-brain astrocyte counts do not yet exist.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

The most hyperpolarised cell in the brain, resting near minus 85 millivolts, within a few millivolts of the potassium equilibrium potential, because its membrane is dominated by the inwardly rectifying potassium channel Kir4.1.

Input resistance is remarkably low, often under 10 megohms, because gap junctions couple it into a syncytium, a continuous multicellular network, and it fires no action potentials whatsoever.

Its excitability lives instead in calcium: transients in fine processes on a timescale of hundreds of milliseconds to seconds, and intercellular calcium waves travelling at roughly 10 to 27 micrometres per second in tissue, with in vivo network events reported up to about 60 micrometres per second.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

It is the third element of the tripartite synapse, its leaflets touching the majority of excitatory synapses in its domain and sensing spillover glutamate through metabotropic receptors, chiefly mGluR3 in adult humans.

It couples to neighbouring astrocytes through gap junctions built of connexin 43 and connexin 30, direct cell-to-cell pores that pass ions and small molecules.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that astrocytes possess no axon, only branched processes that conduct no action potential.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

It releases the gliotransmitters glutamate, adenosine triphosphate (ATP), which extracellular enzymes convert to the calming molecule adenosine, and D-serine, the co-agonist that the N-methyl-D-aspartate (NMDA) receptor requires before it will open.

It is also the brain's principal glutamate recycler, converting reclaimed glutamate to glutamine and handing it back to neurons.

MYELINATION:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that the cell has no axon to myelinate and produces no myelin.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

The astrocyte is the brain's glycogen bank, the only CNS cell storing appreciable glycogen, and it runs much of its own metabolism glycolytically so it can export lactate to neurons, the astrocyte-neuron lactate shuttle.

Glutamate uptake is its single largest energy cost, since each transported glutamate rides in with three sodium ions that must be pumped back out by the ATP-driven sodium pump.

PLASTICITY:

Its leaflets advance and retract around synapses over minutes to hours, tightening coverage during learning and loosening it during states such as lactation in the hypothalamus.

Its calcium responses themselves show experience-dependent change, and its D-serine supply gates whether nearby synapses can undergo long-term potentiation at all.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Endothelial cells and pericytes of the capillaries feed it glucose across the blood-brain barrier it helps maintain, microglia clear its debris and modulate its inflammatory state, and its gap-junction partners share its ionic and metabolic load across the network.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Delete this one type and grey matter fails within minutes: glutamate accumulates to excitotoxic levels, potassium builds until neurons depolarise and fall silent, blood flow stops tracking activity, and the blood-brain barrier leaks.

Every cortical computation described in the neuronal catalogue runs inside conditions this cell curates.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Its signature trick is integration without spikes: each fine process runs its own local calcium microdomain, summing the activity of the synapses it wraps, and only strong or coordinated activity escalates to a whole-cell calcium surge and then to a wave crossing into neighbouring astrocytes at tens of micrometres per second.

The output of this slow computation is chemical and directional - glutamate to synchronise neurons, adenosine from ATP to quieten them, D-serine to permit plasticity, and vasoactive messengers to the blood vessel to buy the territory more fuel.

It is, in effect, a leaky integrator running about a thousand times slower than its neurons, computing a moving average of local activity and adjusting the rules of the game rather than playing a move.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Spillover glutamate at micromolar levels from up to a million synapses; extracellular potassium rising from 3 toward 10 millimolar during activity; ATP from neighbouring cells; noradrenaline during arousal.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Gliotransmitters glutamate, ATP-adenosine and D-serine; lactate to neurons; vasodilator and vasoconstrictor messengers to arterioles; glutamine returned to neurons.

• STATE.

◦ Per-process calcium concentration near 100 nanomolar at rest rising several-fold in transients; membrane voltage near minus 85 millivolts; glycogen store; leaflet coverage per synapse scaled 0 to 1.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Domain diameter about 143 micrometres in human; 270,000 to 2,000,000 synapses per domain; input resistance under 10 megohms; calcium wave speed 10 to 27 micrometres per second.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Glutamate clearance about 1 millisecond at the transporter, tens of milliseconds at the synapse; local calcium transients 0.5 to 5 seconds; whole-cell waves 5 to 30 seconds; leaflet remodelling minutes to hours.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Alexander disease, a fatal leukodystrophy, is caused by mutations in GFAP, making it the textbook primary astrocyte disease.

◦ In hepatic encephalopathy, ammonia from liver failure swells astrocytes and deranges their glutamine cycle, clouding consciousness.

◦ Reactive astrogliosis and glutamate-uptake failure feature in stroke, epilepsy, and amyotrophic lateral sclerosis, and mainstream glial tumours, the astrocytomas and glioblastoma, arise from this lineage.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to model a protoplasmic astrocyte at a tripartite synapse, integrating synaptic activity into calcium and releasing a gliotransmitter when the integrated calcium crosses a threshold.
Create a number variable called calcium with initial value 0.1.
Create a number variable called resting calcium with initial value 0.1.
Create a number variable called decay fraction with initial value 0.05.
Create a number variable called gain with initial value 0.02.
Create a number variable called release threshold with initial value 0.4.
Create a number variable called gliotransmitter released with initial value 0.
Create a list variable called synaptic activity containing the glutamate pulses sensed on each time step.
For each activity value in the list called synaptic activity, perform the following actions.
    Calculate the difference of calcium and resting calcium.
    Multiply the result by decay fraction.
    Subtract that amount from calcium.
    Multiply the activity value by gain.
    Add that amount to calcium.
    If calcium is greater than release threshold is true, then perform the following actions.
        Add 1 to gliotransmitter released.
        Set calcium to resting calcium.
    End the conditional block.
End the loop.
Display gliotransmitter released to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because the essence of this cell is one slow leaky integral compared against one threshold, and Python states that in a dozen transparent lines.

# Tripartite synapse: astrocyte calcium integration and gliotransmitter release.
# The astrocyte low-pass filters synaptic glutamate into calcium and answers
# only sustained activity with a release event (Araque et al., Trends Neurosci
# 1999; Perea & Araque, Science 2007).
import random

REST, DECAY, GAIN, THRESHOLD = 0.10, 0.05, 0.02, 0.40  # micromolar-scale units

def run(rate, steps=400):
    """rate = probability of a glutamate pulse per 100 ms time step."""
    ca, releases = REST, 0
    for _ in range(steps):
        ca -= DECAY * (ca - REST)                 # leak back toward rest
        if random.random() < rate:
            ca += GAIN                            # spillover glutamate pulse
        if ca > THRESHOLD:
            releases += 1                         # D-serine / ATP release
            ca = REST                             # store-emptying reset
    return releases

random.seed(1)
for rate in (0.05, 0.2, 0.5, 0.9):
    print(f"synaptic rate {rate:4.2f} -> gliotransmitter releases {run(rate)}")
# Sparse activity is filtered out; only sustained firing earns a reply.

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

The only cell in the brain that holds a private, non-overlapping territory of up to two million synapses and governs them all without firing a single spike.


CHAPTER 2. FIBROUS ASTROCYTE (White-Matter Tract Astrocyte) (The Cable-Duct Caretaker Cell) (NON-COG)

Beneath the grey rind of the cortex lies a continent of pure wiring, half the human brain by volume, and it too needs a resident caretaker.

The fibrous astrocyte is that caretaker, a long-armed star cell strung out among the myelinated cables, touching the bare gaps in their insulation where the nerve impulse is reborn.

It asks for no credit in any computation; it simply keeps a hundred million metres of cable alive.

Cable-Duct names its home, the white matter, the axon-cable tissue of the brain.

Caretaker names its role: structural support, ionic housekeeping and fuel delivery for axons and their insulating oligodendrocytes.

Cell marks it as glia.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The fibrous astrocyte is the astrocyte of the white matter, supporting myelinated tracts rather than synapses, since white matter contains almost no synapses to tend.

Its processes contact axons at the nodes of Ranvier, the gaps in the myelin insulation where the impulse regenerates, where it buffers the potassium that each passing impulse expels, and its endfeet reach the blood vessels that fuel the tracts.

It serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, so it is tagged (NON-COG).

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

Where the protoplasmic cousin is a dense bush, this cell is a spider: a small body issuing fewer, longer, straighter and much less branched processes that run parallel with or across the axon bundles, richly filled with glial fibrillary acidic protein (GFAP), the astrocyte's signature skeleton protein.

Its processes end on nodes of Ranvier, on blood vessels, and on other astrocytes.

SIZE:

Human fibrous astrocytes have processes commonly 100 to 300 micrometres long, giving a territory notably larger and sparser than the protoplasmic domain, with a cell body of 10 to 20 micrometres, moderate confidence.

Unlike protoplasmic cells, their territories interdigitate rather than tile exclusively.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Regional variants follow their tracts, from corpus callosum to spinal funiculi to optic nerve, differing more in orientation and molecular detail than in basic plan.

WHERE IT LIVES:

All white matter of the central nervous system (CNS): corpus callosum, internal capsule, subcortical and cerebellar white matter, optic nerve, and the spinal cord's outer funiculi.

Its interfaces are the node of Ranvier, the vessel wall, and the glia limitans where tracts meet the brain surface.

NUMBER:

No direct human count exists, but since white matter is roughly half of human brain volume and is populated overwhelmingly by glia, fibrous astrocytes plausibly number in the low billions, low confidence.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Like all astrocytes it rests very negative, near minus 80 to minus 85 millivolts, dominated by Kir4.1 potassium channels, fires no action potentials, is gap-junction coupled, and shows calcium transients evoked by axonal activity and by ATP.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

It forms no tripartite synapses, but keeps real junctional contacts: perinodal processes at nodes of Ranvier, gap junctions with fellow astrocytes and, importantly, with oligodendrocytes, forming the panglial syncytium that moves ions and fuel through white matter.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that astrocytes possess no axon.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

It releases adenosine triphosphate (ATP) and lactate as signalling and fuel molecules, and clears glutamate released along axons, but it commands no synaptic transmitter system of its own.

MYELINATION:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that myelin in the CNS is made exclusively by oligodendrocytes, although this cell physically supports myelinated tracts and supplies their metabolic needs.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Its chief bills are potassium pumping at nodes and glycogen-backed lactate export to axons, which depend on glial lactate during sustained firing.

PLASTICITY:

Modest in health, but it transforms dramatically after injury, becoming the reactive astrocyte that walls off damage and lays down the glial scar.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Oligodendrocytes couple to it by gap junctions, microglia patrol its tracts, and endothelial cells and pericytes supply it across the blood-brain barrier.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Without it, white matter loses ionic stability and fuel logistics: nodes drown in potassium, axons starve during sustained traffic, and long-range conduction, the very product the tracts exist to deliver, degrades.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Its quiet marvel is the panglial syncytium: through gap junctions it forms one continuous cytoplasmic network with other astrocytes and with oligodendrocytes, spatially buffering potassium by siphoning it from busy nodes and discharging it at distant blood vessels, a transport computation done by pure geometry and diffusion.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Potassium ejected at nodes of Ranvier, rising locally from 3 toward 10 millimolar; ATP and glutamate along active axons; glucose from vessels.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Buffered potassium redistributed through the syncytium; lactate to axons; ATP signals to neighbours; scar components after injury.

• STATE.

◦ Membrane voltage near minus 80 to minus 85 millivolts; intracellular potassium load; glycogen store; reactive state scaled 0 to 1.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Process length 100 to 300 micrometres; gap-junction coupling to tens of neighbours; nodes contacted per cell not reliably counted in human.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Potassium clearance hundreds of milliseconds to seconds; calcium transients seconds; reactive transformation days.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Alexander disease strikes fibrous astrocytes hardest, its GFAP-laden Rosenthal fibres accumulating in white matter and destroying it.

◦ In multiple sclerosis its reactive scarring names the disease, sclerosis meaning scar.

◦ In vanishing white matter disease, astrocyte dysfunction leads the collapse of the tracts.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

CODE SAMPLE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

The sparse, long-armed, heavily GFAP-filled star cell lying among myelinated cables, its fingers resting on the nodes of Ranvier - the only astrocyte whose whole life is wiring, not synapses.


CHAPTER 3. INTERLAMINAR ASTROCYTE (Layer-1 Interlaminar Astrocyte) (The Layer-Lacing Plumb-Line Cell) (COG)

At the very surface of your cortex, in the almost neuron-free top layer, sits a cell that rodents essentially do not have.

From that rooftop it drops straight fibres a millimetre down through the working layers of the cortex, like plumb lines hung through the floors of a building.

Nobody yet knows exactly what travels down those lines, but calcium does, and the cell is at its most elaborate in the species reading this sentence.

Layer-Lacing names its geometry: it stitches cortical layer 1 to layers 2 through 4 with long vertical threads.

Plumb-Line names the look of those threads, straight, unbranched, gravity-true descents unlike any other astrocyte process.

Cell marks it as glia.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The interlaminar astrocyte is a primate-enriched, human-prominent astrocyte whose cell body sits in cortical layer 1 and whose defining processes descend, largely unbranched, through layers 2 to 4, spanning distances of up to a millimetre.

It is essentially absent in rodents, elaborated in monkeys and apes, and most developed in humans, making it one of the clearest cellular specialisations of the primate cortex.

Its descending fibres are positioned to carry signals, demonstrated for calcium in tissue, across cortical layers that neurons connect only through synaptic relays, and because that places it inside the associative machinery of the human cortex it is tagged (COG), with the honest caveat that its function is still under active investigation.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

A small soma in layer 1 bears two kinds of process: short bushy fibres that mingle locally near the pial surface, and the signature interlaminar fibres, long, straight or gently tortuous, largely unbranched cables that plunge vertically through the upper layers, sometimes ending in small terminal masses.

The cells collectively form a palisade, a fence of parallel vertical fibres visible in human cortex stained for glial fibrillary acidic protein (GFAP).

SIZE:

The soma is about 10 micrometres, small for an astrocyte, while the interlaminar fibres run hundreds of micrometres, with reported reaches up to about 1 millimetre, ending in layers 3 to 4.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Two forms are described, a pial form with its body at the very surface and a subpial form slightly deeper, and fibre length and straightness vary between cortical areas, moderate confidence.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Layer 1 of the cerebral cortex of primates, across all lobes, with its fibres occupying layers 2 to 4.

It sits at two interfaces at once: the glia limitans beneath the pia, the brain's outermost membrane, and the layer-1 zone where long-range feedback axons terminate, the same zone where the L2/3 IT neuron of the neuronal catalogue spreads its apical tuft.

NUMBER:

No rigorous human count exists; interlaminar astrocytes densely populate layer 1 of all human neocortex, plausibly hundreds of millions of cells, low confidence.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Astrocytic and passive: resting potential near minus 80 millivolts, no action potentials, gap-junction coupling, and calcium excitability, with calcium signals observed to travel along the interlaminar fibres in human tissue slices at astrocytic wave speeds of order 10 to 30 micrometres per second.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Its layer-1 processes lie in the densest feedback neuropil of the cortex and its descending fibres pass through the synaptic territories of layers 2 to 4, contacting neurons, other astrocytes and blood vessels along the way, with gap junctions coupling it into the astrocyte network of the upper layers.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that astrocytes possess no axon, although this cell's millimetre-long unbranched fibres are the most axon-like processes any astrocyte grows.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Standard astrocytic gliotransmission is assumed, adenosine triphosphate (ATP) and glutamate release and glutamate uptake, but direct human evidence for its specific transmitter repertoire is thin, low confidence.

MYELINATION:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that the cell has no axon and its fibres are unmyelinated glial processes.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Presumed typical of astrocytes, dominated by transporter and pump costs, with the added expense of maintaining exceptionally long processes.

PLASTICITY:

Its developmental appearance is late, the fibres elaborating in the first postnatal months in humans, and its palisade degrades in Alzheimer disease and Down syndrome, showing that the structure is actively maintained.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Protoplasmic astrocytes of layers 2 to 4 form the network its fibres descend through, meningeal fibroblasts and the pia lie against its rooftop processes, microglia patrol its territory, and blood vessels supply it directly at the surface.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Layer 1 is where the cortex hears from itself, the landing zone for feedback from higher areas, and this cell is the only astrocyte physically wired to broadcast a layer-1 event straight down to the middle layers without synaptic relay.

Lose it and the human upper cortex loses a vertical channel that rodent cortex never had, a candidate ingredient of what makes human cortical tissue computationally distinctive.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Its standout capability is the cross-layer calcium relay: a calcium event ignited among its bushy layer-1 processes can propagate down the interlaminar fibre as a travelling wave, delivering a chemical signal, gliotransmitter release, potassium handling, vascular messaging, to a patch of layer 3 or 4 hundreds of micrometres below, seconds later.

That converts the cell into a slow, private, vertical bus line running alongside the fast synaptic elevators of the cortical column.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Feedback-driven synaptic spillover and neuromodulators in layer 1; ATP from neighbouring glia; mechanical and ionic signals at the pial surface.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Calcium-triggered gliotransmitter and ATP release along the descending fibre into layers 2 to 4; potassium redistribution across layers; coupling currents into the astrocyte syncytium.

• STATE.

◦ Fibre calcium profile along its length; membrane voltage near minus 80 millivolts; gap-junction coupling strength.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Fibre length 300 to 1,000 micrometres; wave speed 10 to 30 micrometres per second; soma about 10 micrometres.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Local calcium transients 1 to 5 seconds; layer-1-to-layer-4 relay of order 10 to 60 seconds at measured wave speeds; developmental elaboration over postnatal months.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ The interlaminar palisade becomes disrupted and disorganised in Alzheimer disease and in Down syndrome cortex.

◦ As a layer-1 resident it is exposed early to meningeal inflammation and to superficial cortical injury.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to model an interlaminar astrocyte relaying a calcium wave from cortical layer one down its long fibre toward layer four.
Create a number variable called fibre length with initial value 800.
Create a number variable called wave speed with initial value 20.
Create a number variable called wave position with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called layer one calcium with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called ignition threshold with initial value 0.5.
Create a true-or-false variable called wave travelling with initial value false.
Set layer one calcium to the summed feedback activity sensed in layer one.
If layer one calcium is greater than ignition threshold is true, then perform the following actions.
    Set wave travelling to true.
End the conditional block.
While wave travelling is true and wave position is less than fibre length, perform the following actions.
    Add wave speed to wave position.
    Release gliotransmitter at the depth given by wave position.
End the loop.
If wave position is greater than or equal to fibre length is true, then perform the following actions.
    Display the message stating that the signal has reached the deep layers to the console.
End the conditional block.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because a wave crawling down a one-dimensional fibre is a loop over depth and time, and Python makes the crawl visible line by line.

# Interlaminar astrocyte: cross-layer calcium relay from layer 1 to layer 4.
# Calcium waves travel along human interlaminar fibres at roughly
# 10-30 micrometres per second (Colombo & Reisin, Brain Res 2004;
# Oberheim et al., J Neurosci 2009).
FIBRE_UM   = 800     # fibre length, micrometres (layer 1 down to layer 3/4)
SPEED_UMS  = 20      # wave speed, micrometres per second
DT         = 1.0     # time step, seconds
LAYER_TOPS = {"L1": 0, "L2": 150, "L3": 400, "L4": 800}

def relay(layer1_activity, threshold=0.5):
    if layer1_activity <= threshold:
        print("sub-threshold feedback: no wave ignited")
        return
    pos, t = 0.0, 0.0
    while pos < FIBRE_UM:
        pos += SPEED_UMS * DT
        t += DT
        for layer, depth in LAYER_TOPS.items():
            if depth and abs(pos - depth) < SPEED_UMS * DT / 2:
                print(f"t={t:5.1f} s  wave at {pos:5.0f} um -> "
                      f"gliotransmitter released at top of {layer}")
    print(f"layer-1 event delivered to layer 4 after {t:.0f} seconds")

relay(0.3)   # weak feedback dies at the rooftop
relay(0.9)   # strong feedback rides the plumb line down

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

The only astrocyte that hangs millimetre-long unbranched plumb lines from the brain's surface down through the working layers of the cortex, and it is at its best, and possibly only fully itself, in the human brain.


CHAPTER 4. VARICOSE PROJECTION ASTROCYTE (Deep-Layer Varicose Projection Astrocyte) (The Beaded Boundary-Breaker Cell) (COG)

Every astrocyte in this section obeys one law: stay inside your own territory.

Deep in the human cortex lives the outlaw, a cell found only in humans and other great apes, whose beaded processes shoot straight through the domains of its neighbours for millimetres at a stretch.

Discovered in earnest only in 2009, it is arguably the least understood cell in this entire manuscript, and one of the most provocative, because evolution added it to precisely the brains that think about it.

Beaded names the varicosities, evenly spaced swellings strung along its long processes like beads on a wire.

Boundary-Breaker names its defining crime: it is the only astrocyte whose processes routinely violate the non-overlapping domain organisation of the grey matter.

Cell marks it as glia.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The varicose projection astrocyte is a human- and great-ape-specific astrocyte of the deep cortical layers 5 and 6, bearing short spiny local processes plus up to five long projections, 1 to 5 millimetres in length, studded with evenly spaced varicosities, that run through the territories of neighbouring astrocytes in all directions, including up toward the surface and along the deep layers.

Its function is unresolved, but its geometry is that of a long-range point-to-point line laid across the local astrocyte grid, and because it exists only in the most cognitively elaborate primates and sits in the cortex's output layers, it is tagged (COG), with the confidence caveat stated plainly: this is the most hypothesis-rich, data-poor cell in the section.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The cell body resides in layers 5 to 6 and issues numerous short, spiny, straight local processes, an un-bushy look unusual for grey-matter astrocytes, plus one to five long varicose projections whose swellings recur at roughly regular intervals, terminating in the neuropil or on blood vessels.

The varicosities resemble, at least superficially, the transmitter-release boutons of axons, though their contents in this cell remain uncharacterised.

SIZE:

Soma 10 to 20 micrometres; long projections 1 to 5 millimetres, the longest processes of any astrocyte known; local process field of order 100 to 200 micrometres, moderate confidence from the small number of quantified human specimens.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

None established; cells differ in projection number and orientation, and the class itself is variably present between individuals, low confidence.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Cortical layers 5 and 6 of humans and other great apes only, absent in monkeys and rodents.

Its projections cross an interface no other astrocyte crosses, the invisible boundary between astrocyte domains, and some reach the subcortical white matter border.

NUMBER:

Rare and uncounted; it is a sparse minority population among deep-layer astrocytes, plausibly millions rather than billions of cells in a human brain, low confidence.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Presumed standard astrocytic: resting near minus 80 millivolts, no action potentials, calcium excitability; direct electrophysiological recordings from identified varicose projection astrocytes have not been published, and this gap should be stated rather than papered over.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Its local spiny processes presumably join tripartite synapses of layers 5 and 6, and its projections physically traverse the synaptic fields and astrocyte networks of many domains, but its junctional partners along those projections are unmapped.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that astrocytes possess no axon, although the long varicose projection is the closest any astrocyte comes to growing one.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Unknown in specifics; standard astrocytic gliotransmitters, adenosine triphosphate (ATP) and glutamate, are assumed, and the varicosities are an obvious candidate release apparatus awaiting direct study.

MYELINATION:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that the cell has no axon and its projections are unmyelinated.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Presumed astrocytic, with the extra cost of building and maintaining millimetres of projection.

PLASTICITY:

Unknown; its late evolutionary appearance and inter-individual variability hint at an activity- or experience-dependent element, speculation clearly labelled as such.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

The protoplasmic astrocytes whose domains it crosses surround it on all sides, microglia patrol its layers, and deep-layer blood vessels receive some of its projection endings.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Layers 5 and 6 are the cortex's output floor, dispatching commands to brainstem, spinal cord and thalamus, and this cell overlays that floor with millimetre-scale lines that ignore the local grid.

If the astrocyte network is the cortex's slow chemical internet, this cell looks like its long-distance trunk cabling, present only in the ape brains that show the most elaborate cognition.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Its candidate superpower is long-range domain-crossing signalling: a calcium event at the soma could be exported millimetres away in one hop, bypassing the slow domain-to-domain gap-junction relay, with the beads acting as repeater stations or release sites.

At typical astrocyte wave speeds, a signal that would take minutes to gossip across fifty domains could arrive through one varicose projection in a fraction of the time and at a precise address.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Synaptic spillover in layers 5 to 6; ATP and calcium signals from the local astrocyte network; state of the domains its projections traverse.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Hypothesised gliotransmitter release at varicosities millimetres from the soma; vascular contacts at projection endings; gap-junction coupling locally.

• STATE.

◦ Somatic and per-projection calcium; varicosity release readiness; membrane voltage near minus 80 millivolts.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Projections 1 to 5 per cell; projection length 1 to 5 millimetres; varicosity spacing roughly 10 to 30 micrometres, low confidence; local field 100 to 200 micrometres.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Calcium transients seconds; projection-length delivery tens of seconds to minutes at wave speeds, potentially faster if release is triggered electrotonically, unknown.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ No disease is yet pinned to this cell specifically, but its exclusivity to humans and great apes makes it a standing suspect in uniquely human disorders of the cortex, schizophrenia among them, hypothesis, not finding.

◦ As a deep-layer astrocyte it shares the general astrocytic failure modes of glioma, reactive gliosis and hepatic encephalopathy.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to compare how long a calcium signal takes to cross the cortex by domain-to-domain relay against delivery along one varicose projection.
Create a number variable called target distance with initial value 3000.
Create a number variable called domain size with initial value 143.
Create a number variable called wave speed with initial value 20.
Create a number variable called handoff delay with initial value 2.
Create a number variable called relay time with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called projection time with initial value 0.
Calculate the quotient of target distance and domain size.
Round the result up to the next whole number and call it domains crossed.
Calculate the quotient of target distance and wave speed.
Set relay time to the result.
Multiply domains crossed by handoff delay.
Add the result to relay time.
Calculate the quotient of target distance and wave speed.
Set projection time to the result.
If projection time is less than relay time is true, then perform the following actions.
    Display the message stating that the varicose projection wins, together with both times, to the console.
End the conditional block.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because the argument for this cell is a back-of-envelope race between two delivery routes, and Python runs the envelope.

# Varicose projection astrocyte: long-range domain-crossing signalling.
# One 1-5 mm beaded projection versus hop-by-hop relay through 143-um
# astrocyte domains (Oberheim et al., J Neurosci 2009).
import math

DOMAIN_UM   = 143    # human protoplasmic astrocyte domain diameter
WAVE_UMS    = 20     # intracellular calcium wave speed, um/s
HANDOFF_S   = 2.0    # ATP-mediated hop between coupled domains, seconds

def race(distance_um):
    hops       = math.ceil(distance_um / DOMAIN_UM)
    relay      = distance_um / WAVE_UMS + hops * HANDOFF_S
    projection = distance_um / WAVE_UMS          # one cell, no handoffs
    print(f"{distance_um/1000:.1f} mm: relay {relay:6.1f} s over {hops} domains"
          f" | varicose projection {projection:6.1f} s"
          f" | saved {relay - projection:5.1f} s")

for mm in (1, 3, 5):
    race(mm * 1000)
# The beaded trunk line always beats the neighbourhood gossip chain,
# and by more the farther the message must travel.

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

The only astrocyte that breaks the domain law, firing beaded processes up to five millimetres through its neighbours' territories, and the only cell in this section found in no brain outside the great apes.


CHAPTER 5. VELATE ASTROCYTE (Cerebellar Granule-Layer Velate Astrocyte) (The Granule-Swaddling Veil Cell) (NON-COG)

The cerebellar granule layer is the most crowded neural tissue on Earth, packing in most of the brain's neurons, tens of billions of granule cells, at densities no cortex approaches.

An ordinary bushy astrocyte cannot work in such a crush; there is no room for a bush.

So the astrocyte here abandoned branches for veils, wrapping tissue-thin sheets around clusters of granule neurons like cloth around packed glassware.

Granule-Swaddling names its clientele, the tiny granule neurons of the cerebellum, and its method, wrapping them in enclosing sheets.

Veil names the signature structure, lamellar processes a fraction of a micrometre thick spread into curved membranous sheets.

Cell marks it as glia.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The velate astrocyte is the protoplasmic astrocyte of the cerebellar granule layer, remodelled for extreme neuronal density: instead of a bush of fine processes it extends veil-like lamellae that curve around groups of granule cells and around the cerebellar glomeruli, the synaptic knots where mossy-fibre inputs meet granule cell dendrites.

Within those wrappings it does the standard astrocytic housekeeping, potassium buffering, glutamate clearance, and metabolic support, for the densest neuronal population in the brain.

It serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, so it is tagged (NON-COG).

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

A small soma among the granule cells gives rise to broad, extremely thin sheet-like processes, low in glial fibrillary acidic protein (GFAP) compared with fibrous astrocytes, that enclose granule cell bodies and partially ensheath glomeruli.

The geometry maximises membrane surface per unit volume, exactly what an ion-buffering cell needs in crowded tissue.

SIZE:

Soma under 10 micrometres, among the smallest astrocyte bodies; each veil spans of order 10 to 50 micrometres while being well under 1 micrometre thick, moderate confidence.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

None formally; comparable veil-bearing astrocytes also appear in the olfactory bulb's granule layer, the other extreme-density tissue, suggesting the form follows the packing problem rather than the region.

WHERE IT LIVES:

The granule layer of the cerebellar cortex, throughout all lobules, interfacing with granule cell somata, glomeruli and local blood vessels.

NUMBER:

Uncounted directly; the cerebellum holds roughly 49 billion granule neurons served by a much smaller glial population, since cerebellar glia are outnumbered by neurons about 4.3 to 1, placing cerebellar astrocytes plausibly in the low billions, with velate cells a major share, low confidence.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Standard astrocytic: resting potential near minus 80 millivolts, potassium-dominated membrane, no action potentials, gap-junction coupling and calcium transients.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Its lamellae partially enclose the cerebellar glomeruli, giving it a tripartite-style watching brief over mossy fibre to granule cell synapses, and gap junctions couple it to neighbouring astrocytes.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that astrocytes possess no axon.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

It clears glutamate at glomeruli and can release adenosine triphosphate (ATP), but has no distinctive transmitter system of its own.

MYELINATION:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that the cell has no axon and makes no myelin.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Dominated by potassium pumping, since tens of thousands of granule cells firing at high rates within micrometres of each veil eject potassium relentlessly, and by supplying fuel across the least vascularised distances in a packed tissue.

PLASTICITY:

Modest structural remodelling of its veils; principally a stable infrastructure cell.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Local blood vessels supply it, Bergmann glia and other astrocytes couple to it through the cerebellar glial network, and microglia patrol its layer.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

The granule layer only works because something insulates and buffers billions of tightly packed neurons that would otherwise poison each other with potassium; the velate astrocyte is that something.

Every cerebellar computation in the neuronal catalogue rests on its veils.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Its surprise is architectural: it solves an engineering problem, maximum buffering membrane in minimum volume, with a shape found almost nowhere else in the brain, thin curved sheets acting as distributed ionic capacitors around neuron clusters.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Potassium from densely packed granule cells, locally exceeding 10 millimolar during bursts; glutamate spillover at glomeruli; glucose from sparse local vessels.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Redistributed potassium; cleared glutamate returned as glutamine; lactate to granule neurons.

• STATE.

◦ Membrane voltage near minus 80 millivolts; veil coverage of each wrapped cluster; intracellular potassium load.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Veil thickness well under 1 micrometre; veil span 10 to 50 micrometres; soma under 10 micrometres.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Potassium buffering hundreds of milliseconds to seconds; calcium transients seconds.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Cerebellar astrocyte dysfunction features in spinocerebellar ataxias and in medulloblastoma biology, and general astrocytic failures, gliosis, glioma, apply here as everywhere.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

CODE SAMPLE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

The only astrocyte built of veils instead of branches, swaddling the most crowded neurons in the brain in membranous sheets thinner than a wavelength of light.


CHAPTER 6. BERGMANN GLIA (Radial Astrocyte of the Cerebellar Cortex) (The Ladder-Then-Chaperone Cell) (COG)

Every one of the tens of billions of granule neurons in your cerebellum climbed into place along the fibres of this cell, as if the whole structure were built on scaffolding that was never taken down.

Because it was not taken down: in adulthood the scaffold turned chaperone, wrapping nearly every synapse on the Purkinje cell dendrites it stands beside.

It listens to those synapses with receptors normally reserved for neurons, and if you strip those receptors away, the animal's fine motor coordination degrades.

Ladder names its first career: the radial fibre along which young granule neurons migrate during cerebellar development.

Then-Chaperone names its second: the adult cell ensheathes Purkinje cell dendritic spines and supervises their synapses for life.

Cell marks it as glia, a specialised radial astrocyte named for Karl Bergmann.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

Bergmann glia are the radially aligned astrocytes of the cerebellar cortex, their somata seated in the Purkinje cell layer and their parallel fibres ascending through the molecular layer to the pial surface, alongside the flat dendritic trees of the Purkinje neurons.

In development each cell is the migration ladder for granule neurons descending from the external granular layer; in the adult its lamellar appendages wrap almost every dendritic spine of the Purkinje cells, clearing glutamate at both parallel-fibre and climbing-fibre synapses and shaping the plasticity that underlies cerebellar motor learning.

Because its synapse ensheathment demonstrably regulates synapse number, glutamate clearance and fine motor coordination, it is tagged (COG).

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

Each cell extends three to six stout radial fibres from the Purkinje cell layer straight up through the molecular layer to endfeet at the pial surface, and each fibre bristles with thousands of leaflet-like lateral appendages organised into functionally independent microdomains that enwrap Purkinje spines.

It retains radial-glial geometry into adulthood, a rarity shared mainly with the retina's Müller cell and the hypothalamic tanycyte.

SIZE:

The soma is about 15 micrometres; the radial fibres run the full molecular layer thickness, roughly 300 to 400 micrometres in human; each microdomain is a few micrometres across and can respond independently of its neighbours.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

No formal subtypes; about eight Bergmann glia surround and serve each Purkinje cell, moderate confidence from rodent counts.

WHERE IT LIVES:

The Purkinje cell layer and molecular layer of the entire cerebellar cortex, with endfeet forming the cerebellar glia limitans at the pia and other processes contacting blood vessels.

NUMBER:

With roughly 15 million Purkinje cells in the human cerebellum and about eight Bergmann glia per Purkinje cell, a reasonable estimate is on the order of 60 to 120 million Bergmann glia, low to moderate confidence.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Resting potential very negative, near minus 80 to minus 85 millivolts, with an almost purely passive potassium-dominated membrane, no action potentials, and vivid calcium excitability confined to individual microdomains.

Uniquely among astrocytes, it expresses calcium-permeable alpha-amino-3-hydroxy-5-methyl-4-isoxazolepropionic acid (AMPA) receptors, built from the subunits GluA1 and GluA4 without the calcium-blocking GluA2, so glutamate from neighbouring synapses drives calcium directly into the glial cell.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Its appendages ensheath the great majority of Purkinje cell dendritic spines, embracing both parallel-fibre synapses, of which each Purkinje cell carries about 175,000 in humans, and the single climbing fibre, and climbing fibres even make direct ectopic transmitter release onto the glial membrane itself.

Gap junctions couple neighbouring Bergmann glia into a cerebellar glial network.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that Bergmann glia possess no axon, only radial fibres and lamellar appendages.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

It clears glutamate through the transporter GLAST, also called EAAT1, expressed here at the highest density in the brain, and can release adenosine triphosphate (ATP), D-serine and glutamate as gliotransmitters, with taurine release contributing during development.

MYELINATION:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that the cell has no axon and makes no myelin.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Glutamate transport dominates its budget, since parallel-fibre volleys hit its GLAST transporters with every movement the animal makes, and it also supplies fuel to the energy-hungry Purkinje cells it surrounds.

PLASTICITY:

Its spine ensheathment is itself plastic, extending and retracting with the calcium admitted through its AMPA receptors, and forcing those receptors to a calcium-impermeable form makes the glial wrapping retract, slows glutamate clearance, raises synapse numbers and degrades fine motor coordination.

The cell also retains latent radial-glial potential, re-entering progenitor-like states after injury.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Velate astrocytes and the cerebellar glial network couple to it, microglia and blood vessels service its layer, and in development the external granular layer supplied the very neurons that climbed down its fibres.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

The cerebellar molecular layer is the most tightly timed synaptic machine in the brain, and its timing depends on glutamate being removed within milliseconds; Bergmann glia are that removal.

Remove the cell and cerebellar development fails first, granule cells never reaching their layer, and adult motor learning fails second.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Its signature computation is closed-loop synapse ensheathment tuning: glutamate escaping a busy Purkinje synapse enters the glial microdomain through calcium-permeable AMPA receptors, the admitted calcium maintains and adjusts the wrapping of exactly that synapse, and the wrapping in turn sets how far glutamate can spill and how many synapses may form.

Each of the thousands of microdomains on one cell runs this loop independently, so a single Bergmann glial cell is effectively a parallel array of local controllers, one per synaptic neighbourhood, tuning cerebellar plasticity spine by spine.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Glutamate from parallel-fibre and climbing-fibre synapses, including direct ectopic release onto the glial membrane; potassium from molecular-layer activity; ATP and endocannabinoid signals.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Millisecond-scale glutamate clearance through GLAST; per-microdomain adjustment of spine ensheathment; gliotransmitters ATP and D-serine; potassium redistribution to pia and vessels.

• STATE.

◦ Per-microdomain calcium; ensheathment fraction per spine scaled 0 to 1; membrane voltage near minus 80 to minus 85 millivolts.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ About 8 cells per Purkinje cell; 3 to 6 radial fibres per cell; fibre length 300 to 400 micrometres; thousands of microdomains per cell; AMPA receptors GluA1 and GluA4, calcium-permeable.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Glutamate clearance about 1 millisecond at the transporter; microdomain calcium transients 0.1 to 2 seconds; ensheathment remodelling minutes to days; developmental ladder phase weeks.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Loss of its glutamate transporter GLAST features in some spinocerebellar ataxias and in episodic ataxia, and autoantibodies against GLAST occur in autoimmune cerebellar ataxia.

◦ Bergmann glial pathology accompanies alcohol-related cerebellar degeneration and essential aspects of medulloblastoma biology.

◦ Rendering its AMPA receptors calcium-impermeable in animals retracts synapse ensheathment and impairs fine motor coordination, the cleanest demonstration that a glial cell participates in motor learning.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to model one Bergmann glial microdomain adjusting how completely it wraps a Purkinje cell spine, based on the glutamate calcium it admits, and to report the effect on glutamate spillover.
Create a number variable called ensheathment with initial value 0.5.
Create a number variable called calcium with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called learning rate with initial value 0.1.
Create a number variable called target calcium with initial value 0.3.
Create a list variable called synapse activity containing the glutamate release strengths on each trial.
For each activity value in the list called synapse activity, perform the following actions.
    Calculate the difference of 1 and ensheathment.
    Multiply the result by the activity value and call it spillover.
    Multiply the activity value by ensheathment.
    Set calcium to the result.
    Calculate the difference of calcium and target calcium.
    Multiply the result by learning rate.
    Add the result to ensheathment.
    If ensheathment is greater than 1 is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set ensheathment to 1.
    End the conditional block.
    If ensheathment is less than 0 is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set ensheathment to 0.
    End the conditional block.
End the loop.
Display ensheathment and the final spillover to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because the cell's logic is a small feedback controller, and Python lets the control loop and its consequence for spillover sit together in plain sight.

# Bergmann glia: calcium-permeable AMPA receptors tune spine ensheathment,
# which sets glutamate spillover and caps synapse formation
# (Iino et al., Science 2001; Saab et al., Science 2012).
TARGET_CA = 0.30   # homeostatic calcium set point per microdomain
RATE      = 0.10   # ensheathment adjustment per trial

def microdomain(activity_trace, wrap=0.5):
    for glu in activity_trace:
        ca = glu * wrap                       # Ca2+ via GluA1/GluA4 AMPARs
        spill = glu * (1.0 - wrap)            # what escapes the glial sheath
        wrap += RATE * (ca - TARGET_CA)       # active synapse -> tighter wrap
        wrap = min(1.0, max(0.0, wrap))
    return wrap, spill

busy  = microdomain([0.8] * 50)               # a driven parallel-fibre synapse
quiet = microdomain([0.1] * 50)               # a rarely used synapse
print(f"busy synapse : ensheathment {busy[0]:.2f}, spillover {busy[1]:.2f}")
print(f"quiet synapse: ensheathment {quiet[0]:.2f}, spillover {quiet[1]:.2f}")
# Active synapses earn tight glial wrapping and clean, private transmission;
# neglected synapses are left open to spillover and to being outcompeted.

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

The only astrocyte that first builds its region, serving as the migration ladder for the most numerous neuron in the brain, and then spends adulthood wrapping nearly every synapse on the Purkinje cells it stands beside, listening in through neuron-grade calcium-permeable AMPA receptors that no other astrocyte carries.


CHAPTER 7. MÜLLER CELL (Radial Glial Cell of the Retina) (The Living Optical-Fibre Nurse Cell) (NON-COG)

Every photon you will ever see must first cross a quarter of a millimetre of living, scattering tissue, because the human retina is built inside out, with its light sensors at the back.

Evolution's answer to that awkward design is a cell that is simultaneously the retina's janitor, its fuel pump, its chemist, and - astonishingly - a genuine optical fibre made of living cytoplasm.

Light entering the eye is captured by the funnel-shaped inner end of this cell and piped, with low loss, straight through the retinal layers to the photoreceptors waiting below.

No other glial cell in the human body has been drafted into the physics of image formation itself.

Living marks it as a cell doing optics with cytoplasm rather than glass.

Optical-Fibre names its measured light-guiding function, resting on a refractive index higher than the tissue around it.

Nurse names everything else it does: feeding, cleaning, buffering, and recycling for every neuron of the retina.

Cell marks it as a single glial type, the radial glia of the retina.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The Müller cell is the principal glial cell of the retina, a radial glial cell spanning the retina's entire thickness from the inner limiting membrane facing the vitreous gel to the outer limiting membrane at the base of the photoreceptors.

It is the retina's all-in-one astrocyte: it clears the neurotransmitter glutamate, buffers potassium ions, supplies lactate fuel, recycles visual pigment components for the cones, controls local blood flow, and holds the tissue together mechanically.

On top of all that it guides light, transmitting incoming photons through the scattering inner retina to the photoreceptors like a fibre-optic plate, a finding established by Franze and colleagues in 2007.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

A long radial stalk with the nucleus parked in the middle of the retina, in the inner nuclear layer, a broad conical endfoot spread against the vitreous, and side branches that sheathe every class of retinal neuron along the way.

In the plexiform layers, where the synapses lie, the stalk throws out dense lamellar veils that wrap the synaptic junctions, and at the outer limiting membrane it seals to the photoreceptors with adhesion junctions and sends microvilli up between their inner segments.

The whole cell is a column of a tissue-servicing machine threaded through a stack of circuitry it also happens to illuminate.

SIZE:

The cell spans the full retinal thickness, roughly 100 to 250 micrometres depending on location, a micrometre being a thousandth of a millimetre.

The stalk runs a few micrometres wide, the endfoot broadens to roughly 10 to 15 micrometres, and one Müller cell services a column containing on the order of a dozen to sixteen retinal neurons.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Classically treated as one type, though single-cell gene reading has begun to separate central from peripheral Müller cells and a distinct population serving the fovea, the pit of sharpest vision, where elongated Müller cells form the so-called Müller cell cone that braces the pit.

Treat it as one type with regional specialisations, moderate confidence.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Everywhere in the neural retina of both eyes, tiling it completely, with its two ends defining the retina's two limiting membranes - the inner limiting membrane against the vitreous and the outer limiting membrane at the photoreceptor layer.

It therefore sits at both of the retina's interfaces at once, which no retinal neuron does.

NUMBER:

Roughly 8 to 10 million per human retina, so roughly 16 to 20 million per person, low to moderate confidence, derived from measured Müller cell densities of roughly 6,000 to 13,000 per square millimetre over a retinal area near 1,000 square millimetres.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Not excitable, but strongly and characteristically polarised: resting potential near minus 80 millivolts, held there by a very high potassium conductance through Kir4.1 channels, the inwardly rectifying potassium channel concentrated at the endfoot and around blood vessels.

That arrangement lets the cell siphon excess potassium from active layers and dump it into the vitreous and the blood, a process called potassium siphoning, and it makes the Müller cell the dominant source of certain clinical electroretinogram waves.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

It forms no synapses, but its lamellae ensheathe the synapses of both plexiform layers, giving it the tripartite-synapse relationship of an astrocyte: presynaptic terminal, postsynaptic terminal, enveloping Müller process.

It also couples to neighbouring Müller cells and astrocytes through gap junctions, direct cell-to-cell pores, though less extensively than cortical astrocytes do.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that the Müller cell is a glial cell with a radial stalk and processes but no axon.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

It releases no classical transmitter of its own, but it is the retina's glutamate chemist: it vacuums glutamate from the cleft through the transporter GLAST, also called excitatory amino acid transporter 1 (EAAT1), and converts it to harmless glutamine using glutamine synthetase, an enzyme essentially exclusive to Müller cells in the retina.

It can release adenosine triphosphate (ATP), the cell's energy molecule doubling as a signal, and D-serine, a co-agonist that helps open N-methyl-D-aspartate (NMDA) type glutamate receptors, so it holds gliotransmitter credentials even while tagged as substrate.

MYELINATION:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that the Müller cell neither makes myelin nor carries an axon to receive it, and the human retina is normally myelin-free precisely to keep the light path clear.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

The Müller cell is largely glycolytic, meaning it burns glucose without oxygen to make lactate, and it exports that lactate to the photoreceptors, which are among the most energy-hungry cells in the body.

It also stores the retina's main glycogen reserve and shoulders the nitrogen bookkeeping of the glutamate-glutamine cycle, so its own oxygen bill is modest while its chemical throughput is enormous.

PLASTICITY:

In fish and amphibians the Müller cell is a true retinal stem cell that regenerates lost neurons, but in humans that program is locked: injury instead triggers Müller cell gliosis, with swelling and upregulation of the intermediate filament protein GFAP (glial fibrillary acidic protein).

Unlocking the dormant regenerative program of the human Müller cell is one of the central ambitions of retinal repair research.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Retinal astrocytes, which enter along the optic nerve and serve the innermost layer, share the vascular workload; the retinal pigment epithelium behind the photoreceptors handles the rod visual pigment cycle while the Müller cell runs an alternative retinoid loop for the cones; retinal microglia patrol the layers it maintains; and the retinal and choroidal circulations feed it.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove the Müller cells and the retina fails in every mode at once: glutamate pools to toxic levels, potassium floods the tissue, the photoreceptors starve, the layers fall apart mechanically, and the image itself dims and blurs as the inner retina turns from a fibre-optic plate back into a frosted screen.

Vision would not degrade gracefully; it would collapse.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The signature trick is optics: the Müller cell has a refractive index near 1.380, measurably above the roughly 1.36 of the surrounding retina, and its funnel-shaped endfoot collects light at the vitreous surface and guides it down the stalk to the photoreceptors with low scattering loss.

Later work suggests the guidance is even wavelength-tuned, piping green and red light preferentially toward the cones while letting shorter-wavelength light leak to the surrounding rods, improving day vision at minimal cost to night vision.

A cell type has, in effect, been recruited as an element of the eye's optical train, and it performs this duty while simultaneously running the tissue's chemistry.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Photons arriving at the endfoot across the visible band of roughly 400 to 700 nanometres.

◦ Extracellular potassium transients of roughly 3 to 10 millimolar in active layers, and synaptic glutamate at up to millimolar concentrations in the cleft.

◦ Glucose from the retinal and choroidal circulations.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Guided light delivered to photoreceptors; lactate fuel; glutamine returned to neurons; potassium siphoned to vitreous and vessels; ATP and D-serine signals.

• STATE.

◦ Membrane potential near minus 80 millivolts, intracellular potassium and calcium levels, glycogen store, GFAP expression level as a gliosis flag.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Refractive index roughly 1.38 against tissue near 1.36; length 100 to 250 micrometres; Kir4.1 conductance dominating the membrane; roughly one Müller cell per sixteen retinal neurons.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Light guidance effectively instantaneous; potassium siphoning tens of milliseconds to seconds; glutamate clearance milliseconds; gliosis onset hours to days.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Macular telangiectasia type 2, a disease of Müller cell loss in the fovea that undermines the Müller cell cone and central vision.

◦ Müller cell gliosis and swelling drive macular oedema in diabetic retinopathy, and Müller dysfunction contributes to retinoschisis, the splitting of retinal layers.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

CODE SAMPLE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

The only glial cell in the human body that is part of an optical instrument: a full-thickness radial column of the retina, resting near minus 80 millivolts, running the retina's glutamate and potassium economy, and piping the very light it lives in down its own stalk to the photoreceptors.


CHAPTER 8. TANYCYTE (Hypothalamic Radial Glial Cell of the Third Ventricle) (The Blood-Brain Customs-Officer Cell) (NON-COG)

At the floor of the brain's third ventricle, where the hypothalamus meets the bloodstream, the blood-brain barrier is deliberately left open.

Somebody has to police that open border, deciding which hormones, nutrients, and signals pass between blood, brain tissue, and cerebrospinal fluid, and in which direction.

That somebody is a stretched, elegant remnant of the embryonic radial glia, one end bathing in cerebrospinal fluid and the other gripping a leaky capillary.

It tastes the blood for glucose and fat, ferries the hunger hormones inward, and gates the release of the hormones that command the thyroid and the gonads.

Blood-Brain names the border it works, the interface between circulation and hypothalamus.

Customs-Officer names its job: inspecting, admitting, refusing, and escorting molecular traffic across an open frontier.

Cell marks it as a single specialised glial type, from the Greek tanus, meaning stretched.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The tanycyte is a specialised radial glial cell lining the floor and lower walls of the third ventricle, the narrow midline chamber of cerebrospinal fluid (CSF) between the two halves of the hypothalamus.

Its cell body sits in the ventricular wall, touching the CSF, and its single long basal process reaches down into hypothalamic nuclei or into the median eminence, the circumventricular organ where hypothalamic releasing hormones enter the portal blood bound for the pituitary.

It is the operational floor of the circumventricular channel: it forms the barrier that seals the leaky median eminence off from the rest of the brain, it shuttles hormones such as leptin and ghrelin across that barrier, it senses glucose for the feeding circuits, and it physically gates the terminals that release gonadotropin-releasing hormone.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

A cuboidal to elongated cell body in the ventricular lining, crowned not with the beating cilia of an ordinary ependymal cell but with microvilli and at most a single non-motile cilium, and a single stout basal process that can travel hundreds of micrometres before ending on a blood vessel, a neuron, or the pial surface.

Neighbouring tanycytes are stitched together by tight junctions, the protein seals that make sheets of cells watertight, which is precisely what lets them act as a barrier where the vessel walls themselves leak.

SIZE:

The cell body is roughly 8 to 15 micrometres across, and the basal process runs from tens of micrometres up to 300 or more, spanning the distance from ventricle wall to median eminence capillaries or hypothalamic nuclei.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Four canonical subtypes, named by position on the third ventricular wall.

Alpha-1 tanycytes sit highest on the wall and send processes toward the ventromedial and dorsomedial hypothalamic nuclei.

Alpha-2 tanycytes sit below them and project into the arcuate nucleus, the master feeding centre, and express the glucose transporter GLUT2 and the adenosine triphosphate (ATP)-sensitive potassium channel subunit Kir6.2 - the same glucose-sensing toolkit as the pancreatic beta cell.

Beta-1 tanycytes line the lateral recesses of the infundibulum and reach the lateral median eminence.

Beta-2 tanycytes carpet the very floor of the ventricle and plunge their endfeet onto the fenestrated (windowed) portal capillaries of the median eminence, forming the blood-CSF barrier of that open region.

WHERE IT LIVES:

The floor and inferolateral walls of the third ventricle, the median eminence, and in smaller numbers the other circumventricular organs, with thin extensions of the family along the infundibular stalk.

It lives permanently on a triple interface - CSF on one end, brain tissue along its shaft, and fenestrated blood vessels at the other end - which is the entire point of the cell.

NUMBER:

On the order of tens of thousands per human brain, low confidence, extrapolated from rodent counts of a few thousand and the small absolute size of the human third ventricular floor; no direct human census exists.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Non-spiking but electrically responsive, resting near minus 80 millivolts with a potassium-dominated membrane, and answering glucose, sweet and bitter tastants, and amino acids with sharp intracellular calcium rises driven partly by taste-receptor machinery borrowed from the tongue.

Calcium waves triggered at one tanycyte propagate through the sheet by ATP release, so the lining behaves as a slow, chemically coupled sensory epithelium.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

No synapses of its own, but intimate junctional politics: beta-2 tanycyte endfeet ensheathe the neurosecretory terminals of gonadotropin-releasing hormone neurons and physically advance or retract across the reproductive cycle, throttling hormone access to the portal blood.

Tanycytes also couple to one another by gap junctions and connect by contact and released signals to arcuate feeding neurons expressing neuropeptide Y (NPY) and pro-opiomelanocortin (POMC), the hunger and satiety peptides.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that the tanycyte is a glial cell whose single basal process is a transport-and-barrier structure, not an axon.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Its currency is metabolic and hormonal rather than synaptic: it releases ATP and lactate as local signals, shuttles leptin and ghrelin between blood and brain by transcytosis, and expresses deiodinase 2, the enzyme that converts thyroxine into active triiodothyronine, making the tanycyte a local thyroid-hormone factory that governs seasonal and energy-state signalling in the hypothalamus.

MYELINATION:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that the tanycyte neither produces myelin nor possesses an axon to be myelinated.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Moderate, but with an unusual profile: it takes up glucose avidly through GLUT2, runs glycolysis to make lactate for neighbouring neurons, and spends energy on continuous vesicular transport of hormones across its length, a full-time ferry service.

PLASTICITY:

Structurally plastic on hormonal timescales - beta-2 endfeet remodel across the ovarian cycle and with fasting - and, remarkably, tanycytes retain radial-glial stem cell character, dividing and generating new hypothalamic neurons in adult rodents; how much of that persists in adult humans is unresolved, low confidence.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

The fenestrated endothelial cells of the median eminence supply its blood interface, ordinary ependymocytes flank it along the ventricle, hypothalamic astrocytes share its territory, and portal vessel pericytes and the vascular wall complete the little border town it polices.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Without tanycytes the hypothalamus goes deaf to the blood: leptin and ghrelin no longer reach the feeding circuits properly, glucose state goes unreported, thyroid signalling loses its local converter, and the median eminence leaks unsupervised into the brain.

The result is a command centre issuing orders from stale information.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The tanycyte's surprise is that it performs chemosensation with taste-bud hardware and then acts on the result mechanically: alpha-2 tanycytes taste glucose with a beta-cell-style sensor set, sweep calcium waves through the ventricular lining, and beta-2 tanycytes answer hormonal season by physically advancing or withdrawing their endfeet to open or close the gonadotropin-releasing hormone gateway to the blood.

A glial sheet that tastes, computes state, and then moves is as close to behaviour as substrate gets.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Blood glucose roughly 4 to 8 millimolar, leptin, ghrelin, thyroxine, and amino acids arriving at fenestrated capillaries; CSF-borne signals at the apical end.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Transcytosed hormones delivered into the arcuate nucleus; triiodothyronine; lactate; ATP; mechanical gating of neurosecretory terminals.

• STATE.

◦ Intracellular calcium, endfoot position at the portal capillaries, tight-junction integrity, deiodinase 2 expression level.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Resting potential near minus 80 millivolts; process length up to roughly 300 micrometres; four positional subtypes alpha-1, alpha-2, beta-1, beta-2.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Calcium responses seconds; hormone transcytosis minutes; endfoot remodelling hours to days across the ovarian cycle; seasonal thyroid-hormone programs weeks.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Tanycyte barrier and transport failure is implicated in leptin resistance in obesity, in which the hunger-brake hormone no longer reaches its neurons.
