◦ Experimental tanycyte ablation deranges glucose sensing and feeding; tanycyte dysfunction is under investigation in central hypothyroidism and in disorders of puberty timing.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

CODE SAMPLE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

A stretched radial glial survivor with its head in the cerebrospinal fluid and its foot on a leaky capillary, tasting blood with the sensors of a tongue and a pancreas, and opening and closing the hormone gates of the hypothalamus by physically moving.


CHAPTER 9. PITUICYTE (Astrocyte of the Posterior Pituitary) (The Hormone-Throttling Gatekeeper Cell) (NON-COG)

The posterior pituitary is a gland with no gland cells: it is a bag of nerve endings, the terminals of hypothalamic neurons, dripping oxytocin and vasopressin straight into the blood.

Yet those taps are not simply open.

Wrapped around and between the terminals lives a resident astrocyte that, in quiet times, swallows the nerve endings whole and wedges its own body between them and the bloodstream.

When the body demands hormone - dehydration, childbirth, nursing - this cell lets go, retracts, and steps aside, and the hormones pour out.

It is a valve made of glia.

Hormone names its cargo, the two neurohormones oxytocin and vasopressin.

Throttling names its mechanism: physically obstructing or clearing the path from nerve terminal to blood.

Gatekeeper names its post at the final gate between brain and circulation.

Cell marks it as a single glial type, the astrocyte of the neurohypophysis.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The pituicyte is the specialised astrocyte of the posterior pituitary, also called the neurohypophysis, the neural lobe hanging beneath the hypothalamus.

The lobe's hormone output comes from the axon terminals of the magnocellular neurosecretory cells, treated in full in the neuronal catalogue, and the pituicyte's job is to modulate that output structurally: under basal conditions it engulfs neurosecretory terminals and interposes its processes between them and the basal lamina fronting the fenestrated capillaries, and under secretory demand it retracts, surrendering the release sites.

It is the dominant cell of the lobe by number, the terminals' landlord and throttle.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

A compact, irregular cell body with a few broad, sheet-like processes that wrap bundles of neurosecretory axons and endfeet that claim the perivascular basal lamina; the cytoplasm typically carries lipid droplets and, in humans, granules of aging pigment.

Its shape is its function, and its shape changes: the engulfed-terminal configuration and the retracted configuration are two working states of the same cell.

SIZE:

Cell body roughly 10 to 20 micrometres across, with processes extending tens of micrometres; pituicytes occupy roughly a quarter of the volume of the neural lobe, whose total human weight is only about 0.10 to 0.15 grams.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Classical electron microscopy named up to five morphological variants - dark, granular, ependymal-like, oncocytic, and the common light pituicyte - but modern opinion treats these mainly as states or aging variants of one type, moderate confidence.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Only in the neurohypophysis: the posterior lobe proper, the infundibular stalk, and the median eminence border where it meets tanycyte territory.

It lives directly on the blood-brain interface at fenestrated capillaries, one of the few brain regions with no blood-brain barrier.

NUMBER:

On the order of a few million per gland, low confidence, estimated from the lobe's small mass, its cell density, and the fact that pituicytes are its principal intrinsic cell.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Non-spiking, resting near minus 80 millivolts like other astrocytes, but richly receptive: it carries receptors for adenosine triphosphate (ATP), adenosine, noradrenaline, and for oxytocin and vasopressin themselves, and answers them with intracellular calcium rises - so the gate listens to the very hormones it gates.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

No synapses, but the most intimate neuro-glial junctions in the endocrine brain: whole neurosecretory terminals lie enclosed within pituicyte cytoplasm, and pituicyte processes plug the release path at the basal lamina.

Pituicytes are also coupled to one another by gap junctions, coordinating the lobe's glial response as one tissue.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that the pituicyte is an astrocyte and possesses no axon.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

It secretes no hormone, but it speaks chemistry: pituicytes release taurine, an amino acid signal, in response to swelling, and taurine acting on glycine receptors of the neurosecretory system helps tune vasopressin release to blood osmolality; they also handle ATP and adenosine signalling within the lobe.

MYELINATION:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that the pituicyte makes no myelin, and the neurosecretory axons of the lobe are themselves unmyelinated at their terminals.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Modest and steady, with lipid droplets suggesting fat-based reserves; its chief expenditures are cytoskeletal - the actin remodelling that drives engulfment and retraction - plus the housekeeping of a barrier-free, blood-bathed environment.

PLASTICITY:

Structural plasticity is its whole profession: within hours of dehydration, parturition, or the onset of lactation, pituicytes retract from the basal lamina and release their engulfed terminals, and when demand subsides they re-engulf and re-interpose, a fully reversible remodelling cycle demonstrated across mammals.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

The fenestrated endothelial cells and pericytes of the neural lobe's dense capillary bed sustain it, resident macrophages patrol the barrier-free tissue, and the magnocellular neurons themselves supply the activity signals that instruct its remodelling.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

The posterior pituitary without pituicytes would be a tap with no washer: hormone release would lose its gain control, its tuning to demand, and its structural upkeep.

Water balance and the reflexes of birth and nursing would run open-loop.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Its signature capability is mechanical gating of an endocrine organ: a glial cell that decides, by where it physically stands, how much of two hormones reaches the blood of the whole body.

Under basal conditions pituicyte processes enclose terminals and occupy the basal lamina; under sustained demand, driven by adenosine and adrenergic signals, the cell retracts within hours, multiplying terminal contact with the perivascular space - a slow, powerful analogue valve wrapped around a fast neural signal.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ ATP and adenosine spilled from active neurosecretory terminals; noradrenaline; oxytocin and vasopressin feedback; plasma osmolality shifts of a few percent.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Fractional coverage of the basal lamina, which sets hormone access to blood; taurine release; trophic support of terminals.

• STATE.

◦ Engulfed versus retracted configuration, intracellular calcium, gap-junction coupling state.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Resting potential near minus 80 millivolts; roughly 25 to 30 percent of neural lobe volume; basal-lamina occupancy varying from substantial to minimal between states.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Calcium responses seconds; retraction and re-engulfment hours; full remodelling with sustained stimuli such as lactation days.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Pituicytes are the cell of origin of pituicytoma, granular cell tumour, and spindle cell oncocytoma of the sellar region, a family of rare tumours positive for thyroid transcription factor 1 (TTF-1).

◦ Damage to the neural lobe and stalk that destroys terminals and their glial partners produces central diabetes insipidus, the loss of vasopressin with litres of dilute urine per day.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

CODE SAMPLE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

The only astrocyte that works as an endocrine valve: it swallows hormone-releasing nerve endings in quiet times, steps aside within hours when the body calls for water retention, birth, or milk, and answers to the very hormones it releases its grip upon.


CHAPTER 10. GOMORI-POSITIVE ASTROCYTE (Cysteine-Rich Granule-Bearing Astrocyte of the Arcuate Nucleus and Hippocampus) (The Iron-Hoarding Furnace-Room Cell) (NON-COG)

Stain the hypothalamus with the chrome alum haematoxylin recipe of the histochemist George Gomori and a strange population lights up that no routine stain reveals.

Scattered through the arcuate nucleus - the brain's feeding computer - and through parts of the hippocampus sit astrocytes stuffed with dark cytoplasmic granules rich in iron, copper, and sulphur.

The granules are the wreckage of the cell's own mitochondria, the energy furnaces, degraded and swept into lysosomal ash-heaps.

Why a specific astrocyte population in the brain's metabolic sensing regions should run its furnaces hard enough to scar is one of neuroanatomy's quiet unsolved cases.

Iron-Hoarding names the metal-laden granules that define the type under the stain.

Furnace-Room names both its mitochondria-rich, high-metabolism character and the burnt residue that character leaves behind.

Cell marks it as an astrocyte subtype defined, so far, chiefly by histochemistry.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The Gomori-positive astrocyte is a specialised astrocyte of the hypothalamic arcuate nucleus and the hippocampus, distinguished by cytoplasmic granules that stain with Gomori's chrome alum haematoxylin and that derive from degenerated mitochondria sequestered in lysosomes, carrying iron, copper, and cysteine-rich protein.

Functionally it does what neighbouring astrocytes do - transmitter clearance, potassium buffering, metabolic support - but its granule load, its regional placement, and its enriched glucose-handling machinery have implicated it in local metabolic sensing and in the brain's iron economy.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

An otherwise ordinary protoplasmic-style astrocyte whose cytoplasm carries conspicuous electron-dense granules, often clustered near blood vessels and the ventricular wall, with the granules showing the layered debris of mitochondrial membranes inside lysosomal wrapping.

SIZE:

Cell body roughly 10 to 15 micrometres, domain tens of micrometres across, with individual granules of roughly 0.5 to 2 micrometres.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

None established; the population itself is best read as a regional, metabolically specialised state or subtype of the astrocyte family, and its boundaries against ordinary neighbours are graded, low confidence.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Chiefly the arcuate nucleus of the hypothalamus, around the base of the third ventricle adjoining tanycyte territory, and the hippocampus, with scattered representatives in other periventricular regions; densities in the arcuate rise with age.

It lives against two interfaces, the ventricular wall and the local capillaries, exactly where blood-borne metabolic signals enter.

NUMBER:

Unknown in absolute terms in the human; a minority subpopulation of the astrocytes of its two home regions, plausibly in the hundreds of thousands to low millions, very low confidence.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Non-excitable, resting near minus 80 to minus 85 millivolts with the potassium-dominated astrocyte membrane, and presumed to share the calcium-signalling repertoire of its family; no dedicated electrophysiology distinguishes it, low confidence.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

No synapses of its own, but standard astrocytic ensheathment of arcuate and hippocampal synapses and gap-junction coupling into the local astrocyte network; in the arcuate this places its processes among the feeding neurons expressing neuropeptide Y (NPY) and pro-opiomelanocortin (POMC).

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that it is an astrocyte and has no axon.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

None of its own; it clears glutamate and gamma-aminobutyric acid (GABA) like its relatives, and its distinctive chemistry is inward - the accumulation of iron, copper, and cysteine-rich material - rather than outward signalling.

MYELINATION:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that astrocytes do not form myelin.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Its defining feature is metabolic overwork: the cell is rich in active mitochondria as well as dead ones, handles glucose avidly, and its granules are the cumulative exhaust of decades of oxidative work, making it simultaneously one of the hardest-working and most oxidatively stressed astrocytes in the brain.

PLASTICITY:

The granule burden grows with age and with oestrogen exposure in experimental animals, and the population expands in the aged arcuate; this is plasticity of the slow, cumulative, one-way kind.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Local microglia stand by to inherit its iron if it dies, the arcuate capillary endothelium and pericytes supply it, and the adjacent tanycytes share its metabolic-sensing neighbourhood and its ventricular interface.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Its region is the body's weight-and-energy thermostat, and this cell appears to be part of the thermostat's sensing element: famously, the toxin gold thioglucose, which lesions the glucose-sensing hypothalamus and makes animals obese, is taken up by and destroys these very astrocytes, suggesting that losing them helps blind the feeding circuits.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Its most striking capability is chemical containment: it locks redox-active iron and copper - metals that catalyse the production of destructive oxygen radicals - inside cysteine-rich granules, acting as a regional heavy-metal vault that protects the surrounding feeding circuitry at the price of its own cumulative scarring.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Blood-borne glucose at 4 to 8 millimolar and metabolic signals at the arcuate capillaries; iron and copper flux; local synaptic overflow.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Metabolic support and transmitter clearance for feeding and memory circuits; sequestration of transition metals.

• STATE.

◦ Granule number and metal load, functional mitochondrial reserve, oxidative stress level.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Resting potential near minus 80 millivolts; granules 0.5 to 2 micrometres; granule burden rising with age.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Routine astrocyte functions milliseconds to seconds; granule accumulation years to decades.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Experimental destruction by gold thioglucose produces hypothalamic obesity in animals, the classic evidence tying this cell to metabolic sensing.

◦ Its iron-rich, oxidation-scarred profile makes it a suspected local amplifier of age-related oxidative damage in hypothalamus and hippocampus, a hypothesis rather than an established disease, low confidence.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

CODE SAMPLE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

An astrocyte of the feeding and memory centres that a single old histochemical stain picks out by its freight of dead mitochondria packed with iron and copper - a cell whose life's ledger of metabolic work is written, literally, in ash it never throws away.


CHAPTER 11. SURFACE-ASSOCIATED AND GLIA LIMITANS ASTROCYTE (Astrocytes of the Limiting Glial Membrane) (The Border-Wall Mason Cell) (NON-COG)

The brain does not simply end where the meninges begin.

At every point on its outer surface, and around every blood vessel that dives into it, the last cellular word belongs to the brain itself: a continuous pavement of astrocyte endfeet, mortared onto a basement membrane, called the glia limitans - Latin for the limiting glia.

This wall is the brain's inner fence, its immigration checkpoint for immune cells, and, we now know, the pipe wall of its nightly waste-rinsing system.

Border-Wall names the continuous barrier its endfeet form against the meninges and vasculature.

Mason names its manner of working: laying overlapping endfeet like courses of brick and maintaining the basement-membrane mortar.

Cell marks the astrocytes specialised for this duty.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The glia limitans astrocyte is any astrocyte whose processes end in flattened endfeet tiling one of the brain's outer or perivascular surfaces, and the surface-associated astrocyte is the dedicated subpopulation whose cell bodies sit directly at the pial surface.

Together they build the two sheets of the limiting membrane: the glia limitans superficialis under the pia mater, the innermost meninx, and the glia limitans perivascularis around every penetrating vessel.

The wall regulates molecular and cellular traffic between brain tissue and the meningeal and perivascular compartments, anchors the basement membrane, and bounds the paravascular spaces through which cerebrospinal fluid flushes the brain's waste - the glymphatic system.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

Astrocytes of otherwise conventional form whose defining feature is the endfoot: a broad, flattened terminal expansion appressed to the basement membrane, overlapping its neighbours to leave no gaps, packed with the water channel aquaporin-4 (AQP4) and the potassium channel Kir4.1 arranged in dense orthogonal particle arrays.

The superficial sheet is one to several endfeet thick; the human pial surface, with its deep folds, obliges this pavement to cover roughly a quarter of a square metre.

SIZE:

Parent cell bodies 10 to 20 micrometres; individual endfeet several micrometres across; the composite limiting membrane only a few micrometres thick - a national border thinner than a red blood cell.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Two by location - superficial and perivascular - plus the recently profiled surface-associated astrocyte with its cell body at the pia, which single-cell studies mark as transcriptionally distinct; moderate confidence.

WHERE IT LIVES:

The entire outer surface of the brain and spinal cord beneath the pia, and the full length of every penetrating arteriole, capillary, and venule - which means this cell type, through its perivascular duty, touches essentially all of the roughly 600 kilometres of brain vasculature.

It is the definitive interface dweller: everything on one side is brain, everything on the other is not.

NUMBER:

Not separately censused; as a duty shared across much of the astrocyte population, the dedicated surface and barrier specialists plausibly number in the hundreds of millions of the brain's several billion astrocytes, low confidence.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Non-excitable, resting near minus 80 to minus 85 millivolts, with the endfoot serving as the astrocyte's electrical drain: Kir4.1 channels concentrated there dump siphoned potassium into the perivascular space, and calcium signals in endfeet help command vessel diameter.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

None at the wall itself; the relevant junctions are glia-to-glia and glia-to-matrix - gap junctions knitting the endfeet into a continuous electrical and metabolic sheet, and dystroglycan-based anchors bolting each endfoot to the basement membrane.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that it is an astrocyte population and has no axon.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

None released as transmitters at the border; its signalling currency is water and ions through AQP4 and Kir4.1, plus vasoactive messengers such as prostaglandins and potassium delivered to smooth muscle in neurovascular coupling.

MYELINATION:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that astrocytes do not form myelin.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Modest per cell but continuous, spent on maintaining vast membrane area, on water and ion pumping, and on synthesising basement-membrane components; the wall never closes for maintenance.

PLASTICITY:

The wall remodels with state and age: AQP4 polarisation to the endfoot is strongest in youth and during sleep-favourable conditions and degrades with aging and injury, and after any breach the local astrocytes convert to reactive masons, rebuilding the limitans as part of the scar described in the next chapter.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Pial fibroblasts secrete and share the basement membrane from the far side, perivascular fibroblasts and border-associated macrophages occupy the spaces the wall bounds, endothelial cells and pericytes form the vascular side of the neurovascular unit, and microglia reinforce the barrier when astrocyte coverage fails.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Its region is every region's edge: with the wall intact the brain is a gated compartment with a nightly rinse cycle, and with it broken the brain is exposed tissue - immune cells wander in, plasma proteins leak, and waste clearance silts up.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Its surprise is plumbing at organ scale: the polarised AQP4 water channels of its perivascular endfeet form the conductive walls of the glymphatic system, the circuit driven by cerebrospinal fluid (CSF) that flushes solutes - including amyloid-beta, the Alzheimer protein - out of the brain, running chiefly during deep sleep when interstitial space expands by tens of percent.

A sheet of glial endfeet is, in effect, the reason sleep rinses the brain.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ CSF influx along paravascular spaces; siphoned potassium at 3 to 10 millimolar; osmotic gradients; immune traffic pressing from meninges and blood.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Regulated water flux through AQP4; potassium disposal; vasoactive signals; interstitial solute clearance measured in tens of grams of protein turnover per day, order of magnitude only.

• STATE.

◦ Endfoot coverage fraction, normally near-complete; AQP4 polarisation index; basement-membrane integrity.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Resting potential near minus 80 millivolts; wall thickness a few micrometres; perivascular reach along roughly 600 kilometres of vessels.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Water flux milliseconds to seconds; glymphatic exchange tens of minutes, gated nightly by sleep; wall repair after breach days to weeks.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Neuromyelitis optica, in which autoantibodies against AQP4 attack the endfeet themselves, destroying the wall and its astrocytes around optic nerve and spinal cord.

◦ Endfoot detachment and AQP4 depolarisation accompany aging, traumatic brain injury, and Alzheimer's disease, and are implicated in failing glymphatic clearance.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

CODE SAMPLE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

The astrocyte as civil engineer: a few micrometres of overlapping, water-channel-studded endfeet that constitute the brain's entire outer wall and all its pipe linings, and that make sleep itself a plumbing operation.


CHAPTER 12. REACTIVE ASTROCYTE (Injury- and Disease-Induced Astrocyte State) (The Scar-Laying Emergency-Brigade Cell) (NON-COG)

This chapter describes not a resting cell type but a transformation available to nearly every astrocyte in this section.

Wound the brain - by blade, blocked artery, infection, or creeping neurodegeneration - and within hours the local astrocytes change identity: they swell, bristle with new intermediate filaments, re-enter old developmental programs, and in severe injury build a wall of overlapping bodies around the damage.

That wall, the glial scar, seals the wound and saves the surrounding tissue, and then stands for the rest of the patient's life as the barrier through which severed axons cannot regrow.

The same transformation, run chronically and badly, can turn a helper into a killer of neurons.

Scar-Laying names its defining product, the astrocytic border of the glial scar.

Emergency-Brigade names its character: a rapid, all-purpose damage response drafted from the resident population.

Cell marks that every reactive astrocyte is an ordinary astrocyte transformed.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The reactive astrocyte is any astrocyte remodelled by injury or disease into a defensive state, a process called reactive astrogliosis.

Its duties are triage: it seals breaches in the blood-brain barrier and glia limitans, takes up excess glutamate and potassium, walls off dead tissue and inflammatory cells behind a scar border, releases cytokines - immune signalling proteins - and later helps remodel what survives.

The state is graded, from mild and fully reversible hypertrophy to permanent scar formation, and it is heterogeneous, spanning protective and harmful profiles.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The signature is hypertrophy: the cell body and main processes thicken severalfold, stuffed with newly made intermediate filaments, while - a crucial subtlety - each mildly reactive astrocyte still keeps to its own territorial domain.

In severe lesions domains are abandoned: newborn and hypertrophic astrocytes overlap and interweave into the compact astrocyte border of the glial scar, elongated cells palisading the lesion like staves around a wound.

SIZE:

Soma and process calibre increase roughly two- to several-fold over the resting cell, with scar-border astrocytes elongated to tens of micrometres along the lesion edge.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Graded and profiled rather than fixed: mild-to-moderate reactive astrocytes, scar-forming border astrocytes, and transcriptomic profiles famously dichotomised as A1 - inflammation-induced and neurotoxic, marked by complement component C3 - and A2 - ischaemia-induced and neurotrophic; the field now prefers a multidimensional continuum of states over that binary, moderate confidence.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Wherever pathology is: around strokes, contusions, tumours, plaques, and infections in any central nervous system (CNS) region, with a standing gradient of mild reactivity in normal aging.

It is a border cell by vocation, rebuilding broken interfaces - the glia limitans, the vessel wall, the edge between dead and living tissue.

NUMBER:

Zero in the healthy young brain by strict definition, and up to essentially the whole local astrocyte population around a lesion; in neurodegenerative disease, reactive profiles spread through entire regions.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Resting potential often depolarises modestly from the astrocytic minus 80 to minus 85 millivolts as Kir4.1 expression falls, degrading potassium buffering, while intracellular calcium signalling becomes exaggerated - a quieter membrane and a louder chemistry.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Its tripartite-synapse embraces loosen as processes withdraw toward the injury, glutamate transport can fall, and gap-junction coupling reorganises; around scars it contacts invading fibroblasts and immune cells across the new lesion border.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that it is an astrocyte state and has no axon.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Its emergency vocabulary is inflammatory and matrix chemistry: cytokines such as interleukin-1, interleukin-6, and tumour necrosis factor, chemokines, complement component C3 in the neurotoxic profile, growth factors, and chondroitin sulphate proteoglycans - the axon-repelling matrix molecules of the scar.

MYELINATION:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that astrocytes do not form myelin, though reactive astrocytes strongly influence whether oligodendrocytes can remyelinate a lesion.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

High: hypertrophy, filament synthesis, proliferation at scar borders, and cytokine production are all expensive, and the reactive state reroutes metabolism toward glycolysis with increased glycogen turnover.

PLASTICITY:

The state itself is the plasticity: induction within hours, driven by signals including the STAT3 pathway inside the astrocyte and, for the neurotoxic profile, by microglial interleukin-1 alpha, tumour necrosis factor, and complement C1q; mild reactivity resolves in days to weeks, while the scar border, once laid, is permanent.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Microglia are its instructor and partner, arriving first and secreting the cytokines that set its profile; border-associated macrophages and blood-borne immune cells hold the lesion core; fibroblasts lay the collagenous core of the scar it walls in; and surviving endothelium re-establishes the vasculature behind its repaired barrier.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Ablate the reactive response experimentally and lesions enlarge, inflammation spreads, the blood-brain barrier stays open, and outcomes worsen - proof that the scar is first aid, not vandalism.

Yet the same wall and its proteoglycans are a principal reason severed spinal cord axons never cross a healed injury, making this state simultaneously the saviour of the acute brain and the jailer of the chronic one.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Its most surprising capability is fate change: a mature, decades-old astrocyte re-enters the cell cycle at the scar border, re-expresses developmental programs, and in some conditions steps fully back toward a neural stem cell state - and in the neurotoxic profile it acquires the opposite power, actively killing neurons and mature oligodendrocytes, with saturated lipids among the candidate toxins.

One resident cell type thus holds, latent, both a construction brigade and an executioner.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Damage signals: adenosine triphosphate (ATP) spilled from dying cells, microglial interleukin-1 alpha, tumour necrosis factor and C1q, interferons, blood proteins such as fibrinogen crossing a broken barrier.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Cytokines and chemokines; complement C3; chondroitin sulphate proteoglycans; a rebuilt glia limitans; the scar border itself.

• STATE.

◦ Reactivity grade from mild to scar-forming; glial fibrillary acidic protein (GFAP), vimentin, and nestin expression; C3-positive versus neurotrophic profile; proliferation status.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Hypertrophy roughly two- to several-fold; GFAP upregulation up to order-of-magnitude; domain overlap absent in mild states, present at scars.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Molecular induction hours; hypertrophy days; scar border formation one to two weeks; resolution of mild states days to weeks; the scar itself permanent.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Alexander disease, in which mutations in the GFAP gene itself produce astrocytes choked with Rosenthal fibres and a fatal leukodystrophy - the disease of the reactive program's own signature protein.

◦ Neurotoxic C3-positive reactive astrocytes are implicated in Alzheimer's, Parkinson's, Huntington's, multiple sclerosis, and amyotrophic lateral sclerosis; scar-borne proteoglycans block spinal cord regeneration; and dysfunctional reactive astrocytes drive hepatic encephalopathy and contribute to epileptic foci.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

CODE SAMPLE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

Not a cell but a metamorphosis: any astrocyte, anywhere, transformed within hours of injury into a swollen, filament-packed emergency worker that can seal the brain, wall a wound for life, revert toward a stem cell, or - in its darkest profile - turn on the neurons it once served.


SECTION 2. THE OLIGODENDROCYTE LINEAGE - THE INSULATORS

The oligodendrocyte, from the Greek for few-branch cell, is the myelinating cell of the central nervous system (CNS), the brain and spinal cord.

Myelin is the fatty, multilayered insulation wrapped around axons that forces the nerve impulse to jump from gap to gap, multiplying conduction speed up to one-hundred-fold.

For a century this family was filed under plumbing, but the discovery of adaptive myelination - the activity-dependent tuning of insulation to adjust the arrival times of spikes - has moved its two working members onto the cognitive roster.

The lineage runs in one direction: a precursor cell that listens to circuits, a mature cell that wraps axons, and a quiet satellite cousin held in reserve against a neuron's cell body.

Together they own the timing of the brain.


CHAPTER 13. MYELINATING (INTERFASCICULAR) OLIGODENDROCYTE (The Speed-Tuning Insulator Cell) (COG)

Every thought you have is a relay race run over wires of wildly different lengths, and yet the batons arrive together.

The cell that makes this possible never fires a spike of its own: it squats among the white matter cables, reaches out a few dozen arms, and wraps each one around a different axon like electrical tape wound forty times deep.

By choosing where to wrap, how thickly, and how long each wrapped segment runs, it sets the speed of every message that passes, and it re-tunes those speeds as you learn.

Kill this cell throughout the brain and every signal still leaves on time but arrives in disorder, which is the private catastrophe called multiple sclerosis.

Speed-Tuning names its newly proven trade: it adjusts conduction velocity to synchronise spike arrivals, which is a form of learning.

Insulator names the classical trade: it manufactures myelin, the brain's only insulation.

Cell marks it as glia, not neuron - it wraps wires but is not one.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The myelinating oligodendrocyte is the mature, post-mitotic cell of the oligodendrocyte lineage, sitting in rows between the axon bundles (fascicles) of the white matter - hence interfascicular - and extending processes that each terminate in a spiral wrap of myelin around one segment of one axon.

One cell maintains roughly 20 to 60 such wrapped segments, called internodes, distributed across up to 50 different axons, so a single oligodendrocyte is a hub whose failure strips insulation from dozens of wires at once.

Its second job, discovered more recently, is metabolic: the myelin sheath delivers lactate, a fuel molecule, directly to the axon it insulates through the transporter MCT1, so the wrap is a feeding tube as well as tape.

Its third job earns the (COG) tag: myelination is not fixed at development but continues and remodels through adult life in response to neuronal activity, and blocking new oligodendrocyte formation in mice demonstrably prevents motor learning and impairs memory consolidation.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

A small, round, dense cell body with a dark nucleus and remarkably few primary branches - the few-branch cell of its Greek name - each branch running to an internode where the membrane flattens, spirals up to 40 to 60 times around the axon, and compacts by squeezing out nearly all cytoplasm.

Unrolled, the myelin of one internode is a single triangular sheet of membrane; the living cytoplasm survives only in a thin bead at each edge, the paranodal loop, which anchors the sheath beside the node.

Between neighbouring internodes lies the node of Ranvier, the one to two micrometre bare gap - a micrometre being a thousandth of a millimetre - where sodium channels crowd and the impulse is reborn.

SIZE:

The cell body is small, about 10 to 20 micrometres across.

Each internode runs roughly 20 to 200 micrometres in the cortex and short tracts, and up to several hundred micrometres, exceptionally beyond 1,000, on large-calibre spinal axons.

The total membrane one cell maintains is enormous for its body size: on the order of 100,000 square micrometres of myelin, among the largest membrane-to-body ratios of any human cell.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Del Rio-Hortega's classical types I to IV grade from many thin internodes on many small axons (type I, cortex) to one or two thick, long internodes on a single large axon (type IV, spinal cord), a division modern single-cell gene reading has confirmed as a continuum of at least six mature transcriptional states in human white matter.

Treat the family as one trade practised at different gauges of wire.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Everywhere there is myelin: densest in the white matter - corpus callosum, internal capsule, optic nerve, spinal columns, cerebellar core - and sparser in the grey matter, where cortical myelin is famously patchy.

Its working surface is an explicit interface, the periaxonal space, the 15 nanometre cleft between the innermost myelin wrap and the axon membrane, across which fuel and signals pass.

NUMBER:

The human neocortex alone holds roughly 21 billion oligodendrocytes in women and 29 billion in men by stereological count, about three quarters of all cortical glia; a defensible whole-brain estimate is several tens of billions, moderate confidence, since the white matter and cerebellum add heavily to the cortical figure.

That makes this lineage the most numerous glial family in the human brain, outnumbering astrocytes and microglia alike.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Not excitable: it fires no action potentials, and its resting potential sits near minus 80 millivolts, dominated by potassium channels including Kir4.1.

It nonetheless listens electrically, sensing potassium released by active axons at the node and responding with calcium signals inside individual myelin sheaths, and mature cells are coupled to astrocytes through gap junctions, direct cell-to-cell pores, built from connexins 32 and 47.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

It receives no synapses once mature - its precursor did, and lost them on differentiation - but it keeps intimate junctional contact with every axon it wraps at the paranodal loops, sealed by the adhesion complex of contactin, Caspr and neurofascin 155.

Vesicular glutamate release from active axons along the internode still reaches it, signalling through NMDA (N-methyl-D-aspartate) and alpha-amino-3-hydroxy-5-methyl-4-isoxazolepropionic acid (AMPA) receptors on the sheath.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that the oligodendrocyte has no axon of its own - it is the cell that dresses the axons of others.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

It releases no transmitter of its own; its outgoing chemical language is metabolic and trophic - lactate through MCT1, and supportive signals including exosomes (tiny membrane parcels) passed to the axon.

Incoming, it reads glutamate, adenosine and potassium as reports of axonal activity.

MYELINATION:

This cell is not myelinated; it is myelin - the sheath is its own living membrane, roughly 40 percent water and, of the dry mass, about 70 to 80 percent lipid, the highest lipid fraction of any human membrane, which is what makes white matter white.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Myelination is one of the costliest construction projects in the body: during active wrapping a single cell synthesises on the order of its own weight in membrane per day, and the adult brain's cholesterol - which cannot cross in from the blood - is concentrated in myelin.

The mature cell runs a strange economy, glycolytic by preference, passing the resulting lactate down to the axon rather than burning it all itself; this generosity leaves it chronically poor in antioxidant reserve and rich in iron, which is why it is the central nervous system (CNS) cell most easily killed by oxidative stress and ischaemia.

PLASTICITY:

The headline plasticity is adaptive myelination: learning a new motor skill in adult mice triggers the birth of new oligodendrocytes, and blocking that birth blocks the learning; existing cells also remodel, adjusting sheath thickness and internode length on active axons.

Because conduction delay depends on internode length and myelin thickness, this is plasticity of timing rather than of connection strength - the third knob of learning, alongside synaptic weight and excitability.

Human carbon-14 birth-dating shows cortical oligodendrocytes turn over slowly but myelin itself is renewed throughout life.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Astrocytes deliver glucose and cholesterol precursors and couple to it by gap junctions; microglia clear its myelin debris and, in health, promote remyelination; the oligodendrocyte precursor cell is its replacement pool, standing by to replace any mature cell that dies.

The axon itself supports it in return, since axonal activity and axonal neuregulin signalling sustain the sheath - insulation and wire are mutual dependents.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

White matter is this cell's colour in the most literal sense: remove it and the white half of the human brain turns grey, and conduction across the hemispheres slows from tens of metres per second to below one.

Every long-range rhythm that requires millisecond coincidence - the thalamocortical loops of attention, the hippocampal-cortical dialogue of memory consolidation - depends on delays this cell sets.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Its signature computation is delay-line engineering: by lengthening or thinning myelin here and thickening it there, it equalises arrival times across axons of different lengths, the trick first proven in the auditory brainstem, where sound-localisation circuits achieve microsecond precision partly through graded myelination.

Each internode is also a semi-autonomous computing element with its own calcium transients, its own local protein synthesis from transported messenger ribonucleic acid (RNA), and its own fuel valve - one cell running up to 60 partly independent branches.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Axonal activity reports: glutamate and adenosine along the internode, potassium at the node, nanomolar to micromolar transients.

◦ Astrocytic metabolic supply through gap junctions; neuregulin and other axonal growth signals.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Conduction velocity of every wrapped axon, settable from roughly 1 to 100 metres per second by myelin geometry.

◦ Lactate delivered to the periaxonal space; trophic exosomes.

• STATE.

◦ Number, length and thickness of 20 to 60 internodes; per-sheath calcium level; iron and lipid stores.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Resting potential near minus 80 millivolts; internode length 20 to 200 micrometres, more on large axons; g-ratio, the axon diameter divided by the fibre diameter with myelin, optimal near 0.77 in the CNS.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Sheath calcium transients seconds; new internode growth hours to days; a precursor differentiating into a working myelinator days to weeks; myelin remodelling with learning days; whole-life myelin turnover years.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Multiple sclerosis, in which immune attack strips myelin and kills oligodendrocytes, slowing and blocking conduction.

◦ The leukodystrophies, inherited failures of myelin manufacture, including Pelizaeus-Merzbacher disease from mutations of the myelin gene PLP1.

◦ Periventricular white matter injury of premature birth, and hypoxic death of oligodendrocytes in stroke.

◦ In psychiatry, white matter and oligodendrocyte gene abnormalities recur in schizophrenia and depression, consistent with timing disorders of long-range circuits.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to model adaptive myelination, in which an oligodendrocyte adjusts the conduction delay of each axon so that spikes travelling different path lengths arrive at a shared target at the same time.
Create a list variable called path lengths holding the axon lengths in millimetres, for example 20, 50 and 80.
Create a list variable called velocities holding the current conduction velocities in metres per second, with initial value 5 for every axon.
Create a number variable called learning rate with initial value 0.2.
Create a number variable called tolerance with initial value 0.1.
Repeat the following actions for up to two hundred rounds.
    For each axon, calculate the delay in milliseconds as the path length divided by the velocity.
    Calculate the mean of all the delays and call it the target delay.
    For each axon, perform the following actions.
        Calculate the difference of the delay of this axon and the target delay.
        If the difference is positive is true, then perform the following actions.
            Increase the velocity of this axon by the learning rate multiplied by the difference, to represent adding myelin.
        Otherwise, perform the following actions.
            Decrease the velocity of this axon by the learning rate multiplied by the size of the difference, to represent thinning myelin.
        End the conditional block.
        If the velocity of this axon is greater than 100 is true, then set it to 100.
        If the velocity of this axon is less than 1 is true, then set it to 1.
    End the loop over axons.
    If the largest delay minus the smallest delay is less than the tolerance is true, then exit the repetition early.
End the repetition.
Display the final delays to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because the whole story is a small vector of delays converging to one value, and Python states and prints that convergence with no ceremony.

# Adaptive myelination as delay-line tuning: myelin is added or thinned
# per axon until spikes over unequal path lengths arrive synchronously.
# Oligodendrogenesis is required for motor learning (McKenzie et al.,
# Science 2014); delay equalization by graded myelination is established
# in the auditory brainstem (Seidl et al., J Neurosci 2010).
LENGTHS = [20.0, 50.0, 80.0]      # millimetres of axon to a common target
velocity = [5.0, 5.0, 5.0]        # metres per second (mm per ms)
RATE, TOL = 0.2, 0.05             # myelin adjustment gain, sync tolerance (ms)

for step in range(200):
    delays = [L / v for L, v in zip(LENGTHS, velocity)]
    target = sum(delays) / len(delays)
    velocity = [min(100.0, max(1.0, v + RATE * (d - target)))
                for v, d in zip(velocity, delays)]
    if max(delays) - min(delays) < TOL:
        break

print(f"converged after {step} rounds of myelin remodelling")
for L, v, d in zip(LENGTHS, velocity, delays):
    print(f"  axon {L:5.1f} mm  velocity {v:6.2f} m/s  delay {d:5.2f} ms")

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

The only human cell that is mostly not where its body is: a modest 15 micrometre soma whose real substance is dozens of spiral membrane wraps on other cells' wires, and the only glial cell whose daily work measurably rewrites the arrival times of thought.


CHAPTER 14. SATELLITE (PERINEURONAL) OLIGODENDROCYTE (The Neuron-Side Reserve Cell) (NON-COG)

Look at a stained slice of human cortex and you will see, pressed against the flanks of the big pyramidal neurons, small dark round cells sitting like moons against planets.

They are oligodendrocytes, fully credentialled members of the myelinating family, and yet in the resting state they wrap nothing.

They are the insulators' garrison in the grey matter: paid, housed, and waiting.

Neuron-Side names its address, parked directly against a neuron's cell body.

Reserve names its standing role, a non-myelinating cell held ready to support its neuron and, on demand, to myelinate.

Cell marks it as glia.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The satellite oligodendrocyte is a mature oligodendrocyte of the grey matter whose cell body sits directly apposed to a neuronal soma - perineuronal means around the neuron - and which in the healthy resting state maintains no myelin sheaths.

Its documented business is local support: buffering the neuron's ionic and metabolic environment, supplying lactate, and standing by as a reserve that can myelinate nearby axon segments when demand or injury calls.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

A small, round, dark-nucleated cell with scant cytoplasm and few short processes, flattened against the neuronal soma it attends.

It is distinguished from the perineuronal satellite microglia and astrocytes that share the same address only by its dense chromatin and oligodendrocyte marker proteins such as Olig2.

SIZE:

The cell body is about 7 to 10 micrometres across, among the smallest cells in the cortex, dwarfed by the 20 to 60 micrometre neurons it attends.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

No firm subtypes are established; single-cell studies suggest it is transcriptionally close to mature myelinating oligodendrocytes rather than to precursors, with hints of a distinct perineuronal signature - low confidence.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Grey matter throughout the central nervous system (CNS), most conspicuously against the large pyramidal neurons of the cerebral cortex, and also in deep nuclei and spinal grey matter.

Its defining interface is the direct membrane apposition between its soma and the neuronal soma, with no basement membrane between.

NUMBER:

Counts are uncertain: classical estimates place one or a few satellite oligodendrocytes against a substantial minority of large cortical neurons, implying a population in the low billions in the human brain - low confidence, since census methods rarely separate them from other grey matter oligodendrocytes.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Non-excitable, resting near minus 80 millivolts with strong potassium conductance through Kir4.1, well placed to siphon the potassium a firing neuron dumps around its own cell body.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

It receives no synapses and makes none; its junctional life is the intimate soma-to-soma apposition with its host neuron and gap junction coupling into the astrocyte network.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that the satellite oligodendrocyte has no axon and, in its resting state, does not even ensheath the axons of others.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

None released; its chemical output is metabolic support, including lactate transfer to its host neuron.

MYELINATION:

This section largely does not apply for the reason that the resting satellite oligodendrocyte is defined by not myelinating - though upon local demyelination it can activate the myelination programme and wrap nearby axons, which is precisely why it is called a reserve.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Modest at rest, a fraction of its myelinating sibling's budget, rising steeply if recruited into myelin manufacture.

PLASTICITY:

Its plasticity is vocational: injury, demyelination or rising activity can convert it from idle satellite to working myelinator, and its numbers increase around stressed neurons.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Astrocytes couple to it by gap junctions and supply substrates; microglia patrol the same perineuronal niche; its host neuron's activity is likely the signal that retains it in place.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

It gives the grey matter its salt-and-pepper look under the microscope - the classical perineuronal satellitosis of the histologists - and gives each large neuron a private metabolic attendant and a private spare insulator.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Its one surprising capability is the conversion itself: a terminally differentiated cell holding a complete, silenced myelination programme that injury can switch on in place, a fire extinguisher behind glass beside every large neuron.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Potassium and metabolic signals from the host neuron's soma; astrocytic supply through gap junctions; injury signals.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Lactate and ionic buffering to the host neuron; on demand, new myelin sheaths locally.

• STATE.

◦ Idle versus recruited; number of satellites per neuron, typically zero to a few, rising with neuronal stress.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Resting potential near minus 80 millivolts; soma 7 to 10 micrometres.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Ionic buffering milliseconds to seconds; recruitment to myelination days.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Increased perineuronal satellitosis is a classical histological sign of neuronal stress and neurodegeneration.

◦ As oligodendrocytes, these cells die in multiple sclerosis grey matter lesions and are implicated in grey matter demyelination.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

CODE SAMPLE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

A fully licensed myelinating cell that wraps nothing, parked against a neuron's cell body like a spare tyre bolted beside the wheel it may one day replace.


CHAPTER 15. OLIGODENDROCYTE PRECURSOR CELL, ALSO CALLED THE NG2 GLIA OR POLYDENDROCYTE (The Circuit-Listening Recruit Cell) (COG)

Hidden in plain sight, evenly spaced through every cubic millimetre of your brain, is a fourth great glial population that anatomy books missed for a century.

It divides all your life, long after the neurons around it have stopped; it is the commonest dividing cell in the adult human brain.

And it does something no other non-neuronal cell in the body is known to do: neurons wire genuine synapses onto it, and talk to it in the very currency of thought.

It listens to that traffic, and when a circuit works hard enough, it answers by turning itself into a new oligodendrocyte and insulating the busy wires.

Circuit-Listening names its unique privilege: it receives real synapses and hears neural activity directly.

Recruit names its destiny: it is the standing army from which every new myelinating oligodendrocyte of adult learning is drafted.

Cell marks it as glia, though it stands closer to the neurons than any other.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The oligodendrocyte precursor cell (OPC), marked by the NG2 proteoglycan and the growth factor receptor PDGFR-alpha and also called the polydendrocyte for its many branches, is the self-renewing progenitor of all myelinating oligodendrocytes, and it persists in the adult brain as a uniformly tiled, continuously dividing population making up roughly 5 percent of all CNS cells.

Its distinguishing property, established by Bergles and colleagues in 2000, is that it receives bona fide glutamatergic and GABAergic synapses from neurons - the only non-neuronal cell type for which this is proven - so that circuit activity is delivered to it with synaptic precision.

It integrates that activity and decides, cell by cell, whether to keep dividing, stay put, or differentiate into a new myelinating oligodendrocyte, which is the supply line of adaptive myelination and hence of motor learning and memory consolidation.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

A small, stellate cell with many fine, radially spread branches - the polydendrocyte - contacting nodes of Ranvier, synapses, and blood vessels within its territory.

Like the protoplasmic astrocyte it tiles: each OPC holds an exclusive domain and actively repels its neighbours, and if one dies or differentiates, adjacent OPCs divide or migrate within days to refill the vacancy, keeping the lattice complete.

SIZE:

The cell body is about 10 to 15 micrometres across, with a process field spanning roughly 50 to 100 micrometres.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Grey matter OPCs divide more slowly and differentiate less readily than white matter OPCs, and dorsal versus ventral embryonic origins leave lasting transcriptional marks; single-cell studies of human tissue add disease-associated states in multiple sclerosis - moderate confidence in the grey-white division, lower in the finer splits.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Everywhere in the CNS, grey and white matter alike, in an even lattice from cortex to spinal cord to optic nerve - the only glial population with a truly uniform census.

It also touches two explicit interfaces: many OPCs place end-feet on capillaries, and during development they migrate along the vasculature.

NUMBER:

About 5 percent of all cells in the adult CNS; with roughly 170 billion total brain cells this implies several billion OPCs, moderate confidence.

Even in the aged human brain a few percent of OPCs are in the cell cycle at any moment, making them the principal dividing population of the adult parenchyma.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

The most electrically neuron-like of all glia: resting potential near minus 80 to minus 90 millivolts, but equipped with voltage-gated sodium, potassium and calcium channels, and a subset can fire small, broad spike-like depolarisations under strong drive - not full action potentials, but unambiguous excitability.

Its synaptic currents are miniature versions of neuronal ones, with fast alpha-amino-3-hydroxy-5-methyl-4-isoxazolepropionic acid (AMPA) and gamma-aminobutyric acid (GABA)-A receptor kinetics.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

It receives direct synapses from glutamatergic axons - unmyelinated and even myelinated ones, which sprout tiny boutons onto it - and from GABAergic interneurons, with quantal currents of a few picoamperes.

It makes no output synapses; its reply is developmental, metabolic and structural rather than electrical, and the synapses it receives are dismantled as it differentiates.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that the OPC has no axon; it is postsynaptic without ever being presynaptic, a listener with no wire of its own.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

It releases no classical transmitter, though NG2 glia can shape inhibitory signalling through cleaved fragments of the NG2 protein itself and through GABA-related mechanisms under study; incoming, it reads glutamate through AMPA and N-methyl-D-aspartate (NMDA) receptors and GABA through GABA-A receptors.

MYELINATION:

The OPC itself is unmyelinated and makes no myelin, but it is myelin's future: every new sheath in the adult brain begins as one of these cells choosing to differentiate.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Moderate in the listening state, then explosive at differentiation, when the cell must synthesise membrane at one of the highest rates in biology; its perivascular end-feet give it first call on glucose, and OPC differentiation is exquisitely sensitive to thyroid hormone and to oxygen.

PLASTICITY:

Plasticity is this cell's entire profession, in three registers: proliferation tuned by circuit activity, differentiation into new oligodendrocytes driven by learning, and its own synaptic inputs, which show long-term potentiation - a strengthening of transmission - at glutamatergic synapses onto it.

Motor learning in adult mice raises OPC differentiation within hours, and deleting the differentiation programme blocks the learning; social isolation and sensory deprivation push it the other way.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Astrocytes supply substrates and secrete platelet-derived growth factor, its master mitogen; microglia both promote and prune the population and clear the majority of new oligodendrocytes that fail to integrate; the vasculature it touches is its migration rail and fuel line.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

It gives every region a hidden reserve currency: the capacity to lay new insulation on demand, so that a cortex with its OPC lattice intact can retime itself after injury or during learning, and a cortex without it cannot - as multiple sclerosis shows when remyelination stalls because OPCs arrive at lesions and fail to differentiate.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Its signature computation is a slow analogue-to-digital conversion: it integrates synaptic activity over hours to days and converts that continuous listening into a discrete, essentially irreversible decision - divide, wait, or become an oligodendrocyte - executed by dismantling its own synapses as it transforms.

It is also the brain's most vigilant homeostat of its own census, sensing neighbour density through contact repulsion and refilling any vacancy in days.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Glutamatergic and GABAergic synaptic currents, quantal amplitude a few picoamperes; platelet-derived growth factor and neuregulin; thyroid hormone; contact repulsion from neighbouring OPCs.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ New myelinating oligodendrocytes, on the order of a fraction of a percent of the population differentiating per week in adulthood; daughter OPCs; NG2 protein fragments.

• STATE.

◦ Cell cycle phase; integrated activity level; differentiation commitment, effectively a one-way flag; domain occupancy.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Resting potential minus 80 to minus 90 millivolts; population share about 5 percent of CNS cells; cell cycle time weeks in adult grey matter, faster in white matter and after injury.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Synaptic currents milliseconds; calcium signals seconds; proliferative response to activity hours to days; differentiation into a myelinating cell days to weeks; domain refill after loss days.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Remyelination failure in progressive multiple sclerosis, where OPCs stall undifferentiated inside lesions.

◦ Malignant transformation: OPCs are a principal cell of origin of oligodendroglioma and of many diffuse gliomas.

◦ Perinatal white matter injury, where hypoxia kills the pre-oligodendrocyte stage and causes the white matter damage of cerebral palsy.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to model the activity-dependent decision of an oligodendrocyte precursor cell, which integrates synaptic activity over days and then divides, waits, or differentiates into a myelinating oligodendrocyte.
Create a number variable called integrated activity with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called leak fraction with initial value 0.9.
Create a number variable called differentiation threshold with initial value 5.
Create a number variable called proliferation threshold with initial value 2.
Create a number variable called neighbour count with initial value 6.
Create a number variable called full occupancy with initial value 6.
For each day of the simulation, perform the following actions.
    Multiply integrated activity by the leak fraction, to represent forgetting old activity.
    Add the synaptic drive received this day to integrated activity.
    If integrated activity is greater than the differentiation threshold is true, then perform the following actions.
        Declare that the cell differentiates into a myelinating oligodendrocyte.
        Dismantle the cell's synapses and exit the simulation.
    End the conditional block.
    If integrated activity is greater than the proliferation threshold is true, and neighbour count is less than full occupancy is true, then perform the following actions.
        Declare that the cell divides to refill the local territory.
        Increase neighbour count by 1.
    End the conditional block.
End the loop over days.
Declare that the cell remains a listening precursor.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because the decision is a leaky accumulator crossing two thresholds, and Python renders that logic in a dozen transparent lines.

# Activity-dependent OPC fate decision: a leaky integrator of synaptic
# drive with a lower threshold for division and a higher, one-way
# threshold for differentiation into a myelinating oligodendrocyte.
# OPCs receive genuine synapses (Bergles et al., Nature 2000); motor
# learning drives their differentiation (McKenzie et al., Science 2014).
LEAK, P_THR, D_THR = 0.9, 2.0, 5.0
drive_by_day = [0.5, 0.6, 0.4, 2.0, 2.5, 2.2, 2.4, 2.6]  # a training week
activity, neighbours, FULL = 0.0, 5, 6

for day, drive in enumerate(drive_by_day, 1):
    activity = activity * LEAK + drive
    if activity > D_THR:
        print(f"day {day}: activity {activity:.2f} -> DIFFERENTIATE "
              "(synapses dismantled, myelination begins)")
        break
    if activity > P_THR and neighbours < FULL:
        neighbours += 1
        print(f"day {day}: activity {activity:.2f} -> divide "
              f"(territory now {neighbours}/{FULL})")
    else:
        print(f"day {day}: activity {activity:.2f} -> keep listening")

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

The only non-neuronal cell in the human body onto which neurons build true synapses: a permanently juvenile, permanently dividing listener, tiled through the whole CNS, that repays a busy circuit by becoming its insulation.


SECTION 3. THE MICROGLIA - THE RESIDENT IMMUNE CELLS

The microglia are the immune garrison of the central nervous system (CNS), and they are foreigners: born not of nervous tissue but of the embryonic yolk sac, they immigrated into the brain in the first weeks of gestation, before the blood-brain barrier closed behind them.

They then maintained themselves by local division for the rest of the owner's life, a closed population that never again takes recruits from the blood in health.

A generation ago they were catalogued as dormant janitors awaiting injury.

Live imaging destroyed that view in a single afternoon in 2005, revealing the most restlessly active cell in the healthy brain.

One family member carries this entire section, and carries a (COG) tag that would once have seemed absurd for an immune cell.


CHAPTER 16. MICROGLIA (Resident Macrophage of the Central Nervous System) (The Synapse-Pruning Sentinel Cell) (COG)

At this moment, in your skull, tens of billions of fine living fingers are touching your synapses one by one, holding each for a few minutes, and letting go.

The cells doing the touching are not neurons and were not born in your brain; they are immigrant immune cells that arrived before you had a face.

In the womb and through childhood they ate the surplus: perhaps half the synapses you ever made went down their throats, and that eating is why your circuits work.

In adulthood they keep testing, keep pruning, and stand guard; and when they turn bad - too eager, too inflamed, too old - the same mouths that sculpted your mind begin to eat it.

Synapse-Pruning names its cognitive trade: the licensed removal of weak synapses, which is half of learning.

Sentinel names its immune trade: the resident watchman of the central nervous system (CNS), first responder to injury and infection.

Cell marks it as glia by residence, though by passport it is a macrophage, a professional engulfing cell.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The microglial cell is the resident macrophage of the CNS, derived from yolk sac progenitors that colonised the brain around the fourth to fifth gestational week and self-renewed there ever since, making up roughly 5 to 10 percent of all brain cells.

In health it performs continuous surveillance, touching synapses, nodes and vessels with motile processes; it prunes synapses - engulfing weak or surplus ones flagged by complement proteins, the immune system's molecular tags - most heavily during development but demonstrably also in the adult, where it participates in learning and in forgetting.

In disease it transforms into an amoeboid phagocyte, clearing debris and pathogens, and its chronic activation is central to neurodegeneration and to the inflammatory hypothesis of depression - it is the receiving station of the neuroimmune channel between body and brain.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

In the healthy state, a small soma with a crown of fine, exquisitely branched processes radiating in all directions - the ramified morphology - each process studded with receptors and capable of extending a bulbous tip onto a synapse or a wound within minutes.

Upon activation the branches retract, the soma swells, and the cell becomes a compact amoeboid crawler indistinguishable in silhouette from any tissue macrophage; morphology is therefore its mood ring.

SIZE:

The soma is about 7 to 10 micrometres across; the ramified process field spans roughly 30 to 60 micrometres, and neighbouring territories tile with little overlap.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Modern single-cell surveys replace the old resting-versus-activated binary with a repertoire of states: homeostatic microglia, proliferative-region microglia of development, white matter associated microglia, disease-associated microglia around amyloid plaques, and lipid-droplet-accumulating microglia of ageing - states rather than lineages, all interconvertible in principle.

Human microglia differ regionally in density and gene expression, moderate confidence.

WHERE IT LIVES:

All regions of the CNS parenchyma without exception, tiling grey and white matter; densities vary severalfold, higher in white matter tracts and hindbrain, lower in cerebellar cortex.

It is the only immune cell resident inside the parenchyma proper, behind the blood-brain barrier; the perivascular, meningeal and choroid plexus macrophages at the borders are treated separately in Part Six.

NUMBER:

Roughly 5 to 10 percent of all brain cells: with about 170 billion total cells this implies several billion to on the order of 10 billion microglia in one human brain, moderate confidence.

The population is remarkably stable, self-renewing by slow local division balanced by death, with a median cell surviving years, and in human cortex a slow turnover of a few percent per year.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Non-excitable, and unusual among glia in resting comparatively depolarised, around minus 20 to minus 40 millivolts in the surveillant state, with prominent inward-rectifier and voltage-gated potassium currents whose balance shifts with activation - electrophysiologists can read its mood from its current signature.

Its fast signalling runs on calcium transients and on purinergic receptors, meaning receptors for adenosine triphosphate (ATP) and its breakdown products, above all the P2Y12 receptor that steers its processes.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

It neither sends nor receives synapses, but it makes intimate, repeated, activity-dependent contacts with them: individual processes touch a given synapse about once an hour for minutes at a time, lingering longer at more active synapses, and specialised somatic junctions form on neuronal cell bodies where it senses activity and can protect stressed neurons.

Its purinergic receptors read the ATP that active synapses spill, making the contact schedule itself activity-weighted.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that the microglial cell has no axon; its processes are motile feelers, not conducting wires.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

None classical; its outgoing chemical vocabulary is that of an immune cell - cytokines, chemical messengers of the immune system, including tumour necrosis factor alpha and the interleukins 1-beta, 6 and 10, plus brain-derived neurotrophic factor, nitric oxide and complement components.

Incoming, it reads ATP and adenosine, complement, fractalkine from neurons through its receptor CX3CR1, and the survival factor CSF1, on which its very existence depends.

MYELINATION:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that microglia neither make nor carry myelin - though they eat it, clearing myelin debris after injury and engulfing surplus myelin in ageing white matter.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Modest per cell in surveillance, running flexibly on glucose or glutamine, but activation triggers a metabolic switch to fast glycolysis to fund migration, engulfment and cytokine manufacture; phagocytosis, the engulfing of particles, is among the most energetically demanding acts a cell performs.

PLASTICITY:

Microglia are the plasticity of the brain's hardware inventory: complement-tagged synaptic pruning wires the visual system during development, microglial engulfment of synapses in the adult hippocampus drives forgetting of established memories, and depleting microglia impairs learning-induced spine formation, which works through its brain-derived neurotrophic factor.

The cell itself also learns: peripheral infections leave lasting epigenetic marks - chemical annotations on its deoxyribonucleic acid (DNA) packaging - that prime or tolerise its future responses, an innate immune memory inside the skull.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Neurons support it constantly, holding it calm through fractalkine and the do-not-eat-me signal CD47 while feeding it the CSF1 and interleukin 34 it needs to live; astrocytes exchange cytokines with it in both directions; the vasculature is its partner at injury sites.

Remove its trophic support with a CSF1 receptor blocker and virtually the entire population dies within days - and regrows from survivors within a week when the blocker is withdrawn, the most dramatic repopulation in the adult brain.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Every circuit in the brain is a sculpture, and this is the cell that held the chisel: the retinogeniculate system, the callosal projections, the hippocampal circuits all reach adult precision only after microglial pruning, and mice lacking the pruning machinery keep surplus, imprecise connections for life.

Its regional densities and states also colour vulnerability: the white matter microglia that age fastest sit where age-related cognitive slowing begins.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Its signature computation is a distributed eat-or-spare decision executed synapse by synapse: weak or asynchronous synapses are tagged with complement proteins C1q and C3b, active synapses shield themselves with punishment-withholding signals such as CD47, and the microglial process, reading tags against shields through its C3 and SIRP-alpha receptors, delivers a binary verdict with teeth.

The 2005 live-imaging revolution supplied the numbers: processes extend and retract at about 1.5 micrometres per minute, the fastest structural motility known in adult brain, and the population samples the entire brain parenchyma once every few hours without the somata moving at all.

It also senses and seals: within minutes of a microbleed or laser wound, converging processes wall off the site, guided up the ATP gradient the injury releases.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ ATP and ADP gradients through P2Y12, micromolar near injury; complement tags on synapses; neuronal fractalkine and CD47 restraint; CSF1 and interleukin 34 survival signals; cytokines and pathogen signals from the neuroimmune channel.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Synapse engulfment, up to a large fraction of developing synapses in pruning epochs; cytokines including tumour necrosis factor alpha and interleukin 1-beta; brain-derived neurotrophic factor; debris clearance.

• STATE.

◦ Morphological state from ramified through hyper-ramified to amoeboid; epigenetic priming or tolerance; process contact map of the local synapse population; phagocytic load.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Population share 5 to 10 percent of brain cells; resting potential minus 20 to minus 40 millivolts; process speed about 1.5 micrometres per minute; synapse contact frequency about one visit per synapse per hour.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Process response to injury minutes; a synapse contact about 5 minutes; full parenchyma surveillance a few hours; activation state shifts hours to days; population turnover years; primed states months or longer.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Alzheimer's disease, where the strongest genetic risk factors after amyloid - TREM2, CR1, CD33 - are microglial genes, and where aberrant complement-mediated pruning eats synapses.

◦ Nasu-Hakola disease and adult-onset leukoencephalopathy from TREM2 and CSF1R mutations - dementias caused primarily by broken microglia.

◦ The inflammatory hypothesis of depression, supported by microglial activation seen in brain imaging of depressed patients.

◦ Excessive complement-dependent pruning is implicated in schizophrenia through the C4 gene, the strongest common genetic association in that disease.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to decide whether a microglial cell prunes a synapse, by weighing the synapse's recent activity and its protective signal against the complement tags that mark it for eating.
Create a number variable called activity level with initial value equal to the synapse's recent firing measure between 0 and 1.
Create a number variable called complement tag with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called protection with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called pruning threshold with initial value 0.5.
Calculate the difference of 1 and the activity level.
Set complement tag to the result, because weak synapses accumulate the tags.
Set protection to the activity level, because active synapses display do-not-eat-me signals.
Create a number variable called eat drive with initial value 0.
Calculate the difference of the complement tag and the protection.
Set eat drive to the result.
If eat drive is greater than the pruning threshold is true, then perform the following actions.
    Declare that the microglial process engulfs the synapse.
Otherwise, perform the following actions.
    Declare that the synapse is spared and the process moves on.
End the conditional block.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because the verdict is one subtraction and one threshold applied across a population, and Python shows the whole pruning census in a few lines.

# Microglial pruning verdict: complement tagging of weak synapses versus
# activity-dependent protection. Complement C1q/C3 marks less active
# inputs for engulfment (Schafer et al., Neuron 2012); CD47 acts as a
# do-not-eat-me shield on active synapses (Lehrman et al., Neuron 2018).
import random
random.seed(2026)
THRESHOLD = 0.5
NOISE = 0.1                       # biological sloppiness in tagging

synapses = [random.random() for _ in range(12)]   # recent activity, 0..1
kept, eaten = [], []
for i, act in enumerate(synapses):
    tag = (1.0 - act) + random.uniform(-NOISE, NOISE)  # complement C3b
    shield = act                                       # CD47 protection
    if tag - shield > THRESHOLD:
        eaten.append(i)
    else:
        kept.append(i)
    print(f"synapse {i:2d}  activity {act:4.2f}  tag {tag:5.2f}"
          f"  -> {'PRUNED' if i in eaten else 'spared'}")
print(f"pruned {len(eaten)} of {len(synapses)}; "
      "the busy survive, the idle are eaten")

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

An immigrant immune cell, self-exiled inside the skull since the first month of gestation, whose fingers touch every synapse you own on a schedule of hours - the only cell granted a licence to eat pieces of your mind, and the reason the remaining pieces fit.


SECTION 4. THE EPENDYMAL FAMILY - THE VENTRICLE LINERS

The ventricles are the four fluid-filled chambers deep inside the brain, and cerebrospinal fluid (CSF) is the clear liquid that fills them, cushions the CNS, and carries its signals and its rubbish.

The ependymal family is the epithelium of this inner sea: one member lines the shores, the other manufactures the water.

Neither computes, but between them they run the brain's third circulation - after blood and after axonal transport - on which pressure, chemistry and waste clearance all depend.

Their failure has one classical and sometimes lethal signature, hydrocephalus, the flooding of the very chambers they serve.


CHAPTER 17. EPENDYMOCYTE (Ciliated Lining Cell of the Ventricles) (The Fluid-Stirring Lining Cell) (NON-COG)

Deep in your brain there is a coastline: the wall where nervous tissue ends and open fluid begins.
