That wall is a single sheet of cube-shaped cells, each waving a tuft of hair-like cilia into the water at some thirty strokes per second, and together they row the cerebrospinal fluid (CSF) in ordered rivers around the ventricles.

The rivers are not decoration: they carry signalling molecules to precise addresses, sweep debris toward the drains, and guide young migrating cells by the direction of the current.

Fluid-Stirring names its motor trade: the coordinated ciliary beating that streams the CSF along the ventricle walls.

Lining names its architecture: it is the epithelial lining of all four ventricles and the spinal canal.

Cell marks it as glia of epithelial habit, a descendant of the radial glia.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The ependymocyte is the multiciliated cuboidal cell forming the single-layered lining of the brain ventricles and the central canal of the spinal cord, born from radial glia and essentially never replaced in adulthood.

Each cell beats a bundle of motile cilia in a coordinated, planar-polarised rhythm, generating near-wall CSF flow that mixes the fluid, directs signal gradients, and in the walls of the lateral ventricles guides the migration of young neurons; it also forms a partial, leaky barrier between CSF and brain tissue.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

A cuboidal to low-columnar epithelial cell joined to its neighbours by adherens junctions but, unlike true barriers, mostly without tight junctions, carrying an apical tuft of roughly 16 to 60 motile cilia plus microvilli, with basal processes anchoring into the underlying brain tissue.

Each motile cilium has the classical nine-plus-two internal skeleton of moving cilia, and all cilia on one cell, and on neighbouring cells, are rotationally aligned so their strokes agree - a tissue-scale compass called planar cell polarity.

SIZE:

The cell is roughly 8 to 15 micrometres across; each motile cilium is about 8 to 12 micrometres long.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

The classic multiciliated ependymocyte (E1) dominates; a sparser biciliated E2 type and specialised forms near the third ventricle floor grade into the tanycytes of Chapter 8; in the adult spinal canal the cells are more heterogeneous and retain limited stem-like behaviour in other mammals - low confidence for humans.

WHERE IT LIVES:

The walls of both lateral ventricles, the third and fourth ventricles, the cerebral aqueduct, and the central canal of the spinal cord, which in adult humans is commonly closed and partly deciliated.

It is itself an interface: the CSF-brain boundary, one cell thick.

NUMBER:

No rigorous human stereological count exists; the ventricular surface area of very roughly 800 to 1,500 square millimetres implies a population on the order of tens of millions - low confidence, offered as an order of magnitude.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Non-excitable, resting near minus 70 to minus 80 millivolts with strong potassium conductance, and coupled to neighbours by gap junctions; its excitement is mechanical, not electrical - ciliary beat frequency in mammals runs about 25 to 40 Hz and is modulated by serotonin, adenosine triphosphate (ATP) and melanin-concentrating hormone.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

It receives no synapses; its junctional life is gap junctions and adherens junctions with neighbours, and supra-ependymal serotonergic axons from the raphe nuclei crawl along its apical surface, releasing serotonin that tunes its beat - a neuron-to-cilium modulation without a synapse.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that the ependymocyte is an epithelial glial cell with cilia rather than any conducting process.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

None released; it listens to serotonin, ATP and peptides, and its secreted contributions are mucins and the glycoprotein-rich apical coat that keeps the ventricular surface clean.

MYELINATION:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that the ependymocyte neither forms myelin nor possesses any process that could be myelinated.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Substantial for its size, because tens of motile cilia beating at tens of Hz burn ATP continuously in their dynein motors, the molecular engines of ciliary bending; the cells are correspondingly rich in mitochondria.

PLASTICITY:

Limited and mostly for the worse: adult human ependymocytes do not divide, do not regenerate after loss, and respond to injury by stretching and by scarring of the denuded wall; their beat frequency, however, is acutely tunable by neuromodulators, and the beating fields reorient over development to match the flow.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Astrocytes and the subventricular tissue anchor and feed its basal side; the choroid plexus (Chapter 18) supplies the fluid it stirs; the B1 stem cells of Chapter 38 sit within its sheet in pinwheel arrangements, making the lining a mosaic it shares.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

It gives the ventricular system its weather: ordered near-wall currents that deliver guidance molecules along specific streams, so that the geography of CSF signalling - which wall gets which concentration - is written by the beating pattern of this one cell sheet.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Its surprising capability is hydraulic engineering by consensus: thousands of cells align their power strokes through planar cell polarity, itself calibrated during development by the flow the cilia generate - the current teaches the rowers, and the rowers make the current - and the resulting flow map is precise enough to partition the ventricle into distinct signalling territories that shift with body position and time of day.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Serotonin from supra-ependymal raphe axons; ATP; melanin-concentrating hormone; mechanical shear from bulk CSF flow.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Near-wall CSF streaming at tens to hundreds of micrometres per second; directional guidance for neuroblast migration; a maintained CSF-brain interface.

• STATE.

◦ Ciliary beat frequency and phase; planar polarity vector; junctional integrity of the sheet.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Beat frequency about 25 to 40 Hz; cilia per cell roughly 16 to 60; cilium length 8 to 12 micrometres; resting potential near minus 70 to minus 80 millivolts.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ One ciliary stroke tens of milliseconds; beat frequency modulation seconds to minutes; polarity refinement days to weeks in development; no cellular renewal on any timescale in the adult.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Hydrocephalus, when ciliary failure or aqueduct blockage disturbs CSF circulation, and congenitally in the ciliopathies, inherited diseases of cilia such as primary ciliary dyskinesia.

◦ Ependymoma, the tumour of this lining, arising in ventricle walls and spinal canal, commonest in children.

◦ Ependymal denudation and scarring after fetal haemorrhage or infection, a cause of aqueduct stenosis.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

CODE SAMPLE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

The only brain cell with oars: a cube on the shore of the inner sea, rowing thirty strokes a second in formation with a million neighbours, never resting and never replaced.


CHAPTER 18. CHOROID PLEXUS EPITHELIAL CELL (Secretory Cell of the Blood-Cerebrospinal-Fluid Barrier) (The Fluid-Making Barrier Cell) (NON-COG)

Your brain floats.

Its 1,400 grams press on their supports with an effective weight of about 25 grams, because it is suspended in a bath of about 150 millilitres of home-made liquid, replaced three to four times every day - and every drop of that liquid is manufactured, filtered and quality-controlled by one cell type working in four small cauliflower-shaped factories inside the ventricles.

The same cell decides, molecule by molecule, what may pass from blood into this private sea: it is a kidney, a gland and a customs post compressed into a single sheet one cell thick.

It makes the brain's entire fluid inventory roughly three to four times over every day, and when it fails, the brain drowns or is poisoned in its own bath.

Fluid-Making names its factory trade: it secretes the cerebrospinal fluid (CSF), roughly half a litre per day.

Barrier names its customs trade: its tight junctions are the blood-CSF barrier, one of the brain's three great walls.

Cell marks it as a specialised epithelial cell, the secretory cousin of the ependymocyte.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The choroid plexus epithelial cell is a cuboidal secretory epithelial cell, a modified ependymocyte, covering the branching villi of the choroid plexuses in all four ventricles, wrapped around a core of deliberately leaky, fenestrated (windowed) capillaries.

It performs three trades at once: it secretes CSF at about 0.3 to 0.4 millilitres per minute, roughly 500 millilitres per day, by pumping sodium, chloride and bicarbonate toward the ventricle and letting water follow through aquaporin 1 water channels; its apical tight junctions form the blood-CSF barrier; and it is a gland, secreting transthyretin - a carrier protein for thyroid hormone - plus growth factors and micronutrients into the CSF, while transporter pumps eject drugs and wastes back toward the blood.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

A cuboidal cell with the machinery of both kidney and gland: a deeply infolded basolateral membrane facing the blood side, an apical brush of microvilli plus small tufts of cilia facing the CSF, an apical belt of tight junctions stitching it to its neighbours, and one of the densest mitochondrial loadings of any brain cell.

The epithelium drapes over a vascular connective tissue core in fronds, giving the plexus its cauliflower look and a large working surface.

SIZE:

The cell is about 10 to 15 micrometres across; the total apical surface of the human choroid plexuses, with microvilli counted, is on the order of hundreds of square centimetres folded into a few cubic centimetres of tissue.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

The epithelium is fairly uniform, but the plexuses of the lateral, third and fourth ventricles differ in transcriptional profile and secretory emphasis, and the cells everywhere carry position-dependent ageing signatures - moderate confidence.

WHERE IT LIVES:

The choroid plexuses of both lateral ventricles, the roof of the third ventricle, and the roof of the fourth ventricle.

It is an explicit interface twice over: its basal side faces fenestrated blood vessels - one of the few deliberately leaky vascular beds inside the skull - and its apical side faces the CSF, with its own tight junctions the only wall between the two.

NUMBER:

No rigorous count exists; from plexus mass and cell size the population is plausibly in the tens to hundreds of millions - low confidence, an order of magnitude only.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Non-excitable but electrically busy: resting potential is unusually depolarised for an epithelium, near minus 45 to minus 60 millivolts, reflecting the heavy traffic of sodium, bicarbonate and chloride transporters that constitute its secretory engine, and it carries a standing transepithelial potential of a few millivolts, CSF positive.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

It receives no synapses, but sympathetic and cholinergic autonomic fibres innervate the plexus and modulate secretion rate, and its apical surface is patrolled by the epiplexus immune cells of Chapter 49 - junctional neighbours rather than synaptic partners.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that the choroid plexus epithelial cell is a secretory epithelial cell with no conducting process of any kind.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

None in the synaptic sense; its secreted vocabulary is the CSF itself plus named products - transthyretin, of which it is the brain's chief source, insulin-like growth factor 2, folate and vitamin C delivered by dedicated transporters, and, notably, most of the melatonin-independent circadian output of any barrier tissue, since the plexus keeps its own molecular clock.

MYELINATION:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that the cell has no process to myelinate and lies outside the myelinated parenchyma altogether.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Per gram, among the highest of any tissue in the body: moving hundreds of millilitres of water uphill per day against osmotic and pressure gradients makes the plexus epithelium a metabolic furnace, its blood flow per gram several times that of the brain itself, and its sodium-potassium pumps - unusually stationed on the apical membrane, opposite to almost every other epithelium - consume the bulk of its adenosine triphosphate (ATP).

PLASTICITY:

Secretion rate is tuned by autonomic input, hormones and the cell's circadian clock, with CSF production peaking at night; over life the plexus calcifies and its output falls by tens of percent, one reason the aged brain clears waste more slowly.

The epithelium itself turns over slowly and regenerates poorly.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

The fenestrated capillaries and their pericytes and stromal fibroblasts (Chapter 47) feed it plasma to work with; the epiplexus (Kolmer) macrophages police its apical surface; autonomic nerves adjust its throughput; the arachnoid granulations downstream (Chapter 46) drain what it makes, completing the circuit.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

It sets the composition of the entire CSF, and therefore the extracellular chemistry of the whole CNS: potassium held near 2.9 millimolar regardless of blood swings, protein at about 0.3 percent of the blood level, glucose at about two thirds - numbers every clinician reads in a lumbar puncture, all of them this cell's signature.

Through the fluid it also distributes long-range signals, so the plexus is the brain's postal sorting office as well as its waterworks.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Its surprise is that a plumbing cell keeps time and keeps watch: it houses an autonomous circadian clock that schedules CSF production toward night - supporting the sleep-time glymphatic waste flush - and it acts as a sensor and gate for immune traffic, reading inflammatory signals in blood and CSF and licensing immune cell entry, which places a fluid factory on the front line of neuroimmunology.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Plasma delivered at several millilitres per gram per minute; autonomic and hormonal modulation; circadian clock phase; inflammatory signals.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ CSF at about 0.3 to 0.4 millilitres per minute, roughly 500 millilitres per day, against a standing volume of about 150 millilitres, so the pool turns over three to four times daily.

◦ Transthyretin, growth factors, folate and vitamin C into the CSF; drugs and wastes pumped back to blood.

• STATE.

◦ Secretion rate; tight junction integrity; clock phase; transporter inventory; degree of age-related calcification.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Resting potential near minus 45 to minus 60 millivolts; CSF potassium held near 2.9 millimolar; CSF-to-blood glucose ratio about 0.6.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Transport cycles milliseconds; secretion rate modulation minutes to hours; circadian cycle 24 hours; full CSF pool renewal about 6 to 8 hours; calcification decades.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Hydrocephalus of overproduction in choroid plexus papilloma and carcinoma, tumours of this cell, and the commoner obstructive hydrocephalus downstream of its unceasing output.

◦ Failure of amyloid and waste clearance with plexus ageing, implicated in Alzheimer's disease.

◦ The plexus is an entry gate for immune attack in multiple sclerosis and a reservoir route for certain infections of the CNS.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

CODE SAMPLE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

The one cell that manufactures an entire body fluid: a cuboidal factory with its pumps installed on the wrong side, brewing half a litre of crystal-clear brain-bath every day and standing guard, one cell thick, over everything dissolved in it.


PART TWO. THE GLIA OF THE PERIPHERAL NERVOUS SYSTEM

The peripheral nervous system (PNS) is everything outside the brain and spinal cord: the nerves, the ganglia (clusters of neuron cell bodies outside the central nervous system), and the nerve endings in skin, muscle, and gut.

Nearly every glial cell in this territory descends from the neural crest, the migratory embryonic cell population that also builds the sympathetic ganglia and the adrenal medulla.

Where the central glia divide their labour among four great families, the peripheral glia are dominated by a single dynasty, the Schwann cells, which insulate, bundle, repair, cap, and even sense on behalf of the nerves they serve.

The chapters of this part move outward: from the myelin of the nerve trunk, through the ganglia and the gut, to the barriers around the fascicles, and finally to the glia of the skin, where the story ends with cells that touch the world directly.

None of these cells computes a thought, and every one of them is tagged (NON-COG), yet cut any of them out and sensation, movement, and pain all fail from below.


CHAPTER 19. MYELINATING SCHWANN CELL (Myelin-Forming Schwann Cell of the Peripheral Nerve) (The One-Axon Insulation-Wrapping Cell) (NON-COG)

Pinch the skin of your forearm and the report reaches your brain in about a fiftieth of a second, and the reason it does not take half a second is a cell discovered by Theodor Schwann in 1839 that spends its whole life wrapped around a single stretch of a single nerve fibre.

Each of these cells rolls itself around its axon up to one hundred times, squeezing out its own cytoplasm until nothing is left but a spiral of stacked fatty membrane.

The oligodendrocyte of the brain insulates dozens of axon segments at once; this cell commits to exactly one, and that monogamy is the secret of why a cut finger nerve can regrow when a cut spinal cord cannot.

One Axon names its defining rule: one cell, one axon, one segment of insulation.

Insulation-Wrapping names its product, myelin, the fatty multilayer wrap that makes the nerve impulse jump from gap to gap instead of crawling.

Cell, without the word neuron, marks it as glia, a supporting cell of the nervous system.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The myelinating Schwann cell is the myelin-forming glial cell of the peripheral nervous system (PNS), the peripheral counterpart of the central myelinating oligodendrocyte of Chapter 13 of this volume.

It selects a single axon larger than roughly one micrometre in diameter, a micrometre being a thousandth of a millimetre, and wraps one internode, the insulated segment between two nodes of Ranvier, the bare gaps where the nerve impulse is regenerated.

By insulating the axon it converts slow continuous conduction at around one metre per second into saltatory (jumping) conduction at up to 80 to 120 metres per second in the largest human fibres.

It also feeds its axon, maintains the nodal architecture, and, after injury, transforms into the repair cell of Chapter 21, which is why peripheral nerves regenerate.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The mature cell is a long, flattened tube: a central nucleus and thin rim of cytoplasm on the outside, and inside, the compacted spiral of up to one hundred membrane wraps forming the myelin sheath.

Channels of surviving cytoplasm called Schmidt-Lanterman incisures spiral through the compact myelin like a staircase, keeping the living cell connected to its innermost turn.

The whole cell is enclosed in its own basal lamina, a thin collagen-rich outer coat that the oligodendrocyte lacks, and this coat becomes the regeneration tunnel after injury.

SIZE:

The nucleus-bearing cell body region is modest, roughly 10 to 20 micrometres across, but the cell as a whole is one of the longest in the body after neurons: a single internode runs from roughly 200 to 300 micrometres in a newborn to as much as 1 to 2 millimetres in the adult human, lengthening as the limbs grow.

The myelin thickness is tuned to axon diameter so that the g-ratio, the ratio of the inner axon diameter to the total outer fibre diameter, sits near 0.6, the theoretically near-optimal value for conduction speed, with moderate confidence in the exact optimum.

A large limb axon a metre long may be served by 500 to 1,000 of these cells laid end to end.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

The classical anatomy recognises no fixed subtypes, but the cell exists as a series of developmental states: neural crest cell, Schwann cell precursor, immature Schwann cell, promyelinating cell, and mature myelinating cell.

Single-cell gene reading has begun to distinguish myelinating cells of sensory versus motor fibres by molecular signature, and the repair cell of Chapter 21 is this cell in a reprogrammed state, so treat the family as one identity with several costumes.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Every myelinated peripheral nerve in the body: the thirty-one pairs of spinal nerves, most cranial nerves outside the brainstem, the dorsal and ventral roots, and the large fibres of the autonomic system.

Its territory begins precisely at the central nervous system (CNS)-PNS transition zone near the spinal cord, where the oligodendrocyte hands over the same axon, one of the sharpest cellular frontiers in the body.

NUMBER:

No direct census of human Schwann cells exists, but with tens of millions of myelinated axons in the peripheral nerves and hundreds of internodes per long axon, the total plausibly runs to the low billions, low confidence.

For scale, a single human sural nerve, a small sensory nerve of the calf, carries roughly 6,000 to 8,000 myelinated axons, each clothed along its length by hundreds of these cells.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

The cell does not fire action potentials; it rests near minus 70 to minus 80 millivolts, dominated by potassium conductances.

It senses the activity of its axon indirectly, through potassium released at the node, through adenosine triphosphate (ATP) signalling, and through calcium transients in its cytoplasm, and axonal firing during development helps set whether and how thickly it myelinates.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

It makes no synapses, but it maintains intimate, molecularly organised junctions with its axon: adhesion contacts along the internode, septate-like junctions at the paranodes flanking each node of Ranvier, and microvilli that touch the node itself and help cluster its sodium channels.

Gap junctions, direct cell-to-cell pores built here from the protein connexin 32, connect the successive turns of its own spiral through the incisures, shortening the diffusion path from the outside of the sheath to the inside.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that the Schwann cell is a glial cell with no axon of its own, being instead the sleeve around someone else's axon.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

It releases no classical neurotransmitter, but it signals with ATP and communicates with its axon through neuregulin 1 type III on the axon surface, the master signal whose dose tells the cell whether to myelinate and how thickly.

It also secretes trophic (nourishing) factors such as nerve growth factor and glial cell line-derived neurotrophic factor, especially after injury.

MYELINATION:

This cell does not receive myelin; it is myelin.

Its sheath is roughly 70 to 80 percent lipid by dry weight, rich in the PNS-specific proteins myelin protein zero (P0), which glues the wraps together, and peripheral myelin protein 22 (PMP22), whose gene dosage errors cause the most common inherited nerve disease.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Building and holding a millimetre of multilamellar membrane is expensive: the cell synthesises enormous quantities of lipid, and its own metabolism runs partly on glycolysis, allowing it to pass lactate and pyruvate, fuel molecules, inward to the axon it insulates.

The axon segment inside the sheath is metabolically dependent on this delivery, which is why Schwann cell metabolic failure produces axon loss even when the myelin looks intact.

PLASTICITY:

Internode length and myelin thickness are adjusted during growth and after injury, and the cell retains a lifelong capacity that almost no central glial cell matches: it can fully dedifferentiate, divide, and rebuild.

Remyelinated internodes are recognizably shorter and thinner than the originals, a permanent anatomical scar of past injury.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Endoneurial fibroblasts build the collagen bed around it, resident endoneurial macrophages patrol its neighbourhood, capillaries of the blood-nerve barrier feed it, and the perineurial glial cells of Chapter 26 fence the fascicle it lives in.

Its own basal lamina, which it manufactures, is its private scaffold and its axon's future guide rail.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Delete this one cell type and every fast sensation and every fast command below the neck slows fifty-fold: reflexes stretch from milliseconds to seconds, fine touch smears, and muscles receive their orders late and desynchronised.

That thought experiment is not hypothetical; it is a fair description of severe Guillain-Barré syndrome, in which the immune system strips these sheaths in days.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The cell performs a feat of measurement: reading the diameter of its axon through the dose of axonal neuregulin 1 and wrapping precisely enough membrane to hold the g-ratio near 0.6, a continuous mechanical computation sustained for decades.

It is also a construction machine, extending an inner tongue of membrane that crawls around the axon circumference up to one hundred times, guided by actin dynamics, one of the largest coordinated shape changes any human cell performs.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Axonal neuregulin 1 type III level, the myelinate-or-not command; axon diameter above roughly 1 micrometre; axonal activity read as potassium and ATP; injury signals.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ One internode of compact myelin 200 micrometres to 2 millimetres long; nodal sodium channel clustering; lactate and pyruvate delivered to the axon; trophic factors.

• STATE.

◦ Differentiation state from precursor to mature; wrap number 20 to 100; internode length; g-ratio near 0.6.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Resting potential minus 70 to minus 80 millivolts; myelin roughly 70 to 80 percent lipid by dry weight; conduction supported up to 80 to 120 metres per second; myelination threshold near 1 micrometre axon diameter.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Wrapping of an internode over days to weeks in development; internode elongation over years of body growth; dedifferentiation after injury within about 48 hours; remyelination over weeks to months.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Charcot-Marie-Tooth disease type 1A, from duplication of the PMP22 gene, the most common inherited neuropathy, affecting roughly 1 in 5,000 people.

◦ Guillain-Barré syndrome and chronic inflammatory demyelinating polyneuropathy, in which immune attack strips peripheral myelin.

◦ Diabetic neuropathy, in which metabolic injury to Schwann cells and their axons is the commonest neuropathy in the world.

◦ Schwannoma, a usually benign tumour of this cell, including the acoustic neuroma of the hearing nerve.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

CODE SAMPLE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

The only human cell that devotes its entire body to insulating one segment of one axon, wrapped up to one hundred turns inside its own basal lamina tube - and the cell whose willingness to reprogram is the single reason peripheral nerves heal.


CHAPTER 20. NON-MYELINATING (REMAK) SCHWANN CELL (Remak-Bundle Schwann Cell) (The Bare-Fibre Bundle-Sleeving Cell) (NON-COG)

Most of the nerve fibres in your body are too thin to deserve myelin, and among them are the very fibres that carry slow burning pain, warmth, itch, and the emotional brush of a caress.

These bare fibres do not travel naked.

Each is tucked into its own private pocket inside a shared living sleeve, a cell first described by Robert Remak in the 1830s, before anyone knew what either glia or axons really were.

One such cell may hold one axon or more than twenty, each walled off from its neighbours by a film of glial cytoplasm thinner than a soap bubble.

Bare-Fibre names its clients, the unmyelinated C fibres, the thin slow axons of pain, temperature, itch, and the autonomic system.

Bundle-Sleeving names its method: gathering many axons into one bundle, the Remak bundle, and sleeving each in a separate pocket.

Cell marks it as glia, a supporting cell rather than a neuron.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The Remak Schwann cell is the non-myelinating Schwann cell of the peripheral nervous system (PNS), the caretaker of axons below roughly one micrometre in diameter that never receive myelin.

It engulfs a group of such axons, separates them with tongues of cytoplasm so that they do not touch, and provides each with ionic housekeeping, metabolic support, and mechanical protection.

Its cargo includes the C fibre nociceptors (pain-sensing fibres), the warmth fibres, the itch fibres, the C tactile fibres of gentle affective touch, and the postganglionic axons of the sympathetic nervous system.

By fibre count this cell serves the majority: roughly three quarters of the axons in a human cutaneous nerve are unmyelinated and live in Remak bundles.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

An elongated cell with a central nucleus, wrapped not as a spiral but as a hand of fingers: its cytoplasm reaches between the axons so that each sits in its own trough or complete pocket, the whole ensemble enclosed in one shared basal lamina.

In cross-section under the electron microscope the bundle looks like a bag of ovals, each oval an axon, each rimmed by a crescent of glial cytoplasm.

SIZE:

The cell body is roughly 10 to 20 micrometres across, and each cell covers a longitudinal territory of roughly 200 to 500 micrometres of bundle before the next cell takes over, moderate confidence.

The axons in its pockets are 0.2 to 1.5 micrometres in diameter, and the number per bundle varies with location, from more than twenty near the dorsal root ganglion to an average of about three in distal nerve twigs, values established in rodent nerve and consistent with human biopsy material.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

No formal subtypes are named, but bundles differ systematically by cargo: sensory Remak cells of skin nerves, autonomic Remak cells sleeving sympathetic axons, and the specialised olfactory ensheathing cell of Chapter 25, which is essentially a Remak-style cell raised to an art form.

The nociceptive Schwann cell of the skin, Chapter 28, is this cell's terminal cousin at the nerve ending.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Every peripheral nerve, cutaneous nerves above all, plus the grey rami and visceral nerves of the autonomic system, always inside the fascicles behind the perineurial fence of Chapter 26.

NUMBER:

Uncounted in the human, but since unmyelinated axons outnumber myelinated ones roughly three or four to one and each Remak cell tends several axons over a short territory, the population is plausibly of the same order as the myelinating Schwann cells, in the billions, low confidence.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

No action potentials; the membrane rests near minus 70 to minus 80 millivolts.

The cell listens to its axons chemically: it expresses purinergic receptors that detect the adenosine triphosphate (ATP) released by firing C fibres and answers with calcium transients in its cytoplasm.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

No synapses, but continuous adhesive junctional contact with every axon in its pockets, and a documented two-way chemical conversation with them through ATP and trophic factors.

Loss of this contact is not neutral: when the pockets fail and bare axons touch each other directly, spurious cross-excitation between pain fibres becomes possible.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that the cell possesses no axon, being the multichambered sleeve around many small axons belonging to other cells.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

None released as transmission, but the cell signals with ATP and secretes trophic support, including glial cell line-derived neurotrophic factor, on which many nonpeptidergic C fibres depend.

MYELINATION:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that its defining property is the deliberate absence of myelin: its axons are too thin for myelin to pay for itself, and the cell protects them without wrapping.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Modest beside its myelinating sibling, but it still shoulders the housekeeping of many axons at once, buffering their potassium, supplying metabolic substrates, and maintaining the shared basal lamina.

PLASTICITY:

Fully retained: after nerve injury the Remak cell reprograms into the repair cell of Chapter 21 exactly as the myelinating cell does, and in disease it can radially sort and even myelinate axons it should not, a hallmark of certain neuropathies and tumours.

The adult cell also remodels its pockets continuously as axon numbers change with age and injury.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

The same endoneurial neighbourhood as Chapter 19: endoneurial fibroblasts, resident macrophages, blood-nerve barrier capillaries, and the perineurial sheath around the fascicle.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Without this cell the slow half of feeling collapses: burning pain, warmth, itch, and gentle affective touch all travel in its pockets, and the sympathetic outflow that sets blood vessel tone runs through them too.

Damage to Remak cells does not silence these systems cleanly; it makes them scream, because unprotected C fibres become hyperexcitable, and Remak dysfunction is now an established contributor to neuropathic pain.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The cell solves a running partition problem: it continuously counts and separates its axons, rebuilding a wall between every pair so that no two ever touch, for decades, along every centimetre of nerve.

Experiments deleting single signalling genes in this cell alone produce animals with neuropathic pain, proof that the quality of the partition is itself part of the pain system.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ ATP from firing C fibres; axonal adhesion signals; injury cues.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ One separated pocket per axon; potassium buffering; glial cell line-derived neurotrophic factor and allied trophins.

• STATE.

◦ Number of axons pocketed, roughly 1 to more than 20; pocket integrity; differentiation state.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Resting potential minus 70 to minus 80 millivolts; client axon diameter 0.2 to 1.5 micrometres; territory roughly 200 to 500 micrometres of bundle.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Calcium answers to axonal ATP over seconds; pocket remodelling over days; reprogramming to repair state within about 48 hours of injury.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Small fibre neuropathy, including the painful distal neuropathy of diabetes, in which C fibres and their Remak sleeves degenerate, producing burning feet.

◦ Neuropathic pain from disturbed Remak-axon interactions, demonstrated when Schwann cell genes such as LRP1 are deleted.

◦ Neurofibroma, the tumour of neurofibromatosis type 1, arises from cells of the Remak lineage.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

CODE SAMPLE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

A single glial cell holding up to twenty pain fibres in twenty separate cytoplasmic pockets inside one basal lamina sleeve - the landlord of the body's slowest and most emotionally loaded wiring.


CHAPTER 21. REPAIR SCHWANN CELL (Injury-Induced Repair Schwann Cell, the Cell of the Bands of Büngner) (The Regrowth-Rail-Laying Cell) (NON-COG)

Cut a peripheral nerve and something happens that never happens in the spinal cord: within days, the stump downstream of the cut fills with living rails, and over the following weeks and months the severed axons crawl back along them, sometimes all the way to the fingertip.

The rails are cells, and they did not exist before the injury.

Every Schwann cell below the cut, myelinating and Remak alike, tears up its old identity, digests its own myelin, stretches itself into a cord, and joins hands with its neighbours to form the bands of Büngner, named for Otto von Büngner, who described them in 1891.

This chapter is about a state, not a resting type: the emergency profession that every Schwann cell keeps in reserve.

Regrowth names its purpose, the regeneration of severed axons.

Rail-Laying names its method: building continuous cellular tracks that guide each regrowing axon back toward its target.

Cell marks it as glia in a transdifferentiated state.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The repair Schwann cell is the injury-induced state into which both the myelinating Schwann cell of Chapter 19 and the Remak cell of Chapter 20 reprogram after axon damage, under the command of the transcription factor c-Jun, a master gene switch.

It performs four jobs in sequence: it demolishes and helps digest the now-useless myelin; it summons macrophages, the professional engulfing cells of the immune system, to finish the clean-up, called Wallerian degeneration; it secretes a pharmacy of trophic (nourishing) factors that keep the injured neurons alive; and it elongates and aligns into the bands of Büngner, the regeneration tracks.

When a regrowing axon arrives, the repair cell escorts it, and once contact is re-established it converts back into a myelinating or Remak cell and resumes ordinary life.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The transformation is dramatic: the compact tube of Chapter 19 becomes a long, thin, branched and flattened cell, roughly two to three times the length of its former self, overlapping its neighbours end to end inside the old basal lamina tubes to form continuous columns.

Inside, the cell fills with autophagic vacuoles, self-digestion chambers, as it breaks down its own myelin by a process named myelinophagy.

SIZE:

Individual repair cells elongate to roughly 700 to 1,000 micrometres, a micrometre being a thousandth of a millimetre, from the 300 to 400 micrometre resting length of a rodent Schwann cell, and the bands they form run continuously for centimetres, the full length of the degenerated stump.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

None as such, but the state has a time course: an early inflammatory demolition phase, a sustained trophic band phase, and a slow fading, because repair cells that wait too long for an axon, months to years, atrophy and lose their supportive character, which is a principal reason human nerve repair fails over long distances.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Anywhere in the peripheral nervous system (PNS) downstream of an axon injury: the distal stump of a cut or crushed nerve, and around the injury site itself.

It has no address in the healthy body; it is conjured by damage.

NUMBER:

Zero at baseline, and locally enormous after injury, because the resident Schwann cells both convert and proliferate, roughly tripling their local numbers in the first weeks, moderate confidence from rodent counts.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Non-spiking, resting near minus 70 millivolts, but rich in injury signalling: calcium waves sweep through Schwann cell networks within minutes of axotomy, and purinergic and cytokine signalling coordinates the population.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

No synapses, but the state is defined by junctional promiscuity: repair cells adhere to each other end to end to build the bands, present adhesion molecules such as N-cadherin and neural cell adhesion molecule to the regrowing growth cone, and physically hand axons from cell to cell.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that the repair cell has no axon of its own; it is the road, and the axon is the traveller.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

None, but its secretome is the point: nerve growth factor, brain-derived neurotrophic factor, glial cell line-derived neurotrophic factor, neurotrophin 3, and the cytokines that recruit macrophages, a molecular field hospital.

MYELINATION:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that the repair state is defined by the active demolition of myelin; the cell re-acquires the ability to myelinate only by exiting the repair state once an axon returns.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

High and anabolic in reverse: digesting bulk myelin lipid, proliferating, elongating, and running a secretory pharmacy all at once, fuelled by the endoneurial circulation and by the lipid it salvages from its own demolished sheath.

PLASTICITY:

This cell is plasticity incarnate: a fully differentiated adult cell that reprograms in about 48 hours, holds the new identity for months, and then reverses, one of the very few natural transdifferentiation events in adult human biology.

The decay of that plasticity with time and age, chiefly through falling c-Jun activity, is the quiet reason nerve repairs in older adults recover poorly.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Recruited macrophages do the heavy phagocytic lifting and strip the remaining debris; endoneurial fibroblasts and the preserved basal lamina tubes provide the scaffold; new blood vessels grow into the bridge of a cut nerve first, and repair cells famously use those vessels as a bridge to cross the gap.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

This cell is the entire difference between the two halves of the nervous system's response to injury: the PNS regenerates because every Schwann cell carries this emergency profession, and the central nervous system (CNS) does not regenerate in part because oligodendrocytes carry nothing like it and instead leave inhibitory debris in place.

Every recovered hand after nerve surgery, every face that moves again after Bell's palsy, is this cell's work.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

A differentiated adult cell that detects the death of its client, erases its own identity, eats its own most expensive possession, rebuilds itself as infrastructure, and then, when the client returns, resumes its old job as if nothing had happened.

Repair cells also perform collective navigation: at a transected gap they migrate as multicellular cords along freshly grown blood vessels, reading the vasculature as a map.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Loss of axon contact and axonal neuregulin; injury calcium and adenosine triphosphate (ATP); macrophage and fibroblast cytokines.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Bands of Büngner; myelinophagy and debris clearance; trophic factor secretion; guidance of growth cones at roughly 1 to 3 millimetres of regrowth per day.

• STATE.

◦ c-Jun activity level; elongation state; time since injury, the clock that erodes repair competence.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Reprogramming onset within about 48 hours; cell length 700 to 1,000 micrometres; regeneration speed roughly 1 millimetre per day in human clinical rules of thumb.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Demolition over 1 to 2 weeks; band maintenance over months; loss of repair capacity over roughly 6 to 12 months of denervation.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Chronic denervation failure: after long delays or long distances, repair cells atrophy and human nerve regeneration stalls, the central unsolved problem of nerve surgery.

◦ Neuropathic pain and neuroma formation when regrowing axons and repair cells organise into a disordered tangle at the injury site.

◦ Incomplete redifferentiation contributes to the thin, short remyelinated internodes and slowed conduction of healed nerves.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

CODE SAMPLE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

The only cell in this book that exists only after disaster: an adult glial cell that reprograms itself in two days, eats its own myelin, and lies down end to end with its neighbours to become the road home for a severed nerve.


CHAPTER 22. TERMINAL (PERISYNAPTIC) SCHWANN CELL, ALSO CALLED TELOGLIA (Perisynaptic Schwann Cell of the Neuromuscular Junction) (The Junction-Capping Caretaker Cell) (NON-COG)

Every movement you make funnels through hundreds of millions of one-to-one bottlenecks: the neuromuscular junctions, the synapses where a motor nerve hands its command to a muscle fibre.

Sitting on top of each junction, like a hand cupped over a candle flame, are three to five glial cells that never touch the conversation yet hear every word of it.

They cap the synapse, seal it, eavesdrop on its transmitter, and when the junction is damaged they climb down and rebuild it.

Anatomists of the nineteenth century called them teloglia, the end-glia; modern physiology calls them perisynaptic Schwann cells.

Junction-Capping names their station, the glial cap over the neuromuscular junction.

Caretaker names their role: stabilising, monitoring, and repairing the synapse without transmitting anything themselves.

Cell marks them as glia of the Schwann family.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The terminal Schwann cell is the non-myelinating glial cap of the neuromuscular junction (NMJ), the synapse between a motor axon terminal and a skeletal muscle fibre.

It insulates the synaptic gutter from the outside world, senses ongoing transmission through its own receptors for acetylcholine and adenosine triphosphate (ATP), modulates transmitter release, and maintains the structural registration of nerve terminal on muscle endplate for decades.

After nerve injury it becomes the junction's rescue service, sprouting processes that guide regenerating or sprouting axons back onto vacated endplates.

It is the peripheral archetype of the tripartite synapse described for astrocytes in Part One: presynaptic terminal, postsynaptic muscle, and enveloping glia.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

A flattened cell with a plump nucleus perched above the nerve terminal, extending thin sheet-like processes that cover the terminal branches without entering the synaptic cleft, the 50-nanometre gap where transmission happens.

Its processes interdigitate precisely with the terminal's branches, so that a stained junction looks like a leaf, the axon its veins and the teloglia its lamina.

SIZE:

Cell bodies are roughly 10 to 15 micrometres across, a micrometre being a thousandth of a millimetre, covering an endplate territory of roughly 20 to 50 micrometres, and a typical adult human junction carries three to five of them, the number scaling with endplate size, moderate confidence.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

No named subtypes; the meaningful distinction is state, resting cap versus activated sprouting cell, plus a related population capping the sensory muscle spindle terminals.

WHERE IT LIVES:

At every neuromuscular junction of every skeletal muscle in the body, roughly at the muscle's midbelly band where endplates cluster, immediately outside the basal lamina that runs through the synaptic cleft.

NUMBER:

Humans possess on the order of 100 million to several hundred million skeletal muscle fibres, each normally with one endplate carrying three to five terminal Schwann cells, giving a population plausibly near one billion, low confidence.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Non-spiking, resting near minus 70 millivolts, but a genuine physiological listener: high-frequency motor activity evokes calcium transients in these cells through muscarinic acetylcholine receptors and purinergic receptors, and those calcium signals in turn adjust transmitter release, a glial feedback loop demonstrated at living junctions.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

It is a permanent third party at one synapse: enveloping the presynaptic terminal, reading spillover acetylcholine and ATP, and signalling back to nerve and muscle, while never crossing into the cleft itself in health.

In denervation it violates that rule, entering the vacated cleft and even briefly taking up transmitter-like signalling roles.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that the cell has no axon; its longest structures are the guidance sprouts it grows after injury, which are glial processes, not wires.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

It releases no transmitter in health, but signals with ATP, and its receptor panel, muscarinic acetylcholine receptors and purinergic P2Y receptors, makes it a chemical eavesdropper on the synapse it caps.

MYELINATION:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that it is a terminal non-myelinating cell; myelin ends one internode back, and the final stretch of motor axon it caps is bare.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Modest at rest, but its calcium signalling scales with motor activity, and during reinnervation its sprouting programme is a burst of growth comparable to a developing cell.

PLASTICITY:

Behaviourally the most plastic element of the junction: within days of denervation it de-differentiates, sprouts processes up to hundreds of micrometres long, bridges to neighbouring innervated junctions, and physically leads escaped axon branches to empty endplates, the mechanism by which surviving motor neurons adopt orphaned muscle fibres.

That adoption process is why early motor neuron disease can silently lose half its motor neurons before weakness shows.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

The muscle fibre beneath it supplies adhesion and trophic signals; the myelinating Schwann cells of the parent axon deliver the wire; kranocytes, a recently described fibroblast-like cell, roof the whole junction above the teloglia; and the muscle capillary bed feeds it.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Junctions stripped of terminal Schwann cells in experiments do not merely wobble; they disassemble, terminals retract and transmission fails, because the cap is load-bearing.

Every act of strength you will perform at age eighty depends on how well these cells re-route junctions through a lifetime of small nerve injuries.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The cell decodes the intensity of synaptic traffic into graded calcium signals and uses them to tune transmitter release, a bona fide glial modulation of the body's most consequential synapse.

Its second surprise is navigation: after injury it grows a physical bridge across hundreds of micrometres of muscle and leads an axon along it, glia guiding neuron rather than the reverse.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Spillover acetylcholine and ATP from transmission; muscle-derived adhesion and trophic factors; denervation signals.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Junction stabilisation; modulation of transmitter release; guidance sprouts up to several hundred micrometres after injury.

• STATE.

◦ Intracellular calcium; resting cap versus activated sprouting phenotype; number of cells per endplate, three to five.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Resting potential near minus 70 millivolts; endplate territory 20 to 50 micrometres; activation threshold tuned to high-frequency motor bursts.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Calcium responses over seconds; sprouting onset over 1 to 3 days of denervation; junction re-occupation over weeks.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ In amyotrophic lateral sclerosis, terminal Schwann cell dysfunction and failed reinnervation accompany the die-back of motor terminals.

◦ Age-related sarcopenia involves progressive endplate fragmentation with declining teloglial repair.

◦ In autoimmune neuromyotonia and after botulinum toxin, its sprouting programme is pathologically or therapeutically engaged.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

CODE SAMPLE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

Three to five glial cells cupped over every neuromuscular junction in your body, listening to acetylcholine they never release, and ready, the moment the wire breaks, to walk a new one in by hand.


CHAPTER 23. SATELLITE GLIAL CELL (Satellite Glial Cell of the Peripheral Ganglia) (The Cell-Body-Cradling Envelope Cell) (NON-COG)

Just outside your spinal cord, in the dorsal root ganglia, sit the cell bodies of every sensory neuron serving your body below the head - and not one of them is allowed to touch its neighbour.

Each is sealed inside its own custom-fitted envelope of flattened glial cells, wrapped as closely as shrink film, with a gap of only twenty nanometres between neuron and glia.

The arrangement looks like planets each attended by satellites, which is exactly how the cells got their name.

For a century they were dismissed as packing material; they are now front-page cells in the biology of chronic pain.

Cell-Body-Cradling names their position: wrapped around the soma, the cell body, of peripheral neurons, never around axons.

Envelope names their form, a complete multi-cell sheath sealing each neuron into a private unit.

Cell marks them as glia.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The satellite glial cell (SGC) is the enveloping glial cell of the peripheral ganglia: the sensory ganglia of the dorsal roots and cranial nerves, and the sympathetic and parasympathetic autonomic ganglia.

A team of them, typically four to ten per neuron and more around the largest neurons, forms a complete envelope around each neuronal soma, creating a functional unit that is the ganglion's equivalent of a private room.

Inside that room the SGCs do astrocyte work: buffering potassium through inwardly rectifying channels, taking up glutamate, recycling transmitter precursors, and delivering metabolic support.

Because the neuron-glia gap is only about 20 nanometres, a nanometre being a millionth of a millimetre, everything the neuron secretes is read instantly by its envelope, and everything the envelope releases lands at full strength on the neuron.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

Each SGC is a thin, flattened, sheet-like cell, curved to the neuron's surface, with lamellar extensions that interlock with its team-mates to complete the envelope; the whole unit is wrapped in a shared basal lamina continuous with the connective tissue of the ganglion.

Unlike astrocytes, resting SGCs are not star-shaped; they are laminae, sheets moulded to a sphere.

SIZE:

An individual cell body is roughly 8 to 10 micrometres across, a micrometre being a thousandth of a millimetre, its sheet-like extensions spreading over tens of micrometres of neuronal surface, around somata that range from 20 to over 100 micrometres in diameter.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Sensory-ganglion SGCs and sympathetic-ganglion SGCs differ molecularly and in their pathology, and single-cell studies resolve further heterogeneity, including subpopulations with progenitor-like signatures, but the working classification remains one type in two habitats.

WHERE IT LIVES:

All 31 pairs of dorsal root ganglia, the trigeminal and other sensory cranial ganglia, the sympathetic chain ganglia, and the parasympathetic and enteric-adjacent ganglia - everywhere the peripheral nervous system (PNS) stores neuron cell bodies.

NUMBER:

A human dorsal root ganglion contains tens of thousands of neurons, each with several SGCs, and across all ganglia the SGC population comfortably exceeds the peripheral neuron population several-fold, plausibly in the low hundreds of millions to a billion, low confidence.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Non-spiking, with a resting potential near minus 80 millivolts dominated by the inward rectifier potassium channel Kir4.1, the same channel that defines the astrocyte; loss of Kir4.1 in SGCs is by itself sufficient to produce pain behaviour in animals.

They respond to adenosine triphosphate (ATP) and other ligands with calcium transients and are coupled to each other by gap junctions.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

No synapses, but an extreme junctional intimacy: a 20-nanometre cleft with the enclosed neuron across which ATP, potassium, glutamate, and cytokines shuttle in both directions, plus connexin 43 gap junctions, direct cell-to-cell pores, linking SGCs within an envelope and, after injury, between envelopes.

That injury-induced increase in gap junction coupling is a signature finding: following nerve damage or inflammation, coupling between SGCs and across neighbouring units rises several-fold, spreads excitability, and blocking the junctions measurably reduces neuronal hyperexcitability and pain in animal models.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that the satellite glial cell has no axon and confines itself entirely to the neuronal cell body it envelops.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

It releases ATP and cytokines, including tumour necrosis factor alpha and interleukin 1 beta when activated, and it clears glutamate through its transporters - a signalling repertoire of the immune-glial rather than the synaptic kind.

MYELINATION:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that it wraps cell bodies, not axons, and forms no myelin at any stage.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Moderate: it runs the potassium and transmitter housekeeping for one of the most electrically active cell bodies in the body, and it supplies glutamine, a transmitter precursor, to its neuron through the glutamate-glutamine cycle.

PLASTICITY:

Its plasticity is its pathology: within days of peripheral nerve injury SGCs upregulate glial fibrillary acidic protein, proliferate modestly, boost their gap junction coupling, and shift to a cytokine-releasing activated state that can persist for months, an engram of injury written in glia.

Some SGCs also retain progenitor potential, and in culture can give rise to Schwann-type cells.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

The fenestrated, relatively leaky capillaries of the ganglion feed it - the dorsal root ganglion lies outside the strict blood-nerve barrier - and resident macrophages, which multiply after injury, work alongside it, while the connective tissue capsule of the ganglion holds the whole array.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

The ganglion without its satellites would be a hall of naked amplifiers: unbuffered potassium and transmitter would let every strongly firing soma bias its neighbours, and the crisp labelled lines of sensation would blur.

The activated version of this cell recolours the region in the other direction, turning the ganglion into an amplifier of pain, which is why it is now a named drug target for chronic pain.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Its most surprising capability is the injury-triggered construction of a new communication network: by opening gap junctions between envelopes that were previously insulated units, the ganglion rewires its own glial architecture within days, converting isolated neuron-glia units into coupled assemblies - a structural change that outlasts the injury and helps explain why pain can become chronic.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Neuronal ATP, potassium, glutamate, and calcitonin gene-related peptide across a 20-nanometre cleft; injury and inflammatory cytokines.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Potassium and glutamate clearance; glutamine supply; ATP and cytokine release onto the enclosed neuron.

• STATE.

◦ Activation state indexed by glial fibrillary acidic protein; gap junction coupling fraction, rising several-fold after injury; intracellular calcium.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Resting potential near minus 80 millivolts; 4 to 10 cells per envelope; neuron-glia cleft roughly 20 nanometres.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Calcium responses over seconds; activation onset over 1 to 4 days after injury; activated state persisting weeks to months.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Chronic neuropathic and inflammatory pain, including postherpetic neuralgia after shingles, in which the varicella zoster virus itself hides in the ganglion these cells guard.

◦ Trigeminal SGC activation is implicated in migraine and trigeminal neuralgia mechanisms.

◦ Autoimmune and paraneoplastic ganglionopathies destroy the units these cells maintain.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

CODE SAMPLE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

The only glial cell that shrink-wraps neuron cell bodies one by one, sealing each sensory neuron into a private 20-nanometre-walled room - and the cell whose injury-opened gap junctions turn a quiet ganglion into a pain amplifier.


CHAPTER 24. ENTERIC GLIAL CELL (Glial Cell of the Enteric Nervous System) (The Gut-Circuit Housekeeping Cell) (NON-COG)

Your intestines contain a nervous system that can run without the brain: cut the vagus nerve and the gut goes on mixing, propelling, and secreting on its own, which is why the enteric nervous system is called the second brain.

That second brain has its own glia, and they outnumber its neurons.

They look startlingly like astrocytes, express astrocyte proteins, and do astrocyte work, yet they live in the most hostile glial habitat in the body: a wall that stretches and shears with every meal, millimetres from a trillion-member microbial ecosystem and a standing army of immune cells.

They tend the circuits of digestion, hold the gut lining together, and carry messages between neurons, immune cells, and microbes.

Gut-Circuit names their territory, the neural plexuses of the digestive tract from oesophagus to rectum.

Housekeeping names their portfolio: ionic buffering, transmitter handling, barrier maintenance, and immune diplomacy.

Cell marks them as glia.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The enteric glial cell (EGC) is the glial cell of the enteric nervous system (ENS), the semi-autonomous network of roughly 200 to 600 million neurons embedded in the wall of the human gastrointestinal tract.

EGCs surround the neurons and their processes in the myenteric plexus, which drives motility, and the submucosal plexus, which governs secretion and blood flow, and they extend beyond the plexuses into the mucosa, the innermost lining, where neurons scarcely go.

They buffer potassium, handle glutamate and other transmitters, supply metabolic substrates, modulate motility circuits through calcium signalling, regulate the intestinal epithelial barrier, and exchange signals with immune cells and with the microbiome - the glial half of the gut-brain axis.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

A small stellate cell with several branched processes, strongly reminiscent of a protoplasmic astrocyte, expressing the astrocyte markers glial fibrillary acidic protein and S100 beta, but developmentally a neural crest cell, kin to the Schwann family.

Within ganglia its processes wrap neuron cell bodies and synapses; along fibre tracts it ensheathes bundles of unmyelinated axons in Remak-like fashion.

SIZE:

The cell body is roughly 5 to 10 micrometres across, a micrometre being a thousandth of a millimetre, with processes extending some tens of micrometres, on the small side even for glia.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Four morphological types are conventionally distinguished: type I protoplasmic glia inside the ganglia, type II fibrous glia along interganglionic fibre tracts, type III mucosal glia beneath the epithelium, and type IV intramuscular glia running with axons in the muscle layers.

Molecular profiling shows these forms are plastic stations rather than fixed lineages, with cells shifting type as they move.

WHERE IT LIVES:

The entire length of the digestive tube, from the oesophagus to the internal anal sphincter, in all layers from the muscle plexuses to just beneath the epithelial lining - and thus within micrometres of the gut lumen and its microbiota.

NUMBER:

Human counts remain rough: estimates for the ENS run to roughly 200 to 600 million neurons, and enteric glia are generally reported to outnumber enteric neurons in humans by up to several-fold, giving a plausible population of several hundred million to a few billion, low confidence.

By either count this is a glial population of the same order as an entire small mammal's brain.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Non-spiking, resting near minus 60 to minus 80 millivolts with a strongly potassium-selective membrane; the working signal is calcium, with transients evoked by adenosine triphosphate (ATP) and other purines, by transmitters spilled from enteric synapses, and by mechanical stretch.

Calcium responses propagate between coupled glia through connexin 43 gap junction channels, giving slow glial waves that travel with the motility patterns they help regulate.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

No synapses of its own, but it enwraps enteric synapses tripartite-style, reads their purinergic and peptidergic traffic, and releases modulators back; experimental activation of enteric glia alone is sufficient to alter gut motility.

Its junctional partners extend beyond neurons: it contacts epithelial cells, blood vessels, immune cells, and the terminals of extrinsic vagal and spinal afferents.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that the enteric glial cell has no axon; its processes are short glial branches, not conducting wires.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

It releases gliotransmitters, glial signalling molecules, including ATP and its breakdown products, nitric oxide precursors, prostaglandins, and trophic factors such as glial cell line-derived neurotrophic factor and S-nitrosoglutathione, the last a named guardian of the epithelial barrier.

MYELINATION:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that the enteric nervous system is entirely unmyelinated and this cell never forms myelin.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Moderate but unrelenting: the gut's circuits never rest, its epithelium is renewed every four to five days, and the glia supplying both run continuous transmitter and potassium housekeeping in a mechanically working organ.

PLASTICITY:

Considerable: enteric glia shift morphological type, mount a reactive gliosis after inflammation or infection, and, remarkably, retain neurogenic potential, serving as a reserve source of new enteric neurons after injury in animal models, a capacity the brain's mature glia lack.

Their state is also tuned by the microbiome: mucosal glia fail to establish normally in germ-free animals.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

The intestinal epithelium and its microbial signals shape it, the gut's exceptional vasculature feeds it, and the muscularis macrophages, the resident immune cells of the gut wall, are its constant partners in a three-way conversation with the neurons.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Ablate enteric glia in animal experiments and the result is not subtle: motility collapses and, in some models, the intestinal lining breaks down into fulminant inflammation within days, an organ-level demonstration that this housekeeping is load-bearing.

Every mood-altering rumour that travels the gut-brain axis, from microbe to vagus, passes through territory these cells police.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Its most surprising ability is diplomatic: it is a genuine three-way interpreter, reading microbial and epithelial signals on one side, neuronal purinergic traffic on the second, and immune cytokines on the third, and translating among them, so that a change in gut bacteria can end as a change in motility without any neuron sensing the microbe directly.

It can also, under duress, stop being glia altogether and become new enteric neurons, one of the few adult glia with a demonstrated neurogenic reserve role.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ ATP and transmitters from enteric synapses; mechanical stretch of the gut wall; microbial products such as lipopolysaccharide and short-chain fatty acids; immune cytokines.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Potassium and transmitter clearance; gliotransmitter release modulating motility and secretion; barrier-protective factors to the epithelium; trophic support.

• STATE.

◦ Intracellular calcium; morphological type I to IV; resting versus reactive phenotype.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Resting potential minus 60 to minus 80 millivolts; glia-to-neuron ratio up to several-fold in humans; four stationed subtypes.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Calcium transients over seconds; glial waves over tens of seconds with motility cycles; reactive changes over days; neurogenic conversion over weeks.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Enteric glial disturbance is implicated in irritable bowel syndrome and in the inflammation of Crohn's disease and ulcerative colitis.

◦ Chagas disease and paraneoplastic autoimmunity destroy enteric ganglia, glia included, producing megacolon and pseudo-obstruction.

◦ Enteric glia display alpha-synuclein pathology early in Parkinson's disease, part of the evidence that the disease may announce itself in the gut years before the brain.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

CODE SAMPLE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

An astrocyte in everything but birth certificate, living millimetres from a trillion bacteria, outnumbering the neurons of the second brain, and able, in an emergency, to become those neurons itself.


CHAPTER 25. OLFACTORY ENSHEATHING CELL (Olfactory Ensheathing Glial Cell of the First Cranial Nerve) (The Axon-Escorting Bridge Cell) (NON-COG)

The nerve of smell is the only nerve in your body that rebuilds itself for life: its sensory neurons, exposed directly to the air and its poisons, die and are replaced every few weeks, and every replacement must grow a brand-new axon from your nose into your brain.

That axon crosses the one border no other adult axon is permitted to cross, the frontier between peripheral and central nervous tissue, and it does so inside a living escort.

The escort is a glial cell that belongs fully to neither side: born of the olfactory tissue, expressing peripheral and central glial markers at once, and holding open a permanent immigration corridor through the skull.

Because it does professionally what spinal cord repair therapies dream of, it has become one of the most transplanted cells in regenerative medicine.

Axon-Escorting names its daily work: wrapping and guiding the naked axons of olfactory sensory neurons, old and new alike.

Bridge names its unique privilege, spanning the peripheral nervous system (PNS)-CNS frontier that stops every other axon.

Cell marks it as glia.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The olfactory ensheathing cell (OEC) is the specialised glial cell of the first cranial nerve, ensheathing the unmyelinated axons of the olfactory sensory neurons from the olfactory epithelium in the nose, through the perforated cribriform plate of the skull, into the nerve fibre layer of the olfactory bulb.

Like a Remak cell it gathers many thin axons into shared sleeves without myelinating them; unlike any Schwann cell it accompanies them across the transition into central nervous system (CNS) territory and tiles the outer layer of the bulb.

Its standing task is escort duty for the only lifelong-regenerating nerve in the human body: as olfactory neurons turn over, the OEC channels each new axon along the old route to its correct target zone.

Because of that talent it is a leading candidate cell for transplant repair of the injured spinal cord, with human trials ongoing.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

A spindle-shaped or flattened cell with long sheet-like processes that enclose large bundles, sometimes hundreds, of axons of roughly 0.2 micrometres diameter each, in open troughs rather than sealed individual pockets.

It expresses a hybrid marker profile, including the low-affinity nerve growth factor receptor p75, S100 beta, and glial fibrillary acidic protein, part Schwann cell and part astrocyte, and recent single-cell work resolves around five molecular subpopulations with progenitor-like, regenerative, secretory, and microglia-like signatures.

SIZE:

The cell body measures roughly 10 to 20 micrometres, a micrometre being a thousandth of a millimetre, with processes running tens to a few hundred micrometres along the nerve and enclosing fascicles up to tens of micrometres across.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Two anatomical populations are standard: the peripheral OECs of the lamina propria and olfactory nerve, and the central OECs of the bulb's nerve fibre layer, with outer and inner sublayers that sort and then target the arriving axons.

Molecular profiling adds the five subtypes noted above; treat the cell as one lineage with stationed specialisations.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Along the full course of the olfactory nerve: the mucosal lining of the upper nasal cavity, the short fascicles threading the cribriform plate, and the outermost layer of the olfactory bulb - plus a companion population along the vomeronasal nerve.

It is the only glial cell resident on both sides of the PNS-CNS frontier.

NUMBER:

No reliable human census exists; given several million olfactory sensory neurons per side and heavy ensheathment of their axons, the population is plausibly in the tens of millions, low confidence.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Non-spiking, resting near minus 60 to minus 70 millivolts, with potassium-dominated membranes and calcium responses to adenosine triphosphate (ATP) and to the traffic of the axons it enwraps.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

No synapses; its junctional life is adhesive and secretory, wrapping axon fascicles, contacting the astrocytes of the bulb's surface, whose usual hostility to intruding axons it somehow disarms, and phagocytosing the debris of the constantly dying olfactory axons.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that the olfactory ensheathing cell has no axon; it is the sleeve and escort of axons belonging to olfactory sensory neurons.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

None, but its secretome is its fame: nerve growth factor, brain-derived neurotrophic factor, glial cell line-derived neurotrophic factor, laminin-rich extracellular matrix, and adhesion molecules, a portable growth-permissive environment.

MYELINATION:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that olfactory axons are unmyelinated and the cell forms no myelin in its native station, though transplanted OECs can be coaxed to myelinate axons in demyelinated tracts, a hint of latent Schwann-like capacity.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Moderate, with a constant phagocytic surcharge: because the axons it tends are always dying and being replaced, it is a continuous eater of axonal debris, a housekeeping burden no other ensheathing glia carries at baseline.

PLASTICITY:

Exceptional by definition: it maintains a growth-permissive state lifelong that other glia show only transiently after injury, it remodels its fascicles as populations of axons come and go, and in transplantation it survives grafting, migrates, and remodels glial scar tissue.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

The olfactory mucosa's basal stem cells continually supply its clients; fibroblasts of the nerve sheaths and the vasculature of the mucosa and bulb sustain it; and within the bulb it works jointly with astrocytes and microglia at the frontier it manages.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

It is the reason you can still smell at eighty: without its escort corridor, the relentless death of olfactory neurons would sever the nose from the brain within months.

In the lesioned spinal cord, transplanted OECs have repeatedly been reported to modify the glial scar and support axon regrowth in rodents, dogs, and early human studies, making this cell the most direct experimental bridge between this catalogue and hoped-for cures for paralysis.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Its singular capability is border management: it maintains, throughout adult life, an open gate through the CNS frontier, negotiating with astrocytes that repel every other incoming axon, and it re-runs the entire developmental guidance programme perpetually, targeting each new axon to the correct glomerulus zone of the bulb.

It is also a professional phagocyte on the side, clearing the continuous drizzle of dead axons without inflammation.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Growth cones of newborn olfactory axons; axonal ATP and adhesion signals; debris of dying axons; astrocytic boundary signals in the bulb.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Ensheathed, sorted, and targeted axon fascicles; trophic factors and laminin matrix; phagocytic clearance.

• STATE.

◦ Peripheral versus central station; one of roughly five molecular subtypes; phagocytic load.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Resting potential minus 60 to minus 70 millivolts; client axon diameter roughly 0.2 micrometres; client turnover on a scale of weeks.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Axon escort over the days to weeks a new axon takes to reach the bulb; debris clearance over hours to days; lifelong maintenance of the corridor.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Post-viral and post-traumatic anosmia, loss of smell, when regeneration fails to re-thread the cribriform corridor, a failure made famous by COVID-19.

◦ Shearing of the corridor's fascicles at the cribriform plate in head injury causes permanent traumatic anosmia.

◦ Its native tissue is a route of entry for certain pathogens toward the brain, the price of holding a gate open.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

CODE SAMPLE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

The only glial cell with dual citizenship, escorting brand-new axons across the forbidden PNS-CNS border every week of your life - and, for that reason, the glial cell most often packed into a syringe and offered to an injured spinal cord.


CHAPTER 26. PERINEURIAL GLIAL CELL (Perineurial Cell of the Nerve Fascicle Sheath) (The Fascicle-Fencing Barrier Cell) (NON-COG)

Slice a peripheral nerve across and you see a cable of cables: the axons run in bundles called fascicles, and around every fascicle is a glistening concentric sheath, layer upon layer, like the growth rings of a tree.

Each ring is a single continuous sleeve of flattened cells, stitched to their neighbours by tight junctions, the same molecular seams that make the blood-brain barrier.
