This sheath is the perineurium, and it is not packaging: it is a barrier organ, the reason the salty, protein-poor fluid around your axons stays different from the fluid an inch away in the muscle.

Its cells were long booked as fibroblasts; developmental biology has reassigned at least part of the lineage to glia, and in fish the perineurium demonstrably arises from glial cells of the spinal cord itself.

Fascicle-Fencing names its geometry: concentric fences around each bundle of nerve fibres.

Barrier names its function, the blood-nerve barrier's outer wall.

Cell marks its cellular, living nature - this fence metabolises, transports, and repairs.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The perineurial cell is the flattened epithelioid cell of the perineurium, the lamellar sheath enclosing each fascicle of every peripheral nerve, built of roughly 3 to 15 concentric cell layers depending on nerve size.

Each layer is a complete pavement of polygonal cells sealed edge to edge by tight junctions and coated on both faces with basal lamina, and the stack constitutes the diffusion fence of the blood-nerve barrier, partnering the tight-junction capillaries inside the fascicle.

It maintains the endoneurial microenvironment, the slightly positive-pressure, ion-controlled fluid space in which axons and Schwann cells live, and it excludes toxins, proteins, and immune traffic.

The sheath is also mechanical armour: the perineurium bears most of a nerve's tensile strength and keeps the endoneurial pressure that gives nerves their turgor.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

Individual cells are extremely flattened polygons, often under 1 micrometre thick away from the nucleus, a micrometre being a thousandth of a millimetre, tiled into complete sheets; each sheet is one lamella, and successive lamellae are separated by thin clefts containing collagen and elastic fibres.

The cells are rich in pinocytotic vesicles, tiny transport bubbles, and express glucose transporter 1 at high density, a barrier-cell signature shared with brain endothelium.

SIZE:

Cells measure tens of micrometres across but only 0.1 to 1 micrometre thick; the whole perineurium ranges from a few micrometres around the smallest fascicles to tens of micrometres around large ones, with lamella counts of roughly 3 to 15.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

No named subtypes; the meaningful gradations are positional, from the innermost lamellae with the tightest junctions to the outer lamellae grading into the epineurium, and the specialised open endings where the perineurium thins and terminates around nerve endings, deliberately unsealing the very tips.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Around every fascicle of every peripheral nerve from the spinal roots to the fingertips, continuous centrally with structures at the root sleeves where it meets the arachnoid layers of the meninges, and continuous distally until it opens near the nerve terminals.

NUMBER:

Uncounted; given the total length of human peripheral nerve, on the order of tens of kilometres of fascicle, wrapped in multiple lamellae of pavement cells, the population is plausibly in the billions, very low confidence.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Non-spiking and electrically quiet, resting near minus 60 millivolts; its electrically relevant property is not excitability but resistance, since its sealed layers force current and solutes alike to detour, contributing to the high-resistance sheath around each fascicle.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

No synapses; its junctions are its identity: continuous belts of tight junctions built from claudin-1 and allied proteins sealing each lamella, adherens junctions and gap junctions coupling the cells, and no junctional intimacy with axons at all, which it fences rather than touches.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that the perineurial cell is a barrier epithelioid cell with no axon and no processes beyond its flattened sheet.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

This section is essentially not applicable: the cell is not known to release signalling transmitters, and its chemical work is transport, expressing carriers such as glucose transporter 1 and efflux pumps that police what enters the endoneurium.

MYELINATION:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that the cell forms sheets around whole fascicles, not spirals around axons, and makes no myelin.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Low but constant: tight junction maintenance, vesicular transport, and pumping duty across many square centimetres of sheet, fuelled by the epineurial vessels outside the fence it keeps.

PLASTICITY:

Limited in health, but real in disease and repair: after nerve injury perineurial cells proliferate and re-form compartments around regenerating minifascicles, and the perineurium re-establishes its barrier over weeks - imperfectly, which is one reason repaired nerves remain vulnerable.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Epineurial fibroblasts and fat carry and cushion it, the vasa nervorum, the nerve's own blood vessels, supply it from outside, and endoneurial fibroblasts and Schwann cells maintain the inner world it protects.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Everything in Chapters 19 to 23 lives inside this cell's fence: without the perineurium, plasma proteins, toxins, and immune cells would flood the endoneurium, and the finely tuned ionic fluid around every node of Ranvier would drift toward ordinary tissue fluid, degrading conduction everywhere at once.

Local anaesthetics must cross this fence, which is why blocking a nerve takes minutes rather than seconds.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Its quiet marvel is the maintenance of a pressurised, chemically distinct canal system tens of kilometres long: a living pipeline whose walls actively transport glucose inward, pump contaminants outward, and hold a slight positive pressure, effectively a second circulatory boundary rivalling the blood-brain barrier in tightness but built from glia-kin sheets rather than blood vessel lining.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Glucose and nutrients from epineurial vessels; mechanical stretch and pressure; injury and inflammatory signals.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ A sealed endoneurial compartment; regulated glucose delivery; tensile strength and turgor of the nerve trunk.

• STATE.

◦ Junctional integrity; lamella number 3 to 15; barrier permeability, normally near zero for proteins.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Cell thickness 0.1 to 1 micrometre; claudin-1-based tight junctions; slight positive endoneurial pressure of a few millimetres of mercury.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Transport across the sheath over minutes, as every regional anaesthetic demonstrates; barrier re-formation after injury over weeks.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Barrier breakdown in diabetic and inflammatory neuropathies admits proteins and immune attack to the endoneurium.

◦ Leprosy, still the world's commonest treatable neuropathy, breaches this compartment as Mycobacterium leprae colonises Schwann cells behind the fence.

◦ Perineurioma, a rare tumour of this cell, and the loss of barrier competence after nerve transection.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

CODE SAMPLE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

Concentric pavements of cells a tenth the thickness of a hair, sealed edge to edge into a pressurised pipeline tens of kilometres long - the only fence in the body that every sensation and every command must live behind.


CHAPTER 27. BOUNDARY CAP CELL (Boundary Cap Neural Crest Cell of the Spinal Root Entry and Exit Points) (The Frontier-Policing Gatekeeper Cell) (NON-COG)

Twice at every spinal segment, the surface of the embryonic spinal cord is pierced: once where the motor axons leave, once where the sensory axons enter.

Each hole is a security problem, because on one side lies central nervous tissue and on the other lies the periphery, and cells on both sides would happily migrate through.

The embryo posts guards: small clusters of neural-crest-derived cells that cap each root exactly where it crosses, present for a matter of weeks and gone, in their original form, by the time you are born.

While they stand watch, they enforce a one-way traffic law, axons through, cell bodies never, and as they stand down they quietly donate their daughters to the ganglia and nerves they policed.

Frontier-Policing names their post, the central nervous system (CNS)-PNS interface at every dorsal and ventral root.

Gatekeeper names their rule: let the wires cross, keep the cells home.

Cell marks a transient neural crest population, glial in family and destiny.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The boundary cap cell is a transient, neural-crest-derived cell population that clusters at the dorsal root entry zones and ventral motor exit points of the embryonic spinal cord and hindbrain, defined by expression of the gene Krox20, also called Egr2, the same master gene that later drives Schwann cell myelination.

Its first job is border control: it prevents motor neuron cell bodies from sliding out of the cord along their own axons, and it organises the corridor through which sensory axons enter, using repellent semaphorin signalling to turn back cell bodies while letting growth cones pass.

Its second job is stem cell duty: fate-mapping shows boundary cap daughters becoming the Schwann cells of the roots, a share of the satellite glia and some sensory neurons of the dorsal root ganglia, and even terminal glia and nociceptive-lineage cells out in the skin.

It is, in short, the customs house that dissolves into the country it guarded.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

Small, ovoid, migratory-looking cells packed into discrete caps a few tens of micrometres across, straddling the glia limitans, the astrocytic surface wall of the cord, at each root; they have no elaborate processes, because their tools are surface molecules, not shapes.

SIZE:

Individual cells are roughly 8 to 12 micrometres across, a micrometre being a thousandth of a millimetre, in caps of some dozens to a few hundred cells at each of the roughly 120 root crossing points of the human spinal cord, values extrapolated from rodents, low confidence.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Dorsal caps at sensory entry zones and ventral caps at motor exit points differ in position and emphasis, and lineage studies suggest heterogeneous potency within a cap; no further subtypes are established.

WHERE IT LIVES:

In the embryo only, at every point where a spinal or comparable cranial root pierces the cord or hindbrain surface; the population appears in the human around the second month of gestation and is dispersed as a distinct entity by late fetal life, its descendants living on in roots, ganglia, and skin.

NUMBER:

At its peak, plausibly tens of thousands of cells across all caps, and zero in the adult - the only cell in this part of the catalogue whose healthy adult census is none, moderate confidence.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Non-spiking; embryonic membrane potentials for this population are not well characterised, and its known signalling is molecular, through semaphorin-plexin repulsion and Krox20-driven transcription, not electrical.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

None; its junctional life consists of transient adhesive contacts with crossing axons, with the astrocytic surface of the cord, and with its own cluster-mates.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that the boundary cap cell has no axon; it is a checkpoint for the axons of others.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

This section is essentially not applicable: its known output molecules are guidance and repulsion cues, notably semaphorin 6A signalling to plexin-A4-bearing motor neurons, rather than transmitters.

MYELINATION:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that the cell itself never myelinates, although its Schwann-cell daughters myelinate the spinal roots, and its signature gene Krox20 is the master switch of that later myelination.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Those of any small proliferative embryonic population: modest in absolute terms, supplied by the embryonic vasculature of the root sleeves.

PLASTICITY:

Its whole existence is plasticity: a multipotent stem-like population that first serves as an organ, the checkpoint, and then differentiates itself out of existence into Schwann cells, satellite glia, and neurons; grafted boundary cap cells in animal models show striking capacity to generate peripheral glia, making them a subject of regenerative interest.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

The embryonic root environment sustains it: the astrocytic glia limitans it abuts, the meningeal and vascular cells of the forming root sleeve, and the streams of neural crest kin around it.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Delete it experimentally and the frontier fails in the most literal way: motor neuron cell bodies emigrate from the spinal cord and end up stranded along the ventral roots, a demonstration in mice of what this checkpoint prevents.

Every clean CNS-PNS transition zone in your adult roots, the sharp handover from oligodendrocyte to Schwann cell myelin described in Chapter 19, is the surviving monument of this vanished garrison.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Its remarkable trick is selective permeability at cellular scale: distinguishing a growth cone, which may pass, from the cell body dragging behind it, which may not, using repellent signalling read only by somata - a passport control that inspects parts of a single cell differently.

Its second trick is the graceful exit: converting from organ to inventory, seeding the very tissues it once policed.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Positional cues at the root holes; plexin-semaphorin engagement with crossing cells; developmental timing signals.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ A held frontier at roughly 120 crossing points; daughters delivered to root Schwann cell, satellite glial, sensory neuron, and cutaneous glial pools.

• STATE.

◦ Cap intact versus dispersing; proliferative fraction; lineage commitment of daughters.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Krox20 expression as the defining badge; cap size of dozens to hundreds of cells; operational window of weeks in the human embryo.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Checkpoint duty over roughly the second to fourth months of human gestation; dispersal and differentiation over the following weeks.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ In animal models, cap ablation or semaphorin-plexin disruption lets motor neurons escape into the roots; corresponding human root entry zone malformations are presumed but not proven, low confidence.

◦ Its lineage is discussed as one candidate origin for neurofibromatosis-associated tumours arising at the roots.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

CODE SAMPLE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

The only cell in this catalogue you no longer possess: a border garrison of the embryonic spinal cord that let axons out, kept neurons in, and then dissolved itself into the Schwann cells and satellite glia of the adult.


CHAPTER 28. CUTANEOUS (SKIN) GLIA, INCLUDING THE NOCICEPTIVE SCHWANN CELL (Specialised Terminal Glia of the Skin) (The Pain-Sensing Skin-Mesh Cell) (NON-COG)

For more than a century, textbooks taught that pain begins in free nerve endings: naked axon tips in the skin with nothing between them and the world.

In 2019 that sentence acquired an asterisk.

Abdo and colleagues, working at the Karolinska Institute, described a mesh of specialised Schwann cells at the border between dermis and epidermis whose delicate processes wrap the pain fibres and reach with them toward the surface - and showed that the endings are not free after all.

Driving these glial cells alone, with light-activated channels in mice, produced pain behaviour without touching a single neuron, and silencing them raised mechanical pain thresholds.

Pain-Sensing names the discovery: these glia are themselves mechanosensitive and help initiate nociception, the sensing of harmful stimuli.

Skin-Mesh names their architecture, a continuous glial network in the subepidermal border of the skin.

Cell marks them as terminal Schwann-family glia.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

Cutaneous glia are the specialised terminal Schwann cells of the skin's nerve endings, and their headline member is the nociceptive Schwann cell: a mesh-forming glial cell of the subepidermal plexus, intimately wrapped around unmyelinated nociceptor terminals.

The nociceptive Schwann cell is intrinsically mechanosensitive: poke it and it depolarises through mechanically gated channels and excites its partner nociceptor, making the functional pain transducer a glio-neural end organ, a two-cell sensor, rather than a naked nerve tip.

Subsequent work has shown these sensory Schwann cells help set perceptual thresholds for touch and selectively regulate mechanical pain, and that their loss causes nerve terminal retraction and hypersensitivity.

The family also includes the terminal glia of hair follicle lanceolate endings, which wrap the touch-sensitive palisades around every hair, and grades into the corpuscle cells of Chapter 29.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The nociceptive Schwann cell has a small body in the upper dermis and extraordinarily long, thin, radiating processes that interconnect into a mesh parallel to the epidermal border, sending fine extensions alongside nociceptor axons toward and into the epidermal-dermal junction.

Lanceolate terminal glia are blade-shaped, sandwiching each mechanoreceptor ending against the hair follicle in a palisade arrangement.

SIZE:

Cell bodies are roughly 10 micrometres across, a micrometre being a thousandth of a millimetre, with mesh processes extending 50 to 100 micrometres or more; the glio-neural complex occupies the subepidermal band roughly 20 to 50 micrometres beneath the skin surface, values from mouse skin, moderate confidence for the human equivalent.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Three working subtypes: the nociceptive Schwann cell of the subepidermal mesh; the lanceolate terminal glia of hair follicles; and assorted terminal glia of other dermal endings, with the corpuscular lamellar cells treated separately in Chapter 29.

WHERE IT LIVES:

The skin of the entire body, hairy and glabrous (hairless) alike, stationed in the papillary dermis at the subepidermal border, always outside the epidermis proper - the processes approach and abut the epidermis, while the axons they accompany continue alone into it.

NUMBER:

Uncounted in humans; with roughly 1.8 square metres of skin served by a continuous subepidermal glial mesh, the population plausibly runs to the hundreds of millions, very low confidence.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

The defining property of the nociceptive Schwann cell is genuine mechanoelectrical transduction: mechanical force opens cation channels in its membrane, depolarising it, and optogenetic depolarisation of the cell is by itself sufficient to fire the partner nociceptor and evoke pain behaviour.

It rests, like its relatives, near minus 70 millivolts, and signals onward chemically to its axon partner.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

No classical synapses, but a functional excitatory partnership with nociceptor terminals whose mechanism of glia-to-axon transmission is still being defined; the pairing behaves like a sensory synapse in all but name.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that the cell has no axon; its long mesh processes conduct no action potentials and hand their signal to the true axons of the nociceptors they enfold.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

The glia-to-nociceptor signal is under active study, with adenosine triphosphate (ATP) and related messengers as candidates; the cell is not known to use classical fast transmitters, and this uncertainty should be carried honestly into any simulation.

MYELINATION:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that these are terminal non-myelinating glia of unmyelinated and thinly myelinated pain fibres, and they form no myelin.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Modest, with the standing costs of maintaining an enormous surface-to-volume mesh in the body's most frequently injured tissue.

PLASTICITY:

High: the mesh remodels with skin turnover and after injury, and its pathology is dynamic in both directions, since ablating nociceptive Schwann cells produces terminal retraction and mechanical hypersensitivity, while their sensitisation is a plausible contributor to tactile allodynia, pain from light touch.

Their lineage links to the repair programme of Chapter 21, and skin denervation recruits them to guide reinnervation.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Keratinocytes, the skin's own epithelial cells, are active partners, themselves releasing ATP onto sensory endings; dermal fibroblasts build the matrix the mesh lies in; the dense subepidermal capillary network feeds it; and the parent Remak cells of Chapter 20 deliver its axon partners.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

This cell rewrites the first sentence of pain physiology: the skin's alarm system is not bare wire but wire plus glia, and part of the decision that a stimulus is dangerous is taken by a cell that is not a neuron.

Clinically it offers a new address for pain medicine: a peripheral, accessible, non-neuronal target for mechanical pain and hypersensitivity.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

A glial cell that performs primary sensory transduction: converting mechanical energy into membrane current and driving a neuron to fire, a role every textbook reserved for neurons and specialised receptor cells.

In mice, its activation alone suffices for nocifensive behaviour, and its selective silencing raises mechanical thresholds while leaving heat pain largely intact, evidence of a division of labour between glia and axon within one ending.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Mechanical force at the subepidermal border; keratinocyte ATP; inflammatory mediators of injured skin.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Depolarising drive to nociceptor terminals; trophic maintenance of those terminals; guidance of reinnervation.

• STATE.

◦ Membrane potential; mesh integrity; sensitised versus resting state.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Resting potential near minus 70 millivolts; mesh depth roughly 20 to 50 micrometres below the surface; mechanosensitive current activated within milliseconds of indentation.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Transduction over milliseconds; sensitisation over minutes to hours of inflammation; mesh remodelling over days to weeks.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Loss or dysfunction of nociceptive Schwann cells in animal models causes nerve terminal retraction with paradoxical mechanical hypersensitivity, a candidate mechanism in small fibre neuropathy.

◦ Their sensitisation is a candidate contributor to tactile allodynia in neuropathic pain states.

◦ As Schwann-lineage cells they participate in the painful cutaneous neurofibromas of neurofibromatosis type 1.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

CODE SAMPLE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

The glial cell that ended the century-long reign of the free nerve ending: a subepidermal mesh, discovered in 2019, that feels the pinprick itself and tells the pain fibre to fire.


CHAPTER 29. LAMELLAR CORPUSCLE CELLS OF MEISSNER AND PACINI (Lamellar Cells of the Meissner and Pacinian Corpuscles) (The Onion-Layer Touch-Filter Cell) (NON-COG)

Slide a fingertip across silk and you are reading the output of the most refined mechanical instruments in your body: microscopic onions, buried in the skin, built from dozens of concentric layers of flattened glial cells with fluid between them.

At the core of each onion lies the tip of a sensory axon, and the layers around it are not packaging: they are a mechanical filter, deciding which vibrations reach the wire at all.

Press slowly on a Pacinian corpuscle and the layers slip and flow, absorbing the pressure so completely that the axon never notices; flutter at two hundred cycles per second and the layers pass the vibration straight to the core.

The engineering is so exact that the corpuscle's frequency response was worked out by physiologists using the mathematics of mechanical filters, and the recent discovery that the inner layers are excitable cells has only sharpened the wonder.

Onion-Layer names the anatomy, concentric lamellae of flattened cells like the coats of an onion.

Touch-Filter names the function: mechanical band-pass filtering of touch and vibration before any nerve signal exists.

Cell marks these lamellae as living modified Schwann-lineage glia, not inert wrapping.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The lamellar cells are the modified Schwann-lineage glial cells whose stacked, flattened sheets form the two great encapsulated touch organs of human skin: the Meissner corpuscle, the light-touch and low-frequency flutter sensor of the fingertip ridges, and the Pacinian corpuscle, the deep high-frequency vibration sensor.

In the Meissner corpuscle, horizontal stacks of lamellar cells interleave with the winding terminals of one to several rapidly adapting axons just beneath the epidermis of glabrous (hairless) skin.

In the Pacinian corpuscle, an inner core of some 60 densely packed lamellar Schwann cell layers surrounds the single axon terminal, and around that an outer capsule of roughly 20 to 70 perineurial-derived lamellae alternates elastic sheets with viscous fluid.

The ensemble is a mechanical band-pass filter: sustained pressure is absorbed by lamellar creep and never reaches the axon, while rapid oscillations are transmitted to the core, which is why these corpuscles respond only to change and vibration.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

Each lamellar cell is an extremely flattened sheet, often only 0.1 to 0.2 micrometres thick away from its nucleus, a micrometre being a thousandth of a millimetre, wrapped as an incomplete ring with clefts that set the corpuscle's mechanical anisotropy.

The Pacinian corpuscle in cross-section is the most famous onion in histology: a visible ovoid up to several millimetres long, its layers alternating cell sheet and interlamellar fluid, with the inner core cells so intimate with the axon tip that they resemble a glial receptor organ.

SIZE:

Meissner corpuscles are roughly 30 to 140 micrometres long and 40 to 60 micrometres across, fitted one per dermal papilla ridge pocket; Pacinian corpuscles run from roughly 0.5 to over 2 millimetres, large enough to see unaided, with roughly 20 to 70 outer lamellae and around 60 inner core lamellae.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Two clear families by organ: the horizontally stacked lamellar cells of Meissner corpuscles and the concentric inner core lamellar Schwann cells of Pacinian corpuscles, with the Pacinian outer capsule cells being perineurial rather than glial in origin - one end organ, two lineages collaborating.

The related Ruffini ending and the lanceolate complexes of hairy skin carry simpler glial investments and are treated with Chapter 28.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Meissner corpuscles crowd the dermal papillae of glabrous skin, fingertips, palms, soles, and lips, at densities reaching roughly 50 to 150 corpuscles per square centimetre in the young adult fingertip; Pacinian corpuscles lie deep in the dermis and subcutaneous tissue of palms and soles, in the interosseous membranes, and around joints and viscera, a few hundred to a couple of thousand per hand.

NUMBER:

Order-of-magnitude accounting: tens of thousands of Meissner corpuscles and on the order of two thousand Pacinian corpuscles per hand, each containing dozens of lamellar cells, giving millions of lamellar cells in the skin overall, low confidence.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

The outer lamellae are electrically passive, but the inner core is not: recent work in 2025 showed that Pacinian inner core lamellar Schwann cells are themselves mechanosensitive and excitable, carrying mechanically gated currents and voltage-gated sodium channels, and that they potentiate the corpuscle's response to vibration rather than merely cushioning it.

Resting potentials sit near minus 70 millivolts, moderate confidence.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

No classical synapses, but the inner core lamellae form an intimate signalling apposition with the axon terminal, now suspected of chemical glia-to-axon excitation in the manner of the nociceptive Schwann cell of Chapter 28, in addition to their mechanical coupling.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that the lamellar cell has no axon; the single axon at the corpuscle core belongs to a dorsal root ganglion neuron, and the lamellar cells are its wrapping and its filter.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Largely not applicable: candidate glia-to-axon messengers in the inner core are under investigation, and no classical transmitter role is established.

MYELINATION:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that the lamellar cell forms flattened filter sheets rather than compact myelin; the parent axon's myelin, made by ordinary Schwann cells of Chapter 19, ends as the axon enters the corpuscle, and the first node of Ranvier just inside the capsule is where the nerve impulse is born.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Low: the corpuscle's work is done largely by passive mechanics, with metabolic cost concentrated in maintaining the lamellae, their fluid, and the newly appreciated excitability of the inner core.

PLASTICITY:

Structural rather than synaptic: corpuscles are maintained by their axon and remodel with it, Meissner corpuscle density declines several-fold across adult life, a measurable anatomy of ageing fingertip sensibility, and denervated corpuscles atrophy but can be re-occupied by regenerating axons, an old observation that underwrites modern nerve repair.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

The corpuscle is its own support system in miniature: perineurial capsule cells continue the blood-nerve fence of Chapter 26 around the glial core, capillaries enter the capsule to feed it, and dermal fibroblasts anchor the whole instrument in the skin's matrix.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

These cells set the passband of human touch: without lamellar filtering the vibration channels would be deafened by static pressure, and with it a Pacinian afferent can detect skin motion of a few tens of nanometres at its best frequency near 200 to 250 hertz, sensitivity at the scale of large molecules.

Braille reading, tool use, the feel of a surface through a stick - each depends on the filtered channels these onions define.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

This is the catalogue's clearest case of computation performed by mechanics: a differentiator and band-pass filter built from dead-soft materials, tuned across four orders of magnitude of frequency by nothing but layer count, stiffness, and fluid viscosity, operating with zero energy cost per event.

The 2025 discovery that the inner core cells also amplify electrically means the onion both filters and boosts: a passive mechanical computer wrapped around an active glial preamplifier wrapped around a wire.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Skin deformation: Meissner corpuscles tuned to flutter near 10 to 50 hertz, Pacinian corpuscles to vibration near 40 to 400 hertz with best sensitivity near 200 to 250 hertz.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Filtered mechanical drive, and in the Pacinian inner core an added excitatory boost, to one rapidly adapting axon terminal; downstream, one all-or-nothing spike per vibration cycle over much of the range.

• STATE.

◦ Lamellar compression and fluid distribution; inner core membrane potential; corpuscle count, which falls with age.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Outer lamellae roughly 20 to 70; inner core lamellae around 60; Pacinian length 0.5 to over 2 millimetres; threshold displacement down to tens of nanometres at best frequency.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Mechanical high-pass filtering with creep relaxation over tens to hundreds of milliseconds; transduction over fractions of a millisecond; corpuscle density decline over decades.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Age-related and diabetic loss of Meissner corpuscles degrades fine touch and contributes to falls and impaired dexterity.

◦ Vibration white finger, the occupational hand-arm vibration syndrome, damages the vibration-sensing apparatus these cells serve.

◦ Pacinian corpuscle hyperplasia and pacinioma are rare painful lesions of the organ itself.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

CODE SAMPLE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

The only glial cells you could see with your naked eye if the skin were glass: dozens of living onion layers around a single axon tip, filtering the world's vibrations before the nervous system hears of them, and, we now know, whispering an electrical boost of their own into the core.


PART THREE. THE SUPPORTING CELLS OF THE SENSE ORGANS

Every special sense organ is a partnership between a receptor cell that answers to light, sound, tilt, odour, or taste and a quieter cast of non-neuronal cells that make the receptor's work possible.

These supporting cells are glia in all but name: they insulate, feed, clean, and micromechanically tune the sensory cells, and in several organs they set the ionic stage on which transduction is performed.

The receptor cells take the applause, but delete the supporting cells and the organ falls silent, because a receptor without its scaffold cannot hold its shape, recover its ions, or survive the chemical assault of the outside world.

This part catalogues six such supporting populations, one per sense where the supporting cell has a distinct identity, and each is tagged non-cognitive because its role is substrate, not computation.


CHAPTER 30. COCHLEAR SUPPORTING CELLS: DEITERS, PILLAR, HENSEN, BORDER, AND INNER PHALANGEAL CELLS (The Harp-String Scaffold Cells) (NON-COG)

Inside the coiled cochlea the hearing cells sit not on soft tissue but on a rigid, precisely engineered truss, and these are its girders.

They hold the sound-sensing hair cells at exact angles, build the stiff tunnel that lets the whole structure vibrate as designed, and quietly pass the used potassium ions hand to hand back towards the battery that powers hearing.

They are the reason a human ear can tell a violin from a viola, and, tragically, the reason human deafness is permanent: unlike a bird's ear, this scaffold cannot rebuild a lost hair cell.

Harp-String names the tuned, string-like row of hair cells they support along the spiralling basilar membrane.

Scaffold names their true trade, structural and ionic support rather than sensing.

Cells marks them as non-neuronal supporting cells, glia of the inner ear.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

These are the supporting epithelial cells of the organ of Corti, the hearing organ that rides on the basilar membrane inside the fluid-filled cochlea (the snail-shaped hearing chamber of the inner ear).

They are a family, not one cell: the outer and inner pillar cells that build the rigid triangular tunnel of Corti, the Deiters cells (also called phalangeal cells) that cup the base of each outer hair cell and send a thin finger up to help form the reticular lamina (the stiff mosaic top surface of the organ), the inner phalangeal and border cells that cradle the single row of inner hair cells, and the Hensen cells that flank the outer edge.

Together they fix the geometry of transduction and run the potassium recycling loop that keeps the sensory battery charged.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

Each Deiters and pillar cell has a foot-plate body sitting on the basilar membrane that holds the nucleus, and a thin, microtubule-stiffened phalangeal process rising to the apex where its head expands into a plate that locks into the reticular lamina in junctional contact with hair cells and neighbouring supporting cells.

The pillar cells are so heavily packed with cross-linked microtubules and actin that they behave like biological struts, holding the tunnel of Corti open against the constant vibration of sound.

SIZE:

The tall pillar and Deiters cells span roughly the full height of the organ of Corti, on the order of 30 to 70 micrometres from basilar membrane to reticular lamina, a micrometre being a thousandth of a millimetre, with the whole organ and its cells shortening systematically from the low-frequency apex to the high-frequency base of the cochlear spiral.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

The named subtypes are the inner and outer pillar cells, the three to four rows of Deiters cells, the inner phalangeal cells, the inner and outer border cells, and the outer supporting Hensen, Claudius, and Boettcher cells that grade off towards the spiral ligament.

Treat them as a graded scaffold family, each shaped for its position across the width and length of the organ.

WHERE IT LIVES:

They live in the organ of Corti along the entire two and a half turns of the human cochlea, from base to apex, tiling the space between and beneath the hair cells.

They sit at a sharp interface: their apical junctions form the reticular lamina that seals the high-potassium endolymph above from the ordinary perilymph and cell interiors below, a barrier whose failure is itself a cause of deafness.

NUMBER:

The human organ of Corti holds about 3,500 inner hair cells and about 12,000 outer hair cells, and the supporting cells modestly outnumber them, so a reasonable estimate is on the order of 30,000 to 80,000 supporting cells per cochlea, or roughly 60,000 to 160,000 across both ears, low to moderate confidence given how few whole-organ human counts exist.

Each outer hair cell is served by one Deiters cell, which anchors the count.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

These are non-excitable cells: they fire no action potential, resting at a negative membrane potential in the region of minus 60 to minus 90 millivolts set largely by potassium permeability, much like an astrocyte.

Their electrical importance is not spiking but conduction of potassium and small signalling molecules through gap junctions, and they show slow calcium signals and calcium waves that spread cell to cell.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

This section is reinterpreted as junctional relationships, which are central here: supporting cells are extensively coupled to one another by gap junctions built from connexin 26 and connexin 30, forming an epithelial syncytium that carries potassium and calcium waves.

They make no chemical synapses, but they physically embrace the hair cells and the unmyelinated dendrites of the auditory nerve that pass between them.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that these are epithelial supporting cells with no axon.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

They are not classical transmitter-releasing cells, but they do participate in adenosine triphosphate signalling, releasing and responding to extracellular adenosine triphosphate (the cell's energy and signalling molecule) that drives intercellular calcium waves across the supporting-cell network.

MYELINATION:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that supporting cells are not myelinated and make no myelin.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Their metabolic load is moderate and dominated by ion transport, chiefly the pumping and channelling needed to move potassium through the recycling pathway and to maintain the endocochlear environment, plus the cytoskeletal upkeep of the stiff pillar and phalangeal processes.

PLASTICITY:

In the mature human cochlea plasticity is severely limited: crucially, mammalian supporting cells have lost the ability of their bird and fish counterparts to re-enter the cell cycle and transdifferentiate into replacement hair cells, which is exactly why noise- or age-related hair cell loss is irreversible in humans.

After hair cell death they do reorganise, expanding to seal the reticular lamina and forming a flat scar epithelium.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

They are supported by the fibrocytes of the spiral ligament and spiral limbus and by the marginal, intermediate, and basal cells of the stria vascularis (the vascular tissue on the cochlear wall that generates the endocochlear potential), which together complete the potassium loop the supporting cells feed into.

The cochlear blood supply of the spiral modiolar artery feeds the whole apparatus.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove these cells and the organ of Corti collapses mechanically: the tunnel of Corti falls, the reticular lamina unseals, endolymph and perilymph mix, and hearing stops even with hair cells intact.

They are what turns a sheet of receptor cells into a tuned, frequency-mapped mechanical instrument.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Their most surprising capability is collective and mechanical rather than electrical: the connexin-coupled network conducts regenerative potassium and calcium waves that recycle the ions of hearing, and the pillar and Deiters cells act as active mechanical elements whose stiffness shapes how the basilar membrane and outer hair cells amplify faint sounds.

They compute nothing, but they set the physical constants of the cochlear amplifier.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Potassium ions arriving basolaterally from active hair cells; extracellular adenosine triphosphate; mechanical vibration transmitted through the basilar membrane.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Potassium passed laterally through gap junctions towards the spiral ligament; calcium waves; mechanical support forces holding hair cell geometry.

• STATE.

◦ Membrane potential near minus 60 to minus 90 millivolts; intracellular potassium and calcium concentration; cytoskeletal tension.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Gap junctions of connexin 26 and connexin 30; cell height 30 to 70 micrometres; roughly one Deiters cell per outer hair cell across about 12,000 outer hair cells.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Calcium waves spreading over seconds; potassium recycling over the working timescale of continuous hearing; scar formation over days to weeks after injury.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Mutations in the connexin 26 gene (GJB2) are the single commonest cause of hereditary non-syndromic deafness, breaking the potassium recycling loop these cells run.

◦ Loss of supporting-cell integrity contributes to noise-induced and age-related (presbycusic) sensorineural hearing loss, which is permanent because these cells cannot regenerate hair cells.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

CODE SAMPLE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

A row of stiff, microtubule-packed cells cupping the hair cells of the organ of Corti and passing potassium hand to hand through connexin gap junctions, the mammalian scaffold that, unlike a bird's, can never rebuild a hair cell it loses.


CHAPTER 31. VESTIBULAR SUPPORTING CELLS (The Balance-Organ Nurse Cells) (NON-COG)

Deep beside the cochlea sit the tiny organs that tell you which way is up and whether your head is turning, and their hair cells, like the ear's, ride on a bed of supporting cells.

These nurse cells hold the balance receptors in place, mop up the potassium they spill, and secrete the strange internal jellies and crystals that give the balance organs their leverage on gravity and motion.

They are the ear's second, quieter scaffold, and they retain a flicker of the regenerative power their cochlear cousins have lost.

Balance-organ names the vestibular apparatus they serve.

Nurse names their supportive, housekeeping trade.

Cells marks them as non-neuronal glia of the inner ear.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

These are the supporting epithelial cells of the vestibular organs, the balance sensors of the inner ear: the three cristae ampullares of the semicircular canals, which sense rotation, and the two maculae of the utricle and saccule, which sense linear acceleration and the pull of gravity.

They interleave with the vestibular hair cells, sustain them structurally and metabolically, recycle potassium, and help produce the gelatinous cupula and otolithic membrane and its calcium-carbonate crystals (otoconia) that couple head motion to the hair bundles.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

They are tall columnar cells stretching from the basement membrane to the luminal surface, with microvilli at their apex and secretory machinery, interdigitating with the flask-shaped and cylindrical vestibular hair cells and enclosing their afferent nerve calyces.

SIZE:

They are columnar epithelial cells on the order of 20 to 40 micrometres tall, comparable in scale to the hair cells they support.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

They are broadly one supporting type per organ but differ regionally between the cristae and the maculae, and a specialised subset near the striola and the planum semilunatum contributes to fluid and ion secretion.

WHERE IT LIVES:

They live in the sensory epithelia of the vestibular labyrinth: the ampullary cristae at the base of each of the three semicircular canals and the maculae of the utricle and saccule.

They sit at the interface between the potassium-rich endolymph above and the cellular tissue below, and their apical junctions help seal that boundary.

NUMBER:

The human vestibular epithelia contain on the order of 30,000 hair cells across the five organs, and supporting cells are present in similar or somewhat greater numbers, so a rough estimate is tens of thousands of vestibular supporting cells per ear, low confidence.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

They are non-excitable, resting at a negative potassium-dominated membrane potential in the region of minus 60 to minus 80 millivolts, with calcium signalling but no action potentials.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Reinterpreted as junctional relationships: they are gap-junction coupled to one another and physically ensheath the hair cells and their afferent calyces, but they form no chemical synapses.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that these are epithelial supporting cells with no axon.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Not classical transmitter cells, though like other inner-ear supporting cells they participate in extracellular adenosine triphosphate and purinergic signalling.

MYELINATION:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that these cells are not myelinated and make no myelin.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Moderate, dominated by ion transport for potassium recycling and by the secretion of the gelatinous membranes and otoconial matrix.

PLASTICITY:

Notably greater than in the cochlea: mammalian vestibular supporting cells retain a limited capacity to regenerate hair cells and to convert into replacement hair cells, giving partial spontaneous recovery of balance function after damage, though far below the full regeneration seen in birds.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Supported by the underlying vestibular stroma and its fibrocytes and by the dark cells of the vestibular labyrinth (a potassium-secreting epithelium analogous to the stria vascularis) that complete the ionic loop.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove them and the balance organs lose their scaffolding and their otoconial and cupular gels, and the sense of head position and motion fails, producing vertigo and imbalance.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Their surprising capability is their residual regenerative plasticity, converting into new hair cells after injury, together with their secretory manufacture of the otoconia, the tiny gravity-sensing crystals whose displacement is the mechanical basis of the commonest form of vertigo.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Potassium spilled by active hair cells; mechanical and secretory demand signals.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Recycled potassium; secreted gelatinous membrane and otoconial matrix; structural support.

• STATE.

◦ Membrane potential near minus 60 to minus 80 millivolts; intracellular ion levels; secretory activity.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Cell height 20 to 40 micrometres; tens of thousands per ear; gap-junction coupling.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Ion recycling continuous; hair cell regeneration over weeks to months where it occurs.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Otoconial displacement underlies benign paroxysmal positional vertigo; endolymphatic hydrops and ionic imbalance contribute to Meniere disease; supporting-cell and hair-cell loss causes vestibular hypofunction and chronic imbalance.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

CODE SAMPLE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

A columnar scaffold cell of the cristae and maculae that recycles potassium, secretes the gravity-sensing otoconia, and, unlike its cochlear cousin, can still coax out a few replacement hair cells.


CHAPTER 32. OLFACTORY SUSTENTACULAR CELL (The Nose's Bodyguard Cell) (NON-COG)

High in the roof of the nose, wrapped around the only neurons in the body that stand exposed to open air, is a tall guardian cell that shields them, feeds them, and detoxifies the chemical world they must dip into to smell it.

For a century it was ignored as mere packing tissue, and then in 2020 it became one of the most discussed cells in medicine, because this, not the smell neuron itself, is the cell the pandemic virus attacks to steal the sense of smell.

Bodyguard names its defensive, insulating, detoxifying role over the vulnerable olfactory neurons.

Cell marks it as a non-neuronal supporting cell of the nasal lining.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The olfactory sustentacular cell (sustentacular meaning supporting) is the tall columnar supporting cell of the olfactory epithelium, the patch of specialised lining high in the nasal cavity where smell is transduced.

It insulates the olfactory sensory neurons from one another, maintains the ionic and chemical environment around their exposed cilia, detoxifies inhaled chemicals with drug-metabolising enzymes, contributes to the mucus and its odorant-binding proteins, and phagocytoses dead cells.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

It is a tall, polarised columnar cell spanning the full thickness of the epithelium, with a broad apical surface bearing microvilli that meets the mucus, a nucleus held in the superficial layer, and a slender foot reaching the basement membrane, its body wrapping around the thinner olfactory neuron dendrites like insulation around wires.

SIZE:

It spans the epithelial thickness, roughly 50 to 100 micrometres from mucus to basement membrane, one of the taller epithelial cells of the body.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

It is treated as a single type, though it grades in properties across the epithelium and is closely related to the microvillar cells and to the duct and acinar cells of the underlying Bowman glands that share its ACE2 and detoxifying character.

WHERE IT LIVES:

It lives in the olfactory epithelium on the superior nasal septum, the cribriform region, and the superior turbinate, at the very top of each nasal cavity.

It sits at a stark interface, one cell layer between the outside air and mucus above and the brain's most direct portal to the environment below, since olfactory axons pass straight through the skull to the olfactory bulb.

NUMBER:

The human olfactory epithelium covers only a few square centimetres and holds on the order of 6 to 10 million olfactory sensory neurons, with sustentacular cells present in comparable numbers, so a reasonable estimate is several million sustentacular cells per nose, low to moderate confidence and known to shrink with age.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

It is non-excitable, resting at a negative membrane potential in the region of minus 50 to minus 80 millivolts, with potassium channels and calcium signalling but no action potentials.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Reinterpreted as junctional relationships: it forms tight junctions and adherens junctions with neighbouring cells to seal the epithelial barrier and is coupled to its neighbours, but it forms no chemical synapses.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that it is an epithelial supporting cell with no axon.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Not a classical transmitter cell, though it engages in purinergic (adenosine triphosphate) signalling and secretes support and mucus components rather than transmitters.

MYELINATION:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that it is not myelinated and makes no myelin.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Considerable for a supporting cell, dominated by its detoxifying cytochrome P450 and other drug-metabolising enzymes that neutralise inhaled toxins, plus secretion and barrier maintenance.

PLASTICITY:

It is renewed from the basal stem cells of the epithelium and reshapes its coverage as olfactory neurons turn over throughout life, but it does not itself store or compute experience.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

It is supported by the horizontal and globose basal cells that regenerate it (see the next chapter) and by the Bowman glands that share the secretory burden, and it is escorted at the axon exit by the olfactory ensheathing cells.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Damage it and smell fails even while the smell neurons live, because the neurons depend on it for their ionic environment, their protection, and their support, which is precisely the mechanism of pandemic anosmia.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Its most consequential property is its abundant expression of the viral entry proteins ACE2 (angiotensin-converting enzyme 2) and TMPRSS2 (transmembrane serine protease 2), which the olfactory neurons themselves lack, making the sustentacular cell the prime target of SARS-CoV-2 (the virus of COVID-19) in the nose, so that its infection and disruption, not neuron death, causes the sudden loss of smell that affected around half of infected patients.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Inhaled air, odorants, and toxins; potassium and ions from active olfactory neurons; the virus itself.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Detoxified products; secreted mucus and support factors; a stable ionic environment for the neurons.

• STATE.

◦ Membrane potential near minus 50 to minus 80 millivolts; enzyme activity; barrier integrity.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Cell height 50 to 100 micrometres; several million per nose; high ACE2 and TMPRSS2 expression.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Detoxification and mucus turnover over minutes; epithelial renewal over weeks; smell recovery after COVID-19 typically over weeks, sometimes longer.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ SARS-CoV-2 infection of these cells causes COVID-19 anosmia; toxic and inflammatory injury (chemical exposure, chronic rhinosinusitis) and age-related decline degrade smell through this cell.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

CODE SAMPLE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

The tall guardian cell of the nasal roof that insulates and detoxifies for the smell neurons and, by carrying ACE2 while they do not, became the cell whose infection stole smell from the world.


CHAPTER 33. OLFACTORY HORIZONTAL AND GLOBOSE BASAL CELLS (The Smell-Line Stem Cells) (NON-COG)

The nose performs a trick no other part of the human nervous system can: it grows brand-new sensory neurons for a lifetime, replacing them as they wear out in the harsh open air.

The two cell populations in this chapter are the reason it can, the reserve and the everyday stem cells that keep the smell line staffed against constant loss.

Smell-line names the continuously replenished lineage of olfactory sensory neurons they produce.

Stem names their identity as the reserve and active progenitors of that lineage.

Cells marks them as non-neuronal precursor cells of the nasal lining.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

These are the two basal stem cell populations of the olfactory epithelium: the horizontal basal cells, a quiescent reserve pool pressed against the basement membrane, and the globose basal cells, the actively dividing everyday progenitors sitting just above them.

Together they continuously regenerate the olfactory sensory neurons, and also replace the sustentacular and other cells, making the olfactory epithelium the one place in the human body where sensory neurons are renewed throughout life.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

Horizontal basal cells are flattened, keratin-rich cells lying flat along the basement membrane; globose basal cells are rounded cells stacked just above them, less differentiated in appearance and more mitotically active.

SIZE:

Both are small cells on the order of 8 to 15 micrometres across, typical of stem and progenitor cells.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

The two named subtypes are exactly the horizontal basal cell (reserve) and the globose basal cell (active), with the globose pool itself containing a hierarchy from stem-like to committed neuronal precursor.

WHERE IT LIVES:

They live in the basal layer of the olfactory epithelium, along and just above its basement membrane, beneath the neurons and sustentacular cells they generate.

They sit at the epithelial-stromal interface, the deepest layer of the smell lining.

NUMBER:

They are a minority basal population beneath a neuron pool of some 6 to 10 million cells, so likely on the order of one to a few million per nose, low confidence, declining with age.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Non-excitable progenitor cells resting at a negative membrane potential in the region of minus 50 to minus 70 millivolts, with no action potentials.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Reinterpreted as junctional relationships: they adhere to the basement membrane and to their neighbours and respond to local signalling from injury and from the overlying cells, but they form no synapses.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that these are stem and progenitor cells with no axon.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Not applicable as transmitter release; they communicate by growth factors and signalling molecules rather than neurotransmitters.

MYELINATION:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that these cells are not myelinated and make no myelin.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Modest at rest for the quiescent horizontal cells and higher for the actively dividing globose cells, whose demand rises sharply after injury when large-scale regeneration is triggered.

PLASTICITY:

This is their defining feature: they are the developmental plasticity of the adult nose, with horizontal basal cells activating as a reserve after severe injury and globose basal cells providing continuous turnover, together sustaining lifelong olfactory neurogenesis.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

They are supported by the underlying lamina propria and its blood supply and by signals from the sustentacular cells and the surviving neurons that regulate when and how much they divide.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove them and the olfactory epithelium cannot renew, so ordinary wear, viral damage, and toxic injury would progressively and permanently erase the sense of smell instead of being repaired.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Their surprising capability is regenerative decision-making: after injury the normally silent horizontal basal cells sense the loss and switch on to rebuild the entire epithelium, neurons and support cells alike, a multipotent repair response almost unique among adult human sensory tissues.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Injury and turnover signals; growth factors; loss of overlying neurons.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ New olfactory sensory neurons, sustentacular cells, and other epithelial cells.

• STATE.

◦ Quiescent versus activated; cell-cycle position; commitment to a lineage.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Cell diameter 8 to 15 micrometres; one to a few million per nose; two populations, reserve and active.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Continuous turnover producing new neurons over roughly one to a few weeks; large-scale reconstitution over weeks to months after severe injury.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Exhaustion or damage of these stem cells underlies permanent (rather than recoverable) smell loss, including the persistent anosmia that follows some severe viral and toxic injuries and part of age-related smell decline.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

CODE SAMPLE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

The paired reserve-and-active stem cells at the floor of the smell lining that make the human nose the one organ able to grow fresh sensory neurons for a lifetime.


CHAPTER 34. TASTE BUD TYPE I CELL (The Taste-Bud Janitor Cell) (NON-COG)

Crowded inside every taste bud, among the cells that actually taste sweet, sour, salt, bitter, and savoury, is a dark, many-armed cell that does none of the tasting and all of the cleaning.

It wraps its slender processes around its sensing neighbours like an astrocyte around synapses, mops up the transmitters and ions they spill, and keeps each taste cell's signal from bleeding into the next.

It is the taste bud's own glia, and without it the tongue's fine chemical map would smear.

Janitor names its clearing, buffering, insulating housekeeping trade.

Cell marks it as the non-neuronal, glial-like supporting cell of the taste bud.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The taste bud type I cell is the glial-like supporting cell of the taste bud, the onion-shaped cluster of about 50 to 100 cells in the tongue and palate that detects taste.

It makes up around half of all cells in a taste bud, and it wraps thin processes around the receptor cells (the type II sweet, bitter, and savoury cells and the type III sour cells), clears spent neurotransmitter and potassium, and electrically insulates neighbouring taste cells from one another, exactly the role a glial cell plays in the brain.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

It is a dark, elongated, spindle-shaped cell (the classic dark cell of taste histology) with lamellar cytoplasmic sheets that ensheath the other taste cells and their nerve fibres, and apical microvilli reaching into the taste pore.

SIZE:

It is a slender cell spanning much of the taste bud's height, on the order of 30 to 70 micrometres tall, with fine wrapping processes only a fraction of a micrometre thick.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

It is treated as a single glial-like type, distinguished by its molecular markers rather than internal subtypes.

WHERE IT LIVES:

It lives inside taste buds in the epithelium of the tongue's fungiform, foliate, and circumvallate papillae, and in the soft palate, epiglottis, and upper oesophagus.

It sits at the interface between the receptor cells and the incoming gustatory nerve fibres, and its apex reaches the taste pore that opens to the mouth.

NUMBER:

The human tongue and palate hold on the order of a few thousand to 10,000 taste buds, each with roughly 25 to 50 type I cells, giving on the order of a few hundred thousand type I cells overall, low to moderate confidence, with counts declining in old age.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

It is largely non-excitable and does not carry the taste signal, resting at a negative membrane potential and expressing the potassium and voltage-gated channels appropriate to a buffering cell rather than firing informative action potentials.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Reinterpreted as junctional relationships: it does not form the transmitting synapse onto the gustatory nerve, but it wraps and insulates the receptor cells and their synapses, an arrangement described as forming tripartite-synapse-like contacts in the taste bud, analogous to the astrocyte at a brain synapse.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that it is a supporting cell with no axon.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

It is a transmitter-clearing rather than transmitter-releasing cell, expressing the glial glutamate and aspartate transporter (GLAST) to take up glutamate and the ectoenzyme NTPDase2 (nucleoside triphosphate diphosphohydrolase 2) on its surface to hydrolyse the adenosine triphosphate that type II cells release as their taste transmitter.

MYELINATION:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that it is not myelinated and makes no myelin.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Moderate, dominated by ion transport and by the enzymatic clearance of transmitter, comparable to a small clearing astrocyte.

PLASTICITY:

It turns over continuously as the whole taste bud is renewed every one to two weeks from local stem cells, but it does not itself store learned information.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

It is supported by the basal stem cells of the taste bud and the surrounding lingual epithelium, and by the underlying connective tissue and its blood supply and gustatory innervation.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove it and the taste bud loses its housekeeping: transmitter and potassium accumulate, neighbouring taste cells cross-talk, and the sharp separation of sweet, sour, salty, bitter, and savoury signals degrades.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Its most striking property is that it is a peripheral glial cell in disguise, running the same molecular toolkit as a brain astrocyte (GLAST for glutamate, ectoATPase for adenosine triphosphate, potassium buffering) to keep each taste channel clean and private, and it has recently been shown to secrete immune signalling molecules, hinting at a defensive role too.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Glutamate, adenosine triphosphate, and potassium spilled by active taste receptor cells.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Cleared and recycled transmitter and ions; electrical insulation between taste cells.

• STATE.

◦ Membrane potential negative; transporter and ectoenzyme activity; ion levels.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Cell height 30 to 70 micrometres; about half of a bud's 50 to 100 cells; markers GLAST, NTPDase2, blood group antigen H.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Transmitter clearance over milliseconds to seconds; whole-bud renewal every 1 to 2 weeks.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Its loss or dysfunction contributes to taste disturbance (dysgeusia) and taste loss (ageusia) in aging, after radiotherapy and chemotherapy, and in some viral and inflammatory conditions.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

CODE SAMPLE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

A dark, many-armed cell filling half the taste bud that tastes nothing but runs an astrocyte's toolkit, GLAST and ectoATPase and potassium buffering, to keep every taste channel clean and separate.


CHAPTER 35. RETINAL PIGMENT EPITHELIAL CELL (The Photoreceptor's Round-the-Clock Valet Cell) (NON-COG)

Behind the light-sensing cells of the eye lies a single dark, hexagonal layer that never rests: it soaks up stray light, recharges the visual pigment, and every single morning eats the worn-out tips of the photoreceptors and digests them.

Over a human lifetime one of these cells swallows more than a billion spent photoreceptor discs, an appetite matched almost nowhere else in the body.

When this layer finally falters, the centre of sight dies, and that is age-related macular degeneration, a leading cause of blindness.

Round-the-clock names its ceaseless daily service, especially the dawn burst of eating shed discs.

Valet names its attending, cleaning, recycling role for the photoreceptors.

Cell marks it as a non-neuronal supporting epithelium of the retina.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The retinal pigment epithelial cell is the pigmented service cell that forms a single layer between the light-sensing photoreceptors and their blood supply, the choroid.

It absorbs stray light with its melanin, runs the visual (retinoid) cycle that regenerates the bleached visual pigment for the photoreceptors, transports nutrients and waste between blood and retina, forms the outer blood-retinal barrier, and each day phagocytoses (eats and digests) the shed, worn-out tips of the photoreceptor outer segments.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

It is a polarised, roughly hexagonal cell with a smooth basal surface facing the choroid and an apical surface bristling with long microvilli that interdigitate with and embrace the photoreceptor outer segments, its cytoplasm packed with pigment granules (melanosomes) and, after dawn, with phagosomes full of ingested discs.

SIZE:

It is on the order of 10 to 14 micrometres across in the central macula and wider (up to around 60 micrometres) in the periphery, forming a continuous sheet one cell thick.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

It is a single type that varies by region, smaller, taller, and more heavily pigmented in the central macula and larger and flatter in the periphery.

WHERE IT LIVES:

It lives as the outermost layer of the retina, between the photoreceptors and the choroid, across the entire back of the eye.

It sits at a critical double interface, embracing the photoreceptor tips on one side and forming the outer blood-retinal barrier with the choroidal capillaries on the other.

NUMBER:

The human eye holds roughly 3.5 million retinal pigment epithelial cells, serving about 120 million rods and 6 million cones, so each supports on the order of 20 to 30 photoreceptors, moderate confidence.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

It is non-excitable but strongly polarised electrically, maintaining a transepithelial potential and a negative membrane potential and generating the slow standing potential of the eye that clinical electro-oculography measures, with no action potentials.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Reinterpreted as junctional relationships: it is sealed to its neighbours by tight junctions that constitute the outer blood-retinal barrier and interdigitates apically with the photoreceptor outer segments, but it forms no chemical synapses.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that it is an epithelial cell with no axon.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Not a transmitter cell; it secretes signalling and trophic factors such as pigment epithelium-derived factor and vascular endothelial growth factor that maintain the photoreceptors and the choroid, rather than neurotransmitters.

MYELINATION:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that it is not myelinated and makes no myelin.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

High and among the most sustained of any epithelial cell, driven by lifelong daily phagocytosis and digestion of shed discs, active retinoid recycling, ion and fluid transport, and heavy exposure to light and oxygen that loads it with oxidative stress.

PLASTICITY:

It is largely post-mitotic and does not renew readily in the adult human, which is part of why its age-related failure is so consequential, though it can transiently proliferate and migrate in disease.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

It is supported by the choriocapillaris (the dense capillary bed of the choroid) that supplies it, by Bruch membrane on which it sits, and it reciprocally supports the Muller glia and photoreceptors of the retina it serves.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove it and the photoreceptors die, because they cannot shed and renew their outer segments, cannot regenerate their visual pigment, and lose their barrier and blood supply, exactly the cascade of retinal degeneration.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Its most remarkable capability is its industrial-scale, clock-timed phagocytosis: driven by a circadian rhythm, each cell engulfs a burst of shed disc packets one to two hours after light onset, clearing on the order of a few thousand discs per day across the roughly 20 to 30 photoreceptors it serves, and digesting in excess of a billion discs over a 70-year human lifetime, all while recycling the retinoids back to the photoreceptors.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Shed photoreceptor outer segment tips; bleached retinoids; light; nutrients from the choroid.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Digested and recycled retinoids returned to photoreceptors; absorbed stray light; trophic factors; waste passed to the blood.

• STATE.

◦ Transepithelial potential; phagosome load (peaking after dawn); pigment and lipofuscin content.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ About 3.5 million cells per eye; cell width 10 to 60 micrometres; each serving 20 to 30 photoreceptors; over a billion discs digested per cell per lifetime.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Phagocytic burst 1 to 2 hours after light onset, once per day; disc digestion over hours; lipofuscin accumulation over decades.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Its dysfunction and death, with lipofuscin and drusen accumulation, cause age-related macular degeneration, a leading cause of blindness; genetic defects in its retinoid and phagocytic machinery cause forms of retinitis pigmentosa and Leber congenital amaurosis.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

CODE SAMPLE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

A single dark hexagonal layer behind the retina that recharges the visual pigment and eats the photoreceptors' shed tips every dawn, more than a billion discs per cell in a lifetime, and whose failure blinds the centre of sight.


PART FOUR. THE DEVELOPMENTAL AND STEM CELL POPULATIONS

Every cell in the adult brain was once the daughter of one great scaffold-and-factory cell that no longer exists in the finished organ.

This part catalogues the builders: the radial glia that raise the cortex, their vastly expanded human cousins in the outer subventricular zone, and the two adult stem cell reserves that keep a flicker of that building power alive in the walls of the lateral ventricle and in the floor of the dentate gyrus.

They are the developmental scaffolding of the nervous system, cells whose main product is not a signal but other cells, and whose main structure is a ladder rather than a circuit.

Because their role is construction and reserve rather than moment-to-moment computation, all but one are tagged (NON-COG); the single exception, the adult hippocampal stem cell, earns a cautious (COG) only to the extent that the still-contested phenomenon of adult human neurogenesis is real.

Where the evidence is genuinely unsettled, this part says so plainly, because honest uncertainty is itself a house-style value of this catalogue.


CHAPTER 36. RADIAL GLIAL CELL (Apical or Ventricular Radial Glia) (The Ladder-and-Loom Founder Cell) (NON-COG)

Before there is a cortex there is a wall one cell thick, and stretched across that wall, from the fluid-filled ventricle to the outer skin of the brain, stands a single impossibly elongated cell that is at once the bricklayer, the brick factory, and the scaffold the bricks climb.

Nearly every neuron you will ever own, and most of the glia, is the descendant of one of these cells.

When its work is done it mostly vanishes, dismantling itself into the mature cells it spent the pregnancy creating.

Ladder names its scaffolding role: newborn neurons climb its long fibre outward to reach their layer.

Loom names its factory role: it weaves the cortex thread by thread, dividing again and again to spin out neurons in an inside-first order.

Founder marks that it is the ancestor cell from which almost the entire cortex, and the adult stem cells of Chapters 38 and 39, descend.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The radial glial cell is the principal neural stem and progenitor cell of the developing central nervous system (CNS), the brain and spinal cord, a cell whose body sits against the ventricle and whose single long fibre reaches all the way to the pial surface, the outer membrane of the brain.

It does two jobs at once: it divides to produce neurons and glia (directly, or indirectly through intermediate progenitors), and its fibre serves as the physical guide rail along which each newborn neuron migrates from the ventricle out to its final cortical layer.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The classic form is bipolar: a small apical process anchoring the cell body to the ventricular surface, and a long, thin, radially oriented basal fibre running unbroken to the pia.

The nucleus rides up and down the apical region in step with the cell cycle, a movement called interkinetic nuclear migration, rising to divide near the ventricle and sinking again, which is why the embryonic ventricular zone looks stacked and pseudostratified.

SIZE:

The cell body is roughly 8 to 12 micrometres across, a micrometre being a thousandth of a millimetre, but the basal fibre is the remarkable dimension: in the human fetal brain it can span several thousand micrometres, up to a few millimetres, from ventricle to pia, making this one of the longest single cells of the developing body.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

The founding population is the apical (ventricular) radial glia described here, contacting the ventricle.

From it arise the intermediate progenitor cells of the subventricular zone, and the outer (basal) radial glia of Chapter 37, and its late-stage descendants specialise into the persisting radial forms: Bergmann glia of the cerebellum, Müller cells of the retina, tanycytes of the hypothalamus, and the adult stem cells of Chapters 38 and 39.

WHERE IT LIVES:

The ventricular zone, the innermost germinal layer lining the ventricles and the central canal of the spinal cord, throughout the developing CNS.

Its body sits at one interface, the ventricular (apical) surface bathed in cerebrospinal fluid, and its fibre ends at a second interface, the pial (basal) surface, so the single cell physically bridges the two boundaries of the cortical wall.

NUMBER:
