There is no single adult count, because the population is transient and largely gone by term birth; at peak, in mid-gestation, the germinal zones hold on the order of hundreds of millions to a few billion progenitors that will generate the roughly 16 billion neocortical neurons of the mature brain.

Confidence on any single figure is low, because counts depend on gestational age and are extrapolated from small human samples and animal models.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Radial glia are non-spiking but not electrically silent: they rest at a relatively depolarised membrane potential, roughly minus 60 to minus 80 millivolts, and are coupled to their neighbours by gap junctions (direct pore-like connections between cells).

They show spontaneous and propagating intracellular calcium waves through the germinal zone, and this calcium activity is thought to help regulate the rate of cell division.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Radial glia do not form classical synapses, but they receive real signalling contacts: gamma-aminobutyric acid (GABA) and glutamate released non-synaptically by migrating and settling neurons act on radial glial receptors to slow proliferation, a feedback loop by which the growing cortex reports back to its factory.

They are extensively coupled to one another by gap junctions, and their endfeet contact the pial basement membrane and blood vessels.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that radial glia have no axon; their single long process is a basal (dendrite-like) scaffolding fibre, not an impulse-conducting axon.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Radial glia are receivers rather than broadcasters of chemical signals, responding to GABA, glutamate, and neurotrophic and morphogen cues; they are not known to release a classical neurotransmitter, so this aspect is best read as a receptor profile rather than an output.

MYELINATION:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that radial glia possess no axon to be insulated and exist chiefly before myelination of the brain begins.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Proliferation is expensive in biosynthesis rather than in electrical work: dividing radial glia rely heavily on aerobic glycolysis, consuming glucose and glutamine to build the nucleotides, lipids, and proteins of daughter cells, a metabolic style shared with other fast-dividing cells.

Their energy budget is dominated by copying the genome and building membranes, not by pumping ions after action potentials.

PLASTICITY:

Their defining plasticity is fate plasticity: a radial glial cell changes what it produces over developmental time, making deep-layer neurons first, then upper-layer neurons, then glia, in a programmed sequence, and it can switch between symmetric divisions (two stem daughters, expanding the pool) and asymmetric divisions (one stem daughter and one neuron, building the cortex).

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

The radial glial cell is largely self-supporting and is itself the support system for everything else, but its niche is maintained by the cerebrospinal fluid it drinks at the ventricle, by the pial basement membrane its endfoot grips, and by the embryonic vasculature and meningeal cells that supply signalling molecules such as retinoic acid.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove this cell and there is no brain: no neurons are born, no migration ladder exists, and the cortical wall never forms.

Its inside-first building order is why the cortex has layers at all, later-born neurons climbing past their elders to settle in the upper layers, so the very stratification that defines the neocortex is a signature this one cell leaves behind before it disappears.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The surprising capability here is division-of-labour in a single cell: one radial glial cell simultaneously computes its own fate decision (stem versus neuron) through asymmetric inheritance of proteins and organelles at the ventricular surface, and provides a mechanical highway, so that construction schedule and construction scaffold are the same object.

Its propagating calcium waves let a sheet of these cells coordinate their division rate as a tissue, a form of collective timing rather than individual firing.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Morphogen gradients (sonic hedgehog, fibroblast growth factors, Wnt, retinoic acid); Notch signalling from neighbours; GABA and glutamate feedback from young neurons; cerebrospinal fluid growth factors.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ New neurons and intermediate progenitors and glia; a physical radial fibre used as a migration guide; endfoot contact onto the pial basement membrane.

• STATE.

◦ Cell-cycle phase, nuclear position (interkinetic migration), symmetric-versus-asymmetric division mode, competence window (which layer or cell type it is currently making).

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Cell body 8 to 12 micrometres; basal fibre up to several millimetres; cell-cycle length lengthening from roughly 8 hours to more than 24 hours across neurogenesis; resting potential about minus 60 to minus 80 millivolts.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ One cell cycle, hours to more than a day; a single neuron's migration up the fibre, hours to days; the whole neurogenic programme, most of human gestation.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Disrupt the division mode and you get microcephaly (too small a brain, as in mutations of ASPM or WDR62) or megalencephaly; disrupt the fibre or its endfeet and migrating neurons stall, producing lissencephaly (smooth brain), periventricular heterotopia (neurons stranded by the ventricle), and cobblestone malformations.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

CODE SAMPLE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

A single cell spanning the entire thickness of the developing brain wall, dividing at the ventricle while serving as the climbing rope for the very neurons it makes, then all but vanishing by birth into the cortex it built.


CHAPTER 37. OUTER (BASAL) RADIAL GLIAL CELL (Outer Subventricular Zone Radial Glia) (The Untethered Amplifier Cell) (NON-COG)

Take the founder cell of the previous chapter, cut its anchor to the ventricle, and let it drift outward into a new germinal layer of its own, and you have the cell that may explain why the human brain is so much larger and more folded than a mouse's.

It kept the long fibre and the power to divide, but it gave up its place at the ventricular wall to set up a second, enormous factory floor further out.

In that outer zone it multiplies far more freely than its parent, and in doing so it inflates the sheet of the cortex until the skull can only hold it by folding it.

Untethered names its defining trait: unlike the apical radial glia, it has no contact with the ventricle.

Amplifier names its function: it expands the progenitor pool, multiplying the number of neurons the cortex can make.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The outer radial glial cell, also called basal radial glia, is a progenitor of the developing cortex that lives not in the ventricular zone but in the outer subventricular zone (OSVZ), a germinal layer that is thin in rodents and massively expanded in humans and other primates.

It retains a long basal fibre reaching toward the pia but lacks the apical (ventricular) contact of its parent, and it divides repeatedly to amplify neuron production, which is one of the principal cellular explanations for the evolutionary expansion and folding (gyrification) of the human neocortex.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The commonest form is monopolar, carrying a single basal fibre toward the pia and no apical process, though human basal radial glia come in several morphotypes (basal-fibre-only, apical-fibre-only, and both).

Just before dividing it performs a characteristic hop called mitotic somal translocation, in which the cell body jumps rapidly outward along its own basal fibre and then divides.

SIZE:

Cell body roughly 10 to 15 micrometres; the basal fibre spans hundreds to a couple of thousand micrometres of the outer cortical wall, shorter than the ventricle-to-pia span of the apical founder but still long.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Human basal radial glia are transcriptionally and morphologically diverse, defined by the marker HOPX and enriched genes such as PTPRZ1, FAM107A, TNC, and LIFR; morphotypes include basal-process-bearing, apical-process-bearing, and bipolar forms with differing fate tendencies.

WHERE IT LIVES:

The outer subventricular zone of the developing cerebral cortex, above the inner subventricular zone and the ventricular zone.

In human fetal cortex the OSVZ becomes the thickest and most productive germinal compartment, a striking contrast with the mouse, where basal radial glia are sparse.

NUMBER:

No adult count exists because the cell is transient; the point is relative abundance, and in mid-gestation human OSVZ the basal radial glia and their intermediate-progenitor offspring vastly outnumber ventricular-zone progenitors, a difference of degree from rodents that is thought to translate into the difference in cortical size.

Confidence is moderate on the qualitative expansion, low on any exact figure.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Like apical radial glia, they are non-spiking, sitting at a relatively depolarised resting potential (roughly minus 60 to minus 80 millivolts), with calcium signalling and gap-junction coupling; they are not electrically excitable in the neuronal sense.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

No classical synapses; they signal through gap junctions and through an unusually rich self-made extracellular matrix, secreting matrix and growth factors (for example through LIFR-mediated signalling) that act back on the OSVZ niche to sustain self-renewal.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that the outer radial glial cell has no axon; its basal fibre is a scaffolding process, not an impulse-conducting axon.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Not a transmitter-releasing cell; its signalling output is chiefly extracellular matrix proteins and growth factors that potentiate self-renewal, so this aspect is not applicable in the neurotransmitter sense.

MYELINATION:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that it has no axon and acts before myelination of the cortex begins.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

As a fast-proliferating cell it leans on aerobic glycolysis and glutamine metabolism to build daughter cells, its budget dominated by biosynthesis rather than by electrical pumping.

PLASTICITY:

Its key plasticity is self-renewal amplification: the OSVZ niche it helps build lets it undergo many rounds of proliferative division before differentiating, and it too shifts fate output over time, contributing both upper-layer neurons and, later, glia.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

It is unusually self-supporting, manufacturing its own extracellular-matrix niche; beyond that it depends on the developing vasculature of the OSVZ and on signals relayed from the apical radial glia and intermediate progenitors around it.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

This cell colours the human cortex by sheer multiplication: the expanded OSVZ it populates is why the human cortical sheet is so large that it must fold, and why upper cortical layers, the associative layers, are so richly populated in humans.

Its abundance, more than any new cell chemistry, is a leading cellular signature of what makes the human brain human.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The surprising trick is mitotic somal translocation combined with a self-built niche: the cell literally launches its own body outward along its fibre before dividing, and it secretes the very matrix scaffold that keeps its daughters proliferative, so it both climbs and builds the ladder it climbs, generating an exponential rather than linear expansion of the progenitor pool.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Growth factors and morphogens (fibroblast growth factors, sonic hedgehog); self-secreted extracellular matrix (tenascin C, proteoglycans); LIFR and integrin signalling; Notch from neighbours.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Amplified neuron and glia production; a basal migration fibre; extracellular-matrix and growth-factor enrichment of the OSVZ niche.

• STATE.

◦ Cell-cycle phase, morphotype (basal, apical, bipolar), position within OSVZ, self-renewal versus differentiation bias.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Cell body 10 to 15 micrometres; basal fibre hundreds to about 2,000 micrometres; markers HOPX, PTPRZ1, FAM107A, TNC, LIFR; resting potential about minus 60 to minus 80 millivolts.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Cell cycle of many hours; mitotic somal translocation over minutes before division; OSVZ expansion over the second trimester of human gestation.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Under-amplification contributes to microcephaly and reduced gyrification; disordered OSVZ proliferation is linked to malformations of cortical development and is modelled in human cerebral organoids to study these disorders.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

CODE SAMPLE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

A radial-glia-like progenitor with a long fibre but no ventricular anchor, marked by HOPX, dividing after a somal hop in a germinal zone that is a thin sliver in mice and a vast productive floor in humans, whose abundance is a leading explanation for the size and folds of our cortex.


CHAPTER 38. ADULT NEURAL STEM CELL OF THE VENTRICULAR-SUBVENTRICULAR ZONE, THE B1 CELL (Astrocytic Ventricle-Wall Stem Cell) (The Sleeping Reserve Cell) (NON-COG)

Along the walls of the lateral ventricles, hidden among the beating cilia of the ependymal lining, a small number of astrocyte-like cells hold the last embers of the great germinal fire of development.

In a laboratory mouse these cells run a lifelong assembly line, sending new neurons on a long march to the nose to serve smell.

In an adult human the same cells are there, descended from the very radial glia of Chapter 36, but the assembly line has gone quiet, its output dwindling to near nothing within the first year or two of life.

Sleeping names their state: in the adult human they are overwhelmingly quiescent, dormant rather than dividing.

Reserve names their standing: they are a retained but largely dormant stock, and the central hope of regenerative neuroscience is to reawaken them.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The B1 cell is the adult neural stem cell of the ventricular-subventricular zone (V-SVZ), the germinal niche lining the walls of the lateral ventricles, a slow-dividing astrocyte-like cell that is the direct descendant of embryonic radial glia and retains, in principle, the ability to make new neurons and glia.

In many mammals it feeds a lifelong rostral migratory stream of new neurons to the olfactory bulb, but in humans this neurogenic output collapses after infancy, so the adult human B1 cell functions chiefly as a largely quiescent reserve.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The B1 cell has an astrocytic body in the subventricular zone with a thin apical process that squeezes between ependymal cells to touch the ventricle and cerebrospinal fluid, often bearing a single primary cilium, and a basal process contacting local blood vessels.

This arrangement, sampling both the cerebrospinal fluid and the blood, is a direct inheritance of the radial glial body plan.

SIZE:

Cell body on the order of 10 micrometres, with a small apical contact at the ventricle and processes extending tens of micrometres to reach vessels and the ependymal surface.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

The lineage runs B1 stem cell to type C transit-amplifying cell to type A neuroblast (covered in Chapter 40); B1 cells themselves are regionally specialised around the ventricle, and human work distinguishes largely quiescent from rarely activated states, with markers such as glial fibrillary acidic protein (GFAP), SOX2, and NESTIN and quiescence set in part by pathways including SFRP1.

WHERE IT LIVES:

The subventricular zone of the lateral ventricle walls, beneath the ependymal cell layer, forming a pinwheel arrangement in which B1 apical endings are ringed by ependymal cells at the ventricular surface.

Its apical process sits at the cerebrospinal-fluid interface and its basal endfoot at the vascular interface, so like its radial-glia ancestor it bridges two boundaries.

NUMBER:

There is no confident adult human figure; the niche is a thin ribbon along the ventricle holding on the order of thousands to a few tens of thousands of stem-competent cells, and human studies (Sanai and colleagues, 2011) show that after roughly 18 months of age proliferation and the migratory corridor to the olfactory bulb become almost extinct.

Confidence is low on any count and moderate-to-high on the qualitative decline.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Like other astrocytic cells, B1 cells are non-spiking and rest at a hyperpolarised potential near minus 80 millivolts, dominated by potassium permeability, with calcium signalling and gap-junction coupling; they are not electrically excitable.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

They do not form classical synapses but receive strong niche signals: gamma-aminobutyric acid (GABA) released by neighbouring neuroblasts feeds back to keep B1 cells quiescent, dopaminergic afferents and cerebrospinal-fluid-borne factors modulate them, and they are coupled by gap junctions within the niche.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that the B1 cell has no axon; its processes are astrocytic and scaffolding, not impulse-conducting.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

The B1 cell is chiefly a receiver of GABA, dopamine, and cerebrospinal-fluid signals rather than a transmitter releaser, so this aspect is best read as its receptor and niche-signal profile rather than an output.

MYELINATION:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that it has no axon to insulate.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

In quiescence the B1 cell is metabolically frugal, relying on fatty-acid oxidation and low glycolytic flux; on the rare occasions it activates it switches toward glycolysis to fund division, a metabolic gearshift now viewed as part of the quiescence-to-activation switch.

PLASTICITY:

Its central property is reversible quiescence: it can, at least in principle, re-enter the cell cycle in response to injury or signalling, and the ambition of the field is to release the brakes (such as SFRP1) that hold the adult human cell dormant, so as to coax new neuron production from this reserve.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

It is supported by the ependymal cells that surround its apical process and stir the cerebrospinal fluid, by the local vasculature it grips, and by the choroid plexus, whose cerebrospinal-fluid factors bathe the niche.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

In a rodent this cell colours the brain by continuously renewing the olfactory bulb; in an adult human its most striking colour is its silence, a niche kept structurally intact but functionally idled, so the human ventricle wall reads as a preserved factory with the lights off.

Its retained but dormant presence is exactly why it is such a tantalising target for repair.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The surprising capability is deep, reversible dormancy: the B1 cell can sit for years in a quiescent state and yet retain full stem competence, a biological pause button, and the whole enterprise of regenerative neuroscience turns on whether that button can be released in the adult human brain.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ GABA from neuroblasts (quiescence signal); dopaminergic input; cerebrospinal-fluid growth factors; SFRP1 and other Wnt-modulating signals; vascular signals.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ In humans, minimal after infancy: rare transit-amplifying cells and neuroblasts; in other mammals, a steady stream of olfactory-bulb neurons and some oligodendrocytes.

• STATE.

◦ Quiescent versus activated; cell-cycle phase; position along the ventricle wall.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Cell body about 10 micrometres; resting potential near minus 80 millivolts; markers GFAP, SOX2, NESTIN; human neurogenic output near-zero after roughly 18 months of age.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Quiescence lasting months to years; a single activation-and-division cycle over days; the human developmental decline over the first 18 months of life.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Failure of the niche is implicated in the age-related decline of the human olfactory and subventricular germinal capacity; aberrant reactivation of these cells is one proposed cell-of-origin for certain gliomas (brain tumours), so both too little and too much activity are pathological.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

CODE SAMPLE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

An astrocyte-like stem cell that pokes a single ciliated process through the ependymal lining to taste the cerebrospinal fluid, a direct descendant of embryonic radial glia, running a busy neuron factory in a mouse and a near-silent, dormant reserve in the adult human.


CHAPTER 39. ADULT HIPPOCAMPAL RADIAL GLIA-LIKE CELL, THE TYPE 1 CELL (Subgranular Zone Stem Cell) (The Contested Memory-Seed Cell) (COG)

At the inner edge of the dentate gyrus, the hook-shaped entrance gate of the hippocampus, sits a rare radial-glia-like cell that may or may not be quietly seeding the adult brain with brand-new neurons.

In rodents its role is not in doubt: it makes young granule neurons throughout life, and those excitable newcomers sharpen the memory system's ability to keep similar experiences apart.

In humans the very existence of this ongoing process is one of the sharpest controversies in modern neuroscience, with careful laboratories reading the same tissue and reaching opposite conclusions.

Contested is placed first on purpose: this cell earns its (COG) tag only if adult human hippocampal neurogenesis is real, and the honest verdict is not yet in.

Memory-seed names its putative gift: each new granule neuron is a fresh seed planted into the circuit that tells memories apart.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The Type 1 cell is the radial-glia-like stem cell of the subgranular zone (SGZ), the thin germinal layer at the base of the dentate gyrus granule cell layer, a quiescent astrocyte-like cell with a radial process climbing through the granule layer, and it is the proposed source of adult-born dentate granule neurons.

To the degree that adult human hippocampal neurogenesis persists, this cell feeds new, transiently hyper-excitable young granule neurons into the dentate gyrus, where they are thought to enhance pattern separation, the memory operation that keeps similar experiences from blurring together, which is why the catalogue assigns it a cautious cognitive (COG) tag.

The contested-evidence status is treated squarely under PLASTICITY and FAILURE MODE below.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The Type 1 cell has a small triangular body in the subgranular zone, a single radial process that crosses the granule cell layer and branches into the inner molecular layer, and shorter horizontal processes; it expresses both the astrocytic marker glial fibrillary acidic protein (GFAP) and the stem markers NESTIN and SOX2.

Morphologically it is a near-copy of the developmental radial glial cell, miniaturised and retained in the adult dentate.

SIZE:

Cell body roughly 10 micrometres, with a radial process spanning the tens of micrometres of the granule cell layer thickness into the molecular layer.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

The dentate lineage runs Type 1 radial stem cell to Type 2 intermediate progenitor to Type 3 neuroblast to immature and then mature granule neuron (the amplifying stages are covered in Chapter 40); radial (Type 1) and non-radial stem-cell variants are distinguished in rodents.

WHERE IT LIVES:

The subgranular zone of the dentate gyrus of the hippocampus, at the border between the hilus and the granule cell layer, in both hemispheres.

Its radial process crosses an interface, the granule cell layer, positioning its output cells exactly where new granule neurons must integrate.

NUMBER:

No confident adult human figure exists, and this is the heart of the dispute; the subgranular zone is a thin band holding at most thousands of candidate stem cells per dentate gyrus, and estimates of their output diverge wildly between studies (see PLASTICITY).

Confidence is low, deliberately so.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

The Type 1 stem cell itself is non-spiking, resting near minus 80 millivolts with astrocytic potassium-dominated membrane properties and passive current profiles.

Its cognitive relevance, however, lies in its progeny: adult-born granule neurons pass through a window, around 4 to 6 weeks of cell age in rodents, of low firing threshold, high input resistance, and enhanced synaptic plasticity, making them far more excitable and more plastic than mature granule cells.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

The Type 1 stem cell forms no classical synapses but is regulated by its niche: gamma-aminobutyric acid (GABA) from local interneurons keeps it quiescent and later depolarises its immature progeny, and it responds to neurotransmitters, growth factors, and activity-dependent signals.

Its progeny wire into the classic dentate circuit, receiving glutamatergic entorhinal input through the perforant path and projecting mossy fibres to hippocampal area CA3.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that the Type 1 stem cell has no axon; only its differentiated granule-neuron progeny grow a mossy-fibre axon to CA3.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

The stem cell is a receiver, chiefly of GABA (a quiescence and later depolarising signal) and of neuromodulators such as serotonin; its granule-neuron progeny are glutamatergic, releasing the excitatory transmitter glutamate onto CA3.

MYELINATION:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that the stem cell has no axon; the mossy-fibre axons of its mature progeny are largely unmyelinated.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

In quiescence the cell is frugal, favouring fatty-acid oxidation; activation and the subsequent burst of neuron production shift metabolism toward glycolysis, and adult hippocampal neurogenesis is notably sensitive to systemic metabolic state, being boosted by exercise and caloric factors and suppressed by chronic stress and inflammation.

PLASTICITY:

This is where the honesty clause of the catalogue applies in full.

In rodents, adult hippocampal neurogenesis is robust, experience- and activity-dependent, upregulated by exercise and enriched environments and downregulated by stress, and functionally linked to pattern separation and to the antidepressant response.

In humans the picture is genuinely contested: Sorrells and colleagues (2018) found doublecortin-positive young neurons dropping to undetectable levels by adolescence, arguing adult human hippocampal neurogenesis is essentially absent, while Boldrini and colleagues (2018) and Moreno-Jimenez and colleagues (2019), published within the same window, reported new neurons persisting into old age.

Much of the disagreement appears to be technical, especially tissue fixation: Moreno-Jimenez and colleagues showed that long post-mortem fixation destroys the doublecortin signal, which could explain negative results, though this has not fully settled the debate.

The plasticity of this cell, in short, is real in rodents and unresolved in humans, and this catalogue tags it (COG) provisionally rather than confidently.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

It is supported by dentate astrocytes that secrete pro-neurogenic factors, by the local vasculature of the neurogenic niche (neurogenesis is coupled to angiogenesis here), by microglia that clear the many progeny that die before maturing, and by GABAergic interneurons that gate its activity.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

If the process is real in humans, this cell colours the dentate gyrus by continually refreshing it with young, plastic neurons, giving the memory-encoding gate a rolling supply of blank slates and, with them, a temporal tag that could help date memories and separate similar ones.

If it is not appreciably active in adult humans, then the adult human dentate is coloured instead by its stability, and the cognitive contribution described here belongs mainly to early life and to other species.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The remarkable computational idea is that adding a few brand-new, low-threshold, highly plastic neurons to a large fixed population does not merely add capacity, it changes the code: young granule cells are easily recruited and rapidly wired, so overlapping input patterns get pushed onto more distinct sets of active neurons, sharpening pattern separation, and it is this effect that the code below models.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ GABA from interneurons (quiescence and depolarisation); serotonin and other neuromodulators; growth factors (brain-derived neurotrophic factor); systemic signals from exercise, stress, and inflammation; entorhinal glutamatergic drive onto progeny.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ New dentate granule neurons (if active), projecting mossy fibres to CA3; a rolling supply of transiently hyper-excitable young neurons.

• STATE.

◦ Quiescent versus activated stem state; cell-age of each young neuron (0 to about 8 weeks in rodents); niche activity level.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Stem cell body about 10 micrometres; resting potential near minus 80 millivolts; young-neuron critical window roughly 4 to 6 weeks (rodent); human output uncertain, plausibly near-zero to modest in adulthood.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Quiescence over weeks to months; maturation of a new granule neuron over several weeks; the disputed human developmental decline over childhood.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Reduced hippocampal neurogenesis is associated in rodents and by inference in humans with impaired pattern separation, major depression, and cognitive ageing, and is disrupted in temporal-lobe epilepsy (where new neurons mis-migrate) and in Alzheimer's disease; the overarching failure mode in humans, uniquely for this catalogue, is that we do not yet know how much of this cell's function survives into adult human life.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to show how adding a small number of new, low-threshold, highly plastic young granule neurons improves the dentate gyrus's ability to separate two similar input patterns into more distinct output patterns.
Create a number variable called number of granule cells with initial value 500.
Create a number variable called number of active inputs with initial value 50.
Create a number variable called mature threshold with initial value 1.0.
Create a number variable called young threshold with initial value 0.4.
Create a number variable called fraction young with initial value 0.05.
Create a list variable called input pattern A by choosing number of active inputs entorhinal fibres at random.
Create a list variable called input pattern B by copying input pattern A and then swapping ten percent of its active fibres for different fibres, so that A and B are similar but not identical.
Create a procedure called compute output that takes one input pattern and does the following actions.
    Create an empty list called active granule cells.
    For each granule cell in number of granule cells, perform the following actions.
        Calculate the total synaptic drive that this granule cell receives from the active fibres of the input pattern.
        If this granule cell is one of the young cells, then set its threshold to young threshold, otherwise set its threshold to mature threshold.
        If the total synaptic drive is greater than the cell's threshold is true, then add this granule cell to active granule cells.
        End the conditional block.
    End the loop.
    Return active granule cells.
End the procedure.
Set output A to the result of compute output on input pattern A.
Set output B to the result of compute output on input pattern B.
Calculate the overlap of output A and output B as the number of granule cells active in both, divided by the number active in either.
Display the overlap for the case with young cells and for a comparison case where fraction young is zero.
A lower overlap means better pattern separation.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because the claim is quantitative and small - a few hundred model neurons and one overlap number - and Python with a single numerical library shows the pattern-separation effect directly and reproducibly.

# Adult-born young granule cells and dentate pattern separation.
# Model: new neurons are few but have a LOW firing threshold (high excitability),
# so they are recruited readily and de-correlate similar entorhinal inputs.
# Concept per Sahay et al., Nature 2011 (neurogenesis improves pattern separation);
# human persistence of this process remains contested (Sorrells 2018 vs Boldrini 2018,
# Moreno-Jimenez 2019). This is a didactic model, not a fit to human data.
import numpy as np

rng = np.random.default_rng(0)

N_GC = 500          # dentate granule cells (output layer)
N_EC = 400          # entorhinal input fibres
N_ACTIVE = 50       # active input fibres per pattern
MATURE_THR = 1.0    # firing threshold of a mature granule cell
YOUNG_THR = 0.4     # LOW threshold of a young adult-born cell (more excitable)

# Fixed random perforant-path weight matrix (granule cells x input fibres).
W = (rng.random((N_GC, N_EC)) < 0.1).astype(float)

def make_similar_patterns(overlap_frac=0.9):
    a = np.zeros(N_EC); idx = rng.choice(N_EC, N_ACTIVE, replace=False); a[idx] = 1.0
    b = a.copy()
    n_swap = int(N_ACTIVE * (1 - overlap_frac))
    on = np.where(b == 1)[0]; off = np.where(b == 0)[0]
    b[rng.choice(on, n_swap, replace=False)] = 0.0
    b[rng.choice(off, n_swap, replace=False)] = 1.0
    return a, b

def output(pattern, frac_young):
    n_young = int(N_GC * frac_young)
    thr = np.full(N_GC, MATURE_THR)
    thr[:n_young] = YOUNG_THR          # first n_young cells are young/excitable
    drive = W @ pattern
    return (drive > thr).astype(float)

def overlap(x, y):                     # Jaccard overlap of active sets
    inter = np.sum((x == 1) & (y == 1))
    union = np.sum((x == 1) | (y == 1))
    return inter / union if union else 0.0

a, b = make_similar_patterns(overlap_frac=0.9)   # inputs 90% overlapping
for frac_young in (0.0, 0.05, 0.10):
    oa, ob = output(a, frac_young), output(b, frac_young)
    print(f"young fraction {frac_young:>4.2f}  output overlap {overlap(oa, ob):.3f}")
# Typical run: output overlap FALLS as the young fraction rises,
# i.e. adding a few low-threshold new neurons sharpens pattern separation.

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

A miniature radial-glia-like stem cell wedged at the base of the dentate granule layer, expressing GFAP and NESTIN together, whose putative product - a trickle of hyper-excitable young granule neurons - is the single most fiercely contested cell function in the adult human brain.


CHAPTER 40. TRANSIT-AMPLIFYING PROGENITOR AND NEUROBLAST POPULATIONS (Type C Cells and Type A Neuroblasts) (The Assembly-Line Middle Cells) (NON-COG)

Between the patient stem cell and the finished neuron lies a short, frantic middle stage: cells whose whole existence is to divide fast and move.

They are the conveyor belt of the neuron factory, amplifying one rare stem-cell division into many neurons, then packing up and migrating to wherever the new neurons are needed.

None of them lasts long, and none of them is a permanent resident of the adult brain; they are process, not place.

Assembly-line names their function: they multiply the output of a single slow stem cell into a batch of neurons.

Middle names their position: they sit strictly between stem cell and neuron, belonging fully to neither.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

This chapter covers the intermediate dividing populations of the germinal niches: the transit-amplifying cells (called type C cells in the ventricular-subventricular zone and type 2 cells in the hippocampal subgranular zone) and the neuroblasts (type A cells), the migratory immature neurons they produce.

Their job is amplification and delivery: a transit-amplifying cell divides several times quickly to multiply the number of neurons made from each stem-cell activation, and the neuroblast then migrates, in chains through the rostral migratory stream toward the olfactory bulb or a short hop into the dentate granule layer, before maturing into a neuron.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

Transit-amplifying cells are small, rounded, rapidly cycling cells with little cytoplasm; neuroblasts are elongated, migratory cells with a leading process and a trailing process, often travelling in tangential chains ensheathed by astrocytic tubes.

SIZE:

Both are small, roughly 6 to 10 micrometres in cell-body diameter, smaller than mature neurons, with neuroblasts extending a leading process of tens of micrometres during migration.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Two functional stages: the transit-amplifying progenitor (type C in the V-SVZ, type 2 in the SGZ), marked by transcription factors such as ASCL1 (MASH1); and the neuroblast (type A in the V-SVZ, type 3 in the SGZ), marked by doublecortin (DCX) and polysialylated neural cell adhesion molecule (PSA-NCAM), the very markers at the centre of the human-neurogenesis debate of Chapter 39.

WHERE IT LIVES:

The germinal niches and their migratory routes: the ventricular-subventricular zone and the rostral migratory stream leading to the olfactory bulb, and the subgranular zone of the dentate gyrus.

Neuroblasts by definition traverse an interface, migrating from germinal zone to target territory.

NUMBER:

Transient and unstable, so no standing adult count is meaningful; in the human these populations are abundant in the infant brain and dwindle to scarcity in adulthood as neurogenesis declines, consistent with the near-extinction of the human rostral migratory stream after roughly 18 months of age.

Confidence low on any figure.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Transit-amplifying cells are non-spiking, resting near minus 80 millivolts.

Neuroblasts are electrically immature: they express gamma-aminobutyric acid (GABA) and glutamate receptors and respond to GABA with depolarisation (because of their high internal chloride), a signal that guides migration and early wiring, but they do not yet fire mature action potentials until they differentiate.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

They form no classical output synapses, but neuroblasts receive non-synaptic GABA signalling that regulates migration speed, and both stages are embedded in niche signalling by growth factors and cell-adhesion molecules.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that these are dividing and migrating precursors that have not yet grown a mature axon.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Receivers rather than releasers: they respond to GABA (depolarising and promigratory at this stage) and to glutamate, and do not yet release a classical transmitter, so this aspect is a receptor profile rather than an output.

MYELINATION:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that these precursors have no axon to insulate.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

As fast-dividing (transit-amplifying) and actively migrating (neuroblast) cells, they lean on glycolysis for rapid biosynthesis and for the energy of movement, a high-throughput but short-lived metabolic profile.

PLASTICITY:

Their plasticity is developmental and fleeting: the transit-amplifying cell's division number sets the batch size of neurons produced, and the neuroblast's migration and survival are heavily activity- and experience-regulated, with most neuroblasts dying before maturing unless recruited by circuit activity.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

They are supported and guided by the astrocytes that form the glial tubes of the rostral migratory stream, by the blood vessels they migrate along, by the B1 and Type 1 stem cells upstream, and by microglia that clear the large fraction that die.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

These cells colour their niches with motion: in an infant the subventricular zone and rostral migratory stream stream with chains of neuroblasts, a visible river of migrating young cells, and the fading of that river is one of the clearest cellular signatures of the human brain's transition from a building phase to a maintenance phase.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The notable capability is chain migration: neuroblasts move as coordinated trains, each gliding along its neighbours through astrocytic tunnels for remarkable distances (the length of the rostral migratory stream), a collective mode of travel unlike anything in the settled adult circuit, again driven by GABA and adhesion rather than by firing.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Stem-cell activation upstream; growth factors (epidermal and fibroblast growth factors); GABA (migration signal); adhesion cues (PSA-NCAM); vascular and glial-tube guidance.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Amplified batches of immature neurons; migratory delivery of neuroblasts to the olfactory bulb (in species with an active stream) or dentate granule layer.

• STATE.

◦ Division count (transit-amplifying stage); migratory position and speed (neuroblast stage); survival versus programmed death.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Cell body 6 to 10 micrometres; markers ASCL1, DCX, PSA-NCAM; resting potential near minus 80 millivolts; several amplifying divisions per stem activation.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Transit-amplifying divisions over hours; neuroblast migration over days; the whole stem-to-neuron transit over one to several weeks; the human developmental decline over the first roughly 18 months of life.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ These being process rather than resident cells, their pathology is developmental and oncological: disordered amplification and migration contribute to malformations of cortical development, and dysregulated transit-amplifying-like cells are implicated as a proliferative compartment in gliomas; in temporal-lobe epilepsy, dentate neuroblasts migrate aberrantly.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

CODE SAMPLE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

Small, fast-dividing cells and their doublecortin-bearing migratory offspring that exist only in transit between stem cell and neuron, streaming in chains through the infant brain and dwindling toward silence in the adult - process rather than place, and never a permanent resident.


PART FIVE. THE VASCULAR, BARRIER, AND MENINGEAL CELLS

The brain is among the most thirstily vascularised organs in the body, threaded by roughly 600 kilometres of capillary so fine that a red blood cell must fold to pass, and no neuron sits more than about twenty-five micrometres from a vessel.

Yet this plumbing is unlike any other, because nowhere else in the body does the vasculature build a wall this selective: the brain deliberately walls its own blood out, admitting fuel and dismissing almost everything else.

The cells of this Part are the masons, muscle, and mortar of that wall, together with the connective-tissue cells that suspend the brain in fluid and drain its waste.

They are gathered under the name neurovascular unit, the working ensemble of endothelium, pericyte, smooth muscle, astrocyte end-foot, and neuron that behaves as one organ for the delivery of blood.

None of them computes, and so every chapter here is tagged (NON-COG); but delete any one and cognition fails by flood, famine, or poisoning rather than by faulty arithmetic.

They are the substrate that makes the barrier, moves the blood, and keeps the fluid clean, and the modern evidence is that they fail early and quietly in the diseases of the ageing brain.


CHAPTER 41. BRAIN MICROVASCULAR ENDOTHELIAL CELL (Continuous Blood-Brain Barrier Endothelium) (The Gatekeeper Wall Cell) (NON-COG)

Lay one finger on the inside of a brain capillary and you would be touching the tightest sheet of cells in the human body, a living wall so sealed that it turns the bloodstream itself into an outsider.

Every other capillary in the body leaks a little on purpose; this one does not, and that refusal is what lets a neuron fire in a chemically private room.

The cell that builds this wall is not exotic to look at - it is a flat endothelial cell, the same lineage that lines every vessel - but it has been drilled into a fortress by the brain around it.

It is the physical blood-brain barrier, and almost every drug that fails to reach the brain fails here.

Gatekeeper names its function: it decides, molecule by molecule, what crosses from blood to brain.

Wall names its shape: a continuous, unbroken, windowless sheet.

Cell marks it as the endothelial lining cell of the smallest brain vessels.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The brain microvascular endothelial cell is the single flattened cell that lines the inside of every brain capillary, wrapped edge to edge with its neighbours and sealed to them by continuous tight junctions.

Unlike endothelium elsewhere, it has almost no fenestrations (the windows that let peripheral capillaries leak) and it holds its vesicle traffic (transcytosis, the ferrying of cargo through the cell in bubbles) to a near-standstill, so that essentially nothing crosses except by way of a specific transporter it chooses to build.

It is therefore not merely a pipe but a checkpoint: it is the blood-brain barrier made flesh, and it is the wall that the parent framework's Layer-Skipping Channel 1 (the steroid channel, a hormone signal that crosses many layers of organisation in one physical step) dissolves straight through because steroids are fatty enough to slip across the membrane, while Channel 3 (the circumventricular ports) exists precisely because a few brain regions deliberately omit this cell's seal.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

An extremely thin, elongated, tile-like cell, its cytoplasm drawn out to as little as a fifth of a micrometre thick over most of its area (a micrometre being a thousandth of a millimetre), curved into a tube perhaps eight micrometres across.

Its defining feature is invisible without a marker: a continuous belt of tight junction where its membrane fuses with the next cell's, built from the proteins claudin-5, occludin, and claudins-3 and -12, all pinned to the internal skeleton by the scaffolding proteins zonula occludens 1, 2, and 3.

It sits on a basement membrane (a felt of collagen type four, laminin, and proteoglycan) that it shares with the pericyte, and its outer face is tiled by the end-feet of astrocytes.

SIZE:

Roughly 20 to 40 micrometres long and 8 to 12 micrometres wide as a flattened tile, but only about 0.2 to 0.5 micrometres thick across the vessel wall, thinner than the red cell it carries.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Not a family of named types so much as a gradient along the vascular tree: arterial, capillary, and venous brain endothelium differ in gene expression, with the tightest barrier and the densest transporter set at the capillary segment.

Recent single-cell atlases add a distinct venous population that carries the adhesion molecules used to admit immune cells, so the barrier is leakiest, by design, on the venous side.

WHERE IT LIVES:

The inner lining of every capillary, arteriole, and venule of the brain and spinal cord, wherever the barrier is required - which is almost everywhere.

The pointed exceptions are the circumventricular organs (a handful of midline sites such as the area postrema and median eminence) where this cell is replaced by a fenestrated, leaky endothelium so that the brain can sample or dump hormones into the blood.

NUMBER:

On the order of several hundred billion endothelial cells across the brain's roughly 600 kilometres of microvessel, though estimates are soft; a defensible central figure is a few hundred billion, low to moderate confidence, dwarfing the neuron count because each cell covers only a short tube.

The barrier surface they build is enormous, roughly twelve to twenty square metres, close to the area of the skin.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Non-excitable but not silent: a resting membrane potential near minus 30 to minus 60 millivolts, set largely by potassium and chloride channels, with rich internal calcium signalling.

The barrier's electrical signature is its transendothelial electrical resistance, a measure of how hard it is for ions to leak between the cells, which in a healthy brain capillary reaches roughly one to eight thousand ohm square centimetres, one to two orders of magnitude above ordinary endothelium.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

This section is reinterpreted as junctional relationships rather than synapses, of which this cell has many: tight junctions and adherens junctions (via VE-cadherin) to neighbouring endothelium, gap junctions for direct ionic coupling, and peg-and-socket contacts to the pericyte sharing its basement membrane.

It also holds a two-way chemical conversation with astrocyte end-feet and neurons, which instruct it to build and keep the barrier.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that an endothelial cell has no axon, being an epithelial-type barrier cell rather than a projecting neuron.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Reinterpreted as signalling molecules: it releases nitric oxide (a gaseous vasodilator), endothelin-1 (a potent vasoconstrictor), prostacyclin, and it displays receptors for the astrocyte- and neuron-derived signals that regulate barrier tone.

Its most important received signal is Wnt/beta-catenin from the surrounding neural tissue, the master instruction that tells this endothelium, and no other, to become a barrier.

MYELINATION:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that myelin insulates axons for fast conduction, and this cell neither has an axon nor conducts impulses.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Modest in bulk but relentless in specialisation: it runs a dense fleet of transporters, chiefly glucose transporter 1 (GLUT1, encoded by SLC2A1), which ferries the brain's entire glucose supply across the wall, and the efflux pump P-glycoprotein (encoded by ABCB1), which spends adenosine triphosphate (the cell's energy currency) to fling foreign molecules and drugs back into the blood.

A GLUT1 shortfall in this cell causes GLUT1 deficiency syndrome, in which the brain is starved of glucose behind an intact barrier.

PLASTICITY:

The barrier is inducible and reversible, not fixed: it is switched on during development by neural Wnt signals and by contact with pericytes and astrocytes, it can be opened transiently by inflammation or by focused ultrasound for drug delivery, and it degrades measurably with age and in disease.

Its tight junctions remodel over minutes to hours in response to signals such as vascular endothelial growth factor, which loosens them.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Reinterpreted as the cells that support this cell: the pericyte, which shares its basement membrane and induces and maintains the barrier (Chapter 42); the astrocyte, whose end-feet tile its outer surface and supply barrier-maintaining signals; and the neurons that complete the neurovascular unit.

Remove pericyte or astrocyte contact and this cell's tight junctions loosen and its transcytosis rises, so the barrier is a committee product, not a solo act.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Delete this cell and the brain drowns in its own blood chemistry: plasma proteins, ions, and toxins flood the extracellular space, neurons lose the stable ionic bath their firing depends on, and the private chemistry of thought dissolves into the general chemistry of the body.

The barrier is why the brain can hold potassium and glutamate at concentrations that would be lethal if set by the blood.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Its most surprising capability is molecular sorting at scale: this one cell simultaneously runs receptor-mediated transcytosis to import chosen cargo (transferrin-bound iron, insulin, the LRP1 route for clearing amyloid-beta), influx carriers for glucose and amino acids, and a battery of efflux pumps that eject perhaps ninety substrates, effectively computing a whitelist and a blacklist for the entire brain at once.

It is a chemical customs house operating continuously along 600 kilometres of border.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Blood-side glucose 4 to 6 millimolar, oxygen, amino acids, hormones; abluminal Wnt and Sonic hedgehog signals from neural tissue; pericyte and astrocyte contact signals; inflammatory cytokines.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Regulated flux of glucose (via GLUT1) and amino acids into brain; efflux of xenobiotics to blood (via P-glycoprotein and BCRP); nitric oxide and endothelin to set vessel tone; controlled immune-cell adhesion on the venous side.

• STATE.

◦ Tight-junction integrity, transcytosis rate, transporter surface density, adhesion-molecule expression, intracellular calcium.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Wall thickness 0.2 to 0.5 micrometres; transendothelial resistance 1,000 to 8,000 ohm square centimetres; near-zero fenestration; resting potential minus 30 to minus 60 millivolts.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Tight-junction remodelling minutes to hours; transcytosis cycle seconds to minutes; barrier induction over developmental days to weeks; age-related decline over decades.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Barrier breakdown in multiple sclerosis, stroke, traumatic brain injury, and sepsis; chronic leak in Alzheimer's disease and small-vessel disease; GLUT1 deficiency syndrome from SLC2A1 loss; cerebral oedema when the wall fails and water follows.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

CODE SAMPLE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

A capillary lining cell with no windows and almost no vesicle traffic, sealed to its neighbours by claudin-5 into a wall so tight it reads at thousands of ohm square centimetres - the only endothelium in the body that treats the blood as something to be kept out.


CHAPTER 42. PERICYTE (Mural Cell of the Capillary) (The Vessel-Hugging Regulator Cell) (NON-COG)

Wrapped around the outside of the brain's finest capillaries, buried in the same thin sheath as the wall it guards, sits a solitary cell with long arms and a tight embrace.

It is the pericyte, and the brain packs more of them, per length of vessel, than any other organ - because here they are not optional.

The pericyte is the cell that talks the endothelium into becoming a barrier and then keeps it that way, and it can pinch the capillary to throttle blood flow one cell at a time.

When pericytes falter, the barrier leaks and the brain's smallest vessels stiffen and fail, which is now recognised as one of the earliest events in Alzheimer's disease.

Vessel-hugging names its posture: it clasps the capillary from outside, its processes running along and around the tube.

Regulator names its job: it tunes barrier tightness and capillary diameter.

Cell marks it as a mural cell, one of the muscle-lineage cells of the vessel wall.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The pericyte is a contractile mural cell (a cell of the vessel wall) that sits on the outside of a capillary, embedded within the same basement membrane as the endothelial cell it embraces, sharing direct peg-and-socket contacts with it.

It performs three headline jobs: it induces and maintains the blood-brain barrier by signalling to the endothelium, it regulates capillary diameter and therefore local blood flow by contracting and relaxing, and it stabilises the vessel and helps clear waste including amyloid-beta.

Its degeneration is an early and causal event in Alzheimer's disease and in cerebral small-vessel (white matter) disease.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

A cell with a prominent, bump-like cell body ("hump") from which long, thin processes reach out to wrap around and along the capillary, giving it an appearance often likened to an octopus gripping a hose.

It is defined not by shape alone but by position (inside the basement membrane, against the endothelium) and by markers: platelet-derived growth factor receptor beta (PDGFR-beta), the proteoglycan NG2, and, on the arteriole-adjacent "ensheathing" pericytes, some alpha-smooth-muscle actin (the contractile protein).

SIZE:

The cell body is roughly ten to twenty micrometres across, but its processes extend the cell to cover stretches of capillary tens of micrometres long, so a single pericyte may govern a considerable run of vessel.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

A morphological continuum along the vascular tree: "ensheathing" pericytes near arterioles wrap the vessel almost completely and are rich in contractile actin; "mesh" and "thin-strand" pericytes on the deeper capillary bed have sparser, stringier processes and less actin.

The contractile, actin-rich end grades into the true vascular smooth muscle cell of the arteriole (Chapter 43), so pericyte and smooth muscle are two ends of one lineage.

WHERE IT LIVES:

On the capillaries and smallest venules throughout the brain and spinal cord, always on the abluminal (brain-facing) side within the basement membrane.

It sits at the core interface of the neurovascular unit, between the endothelial wall on one side and the astrocyte end-feet on the other.

NUMBER:

The brain's pericyte coverage is the highest in the body, with an endothelial-cell-to-pericyte ratio estimated near one-to-one to three-to-one in the central nervous system (CNS) (versus perhaps a hundred-to-one in skeletal muscle).

Pericyte coverage of brain capillaries runs roughly seventy to eighty percent by the markers NG2 and PDGFR-beta, and pericyte density is on the order of two per millimetre of capillary length in healthy controls, moderate confidence.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

A non-spiking but contractile cell with a resting membrane potential in the region of minus forty to minus fifty millivolts, whose contraction is driven by voltage-gated and store-operated calcium entry rather than by action potentials.

Depolarisation opens calcium channels, calcium rises, and the cell's actin-myosin apparatus shortens, squeezing the capillary.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Reinterpreted as junctions: direct gap junctions and peg-and-socket adhesions to the endothelial cell, and close apposition to astrocyte end-feet, forming a signalling triad.

It receives no classical synapses but reads the same neurovascular signals (potassium, prostaglandins, nitric oxide, adenosine) that the active neuropil releases.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that the pericyte has no axon, being a contractile mural cell rather than a projecting neuron.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Reinterpreted as signalling molecules: it secretes and responds to platelet-derived growth factor beta (the endothelium releases PDGF-BB, the pericyte answers through PDGFR-beta), transforming growth factor beta, and angiopoietin/Tie2 signals, the molecular handshakes that build and hold the barrier.

It contracts in response to vasoactive mediators and relaxes to nitric oxide and adenosine.

MYELINATION:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that the pericyte neither has an axon nor conducts impulses, so insulation is irrelevant to it.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Moderate and contraction-linked: its steady expense is maintaining tone and running the PDGFR-beta signalling that keeps the endothelium sealed, with bursts of adenosine-triphosphate use when actin-myosin contracts the capillary.

It is also a working part of the amyloid-beta clearance machinery, an energy-consuming housekeeping role whose failure lets amyloid accumulate.

PLASTICITY:

Pericytes remodel their processes and coverage over the lifespan, proliferate and migrate after injury, and can, under some conditions, adopt other fates (a contested capacity to behave like a stem or scar-forming cell).

Their coverage declines with normal ageing and falls by roughly thirty-five to forty-five percent of controls in the white matter in Alzheimer's, vascular, and post-stroke dementias.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Reinterpreted as the cells that support this cell: the endothelial cell, its partner across the shared basement membrane and the source of the PDGF-BB it depends on for survival; and the astrocyte, whose end-feet complete the unit and supply metabolic support.

Loss of endothelial PDGF-BB starves pericytes and drops their number, which is how the two cells live or die together.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Delete the pericyte and the region's barrier leaks, its capillaries dilate and lose the ability to fine-tune flow, and, over time, amyloid and other toxins accumulate; experimentally, pericyte-deficient brains show reduced blood flow, white-matter damage, and neurodegeneration.

A region rich in pericytes is a region with a tight wall and a responsive blood supply; a region losing them is one drifting toward vascular dementia.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Its most surprising capability is single-capillary flow control: a lone pericyte can constrict or relax one short segment of capillary, meaning blood flow through the brain is regulated not only at the arteriole but capillary-by-capillary, cell-by-cell.

After a stroke, pericytes can clamp shut in rigor and stay contracted, keeping capillaries plugged even after the artery is reopened - the "no-reflow" phenomenon, a case of a single cell dooming its patch of tissue.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Endothelial PDGF-BB; vasoactive mediators (potassium, prostaglandins, nitric oxide, adenosine, adenosine triphosphate (ATP)) from active neuropil; mechanical wall stress; astrocyte end-foot signals.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Capillary constriction and dilation; barrier-inducing signals to endothelium; basement-membrane and matrix proteins; contribution to amyloid-beta clearance.

• STATE.

◦ Contractile tone, intracellular calcium, process coverage of the vessel, survival signalling.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Coverage 70 to 80 percent of capillary; density about 2 per millimetre of capillary; endothelial-to-pericyte ratio 1:1 to 3:1; resting potential minus 40 to minus 50 millivolts.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Contraction over seconds; barrier-signalling over hours to days; coverage decline over years to decades.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Early loss in Alzheimer's disease and vascular dementia (white-matter pericytes down about 35 to 45 percent); barrier breakdown; no-reflow after stroke; diabetic loss of retinal pericytes causing microaneurysms; cerebral autosomal dominant arteriopathy with subcortical infarcts and leukoencephalopathy (CADASIL) and other small-vessel diseases.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

CODE SAMPLE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

A PDGFR-beta-positive, octopus-shaped cell buried in the capillary's own basement membrane at the highest density found anywhere in the body, able to pinch a single capillary shut - the cell whose quiet early death opens the door to vascular dementia.


CHAPTER 43. VASCULAR SMOOTH MUSCLE CELL OF THE BRAIN VESSELS (Arteriolar Myocyte) (The Blood-Summoning Muscle Cell) (NON-COG)

When a patch of cortex starts to work, it gets thirsty within a second, and something must widen the pipe to feed it.

That something is a ring of muscle cells wrapped around the feeding arteriole, and their relaxation is the physical event that a brain scanner actually detects.

The vascular smooth muscle cell is the effector of neurovascular coupling - the mechanism by which busy brain regions summon extra blood - and its dilation, read out through the oxygen it delivers, is the entire basis of functional brain imaging.

It is a muscle cell that spends its life not moving a limb but rationing the brain's blood supply, moment by moment, region by region.

Blood-summoning names its role: its relaxation calls fresh blood into an active region.

Muscle names its nature: it is a true, contractile smooth muscle cell.

Cell marks it as a mural cell of the arterioles, the pericyte's muscular sibling.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The vascular smooth muscle cell of the brain is the contractile cell wrapped in circular layers around the brain's arteries and arterioles (the small resistance vessels that feed the capillaries).

By contracting or relaxing it narrows or widens the vessel, and this sets both the resting tone of cerebral blood flow and its second-by-second adjustment to neural activity - the phenomenon of functional hyperaemia, in which an active region gets more blood.

Its dilation is the effector step of neurovascular coupling and therefore the source of the signal measured by functional magnetic resonance imaging.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

A long, spindle-shaped cell arranged as a circumferential ring or spiral around the vessel, packed with the contractile proteins alpha-smooth-muscle actin and smooth-muscle myosin heavy chain.

On the smallest arterioles it forms a single continuous layer; on larger arteries, several concentric layers; it sits outside the endothelium and its basement membrane and is coupled to its neighbours by gap junctions so a whole ring contracts as one.

SIZE:

Individual cells are long and thin, on the order of a hundred to two hundred micrometres in length wrapped around the vessel but only a few micrometres thick, so a short segment of arteriole is encircled by relatively few cells.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Graded by vessel calibre: the multilayer smooth muscle of pial and penetrating arteries; the single-layer smooth muscle of parenchymal arterioles; and the transitional zone where the last actin-rich, contractile "ensheathing pericyte" hands off to the first true smooth muscle cell.

Arterial and arteriolar smooth muscle differ in how much they rely on calcium-driven versus other contractile mechanisms.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Around the cerebral arteries on the brain's surface (pial arteries), the penetrating arterioles that dive into the tissue, and the parenchymal arterioles within it, down to the point where the capillary bed and its pericytes begin.

It is the outermost active cell of the arteriolar segment of the neurovascular unit.

NUMBER:

Far fewer than pericytes, because arterioles are a small fraction of the vascular tree by length; a defensible order-of-magnitude figure is billions across the brain's arterial and arteriolar network, low confidence, with exact counts not well established.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

A genuinely excitable cell: resting membrane potential around minus forty to minus sixty millivolts, sitting depolarised enough that a fraction of its voltage-gated calcium channels are always open, generating resting tone.

It shows spontaneous rhythmic calcium events ("calcium sparks" and vasomotion), and depolarisation opens L-type calcium channels, raising calcium and driving contraction; hyperpolarisation (for example by potassium released from active astrocytes acting on inward-rectifier channels) closes them and drives dilation.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Reinterpreted as junctions and contacts: gap junctions to neighbouring smooth muscle cells and, through myoendothelial projections, to the endothelium, so that endothelium-derived signals reach the muscle.

Recent work describes a synaptic-like transmission in which neuronal axons signal almost directly onto arteriolar smooth muscle to drive neurovascular coupling, so it may be, in effect, postsynaptic to the vasomotor drive.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that a smooth muscle cell has no axon, being a contractile effector rather than a projecting neuron.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Reinterpreted as the signals it reads and makes: it contracts to noradrenaline, endothelin-1, and 20-hydroxyeicosatetraenoic acid (20-HETE, a constricting lipid messenger), and relaxes to nitric oxide, prostacyclin, adenosine, potassium ions, and endothelium-derived hyperpolarising factor.

It does not secrete a classical transmitter but it is the target of a rich vasoactive cocktail from neurons, astrocytes, and endothelium.

MYELINATION:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that the smooth muscle cell neither has an axon nor conducts a saltatory impulse.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Continuous, because maintaining vascular tone is active work: the cell spends adenosine triphosphate to cycle actin and myosin and to pump calcium back out after each contraction, and it sustains this baseline tone around the clock.

Its expense rises and falls with the vasomotion and the demands of neurovascular coupling.

PLASTICITY:

Smooth muscle cells are famously phenotype-switchable: they toggle between a quiescent "contractile" state and a proliferative, matrix-producing "synthetic" state, and in disease they migrate, divide, and remodel the vessel wall.

Chronic hypertension thickens and stiffens the arteriolar wall through this remodelling, degrading the vessel's ability to autoregulate.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Reinterpreted as the cells that support this cell: the endothelium, which supplies nitric oxide and hyperpolarising signals through myoendothelial junctions; the astrocyte, whose end-feet release potassium and arachidonic-acid metabolites onto the vessel; and the perivascular nerves and neurons that drive it.

Its behaviour is meaningless outside this ensemble, because it exists to be told what to do by the tissue it feeds.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Delete this cell and the region loses the ability to match blood supply to demand: cerebral autoregulation collapses, active areas are no longer fed on cue, and the entire read-out of functional imaging vanishes for that territory.

A region's smooth muscle is what turns a thought into a measurable rush of blood.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Its most surprising capability is fast, spatially precise mechanical computation of demand: the arteriolar ring integrates neuronal, astrocytic, and endothelial signals and converts them, within a second or two, into a graded change in diameter that can be propagated upstream along the vessel by gap-junction-coupled hyperpolarisation, so that dilation started at a capillary recruits the feeding artery.

It is a muscle that performs distributed, conducted signalling to route blood exactly where the tissue is working.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Neuronal and perivascular-nerve transmitters; astrocyte end-foot potassium (millimolar shifts) and arachidonic-acid metabolites; endothelial nitric oxide and hyperpolarising factor; intravascular pressure and shear.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Graded vessel constriction and dilation (functional hyperaemia); resting cerebral vascular tone; conducted vasodilation propagated upstream.

• STATE.

◦ Membrane potential, intracellular calcium, contractile tone, contractile-versus-synthetic phenotype.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Resting potential minus 40 to minus 60 millivolts; tonic partial activation of L-type calcium channels; dilation onset within about 1 to 2 seconds of neural activity.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Contraction and dilation over 1 to several seconds; vasomotion cycles of seconds; phenotype switching over days; hypertensive remodelling over months to years.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Chronic hypertension and arteriolosclerosis; cerebral amyloid angiopathy (amyloid replacing smooth muscle in vessel walls, causing haemorrhage); cerebral autosomal dominant arteriopathy with subcortical infarcts and leukoencephalopathy (CADASIL, a genetic smooth-muscle small-vessel disease); vasospasm after subarachnoid haemorrhage; impaired neurovascular coupling in ageing and dementia.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

CODE SAMPLE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

An actin-packed muscle ring around the brain's arterioles whose one-to-two-second relaxation feeds an active region and is the very event a brain scanner sees - the muscle that lets thought summon its own blood.


CHAPTER 44. PERIVASCULAR FIBROBLAST (Fibroblast of the Vascular Sleeve) (The Brain's Plumbing Fibroblast Cell) (NON-COG)

Between the wall of a larger brain vessel and the brain tissue itself lies a fluid-filled sleeve, a channel through which cerebrospinal fluid flows to rinse the brain clean.

Lining that sleeve, laying down its collagen and helping shape its space, is a cell that neuroscience only recently learned to name: the perivascular fibroblast.

It is a connective-tissue cell that has been drafted into the brain's waste-disposal system, the glymphatic circuit that runs chiefly during deep sleep.

Overlooked for decades, it is now a suspect in the scarring and stiffening that follow brain injury and accompany neurodegeneration.

Plumbing names its role: it builds and maintains the perivascular channels through which fluid flows.

Fibroblast names its nature: it is a collagen-making connective-tissue cell.

Cell marks it as a stromal resident of the vascular sleeve, not a neuron or glia.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The perivascular fibroblast is a collagen-producing connective-tissue cell that lives in the perivascular spaces (the fluid-filled sleeves, also called Virchow-Robin spaces) around the brain's larger penetrating arteries, arterioles, and veins.

It secretes and organises the extracellular matrix - collagens and other matrix proteins - that gives the perivascular space its structure, and it is therefore part of the physical plumbing of the glymphatic system, the cerebrospinal-fluid-driven waste-clearance circuit that flushes the brain most vigorously during deep, slow-wave sleep.

It is a recently characterised cell, distinguished by single-cell sequencing from the pericytes and smooth muscle it lives beside.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

A flat, elongated, sheet-forming fibroblast with fine processes, lying in the perivascular space between the vessel's smooth-muscle or basement-membrane layer and the glia limitans (the astrocyte end-foot boundary of the brain).

It is identified by markers such as collagen-1-alpha-1, decorin, lumican, and platelet-derived growth factor receptor alpha (PDGFR-alpha, which distinguishes it from the PDGFR-beta of pericytes and smooth muscle).

SIZE:

A flattened cell on the order of ten to thirty micrometres across its spread, thin, conforming to the curved sleeve of the perivascular space.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Single-cell atlases resolve several fibroblast populations along the vessel tree, distinguishing perivascular fibroblasts of arteries and veins from the meningeal (pial, arachnoid, dural) fibroblasts of Chapter 45, with which they form a continuum.

They are largely absent from the smallest capillaries (which have pericytes but no perivascular space) and appear as vessels grow to arteriolar and venular size.

WHERE IT LIVES:

In the perivascular spaces of the larger penetrating vessels of the brain and at the pial surface, wherever a fluid-filled sleeve exists around a vessel; they are not found on bare capillaries.

They sit at the interface between the vascular compartment and the glymphatic fluid channel.

NUMBER:

A minority population among brain vascular cells, not precisely counted; defensibly on the order of tens to low hundreds of millions across the brain, low confidence, concentrated on the larger vessels rather than the vast capillary bed.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

A non-excitable cell with a resting membrane potential (fibroblasts typically rest in the region of minus ten to minus fifty millivolts, and are electrically variable), no action potentials, and calcium signalling used for secretion and mechanosensation rather than firing.

It senses and responds to mechanical strain from the pulsing vessel it surrounds.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

This section is reinterpreted as junctional and contact relationships: it forms cell-matrix adhesions to the collagen it makes and gap junctions to neighbouring fibroblasts, and it abuts pericytes, smooth muscle, and astrocyte end-feet across the perivascular space.

It receives no classical synapses.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that the fibroblast has no axon, being a connective-tissue cell rather than a projecting neuron.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Reinterpreted as signalling molecules: it responds to platelet-derived growth factor and transforming growth factor beta (the master pro-fibrotic signal), and it secretes matrix proteins, matrix-remodelling enzymes, and, when activated, inflammatory and fibrotic mediators.

It does not use a classical neurotransmitter.

MYELINATION:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that the perivascular fibroblast neither has an axon nor conducts impulses.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Modest at rest but rising sharply on activation, because manufacturing and secreting large amounts of collagen is metabolically expensive; a quiescent perivascular fibroblast idles, while an activated, scar-forming one runs its protein-synthesis machinery hard.

PLASTICITY:

Highly plastic in the sense that matters for disease: on injury it proliferates and switches to a myofibroblast-like, scar-producing state, becoming a principal source of fibrotic scar tissue in the injured brain and spinal cord.

This activation is a double-edged sword, walling off damage but also impeding regeneration.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Reinterpreted as the cells it lives among: the vascular smooth muscle and pericytes of the vessel it sleeves, the endothelium that supplies the vessel, and the astrocytes whose end-feet form the outer wall of its perivascular channel.

It also works alongside the perivascular (border-associated) macrophages that patrol the same space (Chapter 48).

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Delete or over-activate this cell and the region's waste-clearance plumbing degrades: healthy perivascular fibroblasts help maintain patent glymphatic channels, while activated ones stiffen and scar the perivascular space, and their matrix changes are associated with enlarged perivascular spaces seen on imaging in small-vessel disease and ageing.
