They colour their region as the difference between a clean, well-drained tissue and a clogged, scarred one.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Its most surprising capability is mechanically tuned matrix engineering: it senses the pulsation of the vessel it wraps and the flow of cerebrospinal fluid through the sleeve, and it lays down or dissolves collagen to shape the very channel that carries that fluid, so it is a cell that builds its own environment and, in doing so, sets the resistance of the brain's drainage network.

On injury the same cell converts, within days, into the chief architect of the fibrotic scar.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Mechanical strain from vessel pulsation and cerebrospinal-fluid flow; platelet-derived growth factor; transforming growth factor beta and inflammatory cytokines after injury.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Collagen and extracellular-matrix proteins; matrix-remodelling enzymes; fibrotic scar matrix on activation; maintenance of perivascular-space architecture.

• STATE.

◦ Quiescent versus myofibroblast-activated; matrix-secretion rate; proliferation.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ PDGFR-alpha-positive, collagen-1-positive identity; perivascular-space width on the order of micrometres, enlarging with age and disease.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Matrix turnover over days; scar formation over days to weeks after injury; perivascular-space enlargement over years.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Fibrotic scarring after traumatic and ischaemic brain and spinal-cord injury; enlarged perivascular spaces and impaired glymphatic clearance in small-vessel disease and ageing; contribution to fibrosis in multiple sclerosis lesions.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

CODE SAMPLE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

A PDGFR-alpha-positive, collagen-making cell found only in the fluid sleeves around the brain's larger vessels, absent from bare capillaries - the newly named plumber of the glymphatic system and the chief mason of the brain's fibrotic scar.


CHAPTER 45. MENINGEAL FIBROBLAST POPULATIONS: DURAL, ARACHNOID, AND PIAL CELLS (Fibroblasts of the Three Coverings) (The Three-Cloak Barrier Cells) (NON-COG)

The brain is not loose in the skull; it is wrapped in three fitted cloaks, and floats in a thin layer of fluid held between them.

The cells that weave those cloaks are fibroblasts, but three molecularly distinct kinds, one for each layer - tough outer dura, web-like middle arachnoid, and gossamer inner pia.

Hidden among them is a barrier as important as the blood-brain barrier: the arachnoid barrier cell layer, sealed by tight junctions, that keeps the blood in the dura from reaching the cerebrospinal fluid.

Long dismissed as mere packing, these meningeal fibroblasts are now known to guard a border, guide development, and channel the brain's immune traffic.

Three-cloak names their arrangement: three layers, dura, arachnoid, and pia.

Barrier names their hidden job: the arachnoid layer is a true sealed barrier.

Cells marks them as fibroblasts, the connective-tissue cells of the meninges.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The meningeal fibroblasts are the connective-tissue cells that build the three meninges (the brain's coverings): the tough, collagen-rich dura mater on the outside; the web-like arachnoid mater beneath it, whose inner face bears the arachnoid barrier cell layer; and the delicate pia mater that hugs the brain surface.

Single-cell studies show each layer has its own molecularly distinct fibroblast type, and their collective jobs are mechanical protection, containment of the cerebrospinal fluid in the subarachnoid space, formation of the meningeal (blood-cerebrospinal-fluid) barrier, and, in development, secretion of signals that pattern the growing brain and its vasculature.

The subarachnoid space between arachnoid and pia is the fluid cushion in which the brain floats.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

Three morphologies for three layers: dural fibroblasts are robust, packed among thick collagen bundles; arachnoid barrier cells are epithelial-like, flattened, tightly packed, and joined by tight junctions (claudin-11, zonula occludens 1) and adherens junctions (E-cadherin, VE-cadherin); arachnoid trabecular and pial cells are thin and stellate, the pia forming a single fine cellular sheet on the brain surface with fibroblast-like leptomeningeal cells.

SIZE:

Flattened fibroblasts, individually on the order of ten to thirty micrometres across, forming layers that range from the millimetre-scale thickness of the dura down to the near-single-cell thinness of the pia.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Explicitly three named families in this chapter: dural fibroblasts (with further subtypes near the skull and near the arachnoid); arachnoid cells (barrier cells and trabecular cells); and pial (leptomeningeal) cells.

Recent human work even proposes an intermediate leptomeningeal membrane, so the exact number of layers remains an active controversy.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Coating the entire brain and spinal cord in three concentric sheets between the skull or vertebrae and the neural surface, following every fold, and dipping inward with the larger vessels as their outermost perivascular covering.

The arachnoid barrier layer sits at the crucial interface between the vascularised, leaky dura and the protected cerebrospinal fluid.

NUMBER:

Collectively a large but uncounted population spread over the roughly two thousand square centimetres of folded meningeal surface; defensibly hundreds of millions of cells across the three layers, low confidence.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Non-excitable connective-tissue cells with a resting membrane potential (fibroblasts rest broadly in the minus ten to minus fifty millivolt range) and no action potentials, using calcium signalling for secretion and gap-junction coupling for tissue-wide coordination rather than firing.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Reinterpreted as junctions: the defining feature is the arachnoid barrier cell's tight and adherens junctions that seal the layer, plus gap junctions coupling meningeal fibroblasts and desmosomes holding the sheets together.

They receive no classical synapses.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that meningeal fibroblasts have no axon, being connective-tissue cells rather than projecting neurons.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Reinterpreted as signalling molecules: in development they secrete retinoic acid, bone morphogenetic proteins, and chemokines such as CXCL12 that guide neuronal migration and vascular growth, and they respond to transforming growth factor beta; they use no classical neurotransmitter.

MYELINATION:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that meningeal fibroblasts neither have an axon nor conduct impulses.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Modest and matrix-linked, dominated by the manufacture of collagen and other matrix proteins, higher in the collagen-heavy dura and lower in the thin pia, rising on injury when they proliferate to scar.

PLASTICITY:

They proliferate and remodel matrix through life and, after injury, meningeal fibroblasts invade the wound and are a major source of the fibrotic scar core that forms in brain and spinal-cord lesions.

The arachnoid barrier can also break down in disease, as in bacterial meningitis, where its claudin-11 junctions are disrupted and it becomes leaky.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Reinterpreted as the cells they work with: the dural blood vessels and their border-associated macrophages, the arachnoid's cap cells that drain fluid (Chapter 46), the pial vessels they sleeve, and the surface (glia limitans) astrocytes of the brain against which the pia lies.

The meningeal layers also host the lymphatic vessels and immune cells of Part Six.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Delete these cells and the brain loses its wrapping and its float: there is no subarachnoid cushion, no meningeal barrier separating dural blood from cerebrospinal fluid, and, in the embryo, no source of the patterning signals that tell the cortex and its vessels how to form.

The arachnoid barrier layer, in particular, is why substances in the vascular dura do not simply diffuse into the cerebrospinal fluid.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Its most surprising capability is that the arachnoid barrier layer builds a second blood-cerebrospinal-fluid barrier out of ordinary mesenchymal fibroblasts, which switch on epithelial genes (E-cadherin) and tight-junction claudin-11 during development to seal a border every bit as real as the blood-brain barrier - a connective-tissue cell reinventing itself as a barrier epithelium.

The meninges also act as an immune interface and a developmental signalling centre, roles far beyond passive packing.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Mechanical load and cerebrospinal-fluid pressure; developmental signals; inflammatory cytokines; contact signals from adjacent vasculature and glia limitans.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Collagen and matrix of the three layers; the sealed arachnoid barrier; developmental patterning signals (retinoic acid, bone morphogenetic proteins, CXCL12); fibrotic scar on injury.

• STATE.

◦ Barrier integrity (claudin-11, E-cadherin); quiescent versus scar-activated; matrix-secretion rate.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Arachnoid barrier 2 to 3 cell layers thick, sealed by claudin-11 and zonula occludens 1; dural collagen thickness on the millimetre scale; pia near single-cell thickness.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Matrix turnover over days; barrier formation over developmental days to weeks; scar formation over days to weeks after injury.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Meningitis (arachnoid barrier breakdown); meningeal fibrosis and scarring after injury; subdural and subarachnoid haemorrhage tracking along these planes; meningioma (a tumour of arachnoid cap cells, Chapter 46); developmental meningeal defects causing cortical malformation.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

CODE SAMPLE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

Three molecularly distinct fibroblast layers wrapping the brain, one of which - the claudin-11-sealed arachnoid barrier - is a connective-tissue cell that turned itself into a barrier epithelium to keep dural blood out of the cerebrospinal fluid.


CHAPTER 46. ARACHNOID CAP AND GRANULATION CELLS (Cells of the Arachnoid Villi) (The One-Way Drain Cells) (NON-COG)

The brain makes about half a litre of cerebrospinal fluid a day, and unless that fluid has somewhere to go, pressure inside the skull climbs until the brain itself is squeezed.

Perched where the arachnoid pushes up into the great venous sinuses are clusters of specialised cells that solve this problem: they form the arachnoid granulations, the classic one-way valves that let fluid out into the blood but not back.

These are arachnoid cap cells, the same cells whose whorls give rise to meningioma, the most common primary brain tumour.

They are a drainpipe made of living valves, quietly holding the pressure inside your head to the right number.

One-way names their behaviour: they let cerebrospinal fluid pass into venous blood but not the reverse.

Drain names their function: they are the outlet of the fluid system.

Cells marks them as specialised arachnoid cells, cap cells of the granulations.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The arachnoid cap and granulation cells are the specialised arachnoid cells that build the arachnoid granulations (also called arachnoid villi, or Pacchionian bodies), the finger-like protrusions of arachnoid that push through the dura into the venous sinuses.

They form the classic pressure-driven, one-way pathway by which cerebrospinal fluid returns to the venous blood: fluid moves out when its pressure exceeds venous pressure, and the valve closes when the gradient reverses.

Recent work adds that granulations also communicate with meningeal lymphatic and dura-arachnoid channels, so they are one, but not the only, route of cerebrospinal-fluid outflow.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

Each granulation has a collagenous core of arachnoid trabeculae threaded by interwoven channels, capped by a dense apical cluster of arachnoid cap cells (a whorled, tightly packed cap about 150 micrometres thick), and covered on the sinus side by venous endothelium.

The cap cells are epithelial-like, joined by desmosomes and tight junctions, and are famous for arranging themselves in concentric whorls.

SIZE:

Individual cap cells are on the order of ten to twenty micrometres, but the granulations they build are visible to the naked eye, from well under a millimetre up to several millimetres across, growing larger and more numerous with age.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

The chapter groups two closely related roles: the arachnoid cap cells (the whorl-forming cells of the arachnoid, from which meningiomas arise) and the granulation cells proper that make up the villus and its drainage channels.

Granulations range from microscopic arachnoid villi to the large macroscopic granulations of the adult.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Along the dural venous sinuses, above all the superior sagittal sinus and its lateral lacunae, and around some spinal nerve roots, wherever arachnoid protrudes into venous blood.

They sit at the interface between the subarachnoid cerebrospinal-fluid space and the venous circulation.

NUMBER:

Hundreds to a few thousand granulations of varying size in the adult, each a cluster of many cap cells, so the cell count reaches into the millions; the granulations increase in number and size with age, moderate confidence.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Non-excitable epithelial-like cells with a resting membrane potential and no action potentials, using calcium signalling and transport rather than firing; their business is bulk fluid movement, not electrical signalling.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Reinterpreted as junctions: cap cells are held together by desmosomes and tight junctions and abut the venous endothelium across which fluid drains; the granulation's channels connect the subarachnoid space to the subendothelial venous side.

They receive no classical synapses.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that arachnoid cap cells have no axon, being epithelial-like arachnoid cells rather than projecting neurons.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Reinterpreted as signalling and transport: they do not release a neurotransmitter, but they express water and ion transporters and the machinery for transcellular vacuolar transport (giant vacuoles) that ferries cerebrospinal fluid across into the blood.

MYELINATION:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that arachnoid cap cells neither have an axon nor conduct impulses.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Moderate and transport-linked, spending energy to run the vacuolar and transporter machinery that moves fluid against the odds when gradients are unfavourable, and to maintain the cell whorls.

PLASTICITY:

The granulations grow and multiply across the lifespan, an adaptive expansion of drainage capacity, and the cap cells retain a proliferative potential that is the origin of meningioma when it goes wrong.

Drainage capacity can also be recruited or impaired, contributing to disorders of cerebrospinal-fluid pressure.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Reinterpreted as the cells they work with: the venous endothelium of the sinus into which they drain, the collagen-producing arachnoid and dural fibroblasts (Chapter 45) that build the granulation's core, and the meningeal lymphatics that share the outflow task.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Delete these cells and cerebrospinal fluid has one fewer outlet: fluid accumulates, intracranial pressure rises, and, if drainage fails badly, hydrocephalus (an excess of fluid distending the ventricles) results.

They colour their region as the pressure-relief valve of the entire central nervous system.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Its most surprising capability is one-way mechanical valving by living cells: the granulation behaves as a pressure-sensitive valve, opening transcellular giant vacuoles and channels to pass fluid into blood only when cerebrospinal-fluid pressure exceeds venous pressure by a few millimetres of mercury, and sealing when the gradient reverses to keep blood from refluxing into the fluid.

It is a valve with no moving parts except the cells themselves.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Cerebrospinal-fluid pressure in the subarachnoid space; venous sinus pressure; the pressure difference across the granulation.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Bulk one-way flow of cerebrospinal fluid into venous blood; contribution to intracranial-pressure regulation.

• STATE.

◦ Open versus closed valve state; vacuolar transport activity; granulation size.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Opening threshold of roughly 3 to 5 millimetres of mercury pressure gradient; apical cap about 150 micrometres thick; granulations sub-millimetre to several millimetres.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Valve response over seconds to the pressure gradient; granulation growth over years to decades.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Impaired absorption causing communicating hydrocephalus and raised intracranial pressure; obstruction after subarachnoid haemorrhage or meningitis; meningioma arising from cap cells; giant arachnoid granulations rarely causing venous obstruction.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

CODE SAMPLE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

Whorl-forming arachnoid cells that push through the dura into the venous sinuses and act as living one-way valves for cerebrospinal fluid - the drain of the skull, and the cell of origin of the brain's commonest tumour.


CHAPTER 47. CHOROID PLEXUS STROMAL CELLS: FIBROBLASTS AND PERICYTES OF THE PLEXUS (Core Cells of the Fluid Factory) (The Leaky-Vessel Support Cells) (NON-COG)

Inside each ventricle hangs a frond of tissue like a small red cauliflower, the choroid plexus, and it is the brain's fluid factory, making cerebrospinal fluid by the half-litre each day.

To do that it needs something the rest of the brain forbids: leaky, windowed blood vessels that let plasma flood out so the epithelium can process it into fluid.

Holding those leaky vessels in place, and filling the core of every frond, are the choroid plexus stromal cells - the fibroblasts and pericytes that support one of the few deliberately porous vascular beds inside the skull.

They are the connective-tissue scaffold of the only place in the brain where the blood is meant to leak.

Leaky-vessel names what they support: the fenestrated, windowed capillaries of the plexus core.

Support names their role: they are the stroma, the structural filling of the frond.

Cells marks them as fibroblasts and pericytes, not epithelium or neuron.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The choroid plexus stromal cells are the connective-tissue cells - fibroblasts and pericytes - that fill the core of each choroid plexus villus, beneath the secretory epithelium that actually makes the cerebrospinal fluid.

Their job is structural and supportive: they form the collagenous stroma that holds the villus together and suspends its capillaries, and the pericytes among them wrap and stabilise those capillaries.

Crucially, the capillaries they support are fenestrated (windowed) and leaky - one of the few deliberately porous vascular beds inside the skull - because the blood-cerebrospinal-fluid barrier here is built by the tight-junction-sealed epithelium above, not by the vessels below.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The stroma is a loose, collagen-rich connective tissue; its fibroblasts are flat and stellate, laying down matrix, while its pericytes hug the fenestrated capillaries within the shared basement membrane.

The whole is capped by the single layer of cuboidal choroid plexus epithelium (the cerebrospinal fluid (CSF) making cell of Chapter 18) that provides the actual barrier.

SIZE:

The stromal fibroblasts and pericytes are ordinary in scale, roughly ten to twenty micrometres for cell bodies with processes extending further, filling villi that are themselves fractions of a millimetre across.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Two cell classes are grouped here: the stromal fibroblasts (matrix-producing connective-tissue cells) and the plexus pericytes (mural cells of the fenestrated capillaries), alongside the resident immune cells (border-associated and epiplexus macrophages) treated in Part Six.

WHERE IT LIVES:

In the core of the choroid plexus in all four ventricles - the two lateral ventricles, the third, and the fourth - underneath the plexus epithelium and around its leaky capillaries.

They sit at the vascular side of the blood-cerebrospinal-fluid interface, the mirror image of the blood-brain barrier's arrangement.

NUMBER:

A small population confined to the choroid plexuses, not precisely counted; defensibly on the order of millions of stromal cells across the four plexuses, low confidence.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Non-excitable cells with a resting membrane potential and no action potentials; the fibroblasts rest broadly in the minus ten to minus fifty millivolt range and the pericytes near minus forty to minus fifty millivolts, using calcium signalling rather than firing, the pericytes contracting to regulate local flow.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Reinterpreted as junctions: cell-matrix adhesions and gap junctions among fibroblasts, and pericyte peg-and-socket contacts to the fenestrated endothelium; they receive no classical synapses.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that these stromal cells have no axon, being connective-tissue and mural cells rather than projecting neurons.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Reinterpreted as signalling molecules: the fibroblasts secrete matrix proteins and respond to platelet-derived growth factor and transforming growth factor beta; the pericytes use platelet-derived growth factor beta signalling with the endothelium; neither uses a classical neurotransmitter.

MYELINATION:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that these stromal cells neither have an axon nor conduct impulses.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Modest, dominated by matrix maintenance in the fibroblasts and by contractile tone in the pericytes; the metabolically expensive fluid-making work of the plexus belongs to the epithelium above, not to these supporting cells.

PLASTICITY:

They remodel the stromal matrix through life, and the choroid plexus stroma accumulates changes with age, including thickened basement membranes and calcification (the "brain sand," psammoma bodies, often seen on scans).

On inflammation the stroma expands and its immune traffic rises.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Reinterpreted as the cells they work with: the fenestrated endothelium they support, the choroid plexus epithelium that forms the barrier above them, and the epiplexus (Kolmer) macrophages and border-associated macrophages that share the plexus (Part Six).

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Delete these cells and the choroid plexus loses its scaffold: its fronds collapse, its leaky capillaries are unsupported, and the fluid factory that supplies the whole central nervous system falters.

They colour their region as the structural guarantee that the brain's one intended leak stays open and organised.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Its most surprising capability is that these cells maintain a deliberately porous vascular bed in the middle of the most barrier-obsessed organ in the body: they support fenestrated capillaries with pores of roughly 60 to 80 nanometres that let plasma flood the stroma, trusting the epithelium above to be the real wall, which inverts the usual brain arrangement where the vessel is the barrier.

It is a controlled leak held open by connective tissue in a sea of sealed vessels.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Blood plasma leaking from fenestrated capillaries; platelet-derived growth factor and transforming growth factor beta; mechanical support demands of the villus.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Stromal collagen matrix; capillary stabilisation (pericytes); regulation of local plexus blood flow.

• STATE.

◦ Matrix-secretion rate; pericyte contractile tone; degree of calcification and basement-membrane thickening.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Capillary fenestrations about 60 to 80 nanometres; barrier residing in the epithelium above (not these cells); cerebrospinal-fluid output of the plexus about 500 millilitres per day.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Matrix turnover over days; age-related calcification and thickening over years to decades.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Choroid plexus fibrosis and calcification with age; stromal inflammation in infection and immune disease; contribution to choroid plexus tumours and cysts; oedema and stromal expansion in hydrocephalus.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

CODE SAMPLE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

The fibroblasts and pericytes filling the core of the choroid plexus, the only cells in the brain whose job is to hold open a fenestrated, leaky capillary bed - support staff for the one place inside the skull where the blood is meant to seep.


PART SIX. THE IMMUNE CELLS OF THE NERVOUS SYSTEM BORDERS

Beyond the microglia that live inside the brain proper, the nervous system keeps a standing garrison at its edges, where blood, cerebrospinal fluid, and nervous tissue press against one another.

These are the border cells: macrophages in the sleeves around blood vessels, on the coverings of the brain, and inside the choroid plexus; a lone macrophage that rides the fluid-facing surface of that plexus; and the lymphocytes, dendritic cells, and mast cells that patrol the meninges and their newly mapped lymphatic vessels.

They are professional immune cells, most of them born not in the brain but in the embryonic yolk sac (the earliest blood-forming sac of the embryo), that took up residence at the frontier and stayed for life.

For a generation they were dismissed as mere sentries, but the modern evidence shows some of them whispering to neurons across the barrier, tuning mood, sociability, and memory from the outside.

Every entry in this part is tagged non-cognitive (NON-COG), because each serves a substrate role of defence, surveillance, and plumbing rather than computation - though the meningeal lymphocytes sit at the very edge of that judgement, and may yet be promoted.


CHAPTER 48. BORDER-ASSOCIATED MACROPHAGES (Perivascular, Meningeal, and Choroid Plexus Macrophages) (The Border-Guard Engulfing Cell) (NON-COG)

Picture a walled city whose citizens are neurons and whose wall is the blood-brain barrier, and then picture the guards who stand not inside the streets but in the gatehouses, the sleeves along the aqueducts, and the customs sheds at the water-gate.

These are the border-associated macrophages, a family of large engulfing cells that took up their posts before the wall was even finished and never left.

They swallow debris, present captured fragments of intruders to passing lymphocytes, and regulate the flow of fluid through the spaces they inhabit.

They are among the oldest cells in the body by lineage, seeded from the yolk sac in the first weeks of embryonic life.

Border names their address: they live at the interfaces of the central nervous system, never in its computing core.

Associated names their loyalty: they are attached to the blood vessels, the membranes, and the fluid factories, not to the neurons.

Macrophage, from the Greek for big eater, names their trade: the professional engulfing and digesting of anything foreign, dying, or discarded.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

A border-associated macrophage (BAM) is a resident immune cell of the central nervous system (CNS) that lives at its borders rather than in its parenchyma (the working nervous tissue itself).

The family occupies three great frontiers: the perivascular spaces (the fluid-filled sleeves, also called Virchow-Robin spaces, that wrap the larger blood vessels as they dive into the brain), the meninges (the three membranes covering the brain and spinal cord), and the stroma (connective-tissue core) of the choroid plexus (the fluid-making tissue inside the ventricles).

Their job is surveillance, phagocytosis (the engulfing and digestion of debris and pathogens), antigen presentation (displaying captured molecular fragments to lymphocytes to trigger an adaptive immune response), and regulation of fluid drainage and barrier function.

With microglia, they form the second receiving station of the neuroimmune layer-skipping channel, the line by which the body's immune state reaches the brain.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

These are large, often elongated or stellate cells with a generous cytoplasm packed with lysosomes (the cell's digestive sacs), vacuoles, and phagosomes (bubbles holding swallowed material).

Perivascular macrophages are typically stretched along the vessel wall within the perivascular space; meningeal and choroid plexus macrophages tend to be more rounded or amoeboid (blob-like and mobile).

Unlike the finely branched, tiled microglia of the parenchyma, border macrophages are less ramified and more clearly macrophage-like in appearance.

They carry the standard myeloid surface machinery, including the markers CD163, CD206 (the mannose receptor), and LYVE-1 on many subsets, alongside the shared macrophage marker CD68.

SIZE:

The cell body runs roughly 10 to 30 micrometres across (a micrometre being a thousandth of a millimetre), with processes and an engorged, debris-filled body extending the perivascular forms further along the vessel.

Confidence is moderate: exact human dimensions vary with activation state, and much of the fine measurement comes from rodent tissue.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Single-cell studies now resolve the family into distinct residents of each border.

Perivascular macrophages sit in the Virchow-Robin sleeves; subdural and leptomeningeal macrophages patrol the soft inner meninges; dural macrophages occupy the tough outer membrane; stromal choroid plexus macrophages live in the plexus core; and the epiplexus (Kolmer) cell rides the plexus surface, treated separately in Chapter 49.

A telling division runs through the family: perivascular and epiplexus macrophages self-renew locally for life, whereas dural and choroid plexus stromal macrophages are gradually topped up and replaced across the lifespan by monocytes (a circulating white-blood-cell type) arriving from the bone marrow.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Three addresses: the perivascular spaces of penetrating arteries and veins throughout the brain and cord; all three meningeal layers, most densely the dura mater; and the connective-tissue stroma of the choroid plexus in all four ventricles.

Each of these is an explicit interface - between blood and brain, between skull and brain, and between blood and cerebrospinal fluid - which is exactly why the immune garrison is posted there.

NUMBER:

Border macrophages are far rarer than the roughly 10 to 15 billion microglia of the human brain, numbering in the low millions overall, with honest low confidence in any precise human figure.

They are a minority myeloid population, outnumbered by microglia by roughly one to two orders of magnitude, but concentrated exactly where blood-borne threats and fluid traffic are highest.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Like other macrophages, these are non-excitable cells: they fire no action potentials.

They hold a resting membrane potential in the region of minus 20 to minus 60 millivolts, unusually depolarised and variable for a cell, set largely by inwardly rectifying and calcium-activated potassium channels.

Their true signalling currency is not voltage but intracellular calcium transients and second-messenger cascades triggered when their receptors bind a threat.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

This section is reinterpreted for a non-neuronal cell: border macrophages form no chemical synapses, but they make functional immune junctions.

They form the immunological synapse, the organised contact zone through which a macrophage presents antigen to a T lymphocyte, and they make intimate physical contact with the endothelial cells of the vessel wall, the epithelium of the choroid plexus, and the fibroblasts of the meninges.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that a macrophage has no axon, being a migratory immune cell rather than a projection neuron.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Reinterpreted as signalling molecules: these cells secrete cytokines and chemokines (immune signalling proteins) including tumour necrosis factor alpha, interleukin-1 beta, interleukin-6, and interleukin-10, along with reactive oxygen species and matrix-remodelling enzymes.

They also bear receptors for the messengers of the nervous and endocrine systems, including glucocorticoid (stress-hormone) receptors, letting the body's hormonal state reprogram them.

MYELINATION:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that macrophages neither make myelin nor are wrapped in it.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Resting surveillance is metabolically cheap, run largely on oxidative phosphorylation (the efficient oxygen-using energy pathway).

On activation the cell undergoes a metabolic switch toward glycolysis (rapid sugar-burning) to fuel a burst of phagocytosis, cytokine manufacture, and the respiratory burst (a flood of reactive oxygen species used to kill engulfed microbes), sharply raising glucose demand for the duration of the response.

PLASTICITY:

Border macrophages are strikingly plastic in the immunological sense: they shift along a spectrum from a pro-inflammatory, pathogen-killing state to a resolving, tissue-repairing, debris-clearing state, tuned by the local signals they read.

They also remodel with age and disease, and the replaceable subsets exchange their resident population for fresh monocytes over months to years.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Their niche is built by the cells they live among: the endothelial cells and pericytes of the vessel wall, the fibroblasts of the meninges and choroid plexus stroma, and the choroid plexus epithelium.

The cytokine macrophage colony-stimulating factor, supplied by these neighbours, is the survival signal that keeps the resident macrophage alive at its post.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove the border macrophages and the brain loses its customs service: perivascular drainage and the clearance of blood breakdown products falter, antigen no longer reaches the lymphatic system efficiently, and the barrier interfaces lose an immune regulator.

Their absence tilts the whole neurovascular frontier toward either unchecked invasion or, paradoxically, unresolved chronic inflammation, because they are as much brakes on immunity as they are its agents.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Their signature capability is phagocytic decision-making at the barrier: a border macrophage continuously samples the perivascular and cerebrospinal fluid, integrates dozens of receptor signals, and decides in real time whether to stay quiet, engulf, or raise the alarm by presenting antigen and releasing cytokines.

Perivascular macrophages also actively shape cerebral blood flow and the clearance of amyloid-beta (the sticky protein of Alzheimer's disease), acting as a hidden hand on the brain's plumbing and waste disposal.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Pathogen and damage molecular patterns via toll-like receptors; complement fragments; cytokines; glucocorticoids; and debris and blood products in the perivascular and cerebrospinal fluid.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Cytokines and chemokines; presented antigen on major histocompatibility complex molecules; reactive oxygen species; and cleared, digested debris.

• STATE.

◦ Position on the inflammatory-to-resolving spectrum; phagosome load; intracellular calcium; and metabolic mode (oxidative versus glycolytic).

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Cell body 10 to 30 micrometres; resting potential about minus 20 to minus 60 millivolts; population in the low millions; self-renewing versus monocyte-replaced by subset.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Calcium signalling seconds; cytokine production hours; phenotype switch hours to days; population turnover of replaceable subsets months to years.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Chronic activation feeds neuroinflammation in multiple sclerosis, Alzheimer's disease, and cerebral small-vessel disease; perivascular macrophage dysfunction impairs amyloid clearance and promotes cerebral amyloid angiopathy; and they are an entry route and reservoir for the human immunodeficiency virus in the brain.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

CODE SAMPLE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

A large, lysosome-stuffed, yolk-sac-descended engulfing cell that lives never in the neural tissue but always at its borders - in the sleeve around a vessel, on the brain's coverings, or in the fluid factory - carrying CD163, CD206, and LYVE-1 where the finely branched microglia inside do not.


CHAPTER 49. EPIPLEXUS (KOLMER) CELL (The Fluid-Riding Watchman Cell) (NON-COG)

Deep in the ventricles, the fluid-filled chambers of the brain, sits the choroid plexus, a frilled tissue that spins cerebrospinal fluid out of blood.

Perched on its outermost, fluid-facing surface, clinging to the epithelium like a barnacle on a pier at the waterline, is a single lonely macrophage that faces not the tissue but the open fluid.

It is the epiplexus cell, and it spends its life dipping into the cerebrospinal fluid, tasting it for debris, blood, and invaders.

First drawn by Walter Kolmer in 1921, it is the brain's watchman on the water.

Epiplexus, from the Greek epi meaning upon and the Latin plexus meaning the woven tissue, names its perch: it sits upon the choroid plexus.

Kolmer honours the anatomist who first described these cells riding the plexus surface.

Cell here marks a solitary sentinel rather than a sheet or a network.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The epiplexus, or Kolmer, cell is a resident macrophage that sits on the apical (cerebrospinal-fluid-facing) surface of the choroid plexus epithelium, inside the ventricle.

It is a specialised member of the border-associated macrophage family of Chapter 48, distinguished by its unique address: it is the only one of them stationed on the outside of an epithelium, directly bathed by cerebrospinal fluid.

Its job is to sample that fluid, engulf debris and blood products, and act as a first responder to bleeding into the ventricles.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

An amoeboid (blob-like, shape-shifting) cell with an irregular, often star-shaped or flattened body that grips the epithelial surface with foot-like processes and thrusts filopodia (fine finger-like protrusions) into the fluid.

Under the electron microscope it shows the classic marks of a free macrophage: an indented nucleus with clumped chromatin, sparse mitochondria and endoplasmic reticulum, abundant free ribosomes, multiple Golgi elements, coated surface pits and microvesicles, and numerous vacuoles and lysosomal dense bodies - a body clearly built for eating and digesting.

SIZE:

Roughly 10 to 20 micrometres across the cell body, with processes reaching further across the epithelial surface; confidence is moderate, as most measurements come from animal tissue.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

No well-established resting subtypes are recognised; the cell is instead described by its activation state, shifting from a resting ramified or flattened form to a rounded, actively phagocytosing form after injury or bleeding.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Exclusively on the apical, ventricle-facing surface of the choroid plexus epithelium, in all four ventricles of the brain.

This is a sharp interface - the boundary between the blood-derived plexus and the open cerebrospinal fluid - making the epiplexus cell the immune guard at the very mouth of the fluid system.

NUMBER:

A small, scattered population studding the surface of the choroid plexus, numbering at most in the tens of thousands across the human ventricles, with low confidence in any exact figure.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

A non-excitable macrophage that fires no action potentials, holding a depolarised resting potential in the region of minus 20 to minus 60 millivolts set by potassium channels, and signalling internally through calcium transients rather than voltage spikes.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Reinterpreted for a non-neuronal cell: it forms no chemical synapses, but it makes intimate physical contact with the underlying choroid plexus epithelial cells and can form immunological synapses with lymphocytes it encounters in the fluid.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this macrophage has no axon.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Reinterpreted as signalling molecules: on activation it releases inflammatory cytokines and chemokines such as tumour necrosis factor alpha and interleukin-1 beta, and reactive oxygen species, into the cerebrospinal fluid.

MYELINATION:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that it neither makes nor receives myelin.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Low at rest, rising sharply with a glycolytic burst on activation to power phagocytosis and the respiratory burst when it engulfs debris or responds to bleeding.

PLASTICITY:

Immunologically plastic, switching rapidly from quiet surveillance to an activated, amoeboid, phagocytic state; unusually among border macrophages, it self-renews locally for life rather than being replaced by blood-borne monocytes.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Supported by the choroid plexus epithelial cells it rides upon, which supply survival cytokines including macrophage colony-stimulating factor, and bathed by the cerebrospinal fluid those same cells manufacture.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

It gives the cerebrospinal fluid system an immune doorman: without it, blood and debris entering the ventricles would go unsampled and uncleared, and the fluid compartment would lose its first line of innate defence.

After an intraventricular haemorrhage (bleeding into the ventricles), these cells are among the very first responders, and their behaviour shapes whether that bleed resolves cleanly or drives inflammation and hydrocephalus (a damaging build-up of fluid).

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Its surprising capability is direct cerebrospinal-fluid immune surveillance: uniquely positioned on the open fluid surface, it continuously samples the whole brain's fluid for danger and makes an engulf-or-alarm decision on behalf of the entire ventricular system.

Strikingly, free-floating macrophages recovered from human cerebrospinal fluid samples carry a transcriptional signature resembling both disease-associated microglia and these Kolmer cells, suggesting the epiplexus cell is a readout of brain health that clinicians can sample from a spinal tap.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Debris, blood breakdown products, pathogens, and molecular danger signals dissolved in the cerebrospinal fluid.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Cleared and digested material; cytokines and reactive oxygen species released into the fluid; and presented antigen.

• STATE.

◦ Resting versus activated amoeboid form; phagosome load; intracellular calcium.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Cell body 10 to 20 micrometres; resting potential about minus 20 to minus 60 millivolts; self-renewing, embryonically derived population in the tens of thousands.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Calcium signalling seconds; activation and phagocytic response minutes to hours; cytokine output hours.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Maladaptive responses to intraventricular haemorrhage contribute to post-haemorrhagic hydrocephalus and inflammation; chronic activation feeds neuroinflammation in infection and neurodegeneration.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

CODE SAMPLE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

The one macrophage in the whole body that lives on the wrong side of an epithelium, clinging to the fluid-facing surface of the choroid plexus and dipping its filopodia straight into the cerebrospinal fluid - the brain's watchman on the water, named for Kolmer since 1921.


CHAPTER 50. MENINGEAL LYMPHOCYTES, DENDRITIC CELLS, AND MAST CELLS (The Frontier Council Cells) (NON-COG)

The meninges, the three membranes wrapping the brain, were long thought to be a passive shell, but they turn out to be one of the most immunologically alive tissues in the body.

Threaded through the tough outer dura, alongside newly rediscovered lymphatic vessels that drain the brain's fluid, sit lymphocytes taking counsel, dendritic cells carrying messages out to the neck's lymph nodes, and mast cells armed with granules of histamine.

Most astonishing of all, the signalling molecules these cells release have been shown to reach across the barrier and tune an animal's sociability, anxiety, and memory - immune cells editing behaviour from outside the brain.

This chapter treats them together as a council of the frontier.

Frontier names their post: they hold the meningeal border, not the brain's interior.

Council names their nature: several distinct cell types deliberating together, exchanging signals, and reaching decisions that ripple inward.

Cells, in the plural, marks that this is a mixed population - lymphocytes, dendritic cells, and mast cells - not a single type.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

This entry covers the adaptive and granule-bearing immune cells of the meninges and their lymphatic vessels: T lymphocytes and B lymphocytes (the adaptive white blood cells that carry immunological memory and make antibodies), dendritic cells (the professional messenger cells that ferry captured antigen to lymph nodes to launch an immune response), and mast cells (granule-packed cells that release histamine and other mediators).

Their collective job is immune surveillance of the brain's borders, drainage of antigen and fluid to the deep cervical lymph nodes of the neck via the meningeal lymphatic vessels, and - a frontier discovery - the release of signalling molecules that modulate mood, social behaviour, anxiety, and learning in the underlying brain.

Because meningeal T cells have been shown to shape behaviour from outside the parenchyma, this entry sits at the very edge of the non-cognitive tag and may yet be promoted.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

A mixed population: lymphocytes are small round cells with a thin rim of cytoplasm around a dense nucleus; dendritic cells are star-shaped, with the veiled, tree-like processes that give them their name and that maximise the surface for capturing and displaying antigen; mast cells are large, round-to-oval cells crammed with dark-staining secretory granules.

They are strung along and around the dural blood vessels and the meningeal lymphatic vessels, densest in the dura mater, the tough outer covering.

SIZE:

Lymphocytes are among the smallest cells here at roughly 6 to 15 micrometres; dendritic cells span 10 to 20 micrometres including their processes; mast cells are larger, roughly 10 to 20 micrometres, their granules the dominant feature.

Confidence is moderate, most fine measurement coming from rodent meninges.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

The lymphocytes divide into T cells (themselves split into helper, cytotoxic, regulatory, and the gamma-delta subset), B cells, and related innate lymphoid cells and natural killer cells.

The dendritic cells fall into conventional and plasmacytoid classes, and the mast cells cluster especially at the arachnoid cuff exit points where vessels cross the meninges.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Chiefly the dura mater, the outermost and most vascular meningeal layer, concentrated around the dural venous sinuses and along the meningeal lymphatic vessels; smaller numbers populate the softer leptomeninges.

The dura is an explicit interface, sitting between skull and brain and connecting, through its lymphatics, to the immune organs of the neck - which is precisely why the adaptive immune council gathers here.

NUMBER:

Collectively a modest population of the order of hundreds of thousands to a few million cells across the human meninges, with low confidence; resident macrophages are the single most abundant meningeal immune type, with lymphocytes, dendritic cells, and mast cells present in smaller and physiologically regulated numbers.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

All are non-excitable and fire no action potentials, resting at depolarised potentials in the region of minus 30 to minus 60 millivolts.

Their functional signalling is through calcium influx: in lymphocytes calcium entry drives activation and cytokine gene expression, and in mast cells a sharp calcium rise triggers the explosive release of their granules.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Reinterpreted for non-neuronal cells: no chemical synapses, but the immunological synapse is central here - the organised contact through which a dendritic cell presents antigen to a T cell to activate it.

Mast cells lie in close apposition to meningeal nerve fibres and blood vessels, forming functional neuro-immune and vascular contacts.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

This section does not apply to these cell types for the reason that immune cells have no axons.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Reinterpreted as signalling molecules: meningeal T cells secrete the cytokines interleukin-4, interleukin-13, and interferon-gamma, which have been tied respectively to learning and memory and to social behaviour, while gamma-delta T cells release interleukin-17a, tied to anxiety-like behaviour.

Mast cells release histamine, heparin, proteases, and further cytokines on degranulation; dendritic cells signal chiefly through cytokines and presented antigen.

MYELINATION:

This section does not apply to these cell types for the reason that immune cells neither make nor receive myelin.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Low at rest and sharply higher on activation: an activated, proliferating lymphocyte switches to glycolysis to fuel clonal expansion, and a degranulating mast cell spends a burst of energy on secretion, though the resting meningeal population is metabolically quiet.

PLASTICITY:

Deeply plastic in the immune sense: lymphocytes proliferate into clones and form long-lived memory cells; dendritic cells mature from sampling to presenting states; and mast cells sensitise and re-arm.

The population composition of the meninges shifts with age, infection, and neurodegeneration, and meningeal T-cell output changes measurably with the animal's behavioural state.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Supported by the meningeal fibroblasts and the lymphatic and blood endothelial cells that build their niche, by border-associated and dural macrophages that share the territory, and by the meningeal lymphatic vessels that supply their drainage route to the cervical lymph nodes.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

These cells give the brain's border an adaptive immune brain of its own, and, remarkably, a dial on behaviour: block meningeal T-cell signalling and, in animal studies, social behaviour, spatial learning, and anxiety all shift.

Interferon-gamma from meningeal T cells raises inhibitory tone mediated by gamma-aminobutyric acid (GABA) and calms hyper-excitable prefrontal neurons that drive social behaviour, so the frontier council is quietly co-writing the mood and sociability of the tissue it guards.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Their most surprising capability is behaviour modulation from outside the barrier: signalling molecules released into the meningeal space and the cerebrospinal fluid reach neurons in the underlying cortex and adjust their excitability, so that immune cells never touching a neuron can nonetheless tune sociability, anxiety, and learning.

A second frontier capability, newly shown, is mechanical: dural mast cells at the arachnoid cuff exit points, on degranulating, release histamine that dilates bridging veins and redirects the flow and drainage of cerebrospinal fluid, making these cells physical regulators of the brain's plumbing.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Antigen drained from the brain and cerebrospinal fluid; cytokines; danger signals; and, for mast cells, allergens, neuropeptides, and complement.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Cytokines including interleukin-4, interleukin-13, interferon-gamma, and interleukin-17a; antibodies from B cells; histamine and proteases from mast cells; and presented antigen from dendritic cells carried to the cervical lymph nodes.

• STATE.

◦ Resting versus activated or degranulated; clonal and memory composition; intracellular calcium; granule load.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Lymphocytes 6 to 15 micrometres, dendritic and mast cells 10 to 20 micrometres; resting potential about minus 30 to minus 60 millivolts; population of the order of hundreds of thousands to a few million.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Mast cell degranulation seconds; calcium signalling seconds; cytokine production hours; lymphocyte clonal expansion days; immunological memory years.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Dysregulation drives meningitis and neuroinflammation; mast cell activation is implicated in migraine and in blood-brain-barrier breakdown; altered meningeal lymphatic drainage worsens Alzheimer's disease and impairs waste clearance; and autoreactive meningeal lymphocytes feature in multiple sclerosis.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

CODE SAMPLE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

A mixed council of adaptive and granule-bearing immune cells camped in the dura around the brain's own lymphatic vessels, draining antigen to the neck and - alone among the border cells - releasing cytokines that reach across the barrier to set the sociability, anxiety, and memory of the brain beneath them.


PART SEVEN. THE ENDOCRINE CELLS OF THE MAJOR GLANDS

Here the catalogue crosses from the wire to the bloodstream, from cells that deliver a message down an insulated cable to cells that tip their message into the circulation and let the heart carry it everywhere at once.

The neuron speaks a private word to one partner across a synapse a few millionths of a metre wide; the endocrine cell shouts a public word that every tissue with the right receptor may hear, seconds to hours later.

Yet the two systems are one architecture, not two, and the pituitary gland at the base of the brain is the hinge where they meet: a lump of glandular tissue hanging from the hypothalamus on a stalk, taking orders from neurons above and issuing hormonal commands to glands below.

Every cell in this Part is tagged (NON-COG), meaning it does not itself compute, remember, or attend; its trade is regulation, manufacture, and release.

But do not mistake substrate for insignificance, because the products of these cells - growth hormone, cortisol, thyroid hormone, the sex steroids, melatonin - set the operating conditions of every cognitive cell in the volumes above, tuning their metabolism, their excitability, their plasticity, and their very survival.

Remove any one of these factories and thought does not glitch so much as slowly fail, by starvation, by flood, or by the loss of the day-night clock that paces it.


SECTION 1. THE PITUITARY GLAND

The anterior pituitary, or adenohypophysis, is a true gland: a dense cord-work of hormone-manufacturing cells fed by a private portal blood supply that carries releasing factors down from the hypothalamus.

Five endocrine cell types share the tissue, each named for its product, together with a star-shaped supporting cell that is glia in all but name.

A surprise runs through this Section and is worth stating at the outset: these gland cells are electrically excitable, firing genuine calcium-based action potentials, so that hormone release here is paced by voltage spikes just as neurotransmitter release is in a neuron.


CHAPTER 51. SOMATOTROPE (Growth-Hormone Cell of the Anterior Pituitary) (The Body-Building Broadcast Cell) (NON-COG)

Somewhere behind the bridge of your nose, in a gland the size of a pea, sits the single most numerous hormone cell in your head, and its one job is to tell the whole body to grow.

Through childhood it built your long bones; through adult life it still rebuilds muscle, mobilises fat, and repairs tissue night after night.

It does its loudest work while you sleep, releasing its hormone in great pulses in the first hours of deep sleep, which is why growth is, quite literally, something children do in the dark.

Body-building names its effect: it drives the growth of bone, muscle, and soft tissue across the entire organism.

Broadcast names its reach: it releases into the bloodstream, so its word travels everywhere at once rather than to one partner.

Cell marks it as a secretory gland cell, not a neuron, despite the action potentials it fires.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The somatotrope is the growth-hormone cell of the anterior pituitary, an acidophil (a cell that stains strongly with acidic dyes because it is packed with protein-filled granules) whose product is growth hormone, also called somatotropin.

It is switched on by growth-hormone-releasing hormone and switched off by somatostatin, both arriving from the hypothalamus down the portal blood, and it releases growth hormone in pulses that drive the liver to make insulin-like growth factor 1, the messenger that actually reaches most tissues.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

A rounded to ovoid epithelial cell arranged in cords against blood sinusoids, its cytoplasm crowded with dense secretory granules and a well-developed rough endoplasmic reticulum and Golgi apparatus, the protein-export machinery of a cell whose life is manufacture.

The granules are the largest and most numerous of any pituitary cell type, which is why the somatotrope stains so densely.

SIZE:

Roughly 10 to 15 micrometres across, a micrometre being a thousandth of a millimetre, with secretory granules of about 300 to 400 nanometres, a nanometre being a millionth of a millimetre.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Functionally a graded population rather than sharp subtypes, ranging from densely to sparsely granulated forms, and overlapping at the edges with the mammosomatotrope, a hybrid cell that stores both growth hormone and prolactin and is thought to be a shared precursor of somatotropes and lactotropes.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Throughout the anterior lobe of the pituitary, concentrated in its lateral wings, sitting in cords one or two cells thick against the fenestrated (windowed) sinusoidal capillaries into which it sheds its hormone.

NUMBER:

The most abundant hormone cell of the anterior pituitary, making up roughly 50 percent of its endocrine cells, moderate confidence; against a whole anterior lobe of very roughly several million to a few tens of millions of cells, the somatotrope population is on the order of millions.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

This is an electrically excitable gland cell, a fact that still surprises: it rests near minus 50 to minus 60 millivolts and fires spontaneous plateau-bursting action potentials that persist when sodium channels are blocked with tetrodotoxin, marking them as calcium spikes carried by voltage-gated calcium channels.

Those prolonged plateau bursts admit enough calcium to trigger sustained hormone release, and large-conductance calcium-activated potassium channels, called BK channels, paradoxically lengthen the plateau here rather than cutting it short; when the depolarising drive is removed the membrane falls toward the potassium equilibrium potential near minus 85 millivolts.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

This section is reinterpreted for a gland cell: it forms no classical synapses, but it has real junctional relationships, receiving hypothalamic releasing and inhibiting hormones at its surface receptors, coupling to neighbours and to folliculostellate cells, and delivering its hormone to distant receptor-bearing target cells in bone, liver, muscle, and fat.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that the somatotrope has no axon, exporting its product through secretory granules at the cell surface rather than down a fibre.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Reinterpreted as signalling molecules, its output is the hormone growth hormone, a 191-amino-acid protein, released by calcium-triggered exocytosis and answering to the hypothalamic peptides growth-hormone-releasing hormone, somatostatin, and the stomach hormone ghrelin.

MYELINATION:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that the somatotrope has no axon to insulate.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

High biosynthetic load, characteristic of a professional protein-secreting cell that must continually rebuild its granule stores, spending its energy on transcription, translation, granule packaging, and the calcium pumping that follows each burst of electrical activity.

PLASTICITY:

The population expands and its output rises during growth spurts, pregnancy, and fasting and falls with age, so that pulsatile growth-hormone secretion declines steadily across adult life, a process called the somatopause.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Supported by the folliculostellate cells of Chapter 57, which relay coordinating signals across the gland, and fed by the fenestrated endothelial cells of the portal sinusoids that both deliver hypothalamic orders and carry away the secreted hormone.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Delete this one cell and the child does not grow, producing pituitary dwarfism, while the adult loses lean mass, bone density, and the nightly tide of repair that deep sleep is meant to bring.

Its excess does the opposite, stretching the body into gigantism before the growth plates close and into acromegaly, the coarsening and enlargement of hands, jaw, and face, after they do.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Its most remarkable trick is pulsatility: rather than dribbling hormone at a steady rate, the somatotrope population synchronises into coordinated bursts, so that growth hormone arrives at the liver in sharp pulses every few hours, and it is the pattern of the pulses, not merely their average height, that the target tissues decode.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Growth-hormone-releasing hormone and ghrelin as stimulators, somatostatin and insulin-like growth factor 1 as inhibitors, arriving through the portal blood.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Pulses of growth hormone into the systemic circulation, peaking in early deep sleep.

• STATE.

◦ Membrane potential, cytosolic calcium concentration, and the size of the readily releasable granule pool.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Resting potential minus 50 to minus 60 millivolts; roughly 50 percent of anterior pituitary endocrine cells; granules about 300 to 400 nanometres.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Plateau bursts lasting seconds; secretory pulses every 2 to 3 hours; the somatopause unfolding over decades.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Somatotroph adenoma causes acromegaly and gigantism through growth-hormone excess, while destruction or deficiency causes growth-hormone-deficient short stature in children and reduced vitality, adiposity, and bone loss in adults.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

CODE SAMPLE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

The most numerous cell of the anterior pituitary, an acidophil crammed with the largest secretory granules in the gland, firing calcium action potentials in plateau bursts and broadcasting growth hormone to the whole body in nightly pulses.


CHAPTER 52. LACTOTROPE (Prolactin Cell of the Anterior Pituitary) (The Milk-Command Cell) (NON-COG)

This is the cell that fills the breast with milk, and it is unusual among the pituitary cells in that its default state is to be held back, not urged on.

The hypothalamus keeps a constant brake on it, a steady drip of dopamine that says stop, and only when that brake is lifted - most dramatically in pregnancy and suckling - does the cell surge into full production.

It is also the cell that, when it grows into a tumour, produces the commonest hormone-secreting growth in the human head.

Milk names its purpose: it drives the manufacture of breast milk.

Command names its mode: it issues a hormonal order to the mammary gland rather than making milk itself.

Cell marks it as a secretory gland cell.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The lactotrope, also called the mammotrope, is the prolactin-secreting acidophil of the anterior pituitary, whose hormone prolactin drives the growth of the mammary gland and the production of milk.

Uniquely, it is under tonic inhibition, meaning the hypothalamus normally restrains it through dopamine, so that cutting the pituitary stalk raises prolactin rather than lowering it.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

A polygonal to ovoid acidophil with prominent rough endoplasmic reticulum and Golgi apparatus, whose secretory granules enlarge dramatically during lactation and which shows striking granule turnover with reproductive state.

SIZE:

About 10 to 15 micrometres across, with granules that in the resting state are small but in the lactating cell can swell to 600 to 700 nanometres, among the largest in the gland.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Sparsely and densely granulated forms exist, together with the mammosomatotrope hybrid that stores both prolactin and growth hormone, reflecting the shared lineage of these two acidophils.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Scattered throughout the anterior lobe, often concentrated in the posterolateral regions, sitting like the other endocrine cells against the sinusoidal capillaries.

NUMBER:

Roughly 15 to 25 percent of anterior pituitary endocrine cells in the non-pregnant adult, moderate confidence, a fraction that expands markedly during pregnancy as lactotropes multiply and enlarge under oestrogen drive.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Electrically excitable like its somatotrope cousin, resting near minus 50 to minus 60 millivolts and firing spontaneous plateau-bursting calcium action potentials that raise cytosolic calcium enough to sustain prolactin release.

Dopamine, the hypothalamic brake, acts partly by opening potassium channels and silencing this spontaneous electrical activity, hyperpolarising the cell toward the potassium equilibrium potential and shutting secretion down.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Reinterpreted for a gland cell: it forms no classical synapses but receives dopamine and thyrotropin-releasing hormone at surface receptors, couples to folliculostellate cells, and delivers prolactin to receptor-bearing target cells of the breast and beyond.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that the lactotrope has no axon.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Its signalling output is the hormone prolactin, a 199-amino-acid protein, released by calcium-triggered exocytosis and governed chiefly by inhibitory dopamine, with thyrotropin-releasing hormone and oestrogen among its stimulators.

MYELINATION:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that the lactotrope has no axon to insulate.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

High and highly variable, rising steeply in lactation when the cell must manufacture and export prolactin around the clock, drawing on the same protein-export and calcium-handling machinery as the somatotrope.

PLASTICITY:

Among the most plastic endocrine cells of the body: the lactotrope population enlarges several-fold during pregnancy and lactation under oestrogen stimulation and regresses afterward, one of the clearest examples of adult endocrine tissue remodelling.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Supported by the folliculostellate cells of Chapter 57 and by the portal sinusoidal endothelium, and controlled at a distance by the dopamine-releasing neurons of the hypothalamic arcuate nucleus.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Its output turns the breast into a milk gland and, in excess, suppresses the reproductive axis, causing absence of periods and infertility; loss of the cell impairs lactation after childbirth.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Its most surprising feature is its inverted control logic: alone among the pituitary cells it is normally switched off, so that regulation works by releasing a brake rather than pressing an accelerator, which makes any interruption of the stalk or any dopamine-blocking drug raise prolactin.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Inhibitory dopamine as the dominant signal, with thyrotropin-releasing hormone, oestrogen, and the suckling reflex as stimulators.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Prolactin into the systemic circulation, surging with suckling.

• STATE.

◦ Membrane potential, cytosolic calcium, granule store, and reproductive-state-dependent cell size and number.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Resting potential minus 50 to minus 60 millivolts; 15 to 25 percent of endocrine cells rising in pregnancy; granules up to 600 to 700 nanometres.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Suckling-evoked release within minutes; population expansion over the weeks of pregnancy; regression over weeks after weaning.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Prolactinoma, a lactotroph adenoma, is the commonest hormone-secreting pituitary tumour and causes hyperprolactinaemia, with milk discharge, absent periods, and infertility; deficiency causes failure of lactation, as in Sheehan syndrome after obstetric haemorrhage.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

CODE SAMPLE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

The one anterior pituitary cell held back by a dopamine brake rather than driven by a releasing hormone, firing calcium spikes when the brake is lifted, and the source of the commonest hormone-secreting tumour of the human head.


CHAPTER 53. THYROTROPE (Thyroid-Stimulating-Hormone Cell of the Anterior Pituitary) (The Metabolic-Thermostat Relay Cell) (NON-COG)

If the thyroid gland sets the metabolic temperature of every cell in your body, this is the cell that reads the thermostat and turns the thyroid up or down.

It is a rare cell, only about one in twenty of the pituitary's hormone cells, but it sits at a control point out of all proportion to its numbers.

Turn it off and the whole body slows, cools, and thickens; leave it running unchecked and the body races and wastes.

Metabolic thermostat names the loop it serves: the feedback control of whole-body metabolic rate.

Relay names its position: it passes the hypothalamus's order on to the thyroid gland.

Cell marks it as a secretory gland cell.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The thyrotrope is the thyroid-stimulating-hormone cell of the anterior pituitary, a basophil (a cell that stains with basic dyes) whose hormone, thyroid-stimulating hormone or thyrotropin, drives the thyroid gland to make thyroxine and triiodothyronine.

It is driven by thyrotropin-releasing hormone from the hypothalamus and switched down by the very thyroid hormones it calls forth, a textbook negative feedback loop.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

An angular to polygonal basophil, often with long cytoplasmic processes squeezing between neighbouring cells, holding small secretory granules and the usual rich protein-export apparatus.

SIZE:

About 10 to 15 micrometres across, with the smallest secretory granules of the anterior pituitary cell types, roughly 100 to 150 nanometres.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Essentially a single functional type; in states of long-standing thyroid failure the cells hypertrophy into large, vacuolated thyroidectomy cells, a state rather than a subtype.

WHERE IT LIVES:

In the anteromedial part of the anterior lobe, scattered among the more numerous cell types.

NUMBER:

The rarest of the five hormone cells, roughly 5 percent of anterior pituitary endocrine cells, moderate confidence.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Electrically excitable, resting near minus 50 to minus 60 millivolts, with action potentials seen in normal anterior pituitary cells and specifically stimulated by thyrotropin-releasing hormone, which increases electrical activity and calcium-driven hormone release.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Reinterpreted for a gland cell: no classical synapses, but surface receptors for thyrotropin-releasing hormone and for feedback thyroid hormone, gap-junctional and paracrine coupling to the folliculostellate network, and hormonal delivery to the thyroid follicular cells.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that the thyrotrope has no axon.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Its output is thyroid-stimulating hormone, a two-chain glycoprotein sharing a common alpha subunit with luteinising and follicle-stimulating hormones and carrying a unique beta subunit, released by calcium-triggered exocytosis.

MYELINATION:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that the thyrotrope has no axon to insulate.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Moderate to high, dominated by the synthesis and glycosylation (sugar-decoration) of its glycoprotein hormone, a more elaborate manufacturing task than making a simple protein hormone.

PLASTICITY:

Strongly feedback-regulated: chronic thyroid hormone deficiency drives the cells to enlarge and multiply, raising thyroid-stimulating hormone dramatically, while excess thyroid hormone shrinks and silences them.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Supported by the folliculostellate cells of Chapter 57 and the portal sinusoidal endothelium, and commanded by the thyrotropin-releasing-hormone neurons of the hypothalamic paraventricular nucleus.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Through the thyroid it sets the metabolic rate of the entire body and brain, so its failure produces the slowing, cold intolerance, weight gain, and mental sluggishness of central hypothyroidism, and in the developing infant a failure here would starve the growing brain of the thyroid hormone it cannot develop without.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Its defining capability is that it is a living feedback comparator: it continuously subtracts the circulating thyroid hormone signal from the hypothalamic drive and secretes the difference, holding whole-body metabolic rate within a narrow band, which is why blood thyroid-stimulating hormone is the single most sensitive clinical readout of thyroid status.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Stimulatory thyrotropin-releasing hormone, inhibitory thyroxine and triiodothyronine feeding back from the thyroid.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Thyroid-stimulating hormone into the systemic circulation.

• STATE.

◦ Membrane potential, cytosolic calcium, granule store, and feedback-set cell size and number.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Resting potential minus 50 to minus 60 millivolts; about 5 percent of endocrine cells; granules about 100 to 150 nanometres.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Minute-to-minute electrical response to thyrotropin-releasing hormone; hours-to-days feedback adjustment of secretion; weeks of hypertrophy in sustained thyroid failure.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Thyrotroph adenoma, a rare cause of central hyperthyroidism, and thyrotrope loss or suppression, causing central (secondary) hypothyroidism.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

CODE SAMPLE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

The rarest anterior pituitary hormone cell, a small-granuled basophil that acts as a metabolic feedback comparator, whose output is the most sensitive clinical readout of thyroid function in the body.


CHAPTER 54. GONADOTROPE (Gonad-Stimulating Cell of the Anterior Pituitary) (The Reproductive-Rhythm Cell) (NON-COG)

This cell is the switchboard of reproduction, and it speaks not in a steady tone but in a rhythm, because it takes its orders from a hypothalamic pulse generator that fires in bursts.

The frequency of those pulses is itself the message: fast pulses call up one hormone, slow pulses another, and from that timing code come puberty, the menstrual cycle, ovulation, and fertility.

It is a single cell that makes two different hormones and releases them in patterns set by a clock upstream.

Reproductive names its domain: the control of the ovaries and testes.

Rhythm names its mode: it decodes a pulsed signal, and the timing carries the meaning.

Cell marks it as a secretory gland cell.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The gonadotrope is the basophil of the anterior pituitary that makes and releases the two gonadotropins, luteinising hormone and follicle-stimulating hormone, which drive the gonads to produce sex steroids and gametes.

It is commanded by gonadotropin-releasing hormone arriving in pulses from the hypothalamus, and the frequency of those pulses biases it toward luteinising or follicle-stimulating hormone.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

A rounded basophil, often containing two granule populations, with well-developed protein-export machinery and, in the post-menopausal or castrate state, a characteristic signet-ring vacuolation.

SIZE:

About 10 to 15 micrometres across, with secretory granules of roughly 200 to 250 nanometres.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Most gonadotropes make both hormones, but subpopulations biased toward luteinising or toward follicle-stimulating hormone exist, and the balance shifts with the pulse frequency of the hypothalamic signal.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Scattered throughout the anterior lobe, frequently positioned close to lactotropes, a proximity thought to allow local paracrine cross-talk.

NUMBER:

Roughly 10 to 15 percent of anterior pituitary endocrine cells, moderate confidence.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:
