Electrically excitable, resting near minus 50 to minus 60 millivolts, but with a distinctive pattern: gonadotropes fire sharp, brief, spontaneous action potentials that on their own drive little secretion, and it is the calcium released from internal stores by gonadotropin-releasing hormone, producing large calcium oscillations, that actually triggers hormone release.

This makes the gonadotrope the pituitary cell in which store-released calcium, rather than the plateau bursts of the somatotrope, dominates secretion.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Reinterpreted for a gland cell: no classical synapses, but surface receptors for pulsed gonadotropin-releasing hormone and for feedback sex steroids and inhibin, coupling to the folliculostellate network, and hormonal delivery to the ovary and testis.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that the gonadotrope has no axon.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Its outputs are luteinising hormone and follicle-stimulating hormone, two-chain glycoproteins sharing the common alpha subunit and carrying distinct beta subunits, released by calcium-triggered exocytosis under pulsatile gonadotropin-releasing-hormone control.

MYELINATION:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that the gonadotrope has no axon to insulate.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Moderate to high, dominated by the synthesis and glycosylation of two glycoprotein hormones and by the calcium signalling that paces their release.

PLASTICITY:

Sensitive to the whole reproductive lifespan: quiescent in childhood, awakened at puberty when the hypothalamic pulse generator matures, cyclically modulated across the menstrual cycle, and disinhibited after the menopause, when falling ovarian feedback drives gonadotropin levels high.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Supported by the folliculostellate cells of Chapter 57 and the portal sinusoidal endothelium, and driven by the gonadotropin-releasing-hormone neurons of the hypothalamus, themselves paced by the kisspeptin pulse generator.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

This cell makes reproduction possible: its awakening is puberty, its rhythm is the menstrual cycle, its mid-cycle surge is ovulation, and its silence is infertility or the menopause.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Its signature capability is frequency decoding: the same cell, receiving the same molecule, produces more luteinising hormone when the pulses are fast and more follicle-stimulating hormone when they are slow, so it reads a temporal code and converts pulse frequency into hormonal balance - which is why continuous, non-pulsatile gonadotropin-releasing hormone paradoxically shuts reproduction down and is used as a medical castration.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Pulsatile gonadotropin-releasing hormone, with feedback from oestrogen, testosterone, progesterone, and inhibin.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Luteinising hormone and follicle-stimulating hormone into the systemic circulation.

• STATE.

◦ Membrane potential, cytosolic and store calcium, the two granule pools, and reproductive-stage-dependent responsiveness.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Resting potential minus 50 to minus 60 millivolts; 10 to 15 percent of endocrine cells; granules about 200 to 250 nanometres.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Gonadotropin-releasing-hormone pulses roughly every 60 to 120 minutes; calcium oscillations over seconds; the ovulatory surge over hours; the menstrual cycle over about 28 days.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Gonadotroph adenoma, usually clinically non-functioning and presenting by mass effect, and gonadotrope failure causing hypogonadotropic hypogonadism and infertility.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

CODE SAMPLE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

The one anterior pituitary cell that makes two hormones and reads a pulse-frequency code, firing sharp spikes but secreting on store-released calcium waves, whose timing sets puberty, the menstrual cycle, and fertility.


CHAPTER 55. CORTICOTROPE (Adrenocorticotropic-Hormone Cell of the Anterior Pituitary) (The Stress-Axis Trigger Cell) (NON-COG)

This is the pituitary's stress cell, the middle link in the chain that turns a frightening thought or a physical threat into a surge of cortisol coursing through the blood minutes later.

It sits at the centre of the hypothalamic-pituitary-adrenal axis, the body's master stress circuit, taking a signal from the hypothalamus and passing an amplified command down to the adrenal glands.

It carves its hormone out of a much larger parent protein, snipping one piece to send and, in other cells, other pieces for other uses.

Stress axis names the loop it anchors: the body's central response to threat.

Trigger names its role: it fires the command that launches the cortisol response.

Cell marks it as a secretory gland cell.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The corticotrope is the basophil of the anterior pituitary that secretes adrenocorticotropic hormone, which drives the adrenal cortex to make cortisol.

It is the pituitary link of the hypothalamic-pituitary-adrenal stress axis, switched on by corticotropin-releasing hormone and vasopressin from the hypothalamus and switched off by the cortisol it ultimately calls forth.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

A basophil, often star-shaped or elongated with slender processes, containing secretory granules that tend to line up beneath the cell membrane and staining strongly with a technique that detects its glycoprotein precursor.

SIZE:

About 10 to 15 micrometres across, with secretory granules of roughly 200 to 400 nanometres.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Chiefly a single anterior-lobe type; the closely related melanotrope of the intermediate lobe, treated in Chapter 56, processes the same parent protein differently.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Concentrated in the anteromedial wedge of the anterior lobe, with some cells extending into the neighbouring posterior lobe.

NUMBER:

Roughly 15 to 20 percent of anterior pituitary endocrine cells, moderate confidence.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Electrically excitable, resting near minus 50 to minus 60 millivolts, firing spontaneous action potentials and shifting into bursting activity when driven by corticotropin-releasing hormone and vasopressin, a bursting that circulating glucocorticoids inhibit as part of the feedback brake.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Reinterpreted for a gland cell: no classical synapses, but surface receptors for corticotropin-releasing hormone and vasopressin and for feedback glucocorticoids, coupling to the folliculostellate network, and hormonal delivery to the adrenal cortex.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that the corticotrope has no axon.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Its output is adrenocorticotropic hormone, a 39-amino-acid peptide cleaved from the large precursor pro-opiomelanocortin, from which the cell also releases beta-endorphin and other fragments, all by calcium-triggered exocytosis.

MYELINATION:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that the corticotrope has no axon to insulate.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Moderate, centred on the synthesis and enzymatic cleavage of the pro-opiomelanocortin precursor and the rapid mobilisation of hormone during a stress response.

PLASTICITY:

Under chronic stress or chronic loss of cortisol feedback the corticotropes enlarge and multiply, and in prolonged cortisol excess or steroid treatment they shrink and fall silent, a suppression that makes abrupt withdrawal of long-term steroids dangerous.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Supported by the folliculostellate cells of Chapter 57 and the portal sinusoidal endothelium, and driven by the corticotropin-releasing-hormone and vasopressin neurons of the hypothalamic paraventricular nucleus.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

This cell is the pivot on which the body's whole response to stress turns, so its excess produces the round face, thin skin, high blood pressure, and muscle wasting of Cushing disease, and its failure produces the fatigue, low blood pressure, and dangerous inability to withstand stress of secondary adrenal insufficiency.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Its most striking capability is precursor processing combined with feedback bursting: it takes one long protein and cuts from it exactly the fragment the anterior lobe needs, while its electrical bursting is tuned in real time by the very cortisol it commands, closing one of the fastest and most vital feedback loops in the body.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Stimulatory corticotropin-releasing hormone and vasopressin, inhibitory cortisol feedback.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Adrenocorticotropic hormone and beta-endorphin into the systemic circulation.

• STATE.

◦ Membrane potential, cytosolic calcium, precursor and granule stores, and stress-history-dependent cell size and number.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Resting potential minus 50 to minus 60 millivolts; 15 to 20 percent of endocrine cells; granules about 200 to 400 nanometres.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Bursting within seconds of a stressor; cortisol appearing in blood within minutes; a daily rhythm peaking near waking; hypertrophy over weeks of chronic stress.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Corticotroph adenoma causes Cushing disease through adrenocorticotropic-hormone excess, and after adrenal removal can grow into the aggressive Nelson syndrome; corticotrope failure causes secondary adrenal insufficiency.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

CODE SAMPLE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

The anterior pituitary cell that cuts adrenocorticotropic hormone from the pro-opiomelanocortin precursor and bursts under corticotropin-releasing hormone, the pivot of the stress axis whose excess is Cushing disease.


CHAPTER 56. MELANOTROPE OF THE INTERMEDIATE LOBE (Melanocyte-Stimulating-Hormone Cell) (The Vestigial Twilight Cell) (NON-COG)

Between the front and back lobes of the pituitary lies a thin band of tissue, the intermediate lobe, that in a frog or a lizard is a busy organ of colour change but in the adult human has all but disappeared.

Its cell, the melanotrope, is a cousin of the stress cell of the previous chapter, cutting a different set of fragments from the same parent protein.

In the human adult it is a near-vestigial remnant, present as scattered cells and cysts, a fossil of a control system our ancestors used to darken their skin.

Vestigial names its status: it is an evolutionary remnant, nearly gone in the adult human.

Twilight names both its dim activity and its home in the shadowy border between the pituitary's two great lobes.

Cell marks it as a secretory gland cell.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The melanotrope is the cell of the pituitary intermediate lobe that processes pro-opiomelanocortin, the same precursor used by the corticotrope, into melanocyte-stimulating hormone, which in many animals darkens the skin by acting on pigment cells.

In the adult human the intermediate lobe is rudimentary, so this cell is largely vestigial, though it is active in the fetus and may contribute to pigmentation and appetite signalling.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

A basophilic granular cell resembling the corticotrope, found in the thin, often cyst-riddled zone between the anterior and posterior lobes.

SIZE:

About 10 to 15 micrometres across, with secretory granules broadly similar to those of the corticotrope.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Essentially one type; in the human it exists more as scattered cells and remnants than as an organised lobe.

WHERE IT LIVES:

In the pars intermedia, the intermediate lobe, a narrow strip between the anterior and posterior pituitary, vestigial in the adult human and better developed in the fetus and in other mammals.

NUMBER:

A very small fraction of total pituitary cells in the adult human, low confidence given how reduced and variable the human intermediate lobe is.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Electrically excitable, resting near minus 50 to minus 60 millivolts and firing spontaneous action potentials, which in the intermediate lobe are directly inhibited by dopamine, the neurotransmitter that silences melanotrope electrical activity and suppresses its hormone release.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Reinterpreted for a gland cell: unusually, the melanotrope in many species receives direct innervation from dopamine-releasing nerve terminals, so its control is more neuron-like than that of the anterior-lobe cells, and it delivers its hormone into the blood to reach pigment and appetite targets.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that the melanotrope has no axon.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Its outputs are the melanocyte-stimulating hormones and related fragments cut from pro-opiomelanocortin, released by calcium-triggered exocytosis and held in check by inhibitory dopamine.

MYELINATION:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that the melanotrope has no axon to insulate.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Low in the adult human, reflecting the vestigial state of the tissue, and higher in the fetus and in species with an active intermediate lobe.

PLASTICITY:

In animals with a working intermediate lobe the melanotrope population responds to background colour and to light, but in the adult human this plasticity is largely lost with the lobe itself.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Supported by local folliculostellate-like and glial cells and, distinctively, controlled directly by dopaminergic nerve fibres rather than only by portal blood.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

In a frog or a lizard, deleting this cell removes the animal's ability to darken to its background; in the adult human its near-absence means it colours little, though excess pro-opiomelanocortin products contribute to the skin darkening seen in some adrenal and pituitary disorders.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Its most instructive feature is comparative: the same precursor that the corticotrope cuts into a stress hormone the melanotrope cuts into a pigment hormone, a vivid demonstration that one gene's product can be tailored into different messengers by the enzymes a cell happens to express.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Inhibitory dopamine delivered by direct innervation, with releasing factors in species where the lobe is active.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Melanocyte-stimulating hormone and related peptides into the blood.

• STATE.

◦ Membrane potential, cytosolic calcium, and precursor and granule stores.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Resting potential minus 50 to minus 60 millivolts; a very small and variable fraction of adult human pituitary cells.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Second-scale electrical activity gated by dopamine; slow, seasonal or background-driven changes in species with an active lobe.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Little distinct human disease given the vestigial lobe, though intermediate-lobe corticotroph tumours occur and dysregulated pro-opiomelanocortin products contribute to abnormal skin pigmentation.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

CODE SAMPLE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

A near-vestigial human cell of the intermediate lobe, directly silenced by dopaminergic nerves, that cuts pigment hormone from the same precursor the corticotrope turns into a stress hormone.


CHAPTER 57. FOLLICULOSTELLATE CELL (Sustentacular Cell of the Anterior Pituitary) (The Gland-Wide Wave Relay Cell) (NON-COG)

Threaded among the pituitary's hormone factories is a second, hidden cell that makes no hormone at all, and for a long time it was dismissed as mere packing material.

It is now known to be the gland's own nervous system in miniature: a network of star-shaped cells holding hands through electrical junctions, over which waves of calcium ripple from one end of the pituitary to the other.

This is the cell that lets the scattered endocrine cells act as a coordinated whole rather than as isolated islands, and it is glia in all but name.

Gland-wide names its reach: its coupled network spans the entire anterior pituitary.

Wave relay names its trick: it passes calcium waves from cell to cell, the way an astrocyte network does in the brain.

Cell marks it as a supporting cell, not a hormone cell.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The folliculostellate cell is the star-shaped, non-hormonal sustentacular (supporting) cell of the anterior pituitary, arranged into a gap-junction-coupled network that relays calcium waves and paracrine signals across the gland.

It supports and coordinates the endocrine cells around it, and because it expresses the marker protein S-100 and behaves like astroglia, it is often called the pituitary's resident glia.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

An irregular, star-shaped cell sending long processes between the endocrine cell cords and around small colloid-filled follicles, joined to its fellows by gap junctions and by tight junctions and holding no secretory granules.

SIZE:

The cell body is on the order of 10 micrometres across, but its processes extend far further, weaving a three-dimensional network across the whole gland.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

A heterogeneous population, some cells acting as agranular support and scavenger cells, some as stem or progenitor cells, and some as immune-like antigen-presenting cells.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Throughout the anterior lobe, distributed among all the endocrine cell types and lining the small follicles from which the cell takes its name, sparse in number but reaching everywhere through its processes.

NUMBER:

Roughly 5 to 10 percent of anterior pituitary cells, moderate confidence.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Unlike its endocrine neighbours the folliculostellate cell is not a classical spiking cell, but it is electrically and chemically excitable, generating and propagating cytosolic calcium waves through its gap-junction-coupled network at a slow measured velocity of about 17.7 micrometres per second.

Its resting membrane potential sits in the range typical of a coupled glial network, and it responds to signals such as acetylcholine and the immune signalling molecule interleukin-6 with calcium rises that spread to its neighbours.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Reinterpreted for a supporting cell: its defining junctions are the gap junctions, built from connexin proteins including connexin 43, that electrically and chemically couple the network, allowing calcium, cyclic adenosine monophosphate, and inositol trisphosphate to pass between cells; it also forms tight junctions around the follicles and makes intimate paracrine contact with every endocrine cell type.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that the folliculostellate cell has no axon, communicating instead through gap junctions and diffusible factors.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Reinterpreted as signalling molecules, it releases paracrine factors including interleukin-6, vascular endothelial growth factor, basic fibroblast growth factor, and nitric oxide, and it relays messages chiefly by passing small signalling molecules through its gap junctions.

MYELINATION:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that the folliculostellate cell has no axon to insulate.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Modest and support-oriented, spending its energy on scavenging, on the manufacture of paracrine factors, and on maintaining the ionic conditions and calcium signalling of its network.

PLASTICITY:

The network remodels with the gland, its coupling and its paracrine output changing with physiological state and inflammation, and its progenitor members can contribute new cells to the pituitary through life.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

As the gland's own support cell it is largely self-supporting, relying on the portal sinusoidal endothelium for nutrients and interacting reciprocally with the endocrine cells it serves.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Without this cell the anterior pituitary would be a bag of independent endocrine cells; with it, the gland becomes a coordinated organ whose scattered cell types can be recruited together, their secretion synchronised and modulated across the whole tissue.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Its signature capability is long-distance communication by calcium wave: a stimulus applied to one folliculostellate cell launches a calcium wave that travels through the gap-junction network across the entire gland, a route that lets one corner of the pituitary talk to another far faster and more directly than hormones diffusing through blood ever could, exactly as astrocyte networks relay calcium waves in the brain.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Acetylcholine, interleukin-6, and other paracrine and immune signals from endocrine cells and the circulation.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Propagated calcium waves through gap junctions and secreted paracrine factors including interleukin-6, vascular endothelial growth factor, and nitric oxide.

• STATE.

◦ Cytosolic calcium concentration and the coupling state of the gap-junction network.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ 5 to 10 percent of anterior pituitary cells; calcium-wave velocity about 17.7 micrometres per second; coupling through connexin 43 and related connexins.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Calcium waves propagating over seconds across the gland; paracrine and inflammatory modulation over minutes to hours.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Folliculostellate cells give rise to the rare tumour once called folliculostellate-cell tumour, now recognised among pituicytomas and spindle-cell oncocytomas, and their dysfunction disturbs the paracrine coordination and immune signalling of the gland.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

CODE SAMPLE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

The star-shaped, granule-free, S-100-positive support cell of the anterior pituitary, coupled by gap junctions into a gland-wide network that relays calcium waves at about 17.7 micrometres per second - the pituitary's astrocyte.


CROSS-REFERENCE. The Posterior Pituitary.

The posterior pituitary, or neurohypophysis, holds no endocrine cell bodies at all, and so has no chapter of its own in this Section.

Its two hormones, oxytocin and vasopressin, are not made here but are manufactured in the brain, in the magnocellular neurosecretory cells of the hypothalamus, and carried down their long axons to be stored and released from nerve terminals in the posterior lobe.

Those cells hold their full chapter in the neuron catalogue at Chapter 90, and their release into the blood is physically throttled by the pituicytes, the specialised astrocytes of Part One, which wrap or unwrap the terminals to open or close the tap.

The posterior pituitary is therefore best understood not as a gland but as a piece of brain hung on a stalk, the place where the nervous and endocrine systems most visibly become one.


SECTION 2. THE PINEAL GLAND

The pineal gland is a tiny pinecone-shaped organ buried near the centre of the brain, and it is the body's clock face turned to hormone: it translates the daily signal of night into a chemical, melatonin, that tells every tissue the sun has gone down.

Its working cell, the pinealocyte, is a distant descendant of a light-sensing photoreceptor, and its supporting cell is an astrocyte in all but name.

Because the pinealocyte is a genuine neuron-gland hybrid, it already holds a full chapter in the neuron catalogue, and is treated here only in brief.


CHAPTER 58. PINEALOCYTE (Melatonin Cell of the Pineal Gland) (The Hormone-of-Darkness Cell) (NON-COG) - CROSS-REFERENCE, neuron catalogue Chapter 283

Deep between the two hemispheres sits a gland that does one beautiful thing: it turns darkness into a molecule.

Each night, on a signal relayed from the body's master clock, this cell pours melatonin into the blood, and every organ reads the message that it is night.

The cell is the biological heir of an eye, a photoreceptor that long ago stopped seeing light directly and instead learned to report its absence.

Hormone of darkness names its product: melatonin, released at night and suppressed by light.

Cell marks it as a secretory gland cell of photoreceptor descent.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The pinealocyte is the melatonin-secreting cell of the pineal gland, a modified photoreceptor descendant that converts the clock's night signal into the hormone of darkness; its full treatment, as a neuron-gland hybrid, lives in the neuron catalogue at Chapter 283, and this chapter is deliberately brief.

It receives, through a long indirect nerve pathway from the eyes and the master clock, a nightly noradrenergic signal that switches on melatonin synthesis.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

A pale, ovoid cell with long processes ending in club-shaped expansions on blood vessels, retaining faint vestiges of the photoreceptor apparatus of its evolutionary ancestors.

SIZE:

The cell body is on the order of 10 to 15 micrometres across, with processes extending to nearby capillaries.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Chiefly one type in the human, though pinealocytes differ from cell to cell in how strongly they express the melatonin-making enzymes.

WHERE IT LIVES:

In the pineal gland, a small midline organ near the roof of the third ventricle, arranged in cords and clusters among blood vessels and supporting cells.

NUMBER:

The great majority of pineal cells, in a gland only a few millimetres across weighing on the order of 100 to 150 milligrams; a precise cell count is not well established, so confidence is low.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

The pinealocyte carries a membrane potential and shows calcium signalling, and as a photoreceptor descendant it retains excitable machinery, but its output is paced by the noradrenergic night signal and its internal clock rather than by a train of action potentials.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Reinterpreted for a gland cell: it receives sympathetic noradrenergic nerve terminals carrying the night signal and delivers melatonin into the bloodstream and the cerebrospinal fluid to reach receptors throughout the body and brain.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that the pinealocyte has no axon, releasing its hormone from vascular end-feet.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Its signalling output is melatonin, and its immediate input transmitter is noradrenaline acting on alpha-1 and beta-1 adrenergic receptors.

MYELINATION:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that the pinealocyte has no axon to insulate.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Marked by a strong daily rhythm, rising at night when the synthesis machinery runs and melatonin output climbs up to roughly a hundredfold.

PLASTICITY:

Its output tracks day length and shifts with the seasons and with the circadian phase, and its nightly melatonin peak declines gradually with age.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Supported by the pineal interstitial glial cells of Chapter 59 and by the gland's fenestrated capillaries.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

This cell gives the body its hormonal night, timing sleep and the seasonal and daily rhythms of physiology; its loss flattens the melatonin signal and disturbs sleep timing.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Its signature capability is enzymatic light-to-hormone conversion: at night, adrenergic signalling raises calcium and cyclic adenosine monophosphate and activates the rate-limiting enzyme aralkylamine N-acetyltransferase up to many-fold, which acetylates serotonin to N-acetylserotonin, and acetylserotonin O-methyltransferase then methylates that to melatonin, so the cell literally manufactures the chemical meaning of darkness.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Noradrenaline from sympathetic terminals carrying the night signal from the master clock.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Melatonin into blood and cerebrospinal fluid at night.

• STATE.

◦ Activity of the enzyme aralkylamine N-acetyltransferase and the intracellular calcium and cyclic adenosine monophosphate that drive it.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Night-time rise in melatonin synthesis up to roughly a hundredfold; gland mass about 100 to 150 milligrams.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ A daily rhythm with melatonin high through the biological night; seasonal changes over months; decline over years with age.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Pineal tumours such as pineocytoma and pineoblastoma, pineal cysts, and pineal calcification, together with the melatonin-rhythm disruption of ageing, shift work, and light at night.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

CODE SAMPLE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

A photoreceptor's descendant that no longer sees but instead manufactures the hormone of darkness, secreting melatonin each night through a two-enzyme pathway - fully catalogued as a neuron-gland hybrid at Chapter 283.


CHAPTER 59. PINEAL INTERSTITIAL (GLIAL) CELL (Astrocyte-Like Support Cell of the Pineal Gland) (The Clock-Tower Keeper Cell) (NON-COG)

Every gland needs a caretaker cell, and the pineal is no exception: among its melatonin-making cells sits a quieter partner that holds the tissue together and keeps its conditions right.

It is an astrocyte in nearly all respects, the same star-shaped glial cell found throughout the brain, here posted to the clock tower.

It makes no hormone, but without it the pinealocytes would have no scaffold, no ionic housekeeping, and no defence.

Clock tower names its posting: the pineal, the gland of the body's daily clock.

Keeper names its trade: it maintains and defends the tissue rather than secreting from it.

Cell marks it as a supporting glial cell.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The pineal interstitial cell is the astrocyte-like supporting cell scattered among the pinealocytes, providing structural scaffolding, ionic and metabolic housekeeping, and defence for the melatonin-making tissue.

It is glia proper, expressing the astrocyte marker glial fibrillary acidic protein, and it plays no direct part in making the hormone.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

An elongated or star-shaped cell with slender processes threading between the pinealocytes and around the gland's blood vessels, containing the intermediate-filament bundles typical of astrocytes.

SIZE:

On the order of 10 micrometres in the cell body, with processes extending among the surrounding cells.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

A minor population of microglia and other immune cells accompanies these astrocyte-like interstitial cells, but the interstitial cell proper is essentially one astroglial type.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Between the pinealocytes throughout the pineal gland, concentrated along the connective-tissue septa and around blood vessels.

NUMBER:

A minority of pineal cells, perhaps roughly 5 to 10 percent, low confidence given how little the human count is documented.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

A non-spiking glial cell, resting at a strongly negative membrane potential near minus 80 millivolts set by a high potassium permeability, as astrocytes throughout the nervous system do, and capable of calcium signalling rather than action potentials.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Reinterpreted for a glial cell: it forms no classical synapses but makes intimate contact with pinealocytes and their vascular end-feet, buffering their environment much as an astrocyte tends its neurons.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that the pineal interstitial cell has no axon.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Reinterpreted as signalling molecules, it can release and take up small gliotransmitters and buffer extracellular ions, but it secretes no hormone.

MYELINATION:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that the pineal interstitial cell has no axon to insulate and does not myelinate.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Modest and support-oriented, spent on ionic buffering, metabolic support of the pinealocytes, and maintenance of the tissue scaffold.

PLASTICITY:

Like astrocytes elsewhere it can become reactive in injury and disease and it participates in the calcification and cyst formation that mark the ageing pineal.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Largely self-supporting as a glial cell, drawing nutrients from the pineal capillaries and working alongside the resident immune cells of the gland.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

By holding the pineal's architecture and internal conditions steady it lets the pinealocytes do their timing work; its reaction to injury contributes to the gritty calcified deposits, the so-called brain sand, of the older pineal.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Its most notable capability, shared with astrocytes across the brain, is calcium-based signalling and homeostatic buffering: it holds potassium, pH, and the local chemical milieu steady around the rhythm-generating pinealocytes, a quiet housekeeping that the clock depends on.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Extracellular ions, metabolites, and injury signals from the surrounding tissue.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Ionic buffering, metabolic support, and reactive responses to injury.

• STATE.

◦ Membrane potential, intracellular calcium, and reactive versus resting status.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Resting potential near minus 80 millivolts; perhaps 5 to 10 percent of pineal cells.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Second-scale calcium signalling; slow reactive change over days after injury; calcification over years.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Reactive gliosis around pineal tumours and cysts, and a contribution to the progressive calcification of the ageing gland.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

CODE SAMPLE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

The pineal's own astrocyte, a glial-fibrillary-acidic-protein-positive support cell resting near minus 80 millivolts among the melatonin cells, keeper of the tissue in which the body's clock is written into hormone.


SECTION 3. THE THYROID AND PARATHYROID GLANDS

Below the voice box, wrapped around the windpipe like a butterfly resting on a branch, sit two glands whose cells do not think but set the terms on which every thinking cell operates.

The thyroid sets the metabolic rate of the entire body and brain, and the parathyroid governs, minute by minute, the concentration of calcium in the blood - the ion without which no synapse can fire and no muscle can twitch.

Four cell types share this small territory: the follicular cell and the parafollicular cell of the thyroid, and the chief cell and the oxyphil cell of the parathyroid.

They are all tagged (NON-COG), because their trade is hormonal regulation rather than computation, yet the developing brain is destroyed without the first of them and every neuron in the body falls silent without the third.


CHAPTER 60. THYROID FOLLICULAR CELL (Thyrocyte) (The Thermostat-Setting Cell) (NON-COG)

Imagine a single dial that sets the burn rate of every cell you own, and imagine that the hand which turns it is a lake of protein locked behind a wall of cells, iodinated drop by drop and then swallowed back whole.

That lake is the follicle, and the wall is a sphere of thyroid follicular cells, the only cells in the human body that concentrate the trace element iodine and forge it into a hormone.

Turn the dial too low in an adult and thought slows, the pulse drops, and the body chills; turn it too low in an infant and the brain never finishes building itself.

No other cell writes so quietly across so much of physiology.

Thermostat names what it sets: the resting metabolic rate of the whole organism, the background burn against which every other regulation plays.

Setting names its slowness: this is a set-point cell working over days and weeks, not a spiking cell working over milliseconds.

Cell marks it as the humble epithelial worker at the base of that grand control.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The thyroid follicular cell, or thyrocyte, is a cuboidal epithelial cell that arranges itself with thousands of its fellows into a hollow ball called a follicle, the ball filled with a protein gel called colloid.

Its job is to manufacture the thyroid hormones thyroxine (T4) and triiodothyronine (T3), the master regulators of basal metabolic rate, heat production, growth, and - critically - the development of the fetal and infant brain.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The cell is polarised, meaning its two ends do different jobs: the basolateral membrane faces the blood and imports iodide and the hormone-driving signal, while the apical membrane faces the colloid inside the follicle and does the chemistry.

At rest the cell is flat and squamous, but under stimulation it grows tall and columnar, its interior crowded with rough endoplasmic reticulum for making protein and lysosomes for digesting it back.

SIZE:

The follicular cell is roughly 15 micrometres tall when active and much flatter when quiet, a micrometre being a thousandth of a millimetre.

The follicle it builds is typically 100 to 300 micrometres across, so that a single follicle is a structure visible to a good light microscope but not to the naked eye.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

There are no accepted molecular subtypes of the follicular cell, but there is a dramatic functional continuum, from the flat, quiescent, colloid-storing state to the tall, active, colloid-resorbing state.

Treat the cell as one type in two postures rather than as a family.

WHERE IT LIVES:

It lives in the two lobes and connecting isthmus of the thyroid gland, which sits in the front of the neck just below the larynx and wraps around the front of the trachea (the windpipe).

The interface it guards is chemical rather than spatial: the apical membrane is the border between the cell's cytoplasm and the extracellular colloid lake where iodination happens outside the cell.

NUMBER:

The adult thyroid weighs roughly 15 to 25 grams and contains on the order of many tens of millions of follicular cells, distributed across hundreds of thousands of follicles, an estimate with low precision because no exact census exists.

Confidence here is low on the absolute count but high on the order of magnitude.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

The follicular cell is non-excitable, resting near minus 50 millivolts, and it does not fire action potentials.

Its true signalling currency is not voltage but the second messenger cyclic adenosine monophosphate (cyclic AMP), raised when thyroid-stimulating hormone (TSH) binds its receptor, which is a G-protein coupled receptor of the stimulatory class.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

This cell forms no synapses, but it is a hormone-receptor target: it carries the TSH receptor on its blood-facing membrane and answers to TSH released by the thyrotropes of the anterior pituitary.

It couples to its neighbours through tight junctions that seal the follicle, so that the colloid lake stays chemically separate from the blood.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that the follicular cell is an epithelial secretory cell with no axon and no projection of any kind.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

The cell's signalling output is hormonal, not synaptic: it secretes T4 (about 80 to 100 micrograms per day) and a smaller amount of T3 directly into the capillary blood.

Its key manufacturing molecules are thyroglobulin, the large protein scaffold on which hormone is built; thyroid peroxidase, the enzyme that iodinates and couples tyrosine residues; and the sodium-iodide symporter, the pump that traps iodide from the blood.

MYELINATION:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that myelin insulates axons and this cell has none.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

The cell is a busy protein factory and iodide concentrator, spending energy chiefly to run the sodium-iodide symporter, which trades on the sodium gradient maintained by the sodium-potassium pump, and to synthesise and recycle large quantities of thyroglobulin.

Its demand rises steeply under TSH drive and falls when the gland is suppressed.

PLASTICITY:

The gland shows dramatic structural plasticity: sustained TSH or iodine deficiency drives the cells to enlarge and multiply, producing a goitre (a visibly swollen thyroid).

At the single-cell level the follicular cell is one of the few adult human cells that continues to divide slowly throughout life, roughly a handful of times over a lifetime.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

The cell is supported by a dense basket of fenestrated (windowed) capillaries lined by endothelial cells, which deliver iodide and carry away hormone.

The parafollicular cells of Chapter 61 share its tissue, and connective-tissue fibroblasts and immune cells complete the local environment.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Delete this cell and metabolic rate collapses: the adult becomes cold, slow, forgetful, and swollen with the mucin of myxoedema, while the untreated infant suffers the permanent intellectual disability once called cretinism.

The thyroid follicular cell is the clearest single-cell demonstration that the brain's ability to think depends on a gland in the neck it will never sense.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The follicular cell's astonishing trick is extracellular chemistry: it exports thyroglobulin and iodide onto its apical surface and assembles hormone outside itself, in the colloid, then swallows the finished product back by pinocytosis and digests it in lysosomes to free T4 and T3.

It is one of the very few cells that store months of their own product outside the cell, as a colloid reservoir large enough to keep the body supplied for weeks even if synthesis stops.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ TSH from the pituitary, typically 0.4 to 4.0 milli-international-units per litre of blood; dietary iodide, requirement about 150 micrograms per day in adults.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ T4 about 80 to 100 micrograms per day and T3 about 5 to 10 micrograms per day, released into the blood; circulating free T4 roughly 12 to 22 picomoles per litre.

• STATE.

◦ Cell height (flat to columnar), colloid volume, intracellular cyclic AMP level, stored thyroglobulin mass.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Resting potential near minus 50 millivolts; follicle diameter 100 to 300 micrometres; iodide trapped against a gradient of roughly 20 to 40 fold.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ TSH-to-hormone response over hours; colloid store buffers weeks; the circulating half-life of T4 is about 7 days and of T3 about 1 day.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Hypothyroidism, most commonly the autoimmune Hashimoto thyroiditis, and in the newborn congenital hypothyroidism, the leading preventable cause of intellectual disability, caught by newborn screening.

◦ Hyperthyroidism, most commonly Graves disease, in which an antibody mimics TSH and drives the cell relentlessly; and thyroid carcinoma arising from these cells.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

CODE SAMPLE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

A cuboidal cell arranged in a hollow, colloid-filled sphere, the only human cell that concentrates iodine and builds its hormone outside itself in an extracellular lake, then swallows it back to set the burn rate of the whole body.


CHAPTER 61. PARAFOLLICULAR (C) CELL (The Calcium-Lowering Coast-Guard Cell) (NON-COG)

Scattered between the follicles of the thyroid, never touching the colloid, sits a quieter cell with a wholly separate ancestry and a wholly separate mission.

The parafollicular cell, or C cell, is not a metabolic thermostat but a calcium sentinel, releasing the hormone calcitonin when blood calcium climbs too high.

Its role in adult humans is so mild that people whose thyroid is entirely removed, and who therefore lose all their C cells, show no calcium disorder at all - a rare example of a hormone cell the body can apparently do without.

Yet the cell endures, a neural-crest immigrant lodged in an unrelated gland, and it turns dangerous when it becomes the cancer called medullary thyroid carcinoma.

Calcium-Lowering names its one clear action: pushing blood calcium down, the mirror image of the parathyroid.

Coast-Guard names its posture: a standby responder that matters only when the tide of calcium runs high, largely idle the rest of the time.

Cell marks it as a single scattered secretory unit, not a follicle-builder.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The parafollicular cell, universally abbreviated to C cell (C for calcitonin), is a neuroendocrine cell that secretes the hormone calcitonin, which lowers blood calcium chiefly by restraining the bone-dissolving osteoclasts.

It is a member of the diffuse neuroendocrine system, the scattered empire of hormone cells that share an amine-handling chemistry and, in this case, a neural-crest origin.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The C cell is a large, pale, rounded cell lying within the follicular wall but not reaching the colloid, its cytoplasm filled with small dense-core secretory granules of calcitonin.

It is revealed by immunostaining for calcitonin rather than by ordinary stains, in which it merely looks clear.

SIZE:

The cell body is roughly 12 to 18 micrometres across, somewhat larger than the follicular cells around it.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

No formal subtypes are recognised, though C cells vary in granule content and are more numerous in the central and upper thyroid lobes where the embryonic ultimobranchial tissue fused into the gland.

WHERE IT LIVES:

It lives inside the thyroid, tucked against the basement membrane of the follicles, concentrated in the middle third of each lobe.

Its developmental interface is notable: unlike the endoderm-derived follicular cells, the C cell descends from the neural crest, the same migratory embryonic population that builds sympathetic neurons and the adrenal medulla, so it is an anatomical bridge between the nervous and endocrine systems.

NUMBER:

C cells are a small minority of thyroid cells, on the order of a few hundred thousand in the adult gland, with low confidence on the exact figure.

They are proportionally far more abundant in the newborn than in the adult.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Unlike the follicular cell, the C cell is a genuine neuroendocrine cell and is electrically excitable, resting near minus 50 to minus 60 millivolts and capable of calcium-based action potentials that trigger secretion.

It senses extracellular calcium directly through the calcium-sensing receptor and secretes when calcium rises.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

The C cell forms no classical synapses, but it is a hormone-receptor system: it reads blood calcium through the calcium-sensing receptor and outputs calcitonin onto receptors on osteoclasts and kidney tubule cells.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that the C cell has no axon despite its neural-crest ancestry.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

The cell's signalling output is the hormone calcitonin, a 32-amino-acid peptide; C cells also contain and can release calcitonin-gene-related peptide and other peptides, reflecting their neuroendocrine character.

MYELINATION:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that the cell has no axon to insulate.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

The C cell is a modest peptide-secreting cell, its energy budget dominated by synthesising and packaging calcitonin, and it is not a heavy energy consumer compared with the iodide-pumping follicular cell.

PLASTICITY:

C cells can proliferate into a benign overgrowth called C-cell hyperplasia, which is a recognised precursor to medullary thyroid carcinoma, especially in the inherited multiple endocrine neoplasia type 2 syndromes driven by mutations in the RET gene.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

The C cell is supported by the same capillary bed and connective-tissue framework that serves the follicular cells, and it sits within the follicular basement membrane that the follicular cells build.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove every C cell, as thyroid surgery does, and almost nothing measurable changes in adult calcium balance, because the parathyroid and vitamin D systems dominate that control.

The cell's true signature on its region is diagnostic and oncological: calcitonin in the blood is a sensitive marker for the tumour these cells can become.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The C cell's most surprising feature is evolutionary rather than computational: in fish the same calcitonin-producing cells form a separate organ called the ultimobranchial gland, and in mammals they migrated into the thyroid, so the human C cell is a fossil of a once-independent endocrine organ now boarding inside another.

Its calcitonin is far more powerful in young, growing bone than in the adult, hinting at a developmental role we still understand poorly.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Extracellular ionized calcium, normally about 1.1 to 1.3 millimoles per litre, sensed by the calcium-sensing receptor; also gut hormones such as gastrin.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Calcitonin, basal plasma level under about 10 picograms per millilitre, rising with high calcium.

• STATE.

◦ Granule stores, intracellular calcium, membrane excitability.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Resting potential near minus 50 to minus 60 millivolts; cell diameter 12 to 18 micrometres.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Secretion responds to a calcium rise within minutes; calcitonin's plasma half-life is about 10 minutes.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Medullary thyroid carcinoma, the malignancy of the C cell, sporadic or inherited as part of multiple endocrine neoplasia type 2; C-cell hyperplasia as its precursor.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

CODE SAMPLE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

A large pale calcitonin-filled cell of neural-crest origin, scattered among thyroid follicles it never joins, a migrated fossil of a fish organ whose adult job is so slight the body barely misses it - until it turns to cancer.


CHAPTER 62. PARATHYROID CHIEF CELL (Principal Cell) (The Calcium-Thermostat Cell) (NON-COG)

Of all the endocrine cells in the body, this may be the one whose failure kills fastest.

The parathyroid chief cell is the minute-to-minute governor of the calcium concentration in your blood, and calcium is the ion on which every nerve impulse, every heartbeat, and every muscle contraction directly depends.

Four glands the size of grains of rice, buried on the back of the thyroid, hold these cells, and if a surgeon accidentally removes all four the patient's muscles seize in tetany within a day.

The chief cell watches calcium with a receptor so sensitive it responds to shifts of a few percent, and it corrects them by dialling a single hormone up or down.

Calcium-Thermostat names its function exactly: it holds blood calcium at a set point the way a thermostat holds a room's temperature.

Cell marks the individual worker behind that whole-body constant.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The parathyroid chief cell, also called the principal cell, secretes parathyroid hormone (PTH), the dominant minute-to-minute regulator of blood calcium.

When calcium falls, the cell releases PTH, which pulls calcium out of bone, reclaims it in the kidney, and activates vitamin D to absorb more from food; when calcium rises, the cell goes quiet.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The chief cell is small and polygonal, with a large central nucleus and pale cytoplasm holding secretory granules of PTH, glycogen stores, and lipid droplets.

The cells pack in dense cords and sheets threaded by capillaries.

SIZE:

The chief cell is about 6 to 10 micrometres across, one of the smaller endocrine cells in the body.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Classical histology recognises light and dark chief cells, reflecting different secretory activity and glycogen content, but these are activity states of one cell type rather than distinct lineages.

WHERE IT LIVES:

It lives in the four (occasionally more) parathyroid glands, usually embedded on the back surface of the two thyroid lobes.

Its critical interface is with the capillary blood it constantly samples, and its uncomfortable anatomical fact is that it is easily mistaken for fat or thyroid tissue and accidentally removed during thyroid surgery.

NUMBER:

The four glands together weigh only about 120 to 160 milligrams and hold on the order of tens of millions of chief cells, an estimate of moderate confidence.

Their tiny mass belies their outsised control.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

The chief cell rests near minus 50 to minus 70 millivolts and is only weakly excitable; its regulation is unusual in that a rise in calcium inhibits secretion rather than driving it.

Extracellular calcium binds the calcium-sensing receptor, a G-protein coupled receptor, which then suppresses PTH release - so this cell's controlling signal runs backwards compared with most secretory cells.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

The chief cell forms no synapses; it is a pure hormone-receptor cell, reading blood calcium through the calcium-sensing receptor and writing PTH onto receptors in bone and kidney.

Its set point, the calcium level at which PTH secretion is half-maximal, sits near an ionized calcium of about 1.1 to 1.2 millimoles per litre.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that the chief cell is a compact secretory cell with no axon.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

The cell's signalling output is the hormone PTH, an 84-amino-acid peptide, secreted from stored granules within seconds to minutes of a fall in calcium.

MYELINATION:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that the cell has no axon to insulate.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

The chief cell is a steady, moderate energy user, its budget going to continuous synthesis, packaging, and regulated release of PTH and to running the calcium-sensing machinery.

Unusually, it also degrades much of the PTH it makes when calcium is high, so part of its metabolic effort is spent destroying its own product.

PLASTICITY:

Chronic low calcium or kidney failure drives the glands to enlarge and the chief cells to multiply, producing secondary and then autonomous tertiary hyperparathyroidism.

A single chief cell that mutates and escapes calcium control produces the benign adenoma responsible for most primary hyperparathyroidism.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

The chief cell is supported by a rich capillary network that lets it sample blood calcium continuously, and by the oxyphil cells of Chapter 63 and connective-tissue stroma sharing the gland.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Lose these cells and calcium falls until nerves and muscles become hyperexcitable, producing the cramping, spasm, and airway-closing tetany of hypoparathyroidism.

Overdrive them and calcium climbs until bones soften, kidney stones form, and the nervous system dulls into the classic picture summarised as bones, stones, groans, and psychiatric moans.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The chief cell's remarkable feature is a control law almost unique among secretory cells: it is switched off by its regulated variable rather than on by it, using the calcium-sensing receptor as an inverse sensor, and it responds within seconds, faster than gene transcription, by releasing pre-made hormone and by throttling intracellular degradation of PTH.

This gives blood calcium one of the tightest homeostatic controls in physiology, held within a few percent day and night.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Extracellular ionized calcium, normally about 1.1 to 1.3 millimoles per litre; also active vitamin D and blood phosphate, which tune PTH gene expression.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ PTH, normal plasma range roughly 10 to 65 picograms per millilitre, rising steeply as calcium falls.

• STATE.

◦ Stored PTH granule mass, calcium-sensing receptor occupancy, rate of intracellular PTH degradation.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Set point (half-maximal secretion) near ionized calcium 1.1 to 1.2 millimoles per litre; resting potential minus 50 to minus 70 millivolts; cell diameter 6 to 10 micrometres.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Secretory response within seconds to minutes; PTH plasma half-life about 2 to 4 minutes, one of the shortest of any hormone, which is what allows minute-to-minute control.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Primary hyperparathyroidism, usually a single-cell adenoma; secondary and tertiary hyperparathyroidism from kidney disease; and hypoparathyroidism, most often surgical, causing dangerous tetany.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

CODE SAMPLE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

A tiny polygonal cell in a rice-grain gland that governs the whole body's blood calcium by an inverse rule - switched off by the very ion it defends - releasing the shortest-lived major hormone in the body to keep every nerve and muscle able to fire.


CHAPTER 63. PARATHYROID OXYPHIL CELL (The Idle Mitochondrial Furnace Cell) (NON-COG)

Beside the busy chief cells sits a larger, deeper-staining cell whose purpose remains, after more than a century of study, genuinely uncertain.

The parathyroid oxyphil cell is crammed so full of mitochondria - the cell's energy-generating organelles - that it stains an intense pink, yet it appears to make little or no hormone and to respond to nothing.

It is nearly absent in childhood and accumulates steadily with age, so that it reads more like a marker of the passing years than a working part of the gland.

The oxyphil cell is one of the honest puzzles of endocrine histology, a cell defined by an organelle it does not obviously use.

Mitochondrial Furnace names its defining feature: a cytoplasm packed almost solid with mitochondria, the pink furnaces that give it its name.

Idle names the mystery: all that machinery with no confirmed job.

Cell marks the individual unit of this enigmatic second population.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The parathyroid oxyphil cell is the second cell type of the parathyroid gland, larger than the chief cell and packed with mitochondria, whose function is still debated.

Long assumed to be inert, it has recently been shown by some studies to contain the parathyroid hormone machinery and possibly to secrete PTH and PTH-related peptide under certain conditions, especially in kidney disease, though its everyday role remains unclear.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The oxyphil cell is large and rounded with a small dark nucleus and an intensely eosinophilic (pink-staining) cytoplasm, the colour arising from a dense mass of mitochondria that nearly fills the cell.

It occurs singly and in clusters among the chief cells.

SIZE:

The oxyphil cell is about 8 to 12 micrometres across, distinctly larger than the neighbouring chief cells.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Transitional oxyphil cells, intermediate between chief and oxyphil in appearance, are described, suggesting the oxyphil may be an aged or transformed state of the chief cell rather than a separate lineage.

WHERE IT LIVES:

It lives in the same four parathyroid glands as the chief cell, interspersed among the chief-cell cords, with no separate territory of its own.

NUMBER:

Oxyphil cells are a minority of parathyroid cells and are essentially absent in early childhood, appearing around puberty and increasing steadily thereafter, so their number is strongly age-dependent and hard to fix precisely.

Confidence on any single count is low.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

The oxyphil cell rests at a membrane potential comparable to the chief cell, near minus 50 to minus 70 millivolts, and is not known to be electrically active in any distinctive way.

It expresses the calcium-sensing receptor, so in principle it can read blood calcium as the chief cell does.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

The oxyphil cell forms no synapses; where it secretes at all it acts as a hormone-receptor cell, and its main relationship is proximity to the chief cells and the shared capillary supply.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that the oxyphil cell is a compact secretory cell with no axon.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Where active, the oxyphil cell's signalling output is the same as the chief cell's, namely PTH and related peptides; in its common quiescent state it releases little or nothing.

MYELINATION:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that the cell has no axon to insulate.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Here lies the paradox: the cell is stuffed with mitochondria, the very organelles of energy production, yet shows little secretory work to spend that energy on, and its oxidative machinery may reflect ageing or metabolic derangement rather than a high functional workload.

PLASTICITY:

Oxyphil cells accumulate with age and expand markedly in chronic kidney disease and long-standing secondary hyperparathyroidism, where oxyphil-rich glands are common, hinting that they can be recruited when the gland is chronically driven.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Like the chief cell, the oxyphil cell relies on the parathyroid's capillary network and connective-tissue stroma, and it lives embedded among the chief cells that dominate the gland.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

In the healthy adult, removing the oxyphil cells would probably change nothing measurable, which is precisely why their function is unresolved.

Their main effect on the region is to complicate the pathologist's job, since oxyphil-rich adenomas and nodules must be told apart from ordinary parathyroid and thyroid lesions.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The oxyphil cell's most surprising quality is its refusal to fit a rule: it hoards the machinery of a high-energy secretory cell while behaving like an inert one, a mismatch that has kept it an open question for over a hundred years.

The most modern reading - that it is a reserve or age-related PTH source unmasked in kidney disease - remains provisional and honestly uncertain.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Extracellular calcium via the calcium-sensing receptor, and the same phosphate and vitamin D signals the chief cell reads.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Little or none in the resting state; possibly PTH and PTH-related peptide when chronically stimulated.

• STATE.

◦ Mitochondrial mass, age of the individual, presence of chronic kidney disease.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Cell diameter 8 to 12 micrometres; resting potential near minus 50 to minus 70 millivolts; onset of appearance around puberty.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Accumulation over years to decades, not minutes - this is a slow, developmental and age-related cell rather than a fast responder.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Oxyphil-cell (oncocytic) parathyroid adenomas and, rarely, oxyphil parathyroid carcinoma; expansion in the parathyroid disease of chronic kidney failure.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

CODE SAMPLE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

A large, intensely pink parathyroid cell packed almost solid with mitochondria yet apparently idle, absent in children and piling up with age - the honest unsolved puzzle of endocrine histology.


SECTION 4. THE ADRENAL GLAND

Perched like a cocked hat on the top of each kidney sits a small gland that is really two glands fused into one, and both halves speak directly to the brain's needs.

The outer rind, the cortex, is built in three concentric layers, each staffed by its own cell type turning cholesterol into a different steroid hormone: salt-balancing aldosterone on the outside, stress-managing cortisol in the middle, and sex-steroid precursors on the inside.

The inner core, the medulla, is a piece of the sympathetic nervous system that traded its wires for the bloodstream, pouring adrenaline into the circulation in an instant of alarm.

The five cell types that follow - three cortical, the chromaffin cell of the medulla, and the glial-like sustentacular cell that supports it - together make the adrenal the single clearest anatomical proof that the nervous and endocrine systems are one architecture.


CHAPTER 64. ZONA GLOMERULOSA CELL (The Salt-and-Pressure Governor Cell) (NON-COG)

The thinnest outer shell of the adrenal cortex holds a cell whose single hormone decides how much salt your body keeps and, through it, how high your blood pressure runs.

The zona glomerulosa cell makes aldosterone, and aldosterone tells the kidney to hold onto sodium and, with it, water.

This cell is the only one in the body that carries the final enzyme for aldosterone, and it answers not to the pituitary but to the salt-and-pressure signals of the renin-angiotensin system and to the level of potassium in the blood.

Its miscalibration is now recognised as one of the commonest curable causes of high blood pressure.

Salt-and-Pressure names what it governs: sodium retention and, downstream, blood volume and arterial pressure.

Governor names its regulatory, set-point role.

Cell marks the individual unit of the cortex's outermost band.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The zona glomerulosa cell is the outermost cortical cell of the adrenal gland, and it is the body's sole source of the mineralocorticoid hormone aldosterone.

Aldosterone acts on the kidney to reabsorb sodium and excrete potassium, thereby controlling salt balance, blood volume, and blood pressure.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The cells are small and arranged in rounded clusters or arcs (glomerulosa means little balls), with relatively little of the fat-droplet content seen deeper in the cortex.

Like all steroid-making cells they carry abundant smooth endoplasmic reticulum and mitochondria with tubular cristae, the sites of steroid chemistry.

SIZE:

The zona glomerulosa cell is roughly 12 to 15 micrometres across, among the smaller cortical cells.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

There are no accepted molecular subtypes, but the layer contains small aldosterone-producing cell clusters, focal groups strongly expressing the aldosterone-making enzyme, which become more common with age and may seed disease.

WHERE IT LIVES:

It lives in the thin outermost band of the adrenal cortex, immediately beneath the gland's fibrous capsule, on top of each kidney.

Its interface is with the capsule above and the zona fasciculata below, and the boundary is dynamic: glomerulosa cells can convert into fasciculata cells as they are displaced inward over time.

NUMBER:

The zona glomerulosa is the thinnest cortical zone, perhaps 5 to 10 percent of cortical volume, holding on the order of millions of cells per gland, with low confidence on the exact figure.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Strikingly for a steroid cell, the glomerulosa cell is electrically responsive: it behaves as a slow oscillator whose membrane potential is set largely by potassium channels, and a rise in blood potassium depolarises it, opening voltage-gated calcium channels and driving aldosterone synthesis.

Its resting potential is unusually sensitive, sitting near minus 80 millivolts and shifting readily with small potassium changes.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

The cell forms no synapses; it is a hormone-receptor cell, reading angiotensin II through its receptor and potassium through its channels, and writing aldosterone onto mineralocorticoid receptors in the kidney.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that the glomerulosa cell is a secretory cell with no axon.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

The cell's signalling output is the steroid hormone aldosterone; its key manufacturing enzyme is aldosterone synthase (CYP11B2), which it alone among adrenal cells possesses.

MYELINATION:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that the cell has no axon to insulate.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

The cell spends its energy importing cholesterol and running the cytochrome P450 enzyme chain that converts it, step by step, into aldosterone, work concentrated in its mitochondria and smooth endoplasmic reticulum.

PLASTICITY:

The zone remodels continuously: cells are born near the capsule, produce aldosterone, then convert into fasciculata cells as they move inward, so the whole cortex is a slow conveyor belt.

Autonomous overgrowth of these cells produces the aldosterone-secreting adenoma of Conn syndrome.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

The glomerulosa cell is fed by the sinusoidal capillaries that perfuse the cortex from the capsule inward, and it depends on the liver-derived cholesterol and the circulating renin-angiotensin system that drives it.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Overactive, these cells cause primary aldosteronism (Conn syndrome), retaining salt and raising blood pressure, now recognised as one of the most common curable secondary causes of hypertension.

Destroyed along with the rest of the cortex in adrenal failure, their loss causes the salt-wasting, low blood pressure, and high potassium of Addison disease.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The glomerulosa cell's surprising trick is that it computes with membrane voltage like a neuron even though it never fires a spike: it runs slow, calcium-carried electrical oscillations set by potassium channels, so that its hormone output tracks tiny changes in blood potassium almost as a graded electrical readout.

This makes it a rare example of an electrically tuned steroid factory.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Angiotensin II from the renin-angiotensin system; blood potassium, normally about 3.5 to 5.0 millimoles per litre; and, less strongly, adrenocorticotropic hormone (ACTH).

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Aldosterone, plasma level roughly 100 to 400 picomoles per litre depending on salt intake and posture.

• STATE.

◦ Membrane potential, intracellular calcium, aldosterone synthase expression, cholesterol stores.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Resting potential near minus 80 millivolts; cell diameter 12 to 15 micrometres; zone thickness a few cell layers.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Minutes for potassium- and angiotensin-driven secretion; hours to days for enzyme induction and cell conversion.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Primary aldosteronism (Conn syndrome) from adenoma or hyperplasia; loss in Addison disease; genetic glucocorticoid-remediable aldosteronism from a CYP11B1/CYP11B2 gene fusion.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

CODE SAMPLE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

The only cell in the body carrying aldosterone synthase, clustered in little balls beneath the adrenal capsule, an electrically tuned steroid factory that reads blood potassium like a voltage and sets the body's salt and blood pressure.


CHAPTER 65. ZONA FASCICULATA CELL (The Stress-Steroid Factory Cell) (NON-COG)

The broad middle layer of the adrenal cortex is the far end of the body's central stress axis and the source of the hormone that touches nearly every tissue you have.

The zona fasciculata cell makes cortisol, the glucocorticoid that mobilises fuel, restrains inflammation, sharpens alertness, and rises every morning to wake you and every time you are stressed to carry you through.

Because cortisol is a small fat-soluble steroid, it slips straight through cell membranes and even through the blood-brain barrier to act on receptors inside neurons - which is why this cortical cell is the launching point of what this catalogue calls Layer-Skipping Channel 1, the steroid hormone channel that speaks to the brain without a single nerve.

This is the cell the brain's stress system ultimately commands.

Stress-Steroid names its product and its trigger: the glucocorticoid cortisol, released on the brain's stress signal.

Factory names its scale: a broad, fat-laden zone built to mass-produce steroid on demand.

Cell marks the individual worker in that factory's long columns.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The zona fasciculata cell is the cell of the thick middle layer of the adrenal cortex, and it is the body's principal source of the glucocorticoid cortisol.

It is the final effector of the hypothalamic-pituitary-adrenal (HPA) axis: the hypothalamus signals the pituitary, the pituitary releases adrenocorticotropic hormone (ACTH), and ACTH drives this cell to make cortisol.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The cells are large and pale, arranged in long straight cords (fasciculata means little bundles) running from the glomerulosa toward the medulla, separated by capillaries.

Their cytoplasm is stuffed with lipid droplets of stored cholesterol, giving them a foamy, spongy appearance and the nickname spongiocytes.

SIZE:

The zona fasciculata cell is roughly 15 to 25 micrometres across, the largest of the three cortical cell types.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

No formal molecular subtypes are established, though inner fasciculata cells shade functionally toward the androgen-making reticularis below them.

WHERE IT LIVES:

It lives in the broad middle zone of the adrenal cortex, which makes up the bulk of the cortical volume, sandwiched between the glomerulosa outside and the reticularis inside.

Its most important interface is invisible and chemical: the cortisol it releases crosses the blood-brain barrier freely to reach receptors inside brain cells, so this cell has a direct line into the very organ that commands it.

NUMBER:

The zona fasciculata is the largest cortical zone, roughly 65 to 75 percent of cortical volume, containing on the order of tens of millions of cells per gland, with moderate confidence on the order of magnitude.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

The fasciculata cell rests near minus 60 to minus 80 millivolts and is not a spiking cell; its controlling signal is the second messenger cyclic AMP, raised when ACTH binds its receptor.

That cyclic AMP surge mobilises cholesterol and switches on the steroid enzymes within minutes.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

The cell forms no synapses; it is a hormone-receptor cell, reading ACTH from the pituitary and writing cortisol onto glucocorticoid receptors present in almost every tissue in the body, including the brain.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that the fasciculata cell is a secretory cell with no axon.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

The cell's signalling output is the steroid hormone cortisol (and a small amount of corticosterone); its distinguishing enzyme is 11-beta-hydroxylase (CYP11B1), which completes cortisol synthesis.

MYELINATION:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that the cell has no axon to insulate.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

This cell is a heavy steroid producer, spending energy to import and store cholesterol, to shuttle it into mitochondria via the steroidogenic acute regulatory protein, and to run the multi-step cytochrome P450 enzyme chain, all sharply upregulated by ACTH.

PLASTICITY:

Sustained ACTH, as in an ACTH-secreting pituitary tumour, makes the whole zone hypertrophy; loss of ACTH drive lets it atrophy, which is why abrupt withdrawal of long-term steroid drugs can leave the gland unable to respond.

Autonomous cortisol-secreting adenomas arise from these cells.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

The fasciculata cell is fed by the cortical sinusoidal capillaries and by liver-derived cholesterol, and it sits between the glomerulosa above and the reticularis below in the cortex's continuous cellular conveyor.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Overactive, these cells cause Cushing syndrome: too much cortisol producing central obesity, a round face, thin skin, high blood sugar, bone loss, and mood and memory disturbance, a vivid demonstration of cortisol's reach into the brain.

Destroyed, their loss causes the fatigue, low blood pressure, low blood sugar, and darkened skin of Addison disease, which is fatal untreated in an adrenal crisis.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The fasciculata cell's most consequential feature is not a computation but a reach: its product is a lipophilic steroid that ignores the barriers that stop nerve signals, entering neurons directly to reprogram gene expression, reshape memory circuits in the hippocampus, and feed back to shut down its own command axis.

This is the anchor cell of the steroid layer-skipping channel, the endocrine system's way of commanding the brain chemically rather than electrically.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ ACTH from the pituitary, pulsatile and following a daily rhythm; the signal itself set by the brain's stress and clock circuits.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Cortisol, roughly 10 to 20 milligrams secreted per day, plasma level high in the early morning (about 140 to 550 nanomoles per litre) and low at night.

• STATE.

◦ Cholesterol droplet stores, intracellular cyclic AMP, steroid enzyme expression.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Resting potential minus 60 to minus 80 millivolts; cell diameter 15 to 25 micrometres; zone about two-thirds of cortical volume.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Cortisol rises within minutes of ACTH; a strong daily rhythm with an early-morning peak; the circulating half-life of cortisol is about 60 to 90 minutes.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Cushing syndrome from cortisol excess; Addison disease and secondary adrenal insufficiency from cortisol loss; congenital adrenal hyperplasia from enzyme defects such as 21-hydroxylase deficiency; adrenal crisis on abrupt steroid withdrawal.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

CODE SAMPLE:
