This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

The large, foamy, cholesterol-stuffed cell in long bundles of the mid-cortex, final effector of the stress axis, whose fat-soluble product cortisol slips straight into the brain that commands it - the launching cell of the steroid channel.


CHAPTER 66. ZONA RETICULARIS CELL (The Androgen-Precursor Cell) (NON-COG)

The innermost layer of the adrenal cortex, right against the medulla, holds a cell that makes the weak sex-steroid precursors that circulate in larger amounts than any other steroid in the human bloodstream.

The zona reticularis cell produces dehydroepiandrosterone (DHEA) and its sulphate, precursor androgens that other tissues convert into testosterone and oestrogen.

This cell is nearly silent at birth, awakens in mid-childhood in an event called adrenarche that helps launch puberty, peaks in early adulthood, and then fades slowly for the rest of life.

It is the adrenal's contribution to the body's sex-steroid economy and one of the clocks of human ageing.

Androgen-Precursor names its product: not a finished sex hormone but the raw material other tissues finish.

Cell marks the individual unit of the cortex's innermost, net-like band.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The zona reticularis cell is the cell of the innermost cortical layer, bordering the adrenal medulla, and it produces the adrenal androgens, chiefly DHEA, DHEA-sulphate, and androstenedione.

These are weak androgens and precursors that peripheral tissues convert into the more potent testosterone and oestrogen.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The cells are smaller and more compact than fasciculata cells and are arranged in an irregular branching network (reticularis means little net), often containing brown lipofuscin, an age pigment, and fewer lipid droplets.

SIZE:

The zona reticularis cell is roughly 12 to 18 micrometres across, intermediate to small among cortical cells.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

No formal subtypes are recognised; the zone is best understood as the innermost, androgen-specialised end of the cortical continuum.

WHERE IT LIVES:

It lives in the thin innermost band of the adrenal cortex, directly against the chromaffin cells of the medulla.

Its interface with the medulla is functional as well as spatial: cortisol made just outside flows past these cells and the medulla on its way through the gland, and the high local cortisol helps the neighbouring chromaffin cells make adrenaline.

NUMBER:

The zona reticularis is a minor fraction of cortical volume, perhaps 10 to 15 percent, holding on the order of millions of cells per gland, with low confidence on the exact figure.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

The reticularis cell rests near minus 60 to minus 80 millivolts and is not a spiking cell; like the fasciculata cell it is driven mainly through the cyclic AMP pathway when adrenocorticotropic hormone (ACTH) binds.

Its distinct androgen output depends less on voltage and more on which enzymes it expresses.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

The cell forms no synapses; it is a hormone-receptor cell, reading ACTH and writing DHEA and androstenedione into the blood for other tissues to convert.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that the reticularis cell is a secretory cell with no axon.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

The cell's signalling output is the androgen precursors DHEA, DHEA-sulphate, and androstenedione; its distinguishing feature is high activity of the enzyme cytochrome b5 alongside CYP17A1, which favours androgen production over cortisol.

MYELINATION:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that the cell has no axon to insulate.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Like the other cortical cells it spends energy importing cholesterol and running cytochrome P450 enzymes, here tuned toward the androgen branch of the steroid pathway.

PLASTICITY:

The zone is the great age-clock of the cortex: it is thin and quiet before adrenarche at around age 6 to 8, expands to peak DHEA output in the twenties, and then declines steadily, a fall sometimes called adrenopause.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

The reticularis cell is supplied by the deepest cortical sinusoidal capillaries, which then carry cortisol-rich blood into the medulla, and it depends on the same liver-derived cholesterol and pituitary ACTH as the rest of the cortex.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Overactive or malformed, these cells contribute androgen excess: in congenital adrenal hyperplasia, blocked cortisol synthesis diverts precursors down this androgen path, virilising an affected female fetus.

Their normal awakening at adrenarche gives children their first body odour, pubic and underarm hair, and is the adrenal opening act of puberty.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The reticularis cell's most surprising quality is its life-long developmental trajectory: rather than holding a set point, it enacts a slow biological clock, silent in childhood, blazing in youth, and fading with age, making DHEA-sulphate one of the most reliable single-molecule markers of human ageing in the blood.

Its output is also unusual in scale - DHEA-sulphate circulates at higher concentration than any other steroid hormone in the body.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ ACTH from the pituitary; developmental signals that trigger adrenarche in mid-childhood.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ DHEA and DHEA-sulphate (the latter circulating in micromolar amounts, higher than any other steroid) and androstenedione.

• STATE.

◦ Cytochrome b5 and CYP17A1 activity, cholesterol stores, lipofuscin accumulation.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Resting potential minus 60 to minus 80 millivolts; cell diameter 12 to 18 micrometres; zone about 10 to 15 percent of cortical volume.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Minutes for ACTH-driven secretion; a decades-long rise-and-fall from adrenarche through adrenopause.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Androgen excess in congenital adrenal hyperplasia and androgen-secreting adrenal tumours; loss of adrenal androgens in Addison disease; premature adrenarche.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

CODE SAMPLE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

The small, net-arranged, pigment-flecked cell of the deepest cortex that makes the body's most abundant circulating steroid as a mere precursor, running a decades-long clock from the dawn of puberty to the fade of old age.


CHAPTER 67. ADRENAL CHROMAFFIN CELL (The Wireless Alarm Cell) (NON-COG) - CROSS-REFERENCE, neuron catalogue Chapter 261

At the very core of the adrenal gland sits a cell that began life as a nerve cell and then gave up its wire for a faster, wider way to shout.

The adrenal chromaffin cell is a sympathetic neuron that traded its axon for direct access to the bloodstream, dumping adrenaline into the whole circulation in the instant of alarm that readies the body to fight or flee.

Because this cell holds a full chapter in the neuronal catalogue, the present entry is a cross-reference and keeps each aspect brief: the full treatment lives at neuron catalogue Chapter 261.

It is listed here so this endocrine roster is complete, and because it is the single strongest anatomical proof that the nervous and endocrine systems are one architecture.

Wireless names its defining choice: it broadcasts a hormone into the blood instead of down a wire.

Alarm names its message: the surge of adrenaline that mobilises the body for emergency.

Cell marks the individual unit of the adrenal core.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The adrenal chromaffin cell is a neuroendocrine cell of the adrenal medulla that secretes the catecholamines adrenaline (about 80 percent) and noradrenaline into the bloodstream, driving the fight-or-flight response; its full treatment lives in the neuronal catalogue at Chapter 261, and this is a cross-reference entry.

Developmentally it is a modified sympathetic neuron of neural-crest origin that lost its axon to gain direct access to the circulation.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

It is a large polygonal cell packed with dense-core granules of catecholamine that stain brown with chromium salts, the reaction that gives the cell its name.

SIZE:

The chromaffin cell is roughly 15 to 30 micrometres across.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Two subtypes exist, the adrenaline-storing and the noradrenaline-storing chromaffin cells, distinguished by whether they carry the final methylating enzyme.

WHERE IT LIVES:

It lives in the adrenal medulla, the core of the gland wrapped by the cortex, and in scattered outposts such as the organ of Zuckerkandl.

NUMBER:

The medulla holds on the order of tens of millions of chromaffin cells per gland, with moderate confidence on the order of magnitude.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Unlike the cortical cells, the chromaffin cell is genuinely excitable and fires calcium-dependent action potentials, resting near minus 50 to minus 60 millivolts, which trigger the exocytosis of its granules.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

It receives a real cholinergic synapse from the sympathetic preganglionic splanchnic nerve, which is its command input - it is, in effect, a gland cell that kept its incoming synapse but lost its outgoing axon.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that the chromaffin cell famously gave up its axon in development in exchange for secreting into the blood.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Its signalling output is the hormones adrenaline and noradrenaline, released into the blood, along with co-stored adenosine triphosphate (ATP) and neuropeptides such as chromogranin-derived peptides.

MYELINATION:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that the cell has no axon to insulate.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

It spends energy synthesising, concentrating, and repackaging catecholamines and running the calcium-triggered exocytotic machinery, with demand spiking during sympathetic activation.

PLASTICITY:

It up-regulates catecholamine synthesis under chronic stress and gives rise to the tumours phaeochromocytoma in adults and neuroblastoma in children.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

It is supported by the sustentacular cells of Chapter 68 and by a specialised portal blood supply that first bathes it in cortisol-rich blood from the cortex, which is what enables it to make adrenaline.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Its surge of adrenaline is what makes the heart pound, the airways widen, and glucose flood the blood in a moment of fright; its tumour, phaeochromocytoma, causes dangerous surges of blood pressure.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Studied by amperometry, a technique that measures the electrical current from oxidising catecholamine as single granules fuse, the chromaffin cell reveals quantal release: each fusion event is a measurable spike of current, and the cell switches between brief kiss-and-run release at low firing rates and full granule collapse at high rates, tuning how much it dumps to how hard it is driven.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Acetylcholine from splanchnic preganglionic sympathetic fibres; cortisol arriving from the surrounding cortex.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Adrenaline and noradrenaline into the blood; resting plasma adrenaline is low (tens of picograms per millilitre) and can rise many-fold in seconds under stress.

• STATE.

◦ Granule stores, membrane potential, intracellular calcium.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Resting potential minus 50 to minus 60 millivolts; cell diameter 15 to 30 micrometres; roughly 80 percent adrenaline, 20 percent noradrenaline output.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Release within milliseconds to seconds of nerve firing; circulating catecholamine half-life only 1 to 3 minutes.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Phaeochromocytoma (catecholamine-secreting tumour) and, in children, neuroblastoma.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

CODE SAMPLE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

A sympathetic neuron that kept its incoming synapse but threw away its axon to shout adrenaline into the whole bloodstream at once - full treatment at neuron catalogue Chapter 261.


CHAPTER 68. ADRENAL MEDULLARY SUSTENTACULAR CELL (The Medulla's Glial Nursemaid Cell) (NON-COG)

Woven among the shouting chromaffin cells sits a quiet, flat cell that never releases a hormone but keeps the alarm cells alive and in order.

The adrenal medullary sustentacular cell is the glia of the adrenal core - so glia-like that it carries the same marker protein as astrocytes and Schwann cells and shares their neural-crest origin.

It cradles the chromaffin cells, buffers their environment, and serves as a local stem cell that can, under the right signals, generate new chromaffin cells.

It is the endocrine gland's version of the support cell that appears again and again across this catalogue: the humble nursemaid standing behind the star.

Glial names its family: this is a support cell of the same lineage and character as the nervous system's glia.

Nursemaid names its role: sustaining, buffering, and replenishing the chromaffin cells it surrounds.

Cell marks the individual unit of this supporting mesh.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The adrenal medullary sustentacular cell is a glial-like supporting cell that envelops clusters of chromaffin cells in the adrenal medulla, providing structural, metabolic, and regulatory support rather than secreting a hormone.

It is the direct counterpart of the sustentacular cells found in the carotid body and other paraganglia.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

It is a flat, elongated cell with fine processes that wrap around the borders of chromaffin cell nests, much as an astrocyte process wraps a synapse.

SIZE:

The cell body is roughly 10 to 20 micrometres across, with processes extending further around the chromaffin clusters.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

No formal subtypes are recognised; it is a single glial-like population defined by its supporting position and its glial markers.

WHERE IT LIVES:

It lives entirely within the adrenal medulla, distributed among the chromaffin cell nests, and it is revealed by staining for the S100 protein and glial fibrillary acidic protein, the same markers used to identify astrocytes and Schwann cells.

NUMBER:

Sustentacular cells are a minority of medullary cells, far fewer than the chromaffin cells they support, with low confidence on any exact figure.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Like other glial cells it is non-excitable, resting at a glial-type membrane potential around minus 70 to minus 80 millivolts, and it does not fire action potentials.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

It forms no synapses; its junctional relationships are supportive contacts with the chromaffin cells it enwraps and, like glia elsewhere, likely gap-junctional coupling within its own network.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that the sustentacular cell is a glial support cell with no axon.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

This section is largely not applicable, as the sustentacular cell is not a hormone or transmitter secretor; its contribution is trophic support and environmental buffering rather than chemical signalling.

MYELINATION:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that the cell insulates nothing and has no axon of its own.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Its energy budget is modest and directed at buffering the ionic and chemical environment of the chromaffin cells and at maintaining its own supportive processes.

PLASTICITY:

Its most notable capacity is as a reserve stem cell: under appropriate signals sustentacular cells can proliferate and give rise to new chromaffin cells, a source of medullary renewal.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

In a role reversal typical of glia, this is the cell that supports others - here the chromaffin cells - while itself relying on the medullary blood supply and the surrounding connective tissue.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

By buffering, cradling, and occasionally replenishing the chromaffin cells, it keeps the adrenaline factory orderly; in tumour pathology, a rim of sustentacular cells around cell nests is a diagnostic hallmark that helps identify phaeochromocytoma and paraganglioma.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The sustentacular cell's most surprising capability is latent stem-cell potential: this quiet support cell retains the neural-crest ability to become a chromaffin cell, so the medulla carries within its scaffolding the seeds of its own regeneration.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Local signals from chromaffin cells and the medullary environment; growth and stress signals that can trigger its proliferation.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Trophic and structural support; potential daughter chromaffin cells; no hormone.

• STATE.

◦ Process coverage of chromaffin nests, proliferative versus quiescent status.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Resting potential near minus 70 to minus 80 millivolts; cell diameter 10 to 20 micrometres.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Supportive functions continuous; stem-cell recruitment over days and longer.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ It has no primary disease of its own, but its presence as a sustentacular rim is a diagnostic marker in phaeochromocytoma and paraganglioma.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

CODE SAMPLE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

A flat, astrocyte-marked glial cell wrapping the adrenaline cells of the adrenal core, secreting nothing yet holding the neural-crest power to become a chromaffin cell itself - the medulla's nursemaid and hidden reserve.


SECTION 5. THE ENDOCRINE PANCREAS

The islets of Langerhans are the endocrine micro-organs of the pancreas, roughly one to two million tiny cell-clusters scattered like islands through a sea of digestive tissue, together weighing only about one to two grams yet governing the fuel supply of every cell in the body.

Each islet is a five-cell democracy in which insulin, glucagon, somatostatin, pancreatic polypeptide and ghrelin are secreted, argued over, and balanced minute by minute against the blood glucose concentration.

Unlike the neurons of the earlier volumes these cells send no axon anywhere, yet several of them are genuinely electrically excitable, firing calcium-based action potentials in glucose-tuned bursts, so this section reports real electrophysiology rather than the placeholder values a truly silent cell would receive.

Remember throughout that although the brain takes up glucose without needing insulin, it is studded with insulin receptors that tune memory circuits, so the cells below set operating conditions for the cells of the first five volumes.


CHAPTER 69. BETA CELL (Insulin-Secreting B Cell of the Pancreatic Islet) (The Glucose-Tasting Insulin Cell) (NON-COG)

Eat a slice of bread and within minutes a hidden electorate of cells buried in your pancreas feels the sugar rise, falls into a rhythmic electrical chatter, and releases the one hormone in the body that can tell every tissue to take the sugar in.

The beta cell is the single most famous endocrine cell in medicine, because its failure is diabetes, the disease that has organised more human biochemistry than almost any other.

It is a small round cell that behaves, astonishingly, like a neuron - it has a resting voltage, it fires calcium action potentials, and it bursts in glucose-tuned rhythms - but instead of talking to another cell down a wire it dumps insulin straight into the bloodstream.

Watch one long enough and you see a fuel gauge that also happens to be a tiny oscillator.

Glucose-Tasting names its trigger: this cell reads the blood sugar concentration directly and converts it into electrical activity.

Insulin names its product, the master storage hormone that unlocks glucose uptake into muscle and fat.

Cell marks it as a secretory endocrine cell, not a neuron, despite its neuron-like electrics.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The beta cell is the insulin-manufacturing cell of the islet of Langerhans, the endocrine cluster of the pancreas, and it is the body's only source of insulin, the hormone that commands muscle and fat to import glucose from the blood.

Its job is glucose homeostasis: when blood sugar rises after a meal it secretes insulin to bring it down, and when blood sugar falls it goes quiet.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

A polygonal-to-rounded epithelial cell packed with hundreds of dense-core secretory granules, each holding crystalline insulin condensed around zinc ions, giving the cytoplasm a granular look under the microscope.

It is rich in mitochondria (the cell's power plants) and rough endoplasmic reticulum (the protein-manufacturing membrane network), because insulin is a secreted protein made in enormous quantity.

Beta cells sit coupled to one another by gap junctions, the direct cell-to-cell electrical bridges built from connexin-36, which let a whole islet burst as one synchronised syncytium (an electrically unified group of cells).

SIZE:

Roughly 10 to 15 micrometres across, a micrometre being a thousandth of a millimetre, holding on the order of 9,000 to 13,000 insulin granules, each granule about 250 to 350 nanometres in diameter, a nanometre being a millionth of a millimetre.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Beta cells are not uniform: recent single-cell work describes hub cells (a small minority that pace the electrical bursting of the whole islet) and follower cells, along with molecularly distinct states differing in maturity and in the surface marker ST8SIA1 and the transcription factor content that sets insulin output.

Treat the beta cell as a functional continuum from immature to highly glucose-responsive, not one fixed type.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Inside the islets of Langerhans, which are dispersed through the exocrine pancreas, with the beta cells forming the bulk of every islet.

In humans the classic rodent core-and-mantle arrangement is relaxed: human beta cells are intermingled with alpha and delta cells throughout the islet rather than sequestered in a central core, an arrangement thought to favour paracrine (neighbour-to-neighbour) cross-talk.

NUMBER:

An adult human pancreas holds roughly one to two million islets containing on the order of one billion beta cells, of moderate confidence, with beta cells making up about 65 to 80 percent of human islet cells.

The total beta-cell mass is only about one to two grams, a startlingly small tissue for so consequential a hormone.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

The beta cell is genuinely electrically excitable, and this is its defining trick.

At low glucose the ATP-sensitive potassium channel, called the K-ATP channel, is open and holds the resting membrane potential near minus 70 millivolts, a millivolt being a thousandth of a volt.

When blood glucose rises to about 7 millimoles per litre and above, glucose metabolism raises the ratio of adenosine triphosphate to adenosine diphosphate (the cell's energy-charge ratio), the K-ATP channels close, and the membrane depolarises to a threshold near minus 50 millivolts.

Beyond threshold the cell fires calcium-based action potentials in bursts: depolarised plateaux lasting several seconds, carrying superimposed spikes, separated by silent repolarised intervals, the whole pattern oscillating with a period of tens of seconds and rising in duty cycle as glucose climbs.

Each spike opens voltage-gated L-type and P/Q-type calcium channels, calcium floods in, and the resulting rise in cytosolic calcium triggers the exocytosis (granule fusion and release) of insulin, so that insulin secretion itself is pulsatile and locked in phase with the calcium oscillations.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

The beta cell forms no chemical synapse in the neuronal sense, but it is richly junctionally connected.

Connexin-36 gap junctions electrically couple neighbouring beta cells so an islet bursts in unison, and paracrine signalling ties it to its neighbours: somatostatin from delta cells inhibits it, glucagon and glucagon-like peptide-1 amplify it, and its own product zinc and insulin feed back on the islet.

It is also innervated by autonomic nerve fibres, with parasympathetic (vagal) input promoting insulin release and sympathetic input suppressing it.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that the beta cell has no axon, releasing its hormone from secretory granules across its general plasma membrane into the blood rather than down a projection.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

The beta cell's signalling molecules are hormones and co-secreted messengers rather than classical transmitters: chiefly insulin, plus amylin (islet amyloid polypeptide, which slows gastric emptying), C-peptide (the connecting peptide cleaved from proinsulin), zinc ions, and gamma-aminobutyric acid (GABA, an inhibitory messenger used here for paracrine signalling to alpha cells).

MYELINATION:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that the beta cell has no axon to insulate, being a hormone-secreting epithelial cell.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

The beta cell is metabolically unusual on purpose: it expresses the high-capacity glucose transporter GLUT1 in humans (GLUT2 in rodents) and the enzyme glucokinase, which acts as the glucose sensor by setting the rate of glucose entry into metabolism across the physiological range.

Because its whole business is to convert glucose flux into an ATP-to-ADP ratio, it deliberately keeps low levels of lactate-producing enzymes, forcing glucose down the mitochondrial pathway so that fuel and signal stay tightly coupled.

PLASTICITY:

Beta-cell mass and output are strongly adaptive: the cells hypertrophy and proliferate, and their insulin content rises, in response to sustained demand such as pregnancy, obesity, or insulin resistance, and shrink when demand falls.

At the functional level the glucose threshold and burst duty cycle are tuned by incretin hormones (gut signals such as glucagon-like peptide 1, GLP-1) and by prior glucose exposure, so the same cell secretes more per glucose rise after adequate priming.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

The beta cell is supported by the islet's dense fenestrated (windowed) capillary network of endothelial cells that carries its insulin away and its glucose signal in, by resident islet macrophages that clear debris, and by pericytes and neural crest-derived glial-like cells that wrap the islet.

Its neighbours the alpha and delta cells are functional partners, and the whole islet is embedded in and fed by the exocrine acinar tissue around it.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Delete the beta cell and the islet loses its anchor: glucagon, somatostatin and the rest continue, but nothing lowers blood glucose, so sugar climbs unopposed and the body starves in the midst of plenty, unable to store the fuel dissolved in its own blood.

This single deletion is, in effect, type 1 diabetes.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The beta cell's most surprising capability is that an entire islet behaves as a coupled electrical oscillator, a biological clock built from metabolism.

Through connexin-36 gap junctions the beta cells synchronise their bursting, and a small set of pacemaker hub cells appears to entrain the rest, so the islet produces coherent, glucose-scaled calcium oscillations with periods of tens of seconds to a few minutes, and these in turn drive the pulsatile insulin secretion seen in the portal blood.

The oscillation frequency and plateau fraction encode the glucose concentration, making the islet a metabolic-to-electrical analog converter rather than a simple on-off gland.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Blood glucose 3 to 20 millimoles per litre (threshold near 7); incretin hormones GLP-1 and glucose-dependent insulinotropic peptide; autonomic acetylcholine and noradrenaline; paracrine somatostatin, glucagon and GABA.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Insulin secreted in pulses, plus amylin, C-peptide and zinc, into the portal bloodstream; typical basal output rising several-fold within minutes of a glucose load.

• STATE.

◦ K-ATP channel open probability, membrane potential, cytosolic calcium concentration, ATP-to-ADP ratio, readily releasable granule pool, total beta-cell mass.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Resting potential about minus 70 millivolts; threshold about minus 50 millivolts; glucose half-maximal response near 8 millimoles per litre; oscillation period tens of seconds to minutes; granule count roughly 10,000.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ K-ATP closure and depolarisation seconds after a glucose step; first-phase insulin release within 1 to 2 minutes; calcium and burst oscillations 10 seconds to a few minutes; beta-cell mass adaptation days to months.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Type 1 diabetes mellitus, the autoimmune destruction of beta cells, abolishing insulin.

◦ Type 2 diabetes mellitus, in which beta cells fail progressively against insulin resistance, with loss of first-phase and pulsatile secretion and eventual dedifferentiation.

◦ Monogenic diabetes of the young, including mutations in glucokinase (the glucose sensor) and in the K-ATP channel subunits, which shift the glucose threshold up or down and cause neonatal diabetes or hyperinsulinism.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

CODE SAMPLE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

A small zinc-granule-packed islet cell that tastes blood glucose through glucokinase and the K-ATP channel, bursts in gap-junction-synchronised calcium oscillations, and is the body's one and only source of insulin - the cell whose death is diabetes.


CHAPTER 70. ALPHA CELL (Glucagon-Secreting A Cell of the Pancreatic Islet) (The Fasting-Alarm Sugar-Raising Cell) (NON-COG)

If the beta cell is the accelerator that stores fuel, the alpha cell is the emergency brake released in reverse: it senses a falling blood sugar and shouts to the liver to make more.

It is the beta cell's lifelong sparring partner, sitting in the same islet, wired to the same nerves, and pushing the glucose lever in exactly the opposite direction.

Between them these two cells hold blood glucose inside the narrow band on which the brain, which cannot store its own fuel, absolutely depends.

Fasting-Alarm names its trigger, the low-glucose state between meals and overnight.

Sugar-Raising names its effect, the release of glucose from liver stores.

Cell marks it as an endocrine secretory cell.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The alpha cell is the glucagon-secreting cell of the islet, and glucagon is insulin's opposing hormone: it commands the liver to break down glycogen (stored glucose) and to make new glucose, raising blood sugar during fasting.

Its job is to defend the body against low blood glucose (hypoglycaemia), the condition the brain tolerates least.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

A rounded epithelial cell filled with dense-core secretory granules of glucagon, structurally similar to the beta cell but distinguishable by hormone content and by granule ultrastructure.

SIZE:

About 7 to 10 micrometres across, somewhat smaller than the beta cell, packed with several thousand glucagon granules.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Alpha cells are relatively uniform but show functional states differing in glucagon output and in their capacity to trans-differentiate; under extreme beta-cell loss a fraction of alpha cells can convert toward an insulin-producing phenotype, a plasticity of great therapeutic interest.

WHERE IT LIVES:

In the islets of Langerhans, forming the second most abundant islet cell; in rodents they ring the periphery, while in humans they are intermingled with beta cells throughout the islet.

NUMBER:

Alpha cells make up roughly 15 to 20 percent of human islet cells, of moderate confidence, numbering in the hundreds of millions across the pancreas.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

The alpha cell is also electrically excitable, and paradoxically it is most active at low glucose.

It expresses adenosine-triphosphate-sensitive potassium (K-ATP) channels, and at low glucose a small K-ATP conductance keeps the membrane in a window where voltage-gated sodium and calcium channels can fire action potentials that drive glucagon release, whereas rising glucose depolarises the cell too far, inactivates those channels, and suppresses secretion.

Resting potential sits in the range of about minus 60 to minus 70 millivolts, with action potentials fired most vigorously when glucose is low.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

No neuronal synapses, but tight paracrine and junctional coupling: insulin, zinc and gamma-aminobutyric acid (GABA) from beta cells and somatostatin from delta cells all inhibit the alpha cell, while sympathetic nerve activity and adrenaline strongly stimulate glucagon release during stress and exercise.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that the alpha cell has no axon, secreting glucagon from granules directly into the blood.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Its signalling molecules are the hormone glucagon, plus proglucagon-derived peptides and co-released messengers; it is a target of GABA and glutamate signalling within the islet.

MYELINATION:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that the alpha cell has no axon.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Like the beta cell it uses glucose metabolism to set its electrical state, but tuned so that low fuel, not high fuel, licenses secretion; it is well supplied with mitochondria to support sustained glucagon manufacture.

PLASTICITY:

Alpha-cell mass expands in diabetes and after beta-cell loss, and the cells show notable lineage plasticity, able under strong signals to reprogram toward a beta-like insulin-secreting state, which is why they are studied as a route to regenerate insulin production.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Supported by the same islet capillary network, resident macrophages, and islet glial and pericyte populations, and functionally partnered with its neighbouring beta and delta cells.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove the alpha cell and the islet loses its counter-regulatory voice: blood glucose defence during fasting weakens, and the brittle low-glucose swings seen when glucagon responses fail in long-standing diabetes illustrate the cost.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The alpha cell's striking feature is its inverted glucose logic - it is switched off by the very fuel that switches the beta cell on - so that the islet contains two excitable cells reading the same glucose signal with opposite sign, a built-in push-pull controller.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Blood glucose (low glucose stimulates); adrenaline and sympathetic noradrenaline; paracrine insulin, zinc, GABA and somatostatin (all inhibitory); amino acids such as arginine (stimulatory).

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Glucagon secreted into the portal blood, rising sharply during hypoglycaemia, fasting and exercise.

• STATE.

◦ K-ATP conductance, membrane potential, sodium and calcium channel availability, glucagon granule pool.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Resting potential about minus 60 to minus 70 millivolts; secretion maximal at low glucose, suppressed above roughly 7 millimoles per litre.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Glucagon secretion responds within minutes to a falling glucose; counter-regulatory surge within minutes of hypoglycaemia; mass changes over weeks to months.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Defective glucagon counter-regulation in type 1 and advanced type 2 diabetes, producing dangerous hypoglycaemia unawareness.

◦ Inappropriate hyperglucagonaemia contributing to the high fasting glucose of type 2 diabetes.

◦ Glucagonoma, a rare glucagon-secreting tumour causing hyperglycaemia and a characteristic rash.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

CODE SAMPLE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

The islet cell that fires and secretes when fuel runs low, releasing glucagon to raise blood sugar - insulin's mirror image, reading the same glucose signal with the opposite sign.


CHAPTER 71. DELTA CELL (Somatostatin-Secreting D Cell of the Pancreatic Islet) (The Islet's Local Referee Cell) (NON-COG)

Every committee needs a chair who can call for quiet, and in the islet that role falls to the delta cell, whose product somatostatin hushes both the insulin cell and the glucagon cell.

It is the least talked-about islet cell yet arguably the most political, reaching out with long slender processes to touch and calm its louder neighbours.

Its job is not to push glucose up or down but to keep the whole conversation from running away.

Local names its reach: it acts within the islet, on the cells beside it, not on the distant body.

Referee names its function, the paracrine braking of the other islet cells.

Cell marks it as an endocrine secretory cell.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The delta cell secretes somatostatin, a universal inhibitory hormone, and within the islet it acts chiefly as a local brake, damping the secretion of both insulin from beta cells and glucagon from alpha cells.

Its job is to smooth and limit islet hormone output, tuning the amplitude of the insulin-glucagon see-saw.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

A dendritic-looking cell: unlike its rounded neighbours it extends long, thin cytoplasmic processes (filopodia) that snake between other islet cells to deliver somatostatin close to its targets, an arrangement discovered to resemble a neuron's reach.

SIZE:

Around 8 to 12 micrometres in the cell body, with slender processes extending several cell-diameters to contact multiple neighbours.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Delta cells are a small population with limited described subtyping; their functional variation lies mainly in how far their processes reach and how strongly they respond to glucose and to the hormone ghrelin.

WHERE IT LIVES:

In the islets of Langerhans, scattered among the beta and alpha cells, positioned to contact many neighbours.

NUMBER:

Delta cells make up roughly 3 to 10 percent of human islet cells, of moderate confidence.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

The delta cell is electrically active and, like the beta cell, is stimulated by glucose: rising glucose and the resulting metabolic and electrical changes trigger calcium-dependent somatostatin release, so that somatostatin output tracks the very stimulus that raises insulin, providing negative feedback.

Resting potential lies in the range of roughly minus 60 to minus 70 millivolts, with calcium action potentials on stimulation.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

No neuronal synapses, but rich paracrine coupling delivered through its filopodial processes; it is stimulated by glucose, by incretin hormones, and by urocortin-3 released from beta cells, forming a feedback loop, and it inhibits both alpha and beta cells through somatostatin receptors.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that the delta cell's slender projections are cytoplasmic filopodia for paracrine delivery, not axons conducting impulses to a distant target.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Its signalling molecule is somatostatin (specifically the SST-14 form in the islet), acting on somatostatin receptors of the surrounding endocrine cells.

MYELINATION:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that the delta cell has no axon.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Modest, matched to a small cell producing a peptide hormone; it uses glucose metabolism as a secretory trigger much as the beta cell does.

PLASTICITY:

Delta-cell number and somatostatin tone shift in diabetes and can be modulated by ghrelin and by beta-cell-derived signals; the loop is adaptive rather than fixed.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Supported by the islet capillary bed, macrophages, and glial and pericyte populations, and functionally interlocked with the beta and alpha cells it regulates.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Weaken the delta cell and the islet loses its damping: insulin and glucagon secretion become less restrained and more volatile, illustrating that a brake, though it raises no hormone level itself, shapes the stability of the whole system.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The delta cell's most notable trick is spatial reach - by projecting motile filopodia it converts a small population into a wide-area paracrine controller, sampling and inhibiting many neighbours at once, so a handful of delta cells can supervise a whole islet.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Blood glucose (stimulatory); beta-cell-derived urocortin-3 and gamma-aminobutyric acid (GABA); ghrelin (stimulatory to somatostatin); incretin hormones.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Somatostatin delivered paracrine-fashion onto alpha and beta cells, inhibiting both.

• STATE.

◦ Membrane potential, cytosolic calcium, filopodial contact pattern, somatostatin granule pool.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Resting potential roughly minus 60 to minus 70 millivolts; somatostatin release rising with glucose in parallel with insulin.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Somatostatin release within minutes of a glucose rise; paracrine inhibition acting over seconds to minutes across the islet.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Somatostatinoma, a rare tumour causing diabetes, gallstones and diarrhoea through excess somatostatin.

◦ Altered delta-cell somatostatin tone contributing to the dysregulated glucagon secretion of diabetes.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

CODE SAMPLE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

The dendritic islet cell that reaches out with slender processes to deliver somatostatin, braking both insulin and glucagon - the islet's built-in referee.


CHAPTER 72. GAMMA (PP) CELL (Pancreatic-Polypeptide-Secreting F Cell of the Islet) (The After-Dinner Satiety Cell) (NON-COG)

Of all the islet cells the gamma cell is the quiet accountant, releasing its hormone after a meal to help tell the brain and the gut that eating is done.

It carries several names - gamma cell, PP cell, F cell - because its story was pieced together late, and it remains the least glamorous member of the islet.

Yet its hormone sits squarely in the modern science of appetite, feeding into the same satiety machinery that today's weight-loss drugs exploit.

After-Dinner names its trigger, the fed state following a meal.

Satiety names its message, the signal of fullness.

Cell marks it as an endocrine secretory cell.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The gamma cell, also called the PP cell or F cell, secretes pancreatic polypeptide, a hormone released after eating that reduces appetite, slows gastric emptying, and dampens pancreatic exocrine (digestive-enzyme) secretion.

Its job is a satiety and feedback signal that helps close the feeding response after a meal.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

A small rounded granular endocrine cell, indistinguishable by ordinary microscopy from other islet cells but identified by its pancreatic-polypeptide content.

SIZE:

About 8 to 12 micrometres across, granule-filled.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

No well-established subtypes; the population is defined by hormone content and by its regional concentration.

WHERE IT LIVES:

In the islets, but with a strong regional bias: gamma cells are concentrated in islets of the head and uncinate process of the pancreas (the pancreatic-polypeptide-rich lobe of different embryonic origin) and are sparse in the body and tail.

NUMBER:

Gamma cells make up roughly 3 to 5 percent of islet cells overall, of moderate confidence, but are far more abundant in the head of the pancreas than that average suggests.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Gamma cells are electrically active endocrine cells with resting potentials in the same range as their neighbours, roughly minus 60 to minus 70 millivolts, using calcium-dependent secretion; their electrophysiology is less characterised than the beta and alpha cells but follows the same excitable-secretory template.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

No neuronal synapses; strongly controlled by the vagus nerve, with cholinergic (acetylcholine) vagal input being the principal driver of pancreatic-polypeptide release after a meal, alongside gut hormone and paracrine signals within the islet.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that the gamma cell has no axon, releasing its hormone directly into the blood.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Its signalling molecule is pancreatic polypeptide, a member of the neuropeptide-Y family, acting on Y-family receptors in the brain and gut.

MYELINATION:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that the gamma cell has no axon.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Modest, matched to a small peptide-secreting cell.

PLASTICITY:

Pancreatic-polypeptide output rises with vagal tone and falls after vagotomy; gamma-cell responses are modulated in obesity and after bariatric surgery, reflecting adaptive appetite signalling.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Supported by the islet capillary network, macrophages, and islet glial and pericyte cells, and integrated with its islet neighbours and the autonomic nerve supply.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

The gamma cell's loss is subtle, since pancreatic polypeptide is one of several overlapping satiety signals, but its concentration in the head of the pancreas marks a genuine developmental fault line in the organ, a reminder that the pancreas is built from two fused buds.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The gamma cell's most interesting feature is its almost hard-wired dependence on the vagus nerve, so that circulating pancreatic polypeptide serves clinically as a readout of vagal (parasympathetic) tone - a hormone used as a nerve-activity gauge.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Vagal acetylcholine (principal stimulus); a protein meal; gut hormones; low blood glucose also raises it.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Pancreatic polypeptide into the blood, rising for several hours after a meal.

• STATE.

◦ Membrane potential, cytosolic calcium, pancreatic-polypeptide granule pool, vagal drive.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Resting potential roughly minus 60 to minus 70 millivolts; secretion peaking within about 1 to 2 hours of eating.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Release within minutes of vagal stimulation; postprandial elevation lasting hours.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ PPoma, a rare pancreatic-polypeptide-secreting tumour.

◦ Loss of the pancreatic-polypeptide response as a marker of autonomic neuropathy in long-standing diabetes.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

CODE SAMPLE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

The vagus-driven islet cell concentrated in the head of the pancreas that releases pancreatic polypeptide after a meal - the after-dinner satiety signaller and living gauge of parasympathetic tone.


CHAPTER 73. EPSILON CELL (Ghrelin-Secreting Islet Cell) (The Rare Hunger-Hormone Cell) (NON-COG)

The rarest citizen of the islet is the epsilon cell, so scarce that it was only firmly identified in the early twenty-first century, and it secretes the one islet hormone associated not with eating but with wanting to eat.

It makes ghrelin, the hunger hormone better known from the empty stomach, and its presence in the islet hints at a role in tuning the other islet cells before food arrives.

It is a small footnote of a cell with an outsised name in the science of appetite.

Rare names its scarcity, well under one in a hundred islet cells.

Hunger-Hormone names its product, ghrelin.

Cell marks it as an endocrine secretory cell.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The epsilon cell is the ghrelin-producing cell of the islet, and ghrelin is the appetite-stimulating hunger hormone; within the islet its ghrelin appears to modulate the secretion of the other islet cells, notably restraining glucose-stimulated insulin release.

Its job is a minor local and systemic contribution to the ghrelin signal that governs hunger and islet tone.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

A small granular endocrine cell resembling its islet neighbours, identifiable only by its ghrelin content and specific markers.

SIZE:

About 8 to 12 micrometres across.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

No established subtypes; the epsilon cell is more abundant in the fetal and neonatal pancreas than in the adult, so its main variation is developmental.

WHERE IT LIVES:

In the islets of Langerhans, typically at the periphery, and also scattered in the pancreatic ducts during development; it is a prominent islet cell before birth and dwindles thereafter.

NUMBER:

Epsilon cells make up less than 1 percent of islet cells in the adult, of moderate confidence, the rarest of the five islet endocrine types.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

The epsilon cell is presumed to be an excitable secretory cell in the islet mould, with a resting potential in the region of minus 60 to minus 70 millivolts and calcium-dependent ghrelin release, but its electrophysiology is poorly characterised because the cells are so rare - a point of honest low confidence.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

No neuronal synapses; it acts paracrine-fashion within the islet, its ghrelin inhibiting glucose-stimulated insulin secretion from nearby beta cells.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that the epsilon cell has no axon, releasing ghrelin directly.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Its signalling molecule is acyl-ghrelin, the active form of the hunger hormone, acting on the growth-hormone-secretagogue receptor.

MYELINATION:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that the epsilon cell has no axon.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Modest, matched to a small peptide-secreting cell.

PLASTICITY:

Epsilon-cell abundance is developmentally regulated, high in the fetus and declining after birth, and its numbers can shift in metabolic disease, though data are sparse.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Supported by the islet capillary network, macrophages, and islet glial and pericyte populations, and embedded among its islet neighbours.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Because it is so rare, the loss of the epsilon cell has no clear isolated syndrome, but its very existence reveals that the islet contains a hunger-signal cell alongside its fuel-storage and fuel-release cells, completing the appetite circuitry of the organ.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The epsilon cell's most surprising feature is developmental: it is far more prominent before birth than after, suggesting a role in building or priming the islet, so that a cell almost invisible in the adult may help pattern the islet in the fetus.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Fasting and developmental signals; islet paracrine environment; nutrient status.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Ghrelin into the local islet and blood, restraining glucose-stimulated insulin release.

• STATE.

◦ Membrane potential, cytosolic calcium, ghrelin granule pool, developmental stage.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Resting potential roughly minus 60 to minus 70 millivolts (low confidence); population under 1 percent of islet cells.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Paracrine ghrelin action over minutes; abundance changing across developmental time, months to years.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ No well-defined isolated disease; altered islet ghrelin signalling is implicated in dysregulated insulin secretion, and rare ghrelinomas exist.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

CODE SAMPLE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

The rarest islet cell, prominent before birth and scarce after, secreting the hunger hormone ghrelin to restrain insulin - the islet's small voice of appetite.


SECTION 6. THE GONADS AS ENDOCRINE ORGANS

The gonads, the testes and the ovaries, are famous as the factories of sperm and egg, but they are also full endocrine glands whose steroid hormones set the body's sex and season.

Their secret is teamwork: in both sexes hormone synthesis is split between two cell types, one that answers to luteinising hormone and makes androgens, and one that answers to follicle-stimulating hormone and finishes the job - the celebrated two-cell, two-gonadotropin arrangement.

The testis pairs the Leydig cell (its androgen maker) with the Sertoli cell (its nurse and aromatase cell); the ovarian follicle pairs the theca cell (its androgen maker) with the granulosa cell (its oestrogen maker).

The four chapters below treat these partners, which are anatomical mirror images across the sexes, all tagged (NON-COG) because their business is hormonal manufacture, not computation - yet their products, testosterone and oestrogen, reach the brain and reshape it.


CHAPTER 74. LEYDIG CELL (Interstitial Cell of the Testis) (The Testosterone Furnace Cell) (NON-COG)

Tucked into the connective tissue between the sperm-making tubules of the testis sits a cell that produces most of the testosterone in a man's body, quietly running the chemistry that builds muscle, bone, libido, and much of the male brain's tuning.

The Leydig cell answers to a single pituitary command, luteinising hormone, and converts cholesterol into androgen with the eager, lipid-rich machinery of a dedicated steroid factory.

It is the testis's endocrine half, working beside but separately from the sperm-nursing cells.

Testosterone names its product, the principal male sex steroid.

Furnace names its style, a cholesterol-burning steroidogenic engine.

Cell marks it as an endocrine secretory cell.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The Leydig cell is the testosterone-producing cell of the testis, sitting in the interstitium (the tissue between the seminiferous tubules), and it makes the great majority of the body's testosterone in response to luteinising hormone (LH) from the pituitary.

Its job is to supply androgen for male development, fertility, and the body-wide and brain-wide effects of testosterone.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

A large polygonal cell with abundant smooth endoplasmic reticulum and lipid droplets (the hallmarks of a steroid-making cell, since steroids are built on membranes and cholesterol), many mitochondria with the specialised enzymes of steroidogenesis, and in humans distinctive crystalloid inclusions called Reinke crystals.

SIZE:

Roughly 15 to 20 micrometres across, a large cell for its lipid-processing load.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

There are developmentally distinct populations - fetal Leydig cells (which masculinise the fetus) and adult Leydig cells (which arise at puberty) - representing successive waves rather than coexisting types.

WHERE IT LIVES:

In the interstitial tissue of the testis, clustered around the blood vessels between the seminiferous tubules.

NUMBER:

An adult human testis contains on the order of two to five hundred million Leydig cells, of moderate confidence, declining slowly with age.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

The Leydig cell is not an excitable spiking cell; it maintains a resting membrane potential in the region of minus 30 to minus 60 millivolts and uses calcium signalling as part of its response to LH, but it fires no action potentials - its output is steroid, released continuously rather than in electrical pulses.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

No neuronal synapses; its controlling connection is hormonal, through the LH receptor on its surface, and it engages in paracrine cross-talk with the neighbouring Sertoli cells and with testicular macrophages that support its steroidogenesis.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that the Leydig cell has no axon, secreting testosterone into the interstitial fluid and blood.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Its signalling molecules are steroid hormones, chiefly testosterone, plus insulin-like factor 3; it responds to LH and to local factors rather than releasing classical transmitters.

MYELINATION:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that the Leydig cell has no axon.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

High lipid and cholesterol traffic, since its product is made from cholesterol on the mitochondrial and smooth-endoplasmic-reticulum membranes; the rate-limiting step is the movement of cholesterol into the mitochondrion by the steroidogenic acute regulatory protein (StAR), driven by LH.

PLASTICITY:

Leydig-cell steroid output rises at puberty and is tuned throughout life by LH, adapting to demand; the cells can be lost or suppressed by chronic illness, ageing, and exogenous testosterone or opioids that switch off the LH drive.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Supported by testicular macrophages (which supply cholesterol and trophic signals), by the interstitial blood vessels that carry testosterone away, and by paracrine partnership with the Sertoli cells whose function it in turn sustains.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Delete the Leydig cell and the testis loses its testosterone: the seminiferous tubules, which need very high local androgen to make sperm, fail, and the male body-wide androgen programme collapses - the Leydig cell is the endocrine engine on which the sperm factory depends.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The Leydig cell's remarkable feature is its role in the two-cell logic of the gonad: it is the LH-driven androgen source that, in the ovary, is mirrored by the theca cell, showing that the testis and ovary run the same modular hormone-assembly line with the parts relabelled.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Luteinising hormone (principal driver); cholesterol supply; local factors from Sertoli cells and macrophages.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Testosterone (several milligrams per day in an adult man) and insulin-like factor 3 into blood and interstitial fluid.

• STATE.

◦ LH-receptor occupancy, cyclic-AMP level, StAR activity, cholesterol pool, membrane potential.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Resting potential roughly minus 30 to minus 60 millivolts; daily testosterone output about 5 to 7 milligrams; cell count hundreds of millions per testis.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Steroid output rising over minutes to hours after an LH pulse; diurnal rhythm with a morning testosterone peak; developmental waves over years.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Primary hypogonadism (testosterone deficiency from Leydig-cell failure), as in Klinefelter syndrome or after damage.

◦ Leydig-cell tumour, an androgen- or oestrogen-secreting testicular tumour.

◦ Age-related decline and suppression by exogenous androgens or opioids.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

CODE SAMPLE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

The large lipid-rich interstitial cell of the testis, carrying human-specific Reinke crystals, that answers luteinising hormone by turning cholesterol into most of the body's testosterone.


CHAPTER 75. SERTOLI CELL (Sustentacular Cell of the Seminiferous Tubule) (The Sperm-Nurse Barrier Cell) (NON-COG)

Inside the coiled tubules where sperm are made, one tall cell spans the whole wall from base to lumen and cradles the developing sperm within its branches like a tree full of nesting birds.

The Sertoli cell is the nurse, the architect, and the border guard of sperm production, and the outline rightly calls it the gonadal analogue of an astrocyte - a supportive cell that feeds, insulates, and protects the cells entrusted to it.

It answers to follicle-stimulating hormone and builds the blood-testis barrier, one of the tightest walls in the body.

Sperm-Nurse names its central job, sustaining the developing germ cells.

Barrier names its second job, the blood-testis barrier it forms with its neighbours.

Cell marks it as a supportive endocrine and sustentacular cell.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The Sertoli cell is the nurse cell of the seminiferous tubule, physically supporting and nourishing the developing sperm cells and orchestrating spermatogenesis, and it is also an endocrine cell, secreting inhibin (which feeds back to suppress follicle-stimulating hormone) and anti-Mullerian hormone (which in the fetus removes the female duct system).

Its job is to make sperm production possible and to report on it hormonally.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

A tall columnar cell stretching from the basement membrane to the tubule lumen, with an elaborate branching shape that surrounds the germ cells at every stage; adjacent Sertoli cells are joined near their base by specialised tight junctions that create the blood-testis barrier.

SIZE:

A large cell spanning the tubule wall, roughly 70 to 80 micrometres tall, each one supporting on the order of thirty to fifty germ cells.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Sertoli cells are essentially one type but pass through proliferative (immature, before puberty) and mature non-dividing states, with the adult number of Sertoli cells fixing the maximum sperm output of the testis.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Within the seminiferous tubules of the testis, forming the epithelium in which sperm develop.

NUMBER:

An adult human testis contains on the order of several hundred million to about a billion Sertoli cells, of moderate confidence, set during childhood and fixed at puberty.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

The Sertoli cell is a non-excitable support cell with a resting membrane potential in the region of minus 40 to minus 70 millivolts; it uses calcium and second-messenger signalling in response to follicle-stimulating hormone but does not fire action potentials.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

No neuronal synapses; it is coupled to germ cells and to neighbouring Sertoli cells by tight junctions, gap junctions and specialised adhesion junctions (the ectoplasmic specialisations), and it responds hormonally through the follicle-stimulating-hormone receptor and to testosterone from the Leydig cells beside it.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that the Sertoli cell has no axon, being a sustentacular and secretory epithelial cell.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Its signalling molecules are hormones - inhibin B, anti-Mullerian hormone, and androgen-binding protein (which concentrates testosterone in the tubule) - rather than classical transmitters.

MYELINATION:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that the Sertoli cell has no axon.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

High and specialised: the blood-testis barrier cuts the developing sperm off from the blood, so the Sertoli cell must feed them, notably supplying lactate as the germ cells' fuel, in a metabolic partnership that directly echoes the astrocyte feeding lactate to neurons.

PLASTICITY:

The Sertoli-cell population is set during a developmental window and largely fixed in adulthood, so its number, decided before puberty, caps lifelong sperm output; its secretory activity is tuned by follicle-stimulating hormone and testosterone.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Supported by the Leydig cells (whose testosterone it requires), by the peritubular myoid cells that wrap the tubule and help it move sperm along, and by the testicular vasculature; in turn it supports the germ cells absolutely.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove or damage the Sertoli cell and spermatogenesis stops, the blood-testis barrier fails, and the immune system may attack the germ cells it can now see - the Sertoli cell is the keystone without which the sperm-making tissue disintegrates.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The Sertoli cell's most astonishing capability is immune privilege: by building the blood-testis barrier and secreting immunosuppressive signals it hides the developing sperm, whose surface proteins the immune system has never learned to tolerate, so effectively that Sertoli cells are studied as tools to protect transplanted tissue - a support cell that is also a licensed keeper of secrets.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Follicle-stimulating hormone (principal endocrine driver); testosterone from Leydig cells; signals from the germ cells it nurses.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Inhibin B, anti-Mullerian hormone, androgen-binding protein, lactate and growth factors to the germ cells; structural support and barrier.

• STATE.

◦ Follicle-stimulating-hormone-receptor occupancy, junction integrity, lactate output, secretory activity, membrane potential.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Resting potential roughly minus 40 to minus 70 millivolts; supports about 30 to 50 germ cells each; number fixed at puberty.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Hormonal responses over minutes to hours; the spermatogenic cycle it governs lasts about 74 days in humans.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Sertoli-cell-only syndrome (tubules with no germ cells), causing infertility.

◦ Disruption of the blood-testis barrier by toxins, heat or trauma, causing autoimmune orchitis and infertility.

◦ Sertoli-cell tumour, a rare gonadal tumour.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

CODE SAMPLE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

The tall tubule-spanning nurse cell that cradles developing sperm, builds the blood-testis barrier, feeds its charges lactate like an astrocyte, and reports on sperm production through inhibin - the testis's supporting keystone.


CHAPTER 76. GRANULOSA CELL (Follicular Cell of the Ovary) (The Oestrogen-Finishing Egg-Nurse Cell) (NON-COG)

Around every developing egg in the ovary lies a growing shell of cells that both nurse the egg and manufacture the hormone that defines the female cycle.

The granulosa cell is the ovary's answer to the Sertoli cell - a follicle-stimulating-hormone-driven nurse and hormone factory - and it performs the last, decisive chemical step of oestrogen synthesis, converting androgen handed to it by its partner into oestrogen.

After the egg is released it transforms again, becoming the progesterone cell that maintains a possible pregnancy.

Oestrogen-Finishing names its signature reaction, the aromatisation that completes oestrogen synthesis.

Egg-Nurse names its supportive job, sustaining the developing egg.

Cell marks it as an endocrine and sustentacular cell.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The granulosa cell is the follicular cell surrounding the developing egg in the ovary, and it is the oestrogen-producing cell: driven by follicle-stimulating hormone (FSH), it uses the enzyme aromatase to convert androgen precursors into oestrogen (chiefly oestradiol).

Its job is to nurse the egg and to manufacture the oestrogen that drives the menstrual cycle, and after ovulation it becomes the progesterone-secreting lutein cell of the corpus luteum.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

An epithelial cell forming the layered wall (the membrana granulosa) of the ovarian follicle around the egg and its fluid-filled antrum; on luteinisation after ovulation it enlarges and fills with lipid and steroidogenic machinery to become a granulosa-lutein cell.

SIZE:

Roughly 10 to 15 micrometres in the follicular state, enlarging substantially on becoming a lutein cell.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

There are position-defined subtypes within the follicle - the cumulus granulosa cells immediately around the egg (which nurse it directly) and the mural granulosa cells lining the follicle wall (more steroidogenic) - and the post-ovulatory granulosa-lutein cell is a further transformed state.

WHERE IT LIVES:

In the ovary, forming the wall of every growing follicle, and after ovulation forming the bulk of the corpus luteum.

NUMBER:

Highly variable, since it depends on how many follicles are growing; a single mature follicle contains on the order of fifty million granulosa cells, of low-to-moderate confidence, and the population turns over continuously with each cycle.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

The granulosa cell is a non-excitable secretory cell with a resting membrane potential in the region of minus 30 to minus 70 millivolts; it is extensively coupled by gap junctions (built partly from connexin-43) through which it and the egg exchange ions and small molecules, and it uses calcium and cyclic-AMP signalling rather than action potentials.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

No neuronal synapses; it is joined to the egg and to neighbouring granulosa cells by gap junctions that are essential to nursing the egg, and it responds hormonally through the FSH receptor and later the LH receptor, working in obligatory partnership with the theca cell.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that the granulosa cell has no axon, secreting hormones directly into the follicular fluid and blood.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Its signalling molecules are steroid and peptide hormones - oestradiol, then progesterone after ovulation, plus inhibin and anti-Mullerian hormone - not classical transmitters.

MYELINATION:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that the granulosa cell has no axon.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Substantial lipid and steroid processing, especially after luteinisation when it becomes a progesterone factory; it also metabolically supports the egg, passing nutrients and signals through its gap junctions.

PLASTICITY:

The granulosa cell is dramatically plastic across the cycle: it proliferates as the follicle grows, switches from oestrogen to progesterone production at ovulation, and either sustains the corpus luteum or dies back if no pregnancy follows, making it one of the most cyclically remodelled cells in the adult body.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Supported by the theca cells (its obligatory hormone partner and blood supply, since the avascular granulosa layer depends on the theca's vessels for cholesterol and precursors) and by the ovarian stroma and vasculature.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Delete or silence the granulosa cell and the ovary makes no oestrogen and nurses no egg: the menstrual cycle stops, ovulation fails, and the oestrogen-dependent tissues of the body, including oestrogen-sensitive brain circuits, lose their signal.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The granulosa cell's most remarkable capability is its wholesale reprogramming at ovulation - within about a day the same cell stops making oestrogen and starts making progesterone, converting an egg-nursing follicle into a pregnancy-supporting gland - a hormonal identity switch few cells in the body can match.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Follicle-stimulating hormone, then luteinising hormone; androgen precursors handed over by theca cells; signals from the egg.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Oestradiol (rising to a pre-ovulatory peak), then progesterone after ovulation, plus inhibin and anti-Mullerian hormone.

• STATE.

◦ FSH- and LH-receptor occupancy, aromatase activity, gap-junction coupling to the egg, luteinisation status, membrane potential.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Resting potential roughly minus 30 to minus 70 millivolts; tens of millions of cells in a mature follicle; oestradiol output peaking just before ovulation.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Follicle growth over about two weeks; the oestrogen-to-progesterone switch within roughly a day of the LH surge; corpus luteum lifespan about two weeks without pregnancy.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Polycystic ovary syndrome, in which follicle maturation and granulosa-cell aromatisation are disturbed.

◦ Premature ovarian insufficiency, the early loss of follicles and their granulosa cells.

◦ Granulosa-cell tumour, an oestrogen-secreting ovarian tumour.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

CODE SAMPLE:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that this cell serves a substrate role rather than a computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

The follicle-stimulating-hormone-driven follicular cell that nurses the egg through gap junctions and completes oestrogen synthesis by aromatisation, then reprograms overnight into the progesterone cell of the corpus luteum.


CHAPTER 77. THECA CELL (Theca Interna Cell of the Ovarian Follicle) (The Androgen-Supplier Partner Cell) (NON-COG)

Wrapping the outside of the growing ovarian follicle is a layer of cells that never finish the hormone themselves but supply the crucial raw material, handing androgen inward to the granulosa cells that turn it into oestrogen.

The theca cell is the ovarian twin of the testis's Leydig cell - luteinising-hormone-driven, androgen-making - and it is the second half of the two-cell, two-gonadotropin partnership that no single ovarian cell can perform alone.

It is the supplier in a two-station assembly line, and the whole female cycle depends on the handoff.

Androgen-Supplier names its product and role, the aromatase substrate it provides.

Partner names its defining relationship, its obligatory pairing with the granulosa cell.

Cell marks it as an endocrine secretory cell.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The theca cell is the androgen-producing cell of the ovarian follicle, sitting in the theca interna layer around the granulosa cells; driven by luteinising hormone (LH), it converts cholesterol into androgens (chiefly androstenedione) which it hands to the granulosa cells for conversion into oestrogen.

Its job is to supply the precursor that the granulosa cell, which cannot make androgen itself, needs to complete oestrogen synthesis - the essence of the two-cell, two-gonadotropin model.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

A steroid-making cell of the theca interna with the lipid droplets, smooth endoplasmic reticulum and steroidogenic mitochondria typical of its trade, sitting outside the follicle's basement membrane and richly served by blood vessels.

SIZE:

Roughly 10 to 15 micrometres across.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

There are two theca layers - the hormone-producing, vascular theca interna and the outer fibrous theca externa (which is structural and contractile) - and after ovulation the theca interna cells become the theca-lutein cells of the corpus luteum.

WHERE IT LIVES:

In the ovary, forming the theca layers that wrap each growing follicle, just outside the granulosa layer and its basement membrane.

NUMBER:

Variable with the number of growing follicles; each mature follicle is wrapped by a theca population smaller than its granulosa population, turning over with each cycle, of low-to-moderate confidence.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

The theca cell is a non-excitable steroidogenic cell with a resting membrane potential in the region of minus 30 to minus 60 millivolts; it uses cyclic-AMP and calcium signalling in response to LH and fires no action potentials.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

No neuronal synapses; its defining connection is the paracrine handoff of androgen across the follicular basement membrane to the granulosa cells, and it responds hormonally through the LH receptor, in obligatory partnership with the granulosa cell.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that the theca cell has no axon, releasing androgens into the follicular tissue and blood.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Its signalling molecules are steroid hormones, chiefly the androgen androstenedione (and some testosterone), the aromatase substrate for the granulosa cell.

MYELINATION:

This section does not apply to this cell type for the reason that the theca cell has no axon.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Cholesterol and lipid processing typical of a steroid-making cell; unlike the avascular granulosa layer it lies next to blood vessels and draws cholesterol directly from the circulation, which is one reason the labour is divided between the two cells.

PLASTICITY:

Theca cells are recruited and expand as follicles grow, become theca-lutein cells after ovulation, and regress with the corpus luteum, cycling with the ovary; excess theca-cell androgen production is central to common ovarian disorders.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Supported by the ovarian stroma and its own blood vessels, and locked in mutual dependence with the granulosa cells it supplies and which in turn depend on it for precursor.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove the theca cell and the granulosa cell has no androgen to aromatise, so oestrogen synthesis collapses even if the granulosa cell is intact - the theca cell is the indispensable upstream supplier of the ovarian hormone line.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The theca cell's most illuminating feature is that it makes the two-cell, two-gonadotropin division of labour physically necessary: androgen synthesis (LH-driven, in the theca) and aromatisation (follicle-stimulating hormone (FSH)-driven, in the granulosa) are placed in separate cells across a basement membrane, so oestrogen production is a genuine two-station assembly line requiring both cells and both gonadotropins - and its testicular mirror is the Leydig-and-Sertoli pair.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Luteinising hormone (principal driver); cholesterol from the blood; local factors from granulosa cells.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Androgens, chiefly androstenedione, handed to the granulosa cells; some androgen to the blood.

• STATE.

◦ LH-receptor occupancy, cyclic-AMP level, androgen output, luteinisation status, membrane potential.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Resting potential roughly minus 30 to minus 60 millivolts; androgen output scaling with LH and follicle stage.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Steroid output rising over minutes to hours after LH; recruitment and regression over the roughly four-week ovarian cycle.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Polycystic ovary syndrome, in which theca cells over-produce androgen, driving the disorder's hyperandrogenism and disrupted ovulation.

◦ Ovarian hyperthecosis, a more severe theca-driven androgen excess.

◦ Theca-cell (thecoma) tumours, which can secrete hormone.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:
